# Decision Log — Boston Startup Tracker ServiceNow Replatform

The single source of truth for **why** every non-trivial choice in this delivery was made the way it was.

The delivery replatforms the Boston Startup Tracker — a tracker of venture-backed companies headquartered in the Boston area — from a Flask, React, PostgreSQL, Redis, Elasticsearch, Celery and Docker scaffold onto the ServiceNow Now Platform, as one scoped application: `x_bst_startuptrk`, vendor prefix `x_bst`, version `1.0.0`.

## Purpose and Rule 1 compliance

This file is the deliverable required by **Rule 1 (Explainability)**. That rule requires every non-trivial implementation decision to be documented with rationale, defines a decision as non-trivial when a competent engineer could reasonably have chosen differently, and requires the record to take the form of a Markdown table stating what was decided, what alternatives existed, why the choice was made and what risks it carries. Those four things are the four substantive columns of every table below.

Rule 1 imposes three further obligations that shape this file:

1. **The non-trivial test is the inclusion criterion.** A choice with exactly one sane option earns no row. Defining tables through the platform's table dictionary, for instance, is not a decision — there is no other mechanism. Storing `portfolio_count` rather than computing it on every read is a decision, because both are workable and a competent engineer could have picked either.
2. **Every deviation from a literal or obvious reading of the requirements has its own explicit row**, because Rule 1 treats an unexplained deviation as a defect. **256** rows are so marked — the count is verifiable by counting the `DEV` cells in the Flags column, and it agrees with the figure in [Summary counts](#summary-counts) — and [Index of deviation rows](#index-of-deviation-rows) lists every one.
3. **No rationale lives in code.** Script Include headers, business rule comments and platform record `description` fields in this delivery state only what an artifact does and which requirement it implements. They are pointers, never arguments. Every *why* is here.

**Sixteen documents state that they carry no rationale and defer every *why* to this file.** They are enumerated here so the count is checkable rather than asserted — five top-level documents of the deliverable package, `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/`: `data-model.md`, `access-control.md`, `api-reference.md`, `gaps-and-flags.md`, `manual-build-instructions.md`; the six manual-build guides beneath it, `manual-build/01-connection-credential-aliases.md` through `manual-build/06-staging-table-csv-import.md`; `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/sample-data/README.md`; this log's sibling `./TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md`; and the three operational documents, each of which makes the declaration in the vocabulary of its own genre — `deployment-runbook.md` states that it carries **procedure** only, `validation-gates.md` that it carries **assertions and their pass and fail conditions** only, and `validation-checklist.md` that it carries **criteria, evidence and pass conditions** only, all three naming this file as the home of every decision behind them. That is 5 plus 6 plus 1 plus 1 plus 3, which is sixteen. `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/README.md` additionally directs every *why* to this governance tree without using the same wording, and is not counted among the sixteen.

This file is therefore written to be **self-contained**: it never sends a reader elsewhere to find a reason. References to sibling documents below point at *specifications* — what a thing is — never at explanations.

## How rationale is partitioned

| Question | Where it is answered |
| --- | --- |
| **What** an artifact is, and which requirement it satisfies | The deliverable package documents, and the `description` field of each platform record |
| **How** to build or deploy it | The six manual-build guides and the deployment runbook |
| **Why** it is that way, what else was considered, what risk it carries | **This file, and only this file** |
| **Which** source construct or requirement each artifact traces to | `docs/decisions/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` |

Rule 1 bundles a second obligation into the same clause as the decision log: for a migration or refactor, a **bidirectional traceability matrix** mapping source constructs to target implementations at full coverage with no gaps. That obligation is discharged by the sibling file **`docs/decisions/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md`**, not here, and this log deliberately duplicates none of its content. Both halves must exist for Rule 1 to be satisfied.

## Which side governs

**The frozen prompt and the Agent Action Plan govern. A delivered artifact never does.**

Every count, inventory, gate set and field list in this project has an authority, and it is always the specification. Where a delivered artifact departs from a plan-mandated figure or rule, that departure is a **defect in the artifact** and it remains one until the artifact is aligned. It is not a revision of the plan, it is not licence to restate the plan's figure as an estimate, and it is not settled by noting the difference.

Three consequences bind this log, and an earlier revision of it broke all three:

1. **A row may not justify exceeding a plan-mandated figure by describing what was built.** Where the plan states an exact count — eight Script Includes at section 0.4.5, eleven system properties at section 0.4.7, three business rules and one scheduled job at section 0.4.2, eleven post-commit gates at section 0.11.2 — the delivery matches it or the delivery is wrong. `D-036` and `D-069` are the two rows that once read the other way, and both now record the correction rather than the excess.
2. **A row may not resolve a conflict between the specification and a delivered design choice in the design choice's favour.** Where the two conflict, the artifact changes. `D-068` is the row that once did this, and it also rested on a platform claim that was too narrow; both are recorded in that row's own supersession note, which names `D-310` and `D-311` as the rows that now describe the delivery. **Where the artifact cannot change** — because the plan's literal form is unavailable on the platform, as a row read of a sealed table is — the departure is not argued into equivalence either: it is accepted by a named human, which is what `D-455` and section B4a of the validation checklist are for.
3. **Approximation is honoured as approximation.** Where the plan writes a figure as approximate — "~85 dictionary records", "~250 to 350 update records" — a delivered figure inside a reasonable band of it satisfies the plan, because an approximation is what the plan mandated. That is not the same as exceeding an exact figure, and this log does not conflate them.

The sibling `docs/decisions/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` states the same rule as its **Status** column: every measure is marked `Matches` or `GAP`, `GAP` means an open defect in the artifact, and no row is asserted on the delivered figure's own authority.

**Two schema departures were carried by earlier revisions of this log, and both are now closed by changing the artifact.** The staging table's `import_state` list carried a fifth member beyond the four AAP section 1.0 freezes, and the rate-limit counter carried a fifth column beyond the four AAP section 0.4.2 names. Each was declared here rather than corrected — which is precisely the failure this section forbids, because a decision row cannot authorize an implementation that conflicts with the specification, however carefully the conflict is disclosed. Both columns and the extra state are **deleted from the delivered Update Set**: `D-386` returns the claim to the existing `import_run` column under a reserved prefix, `D-387` returns row identity to the four-column tuple, and `D-411` records the principle. **No row of this log now records an outstanding departure from a plan-mandated schema, count or field list.** Where a row's text still describes one, it carries an amendment marker naming the row that closed it.

## Behaviour preservation is not the invariant

Ordinary migration logic — preserve observable behaviour, change only the implementation — does not apply here, and saying so up front prevents a whole class of misreading.

**The legacy application cannot start.** Twelve broken references were verified directly in the tree. The continuous-integration workflow installs from a requirements file that does not exist [`.github/workflows/ci.yml:L24`] and then runs a frontend install in a directory that has no manifest [`.github/workflows/ci.yml:L36-L37`]. The root manifest declares an entry point that is absent [`package.json:L5`] and is not valid JSON in any case, because a comment block begins after the closing brace [`package.json:L65`]. The test configuration collects files matching a leading `test_` [`pytest.ini:L9`] while all 21 test modules on disk carry a trailing `_test.py`, so 0 of 27 test files are collectable. A `src/utils/` package is imported by three modules [`src/data_collection/api_integrators/crunchbase_integrator.py:L8`] and does not exist. The single-page application imports a theme module [`src/frontend/App.tsx:L12`] that was never authored — `src/frontend/styles/` contains only `global.css`. Three Compose services declare a Dockerfile [`docker-compose.yml:L6-L8`] that exists nowhere in the tree.

There is consequently **no runtime behaviour to preserve**. What is preserved instead is the **domain-model semantics** — the entities, their relationships and their meaning — and the **route and entity inventory**. What is deliberately *not* preserved is the API contract, the field list, the enumerations, the role model and the cleaning rules, every one of which the requirements re-specify. The safety mechanism substituted for incremental behaviour comparison is deployment-level: validate, preview, require an empty error-type problem set, commit, gate, and roll back on failure.

## How to read this log

Every table in this file carries the **same seven columns**.

| Column | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| **ID** | A stable `D-###` identifier, zero-padded to three digits, allocated in section order and never reused or renumbered. Sibling documents and the Rule 3 review cite these. |
| **Decision** | What was decided. The first of Rule 1's four mandated items. |
| **Alternatives considered** | What else a competent engineer could have chosen. The second mandated item. Never left empty — a decision with no real alternative is trivial and earns no row. |
| **Rationale** | Why this choice, citing the code, convention or verified finding behind it. The third mandated item. |
| **Risks** | What the choice costs or exposes. The fourth mandated item. Stated concretely, or explicitly recorded as none identified with the reason. |
| **Risk level** | High, Medium or Low, in the vocabulary Rule 3 uses. |
| **Flags** | Cross-reference markers, described below. |

The three columns beyond Rule 1's four are additive and are themselves logged as a deviation at **D-083**.

**Flag markers.**

| Marker | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `R3-1` … `R3-5` | One of the five entries of `docs/review/CRITICAL_DECISIONS.md`, required by **Rule 3 (Critical Decision Review Document)** to be a strict subset of this log. The number is that document's rank order, highest risk first. |
| `DEV` | A deviation from a literal or obvious reading of the requirements, entered explicitly as Rule 1 demands. |
| `AMB-1` … `AMB-4` | An assumption that resolved an ambiguity in the request. Rule 3 requires these to be called out. |
| `IRREV` | An irreversible operation. Rule 3 requires these to be called out. |
| `AUTHZ` | An authorization decision. Rule 3 requires these to be called out. |

Marking is what makes the Rule 3 subset relationship **demonstrable by inspection** rather than asserted: filtering the `Flags` column for `R3-` yields exactly five rows, and those five are the review document's five entries.

## 1. Authority and scope

Seven decisions. These govern every later one, because they settle which input wins when the inputs disagree — and they did disagree, materially.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-001** | Split authority between the two governing inputs: the requirements prompt is definitive for all **application content** — scope name, tables, fields, roles, access controls, REST resources, flows and portal routes — while the deployment environment brief is definitive only for **deployment mechanics**, with its application-specific identifiers substituted throughout. | Follow the brief literally, and build what it names. Or discard the brief entirely and rely on the prompt alone. | The brief describes a **different application**. Its scope namespace `x_casemgmt`, its three case tables, its three case roles, its artifact path under a `servicenow-case-management-poc/` directory, its `acm-*` source directories and its `pom.xml` were each searched for tree-wide and **none exists in this repository**. Following it literally would build the wrong application; discarding it would leave the Update Set with no defined import path, since it is the only source for the upload, preview, commit, gate and rollback procedure. The prompt claims sole authority over requirements and the data model; the brief's own scope section frames itself purely as a deployment and testing procedure. Each is therefore followed where it is competent. | A reader comparing the brief to the delivery finds no identifier in common and may conclude the brief was ignored. Mitigated by stating the substitution explicitly wherever the procedure appears. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-002** | Deliver into the **same repository** as a new top-level `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/` subtree, laid out as `update-set/`, `scripts/`, `docs/` and `sample-data/`. | A separate repository for the platform deliverable. A subtree at a different path, or a flat layout with no subdirectories. | Nothing in the requirements asks for a new repository, and a split would separate the deliverable from the legacy tree that the traceability matrix must keep readable. The layout is the environment brief's own `servicenow-<app>-poc/{update-set,docs}/` convention transposed onto this application, so an operator who knows the convention finds the Update Set and the gate document where expected. | The repository now holds two unrelated technology stacks, which can confuse a newcomer about what is live. Mitigated by the root README pointer and the supersession notice recorded at D-080. | Low | — |
| **D-003** | Retain the entire legacy tree **read-only**: nothing under `src/`, `tests/`, `config/`, `documentation/` or the retired manifests is deleted, and it is read only for repository conventions, the documentation inventory and the traceability spine — never for schema, roles or endpoint structure. | Delete the retired stack, since it cannot run. Or treat it as a design reference and port its schema and routes forward. | Deleting it would break the traceability matrix, which must map source constructs to target artifacts and therefore needs the sources to remain readable. Treating it as a design reference is forbidden by the requirements and would be actively harmful: its cleaning code contradicts every mandated cleaning rule, and its startup model carries four attributes the binding field list excludes. The narrow reading — conventions, inventory and spine — is what lets the tree serve traceability without contaminating the design. | A future maintainer may mistake retained code for supported code. Mitigated by the root README supersession notice. | Medium | — |
| **D-004** | Treat the requirements' statement that refactoring is not applicable as scoped to **ServiceNow code**, while treating the repository-level work as a technology-stack migration that triggers Rule 1's traceability matrix obligation. | Read the statement globally and produce no matrix. Or ignore it and treat the whole exercise as a refactor of the legacy tree. | Both statements hold at once once the scope of each is fixed. No ServiceNow code pre-exists inside `x_bst_startuptrk`, so nothing there is being refactored and the statement is true as written. At repository level an entire polyglot stack is replaced by declarative platform metadata, which is unambiguously a migration, and Rule 1 requires a matrix for one. Producing no matrix would fail Rule 1 outright. | A reviewer reading only the requirements may see the matrix as unrequested scope. Mitigated by this row and by the matrix's own statement of provenance. | Low | — |
| **D-005** | Partition rationale strictly: platform record `description` fields, Script Include headers and business rule comments state only **what** an artifact does and **which** requirement it implements; every *why*, alternative and risk lives in this file alone. | Follow platform convention and write explanatory headers into Script Includes and record descriptions. Or omit descriptions entirely to avoid any appearance of rationale in code. | Rule 1 forbids rationale in code comments, while platform convention expects descriptive headers and gives every record a `description` field. Reducing those fields to a pointer — naming the behaviour and the requirement, arguing nothing — satisfies the rule literally and keeps the records useful. Omitting descriptions would make the delivered artifacts harder to navigate for no compliance gain. | A maintainer editing a Script Include sees no reasoning at the point of change and may not know this log exists. Mitigated by each record naming the requirement it implements, which is the lookup key into this log. | Medium | — |
| **D-006** | Adopt **domain-model semantics plus the route and entity inventory** as the migration invariant, and explicitly abandon behaviour preservation. | Attempt behaviour-preserving migration with before-and-after comparison. Or declare the work greenfield and carry nothing forward. | The legacy application cannot start, so there is no behaviour to compare against — twelve broken references were verified, among them a test pattern matching 0 of 27 files [`pytest.ini:L9`] and an imported theme module that was never authored [`src/frontend/App.tsx:L12`]. Declaring the work greenfield would discard the entity and route inventory, which is the one thing the legacy tree legitimately contributes and which the traceability matrix needs. | Without behavioural equivalence there is no automatic regression signal, so correctness rests entirely on the test suite and the acceptance criteria. Accepted, and the reason the coverage gate is a hard delivery precondition. | High | — |
| **D-007** | Require **every** payload record in the Update Set to carry the scoped application as its scope, and target no table outside the application. | Let records default their scope at import time. Or ship a small number of global-scope helpers for convenience. | A record emitted without an explicit scope lands in the Global scope on import, which breaches the prohibition on modifying anything outside `x_bst_startuptrk` at the very moment of import — a compliance failure caused by an omission rather than an intent. Global helpers would breach the same prohibition deliberately. | Nothing the application needs may live outside the scope, which is what forces the impersonation users to be created at run time rather than shipped, recorded at D-026. | Medium | — |

**On D-001.** The substitution is mechanical and total: `x_bst_startuptrk` replaces the brief's scope namespace, this application's seven entity tables and three roles replace its case tables and case roles, and the deliverable path becomes `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/`. What is adopted unchanged is everything application-agnostic: the instance URL, Basic authentication through the two environment secrets — which **D-144** later qualifies, because four steps of the sequence go through the platform's own web routes and authenticate by session cookie and form token instead — the brief's three pre-flight checks, to which a release-floor check is added as a fourth, the six-step import sequence with its polling intervals and its 300-second load, 600-second preview and 1200-second commit timeouts, the post-commit gating, the scope-deletion rollback and the failure-handling matrix.

---

## 2. Data model

Sixteen decisions, `D-008` through `D-023`. The field list itself is binding and complete, so almost nothing here is about *which* columns exist — it is about the mechanisms the platform offers for expressing them, where more than one mechanism would work.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-008** | Read "exactly 7 custom tables" as a count of **entity** tables, and create **10** physical tables: the 7 entity tables carrying 53 columns, plus a join table, a staging table and a rate-limit counter table, each documented separately as a supporting artifact. | Count all physical tables and report a breach of the seven-table constraint. Or force the supporting structures into the seven, for example by storing staged rows on the entity tables with a status column. | The requirements name the join table `x_bst_startuptrk_m2m_round_investor` explicitly, mandate a staging table for the fallback dataset, and fix a rate-limit response body that cannot be produced without per-caller accounting. All three are therefore required by the same document that says seven, so seven cannot mean seven physical tables. Reading it as seven entity tables makes every requirement satisfiable at once and keeps the acceptance count unambiguous. Folding staged rows onto entity tables would put unvalidated upstream data inside the tables the portal reads. | An acceptance reviewer counting tables finds ten. Mitigated by the counting rule being stated wherever the count is asserted, and by the 53 columns being counted only across the seven. | Medium | `DEV` `AMB-1` |
| **D-009** | Drop the legacy `User` model, route and service entirely, and express identity as the platform's own user table plus the three scoped roles `x_bst_startuptrk.admin`, `x_bst_startuptrk.user` and `x_bst_startuptrk.premium_user`. | Recreate a custom user table inside the scope. Or keep a scoped profile table joined to the platform user for application-specific attributes. | The platform already supplies authentication, identity and role membership, and reimplementing them would mean maintaining a second password store — the legacy model carried its own hash column [`src/backend/models/user.py:L11`] — with no requirement asking for one. The legacy role model was a bare string column with a default and no enforcement [`src/backend/models/user.py:L14`]. No application-specific user attribute survives the binding field list, so a profile table would have no columns. | The three role names coincide exactly with the members of the legacy enumeration [`src/shared/types.ts:L72-L75`], so a reader may assume the role model was ported. It was not: these are platform roles evaluated by the access-control engine, not a string compared in application code. | Low | — |
| **D-010** | Make the **join table `x_bst_startuptrk_m2m_round_investor` authoritative for every write**, and carry `participating_investors` on the funding round as a **stored, read-only `glide_list` projection** — `read_only` in the dictionary, 4000 characters — refreshed one way from the join table by `InvestorPortfolioService.refreshRoundParticipants()` and never a write target. **Superseded in part by D-434, completed in the artifact at D-448:** the column ships **calculated and virtual** rather than stored — its calculation calls `InvestorPortfolioService.participantList()` on every read — so nothing is projected, nothing is refreshed and nothing can drift. The join table being authoritative stands unchanged, and it is now the **only** store. Surface the join table on the form as a related list beside it. | Omit the column entirely and rely on the related list alone. Or make the column the store of record and drop the join table. Or make the column writable and fan writes out into join rows, keeping both in step. | The requirements describe the field as a List and, in the same clause, name a physical join table — two different platform mechanisms, only one of which can own the data. **The column cannot be omitted**: it is one of the 53 columns of a field list the requirements declare binding and complete, so its absence would be a breach. **The join table cannot be dropped**: it is named explicitly. The projection satisfies both by making the join table the single write path and the column a derived read, refreshed in one direction only. That is not a dual write — nothing writes the column except the refresh, and nothing reads the join table to decide what to write into it. Making the column writable **would** be a dual write, and dual writes drift silently and would corrupt the portfolio derivation. The column is also what the platform list and form views and the portal actually read, because each reads a field on the row and cannot dot-walk a many-to-many; making it read-only and derived gives the List experience with **one** write path. | The column is a derived value stored on the row, so it drifts if a join row is created on a path that skips the refresh — the same class of risk as `portfolio_count` at D-013, and mitigated the same way: the refresh is invoked from the join table's business rule and from `IngestionMapper.linkParticipants()`, and the service exposes a recalculate path to run after any bulk load. The projection is also **bounded**: the column's declared length of 4000 holds 121 comma-separated 32-character identifiers, and a round linking more investors than that has its projection **emptied and the overflow logged**, never stored in part — a truncated identifier list is indistinguishable from a complete one when read, so an empty column plus an error entry is the safer failure. The join table remains complete in that case, and both the API array and `portfolio_count` remain correct because neither reads the column. A reviewer must read participants from the join table, never from the column, when completeness matters. Put plainly, the derived column is a **cache**: it is exactly as current as the rule that maintains it, and a join row written on a path that skips business rules leaves the column stale while the join table stays correct. **None of that drift risk survives D-434**, and this paragraph describes the superseded stored projection rather than the delivery: the value is computed on every read, so there is no cache to go stale and no refresh to skip. The residual risks are a failed calculation, which costs the form its inline display for one round and nothing else, and a round linking more than 121 investors, whose value is **cut at a whole-identifier boundary** with a `gs.warn` naming the round, the number linked and the capacity — not emptied. | Medium | `DEV` `AMB-2` `R3-4` |
| **D-011** | Have every API response assemble `participating_investors` as a **read-only JSON array** of investor references read from **the join table itself** through one batched call, `InvestorPortfolioService.participantsForRounds()`, de-duplicated — rather than from the stored column of D-010 and rather than as a writable field. | Serialise the stored column, which is already on the row and needs no second query. Or emit it as a delimited string to imitate a list column. Or omit it from responses and require a second call against the join table. Or accept writes to it and fan them out into join rows. | An API consumer expects a list-valued attribute to arrive as an array, so an array is the honest projection of the join rows. Assembling from the join table rather than the stored column is deliberate: the join table is complete where the stored column is length-bounded, so the API is correct even for a round whose projection was truncated. Accepting writes would reintroduce the dual-write drift that D-010 exists to prevent, since the same data would then have two write paths. Omitting it would force every client to understand an internal structure. One batched call rather than one per row is what keeps a page of funding rounds to a single query, and it gives the REST operations and the refresh business rule one shared reader so the two cannot diverge. | The asymmetry is real: the field can be read but not written, which will surprise a client that assumes symmetry. And the API array and the stored column can differ for a round beyond the projection bound — the array is the correct one. Mitigated by documenting the field as read-only, by the API reading the join table, and by keeping the lead investor a genuinely separate single reference. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-012** | Set **cascade delete** on all five mandatory `startup` child references and on both parent references of the join table. | Clear the reference instead of deleting the child, leaving orphaned rows. Or restrict the delete so a parent with children cannot be removed. | The requirements do not mention cascade behaviour at all, so a choice was unavoidable. Clearing is not available in substance: the child references are mandatory, so a cleared reference produces a row that violates its own dictionary constraint, and orphaned funding rounds would keep contributing to the portfolio derivation for a startup that no longer exists. Restricting the delete would leave an administrator unable to remove a startup without manually clearing five child tables first. | Cascade is destructive: deleting one startup silently removes its founders, executives, funding rounds, job postings and news articles. A reviewer must exercise a startup deletion end to end before accepting this. | High | — |
| **D-013** | Maintain `portfolio_count` as a **stored integer** kept current by business rules, rather than as a dictionary-level calculated value. | A calculated field evaluated on read. Or a scheduled recomputation of every investor on a timer. | The requirements declare the type as integer and the field as calculated, which the platform can express either way. A calculated value would re-run a two-table traversal on every read and could be neither queried nor sorted efficiently, and the investor profile surfaces it alongside sortable lists. A timer would leave the value visibly stale between runs. | A stored aggregate drifts whenever data arrives on a path that skips business rules — an import transform with rule execution disabled is the realistic case. Mitigated by requiring the flows to run business rules and by the service exposing a recalculate-all method to run after any bulk load. | High | — |
| **D-014** | Derive the count as the number of **distinct** startups reachable by **either** path — a round whose lead investor is the investor, or a join row linking the investor to a round — unioning the two identifier sets before counting, and recalculate only the **affected** investors on change, including both the previous and the new lead investor on an update. | Count rounds rather than distinct startups. Count only one traversal path. Or recompute every investor on any change. | Counting rounds would inflate the figure for an investor that backed one company twice, and counting one path would silently omit either lead or participating relationships. Unioning before counting means an investor that led one round and participated in another round of the same company counts that company once, which is what a portfolio count means. Recomputing every investor on any change is quadratic in the data volume and would make bulk ingestion pathologically slow. | Missing the previous lead investor on an update leaves that investor's count too high, and the error is silent. This is the specific case a reviewer should exercise. | Medium | — |
| **D-015** | Implement `focus_areas` as a **choice-backed multi-select** drawing on the same values as the startup industry list. | A list over a reference table of industry records. Or a plain delimited string with no choice backing. | The requirements describe it as multi-choice with the same values as an existing choice list, which is a description of a choice-backed control, not of a reference relationship. Creating an industry reference table to hold six fixed values would add an eleventh table for no gain and would put the same vocabulary in two places. A plain string would accept any value and defeat normalisation. | Multi-select values are stored delimited, so a query for one focus area must use a contains match rather than equality. Accepted; the portal renders them as read-only labels and does not query on them. | Low | — |
| **D-016** | Use the platform **currency** type for the four money columns rather than a plain decimal. | A plain decimal or floating-point column, given that the column names already fix the denomination as United States dollars. | The requirements declare the type as Currency and that declaration is binding, so a decimal would contradict it. The consideration worth recording is that the currency type carries a reference and a session currency, which is more machinery than a dollar-denominated figure strictly needs. | Currency is more machinery than a dollar-denominated figure needs, and the platform's own form and list views render it with a session-currency conversion the API does not. **The serialised shape is a bare string, not an object**: `RestResponseBuilder.valueOf()` answers `String(record.getValue(field))` for a `currency` column, and `null` when the column is empty — so `"total_funding_usd": "48000000"`, never `{"value": …, "display_value": …}`. The object form is reserved for **reference** columns alone. A consumer therefore parses a decimal string and must not assume a number type; the API reference states the shape for every one of the four columns. | Low | — |
| **D-017** | Keep **Founder and Executive as two separate tables** with distinct title choice lists, rather than one person table with a role flag. | One person table with a role or type column, which would halve the table count and share one set of access controls. | The requirements state the separation is intentional and must be preserved, so a single table is not available. It is worth recording that the legacy repository had independently made the same choice, and that its executive model carried a column set of the same shape as its founder model [`src/backend/models/executive.py:L8-L12`] — so the separation is not an artifact of the requirements alone. | Two tables mean two of everything: two sets of access controls, two field-level email controls, two test suites. Accepted as the cost of a binding directive. | Low | — |
| **D-018** | Serve all six ingested record types from **one** staging table, discriminated by a `record_type` column. | One staging table per record type, giving six tables each matching its own payload shape exactly. Or a single table holding only an untyped payload column. | The requirements say "a staging table", singular. Six tables would multiply the import configuration sixfold, and the fallback branch would then need to know which table to read for which entity. One table keeps the fallback branch uniform and the import mapping one-to-one by header name. | The single table is a wide union of columns, so most columns are empty on most rows, and a reader inspecting it sees a sparse structure. Accepted; the row's `record_type` states which columns are meaningful. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-019** | Carry **both** a flattened set of scalar columns **and** a `raw_payload` JSON column on every staging row. | Flattened columns only, discarding the source shape. Or the raw payload only, parsing it in the transform. | The requirements ask the staging shape to mimic the expected API response, which the raw payload preserves verbatim; they also make CSV import the fallback load mechanism, and a flattened column set is what makes that import a direct mapping by header name with no scripted parsing. Each column serves a requirement the other cannot. Discarding the raw payload would lose the source vocabulary; parsing JSON in the transform would make a hand-edited fallback CSV impractical to author. | The same value exists twice on one row, so the two can disagree if a row is edited by hand. Mitigated by the transform reading the flattened columns and treating the payload as provenance only. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-020** | Express staging references as **natural keys** rather than record identifiers, and give the staging `active` column a string type with **no dictionary default**. | Reference the target records by identifier, which would require the CSV author to know platform identifiers that do not exist until after import. Or give `active` a boolean type with a default of true. | Natural keys are the only form a hand-authored fallback CSV can carry, because record identifiers are assigned at insert time. On `active`, a boolean column coerces a blank to false and a defaulted column substitutes a value — either way the absence of a mandatory value becomes invisible, and the requirements mandate **rejecting** a record that lacks a mandatory field rather than filling it. A defaultless string lets the blank survive to the validation step, which then rejects the row. | Natural keys must resolve at transform time, so a typo in a company name produces an unresolved reference rather than a load error. Mitigated by the transform logging the unresolved key and skipping the row. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-021** | Introduce a dedicated **rate-limit counter table**, which has no legacy counterpart, holding a window key, the caller, the resource, the window start and the request count. | Reuse an existing platform counter or log table. Or hold the counters in a cache or in memory. | The mandated rate-limit response body includes a computed retry value in seconds, and no such body can be produced without knowing when the current window started and how many requests it has taken. No platform table records that per caller and per resource. In-memory state does not survive across nodes or restarts, and caching is excluded from scope. | A fifth table exists purely as internal machinery and must be kept out of reach of the two non-administrative roles. Handled by the supporting-table access controls. | Medium | — |
| **D-022** | **Replace** the legacy enumerations rather than translate them, authoring no old-to-new mapping table. Route an unmatched incoming value to "Other" on the **five** entity choice lists that declare that member — `startup.industry`, `founder.title`, `executive.title`, `investor.focus_areas` and `job_posting.department`. On the **six** that do not — `startup.funding_stage`, `startup.employee_count_range`, `investor.type`, `funding_round.round_type`, `job_posting.remote_type` and `job_posting.seniority` — leave the column **unwritten** and log the value; **the record itself still inserts**, because none of the six is a mandatory column and rejection is reserved for a record missing a mandatory field. | Author a mapping table from the legacy vocabularies to the new ones. Or add an "Other" member to the six lists that lack one, for symmetry with the five that have it. Or reject the whole record when a choice value does not match. Or store the unmatched value verbatim. | The vocabularies do not overlap enough for a mapping to be meaningful: the legacy funding stages ran from `SEED` to `IPO` across eight values [`src/shared/constants.ts:L11-L20`] none of which matches a target label, investor types carried six values including `INCUBATOR` [`src/shared/constants.ts:L23-L30`] against five targets, and job departments carried ten [`src/shared/constants.ts:L33-L44`] against six. The six-versus-five split is not a choice this project made: prompt section 1.0 enumerates each list member by member and declares the field definitions binding and complete, and six of the eleven lists it enumerates simply contain no "Other". Adding the member is therefore unavailable, and the requirement that an unmatched value map to "Other" cannot be met on those six — the two requirements conflict and the binding one wins. Of the remaining options, rejecting the record contradicts the requirement's own division of outcomes, since rejection is specified for a **mandatory** field and none of the six is mandatory; and storing the value verbatim defeats the column, leaving a filter, a report and a chart bucket each disagreeing about the value set. Leaving the column unwritten and logging is the only outcome that keeps the record, keeps the column inside its own list and keeps the event visible. `IngestionMapper.normaliseChoices()` deletes the key rather than assigning a blank, so on an **update** a good value already on the record survives a later run that supplied a bad one. | The asymmetry is easy to miss: five lists coerce and six leave the column empty, so the same malformed feed degrades one field silently on six columns and visibly on five. Mitigated by logging every occurrence as a `choice_unmatched` event at warn severity carrying the column, the value and an `outcome` of `stored as Other` or `left unwritten`, by asserting both branches in the ATF suites, and by flagging the whole conflict as **F13** in `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/gaps-and-flags.md` rather than leaving it to be discovered. A build that "fixed" the asymmetry by adding the member would pass a naive reading of the cleaning rule and violate the binding field list. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-023** | **Refuse to add** the attributes the original stakeholder brief requested that the binding field list omits, and record them as consciously excluded rather than quietly missing. | Add them as extra columns, since the brief that requested them is a genuine project input. Or add them to the staging table only, as a staging area for later promotion. | The requirements declare the field definitions binding and complete and explicitly supersede the earlier brief, so any addition is a breach. This matters more than it first appears, because the legacy startup model **actively carried four of them** as real columns — `sub_sector`, `local_employee_count`, `headcount_growth_rate` and `is_hiring` [`src/backend/models/startup.py:L9-L21`]. Their absence is therefore a removal, not something that never existed. | Genuine capability is lost: growth-rate comparison becomes unbuildable, which is why one source requirement is excluded at D-094. Accepted as the consequence of a binding field list. | Medium | — |

**On D-023, what survived and what did not.** Three of the earlier brief's signals do appear, as `active`, `headquarters_location` and `institutional_funding_last_5yrs`, and their provenance is traceable to the requirements document as well [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L218-L219`]. Two more are partial matches rather than omissions: `employee_count_range` is a five-value band, not the integer headcount the brief asked for, and "what the company does" becomes `description`. The genuinely unmatched attributes are sub-sector, employees counted locally, twelve-month headcount growth, a hiring flag, employee count broken down by department, competitors, site traffic, revenue and tech stack. None is a column, and none is a staging column either.

**On D-014, the two paths.** The lead investor is a single reference on the funding round and the participating investors are join rows, so the two traversals start from different structures and can return overlapping startup sets. Unioning the identifier sets before counting is the whole of the de-duplication.

---

## 3. Access control

Eight decisions. This is the highest-risk section in the delivery, because every failure mode in it is **silent**: the application appears to work, and the premium data is readable by someone who should not see it.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-024** | Enforce the seven premium fields through **two mandatory access-control layers** — a table-level read control granting all three roles, and a field-level read control on each premium field granting only the administrator and premium roles — and treat neither layer as optional. | Rely on the table layer alone and hide the fields in the interface. Or rely on field-level controls alone and omit the table grants. Or perform the check in application code on each read. | Both single-layer variants fail silently and in opposite directions. Omit the table-level read controls and **nothing** is readable, so the field-level controls never evaluate at all and a passing test proves nothing. Omit the field-level controls and **everything** is readable, because evaluation falls back to the table-level grant that already said yes. Interface hiding is explicitly insufficient: the requirement is that the base role receive a genuine access denial, not a field it cannot see on a form. A code-level check would place the decision back in application logic, which is what the replatform exists to remove — the legacy decorator authenticated only and left its role check unwritten [`src/backend/utils/auth.py:L36`]. | Both failure modes present as a working application. Only a per-role, per-field assertion distinguishes them, which is why the 21-cell cross-product at D-064 is not optional coverage. | High | `R3-1` `AUTHZ` |
| **D-025** | **Omit** a denied field's key from the response object entirely, rather than emitting it with an empty or null value, and place that gate in exactly one place — the shared response builder used by all 31 REST operations and every widget server script. | Emit the key with a null or empty value. Or return an error for the whole request when any field is denied. Or let each operation build its own response object. | The distinction is not pedantry: the secured read path returns an **empty string** for a denied field rather than raising, so a serialiser that walks every column produces a present-but-empty key — which is exactly the nulled behaviour the requirements forbid. Omission therefore requires an explicit per-element read check and a skipped key. Failing the whole request would deny the base role the 12 fields it is entitled to. Per-operation serialisation would let 31 implementations drift, and one of them would eventually emit the empty key. | A widget server script that builds a response by any other means bypasses the gate and can leak a field the API would deny. Mitigated by the single-gate rule and by the portal walkthrough checking a denied field's absence. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-026** | Read through the **access-control-respecting** record API in every path that returns data to a caller, and permit the unsecured path only in the four services whose work is not performed on a caller's behalf: the rate-limit service, the portfolio derivation, the ingestion mapper and the retention service — none of which returns a record or a field value to a response or a widget. | Use the secured path universally, including in internal maintenance code. Or use the unsecured path with a code-level role check layered on top. | Ordinary record access does not consult access controls on server-side reads, so the secured path is the only mechanism that makes the field-level controls actually bind. The four exceptions are genuine: the rate-limit service maintains counter rows a caller must never see, the portfolio derivation aggregates rows outside any request, the mapper writes under the flow's own identity, and the retention service prunes rows no ordinary caller can reach. Forcing the secured path on those four would make them fail under a caller with no roles at all, which is the flow's situation. | The anti-pattern is specific and must be stated so it is recognisable: a Script Include that reads a premium field through the unsecured path and hands it back to a REST operation is the bypass the requirements prohibit, and it would pass every functional test. Mitigated by no Script Include being client-callable or elevated, and by the four exceptions being enumerated rather than discretionary. | High | — |
| **D-027** | Close the **cross-scope write and alternate-invocation routes** while leaving one read route open: the three supporting tables ship `package_private` with every cross-scope capability flag false and `ws_access` false; the seven entity tables ship `public` with **read only** across scopes — create, update and delete all false — and `ws_access` true; and add a fifth control layer of two endpoint-execution controls governing whether the REST API may be invoked at all, reinforced by a shared guard called in all 31 operation scripts. **Superseded in part by D-310:** the sentence describing the seven entity tables as `public` with `ws_access` true no longer describes the delivery — all ten tables are sealed. The fifth control layer and the closed write routes stand as recorded. | Close all ten tables to both routes, as an earlier revision did. Or leave every table public with cross-scope write enabled too, which is the friendlier default. Or rely on the four record-level layers alone and accept the platform's default REST access control. | An open **write** route defeats the field-level design completely: a script in another scope could mutate rows the record-level controls never see, and they would report no denial because they were never reached. A **read** route is different in kind — the platform evaluates the same table-level and field-level controls on the native table interface, so the denial a base-role caller receives there is the denial the application's own API gives, and closing that route instead makes the denial **unobservable**, since a table that cannot be reached answers identically whether the field is gated or not. The internal machinery is read by nobody outside the scope, so the three supporting tables stay closed on both routes. On the fifth layer, a new Scripted REST API otherwise falls back to the platform's default control, which denies the external-user role but is not a scoped-role control: **any authenticated internal user satisfies it**. The layer is what makes "only the three application roles may call this API" true rather than assumed, and the coded guard means enforcement does not rest on control evaluation alone. | No route from outside the scope reads or writes application data, so the residual is confined to instance-level privilege: an operator holding it can change a table's posture after the commit and reach any table on the instance by other means. Bounded rather than eliminated — the class-3 diagnostic `GATE-SEC-01` records the posture of all ten tables on every deployment, and the seven required table gates assert it per table, so drift is visible in the deployment record rather than silent. One property is stated rather than implied: the fixed-window rate limiter on the surviving route is a fair-use control and **not** a security boundary, and a caller inside its budget is subject to every access control on every field it reads. The earlier reading of this row — the seven entity tables open to the native interface, with three residual differences from the application's own API — no longer describes the delivery; the correction is at **D-310**. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-028** | Create the three impersonation users **at run time** in test setup steps rather than shipping user records, and keep them distinct from the authenticating caller the REST tests need. | Ship the three users and their role grants in the Update Set. Or reuse one existing instance user and swap its roles between assertions. | The platform user table sits outside the application scope and the requirements forbid modifying anything outside it, so shipping user records would breach scope containment at import — the same constraint recorded at D-007. The delivered Update Set carries three role **definitions** and zero role **assignments** for exactly this reason. Swapping roles on one user between assertions would leave residue from the previous assertion and make a failure ambiguous. The split from the REST caller is necessary because impersonation needs no password while an authenticated REST call does; that caller is itself disposable and web-service-access-only, per D-119, so nothing this suite creates outlives its own transaction beyond one credential-less shell record. | Test users created at run time are removed by the framework's rollback, so nothing persists as evidence after the run. Mitigated by the result record itself being the evidence. | Medium | — |
| **D-029** | Resolve the ambiguity over `institutional_funding_last_5yrs` by **excluding it from the inclusion filter**: the filter tests exactly `active` being true and a headquarters match against Boston or Cambridge, and the flag remains a premium-gated display attribute. Records failing the filter stay in the table for administrative visibility and are excluded only from portal-facing and non-administrative queries. | Include the flag in the filter, since it appears in the inclusion-criteria field list. Or make it a third filter condition applied only for callers entitled to read it. | The field appears in **both** the inclusion-criteria field list and the premium-gated field list, so a choice was forced. The operative rule text names only the active flag and the headquarters match, so the narrow reading is also the literal one. The consequence settles it: the base role **cannot read** that field, so a filter depending on it would be unbuildable for that role — the portal list would either fail or silently return nothing. A role-dependent filter would make the same query return different result sets for different roles, and the total count would then disagree with the page contents for one of them. Deleting failing records instead of filtering them would destroy administrative visibility of data the ingestion actually collected. | Someone may later "correct" the filter by adding the flag, reintroducing exactly the unbuildable case. Called out here and wherever the filter is specified so the omission reads as deliberate. | High | `AMB-3` |
| **D-030** | Treat the **administrator override** as a verification hazard rather than a platform defect, and require every access-control assertion to run under impersonation by a user holding exactly one scoped role and none of the elevated platform roles. | Verify by walkthrough as the instance administrator, which is the operator's normal identity. Or disable the override on the record controls. | Record-level controls allow the platform administrator role to override them by default, and every personal-developer-instance operator holds that role. A walkthrough performed as the instance administrator therefore displays all seven premium fields and **appears to prove enforcement that was never tested even once**. Disabling the override is not appropriate: it is platform-wide behaviour, not application configuration, and changing it would reach outside the scope. | This is the single most consequential verification risk in the delivery: without impersonation the entire per-role, per-field evidence set is worthless while looking complete. Mitigated by making impersonation a procedural requirement of all 21 assertions, the omission spot check and the portal walkthrough. | High | — |
| **D-031** | Grant table-level read to **all three roles on all seven entity tables**, so that role differences exist only at field level and record-level visibility is identical for every caller. | Restrict record-level visibility by role, for example showing only a subset of startups to the base role. | No requirement asks for record-level differentiation — the premium distinction is entirely about which **fields** are legible. Keeping record visibility uniform also makes an aggregate count a correct total for every caller, which is what the counting decision at D-041 depends on. | This is an **invariant, not a coincidence**, and breaking it breaks the count silently: the moment anyone adds a record-level restriction, an aggregate count over-reports for restricted callers. Recorded here precisely so a future change cannot make the count wrong without someone noticing. | Medium | — |

**On D-024, the seven premium fields.** They are `startup.total_funding_usd`, `startup.institutional_funding_last_5yrs`, `founder.contact_email`, `executive.contact_email`, `investor.aum_usd`, `fundinground.amount_usd` and `fundinground.valuation_usd`. The layered scheme delivers 49 access-control records across five layers with 74 role joins, of which exactly 7 are the field-level read controls.

## 4. API layer

Eleven decisions. Two of them break the legacy contract deliberately, and one of them — the rate-limit mechanism — exists only because the mandated response body cannot be produced any other way.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-032** | Publish the API at the **physical** namespaced base path `/api/x_bst_startuptrk/v1/` rather than the logical `/api/v1/` the requirements name. | Deploy the API in the global scope, where the namespace segment would be absent. Or place a rewrite in front of the instance to expose the shorter path. | A scoped Scripted REST API always carries its namespace segment in the path; the shorter form is not achievable while the API stays inside the scope, and moving it out would breach the scope-containment prohibition recorded at D-007. The requirements' own phrasing anticipates this by naming the namespace alongside the path. A rewrite would mean infrastructure outside the platform, which is out of scope. | A consumer written against the documented logical path receives a 404 with no hint as to why. Mitigated by the physical path being stated wherever the contract is specified, and by the difference being flagged in the gap inventory. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-033** | Break the legacy contract with **no compatibility layer**: `sysparm_limit` and `sysparm_offset` replace `page` and `per_page`, and the envelope key `total_count` replaces `total`. No shim, alias or dual-parameter support is built. | Accept both parameter spellings and emit both envelope keys, for a transition period. Or keep the legacy names and ignore the mandated ones. | The requirements mandate the new names, so keeping the old ones is not available. A shim is unnecessary rather than merely undesirable, and the evidence is sharp: the legacy application registered all six blueprints **without a URL prefix**, so the documented endpoint paths were never actually served and the old contract had no consumers to protect. The legacy names are visible at [`src/backend/routes/startup.py:L12-L13`] and the old envelope key at [`src/backend/routes/startup.py:L31-L37`]. | If an undiscovered consumer exists, it breaks with no fallback. Assessed as no real exposure, because the routes were never reachable. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-034** | Serve executive records through a **nested sub-resource** under founders, rather than promoting Executive to a seventh top-level resource. | A seventh top-level resource. Or a filter parameter on the founders resource that switches the returned type. | The requirements fix the resource count at six and name the nested path explicitly, so a seventh resource would breach the count. A type-switching filter on one resource would return two different object shapes from one path, which is worse for a consumer than a distinct path. | The asymmetry is unusual: executives are readable through founders but the two are separate tables with separate access controls. Mitigated by the nested path being documented as its own operation. | Low | — |
| **D-035** | Implement the business logic as a **Script Include service layer** in which each rule exists exactly once, called from the REST operations, the business rules, the flows and the widget server scripts alike. | Implement each rule at its point of use, which is simpler per call site. Or centralise in fewer, larger classes. | A rule with more than one home drifts, and the legacy tree demonstrates precisely this failure: its cleaner deduplicated startups on name plus website [`src/data_collection/data_cleaning/startup_cleaner.py:L57`] while the requirements specify Boston-headquarters criteria — a divergence that survived because the rule had no single owner. One home for the inclusion filter is what makes the API and the portal unable to disagree about which startups exist. | Renaming any class breaks every caller at once, and the coupling is wide: all 31 operations depend on the query helper, the response builder and the rate-limit service. Mitigated by the call graph being documented as a rename-impact list. | Medium | — |
| **D-036** | Hold the delivered inventory to the plan's **exact** figures — 8 Script Includes at AAP section 0.4.5, 11 system properties at section 0.4.7, 3 business rules and 1 scheduled job at section 0.4.2 — so **do not build a staging-table privacy lifecycle**: no retention Script Include, no retention properties, no minimisation sweep, no age-bounded pruning and no erasure service. Protect the staging table by its **access posture** instead — administrator-only on all four operations, `package_private`, with web-service access false — and re-home the retention and erasure obligations as a **documented operator-owned procedure** in `data-model.md`. | Ship the lifecycle, which an earlier revision did: a ninth Script Include, two further properties and a second scheduled job. Or ship the sweeps without the erasure service. Or say nothing about retention at all. | Neither the frozen prompt nor the plan asks for a retention lifecycle, and the plan's inventories are binding, so shipping one put four records beyond the specified counts; all four were removed. The access posture already denies every non-administrative caller and every external route, and the obligation is stated in full rather than silently dropped. | The residual exposure is real and is accepted honestly: verbatim upstream payloads and unvalidated person data persist on the staging table until an operator removes them, and an erasure request is serviced by hand across three surfaces. The exposure is therefore neither built around nor hidden: it is stated as flag **F19** of `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/gaps-and-flags.md` and escalated for a human decision, and the earlier reading of this row — a lifecycle delivered beyond the planned inventory, at nine Script Includes and sixteen properties — no longer describes the delivery. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-037** | Account for rate limits in the **application**, with a fixed-window counter per caller and per resource, rather than using the platform's own inbound rate-limit rules. | Use the platform's inbound rate-limit rules, which need no table and no code. Or omit rate limiting and treat the mandated response as documentation only. | The platform rules do return the right status code, but their response body and headers are platform-controlled and carry **neither** the mandated error string **nor** a retry member, so they cannot satisfy a response body that is fixed exactly. They also cannot be exhausted deterministically inside a test without firing an entire request budget. Omitting the mechanism would leave one of the five acceptance criteria unmeetable. | A second, application-level limiter can disagree with a platform rule if both are configured. Mitigated by treating any platform rule as an optional higher-threshold backstop rather than the contract. | Medium | — |
| **D-038** | Make the rate-limit assertion deterministic by **pre-seeding a counter row already at the configured budget**, so the very next single call trips the limit — and require the seeding test to **own the whole `caller` + `api_resource` + `window_start` tuple**: delete every row for the triple and assert zero, seed exactly one and assert one, and delete every row and assert zero again afterwards. | Fire the full budget of requests in a loop inside the test. Or lower the budget property for the duration of the test and restore it afterwards. Or seed a row without asserting the tuple's row count, which is what a naive reading of "pre-seed a row" produces. | A loop of a hundred calls is slow, is sensitive to the window boundary falling mid-loop, and fails intermittently for reasons unrelated to the contract. Mutating a property mid-suite leaves the application in a modified state if the test aborts. Seeding one row exercises exactly the branch under test in one request. Tuple ownership is required because the triple carries **no uniqueness constraint** and `RateLimitService.consume()` selects its row with a query limited to one result: where several rows share the triple, the row it finds need not be the seeded row and the request returns `200`. Counter rows are created by inbound HTTP requests in their own sessions and are therefore **not rolled back with the test**, so a run that leaks rows corrupts the *next* run's baseline rather than its own. | **A seeded row alone proves only the threshold branch.** The threshold check could be correct while the increment logic were broken, and the assertion would still pass. The mitigation is **not** that the pagination calls happen to increment — an ordinary call proves nothing unless its effect is asserted from a known starting point. The increment is therefore asserted explicitly, from a **clean baseline**: after the delete-and-assert-zero, the first ordinary call must leave exactly one row reading `1` and the second must leave that same row reading `2`. Without those two assertions the `429` evidence rests on a hand-built row and the increment path is untested. The transition the test asserts is the **serial** one, which is all a test makes; the concurrent behaviour is a separate contract and is not what these two assertions cover. Note that the counting itself is **not** a read-increment-write cycle: `D-040` replaced that with the database's own atomic `addValue()` increment plus an aggregate `SUM` read-back, so `n` concurrent requests against a fresh window end at exactly `n`. Any reading of this row that treats the counter as racy predates `D-040`. | Medium | — |
| **D-039** | Also emit a standard **`Retry-After` response header** alongside the mandated response body, rather than the body alone. | Emit the mandated body only, as a strictly literal reading requires. | The header is the conventional transport for this value and costs nothing; a generic client honours it automatically without parsing the body. It adds to the mandated contract and changes no part of it. This is logged rather than done silently precisely because it exceeds a literal reading. | A consumer could read the header and the body and find them inconsistent if one is computed twice. Mitigated by both being rendered from the single computed value. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-040** | **Amended by `D-387` and then by `D-435`, which restores the `window_key` column as the single queried identity; the counting mechanism below stands and now addresses that key.** Count each request with the database's **own atomic increment** — `GlideRecord.addValue('request_count', 1)` — and then **read the window total back** with a `GlideAggregate` SUM over every row matching the window's four-column tuple — `caller`, `api_resource` and the bucket-aligned `window_start` — so the count a request decides on includes every increment applied concurrently with it. | Accept the read-increment-write race and let the effective limit overshoot slightly under concurrency, which a prototype focus would permit. Or build an application-level coalesced update or lock. Or lean on a platform rate-limit rule as a hard backstop and treat the application counter as advisory. | A read-modify-write cycle loses updates: two concurrent requests both read `n`, both write `n+1`, and the budget silently stretches. `addValue()` is the platform's documented atomic numeric increment and closes that gap **without** any additional machinery, so the prototype focus is no longer a reason to accept the race — the correct behaviour is the cheaper one. The aggregate read-back additionally makes a duplicate counter row harmless, because every row matching the tuple is summed rather than one row being trusted — which is what lets the table carry no uniqueness constraint and no key column at all. The behaviour is now well defined: `n` concurrent requests against a fresh window end at exactly `n`. | The first request in a window still **inserts** a row rather than incrementing one, so two simultaneous first requests can produce two rows; the summed read-back is what makes that outcome correct rather than a lost count, and a rejected insert is reported as retryable rather than silently dropped. The read-back costs one extra aggregate query per counted request. A platform rate-limit rule, if separately configured, can still disagree with this counter — it is treated as an optional higher-threshold backstop, not as the contract. The earlier reading of this row — that the race was accepted and the mitigation deliberately not built — no longer describes the delivery. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-041** | **Amended by `D-267`, which replaces the probe read with a role test, and by `D-406`, which states the counted-table set as eight.** Compute `total_count` as the **exact filtered cardinality** with one aggregate rather than by iterating a secured read; gate that aggregate behind an explicit table-level read check so a caller who may not read the table receives a **403 rather than a zero**; and apply the inclusion filter identically to the result set and to the count. | Count by iterating the secured read, which is access-control-correct by construction but walks every row. Or cap the count at a ceiling and add a member reporting that it was capped. Or return zero when the read check fails. Or omit the total from the envelope. | Aggregate counting does not itself apply access control, and counting through the secured path means walking every row. The tension never bites here **only because** of the invariant recorded at D-031: all seven tables grant table-level read to all three roles, so record-level visibility is identical for every caller and an aggregate is a correct total for each of them. The explicit read check is what makes the aggregate defensible rather than merely convenient. Capping is not available — the requirements mandate `total_count` in every list envelope and the plan mandates an aggregate, so a ceiling plus an extra envelope member would itself be the deviation; it was built that way once and has been removed. Returning zero would report "no matching records" for what is actually "you may not ask", which is a worse answer than an error. | Two things break it, both silently. A record-level restriction added later makes the aggregate over-report for restricted callers, which is why D-031's invariant is recorded as load-bearing wherever the count is documented. And because the inclusion criteria are a query **filter** rather than an access control, applying them to the result set but not to the count makes the total disagree with the page contents. | High | — |
| **D-042** | Externalise the page-size defaults as configuration properties — 20 by default, 50 at most — rather than hard-coding them. | Hard-code both values in the query helper. Or accept any limit a caller asks for, with no ceiling. | Externalising them means the portal and the API read the same two values and cannot diverge. The values themselves carry forward from the legacy shared constants [`src/shared/constants.ts:L5`, `src/shared/constants.ts:L8`], which is the one place the legacy tree supplies a usable number. No ceiling would let one request ask for every row. | The legacy tree shows exactly the drift this prevents: its startups route hard-coded a per-page default of 10 [`src/backend/routes/startup.py:L13`] while its own shared constant declared 20 [`src/shared/constants.ts:L8`]. Two hard-coded copies had already disagreed. | Low | — |

**On D-035, the service layer as delivered.** **Eight** classes, matching AAP section 0.4.5 exactly: `StartupSearchService`, `InvestorPortfolioService`, `IngestionMapper`, `IngestionLogger`, `RateLimitService`, `RestResponseBuilder`, `RestQueryHelper` and `AppProperties`. A ninth an earlier revision shipped was removed at D-036. None is client-callable and none runs with elevated privilege. The 31 operations are distributed 5, 6, 5, 5, 5 and 5 across the startups, founders, investors, funding-rounds, jobs and news resources, the sixth founders operation being the nested sub-resource of D-034.
