## 5. Ingestion

Fifteen decisions — `D-043` through `D-054`, plus `D-102`, `D-103` and `D-104` at the end of the numbering. The requirements name the sources and the cleaning rules but leave the allocation, the cadence mechanism, the artifact decomposition and the failure branch open, so most of this section fills genuine gaps rather than choosing between styles.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-043** | **Amended by `D-390`, which corrects the column an alias lookup matches on, and by `D-391` and `D-392`, which name the single primary Crunchbase store and the endpoint-authority test that precedes any use of credential material.** Reference both credential aliases **by name only**, with zero credential material anywhere in the Update Set, any flow input or any script step, and split the alias record set in two: **build the definition records** — the `sys_alias` and `http_connection` records, neither of which holds a secret — where they are absent and confirm them where they are present, while **every credential record is confirmed and never created**, remaining its owner's to provision and tracked as a pending item until it is. | Ship the aliases with their credential records in the Update Set. Or read the credentials from system properties. Or pass them as flow inputs at run time. Or create nothing at all and treat a zero-record instance as a completed build, which is what an earlier draft of guide 01 did. | Every alternative puts secret material somewhere it can be read. Shipping credential records places them in a file that is committed to a repository and imported into an instance; properties are readable by anyone who can read properties; flow inputs are visible in the execution log. The requirements forbid hard-coding keys, secrets or tokens in any of those places. Confirm-never-create follows for the **credential** records from the credentials being provisioned on the instance by their owner, so creating one would mean inventing a secret. It does **not** follow for the **definition** records: an alias record and a connection record carry a name, a scope, a type, a connection URL read from a non-secret property and a reference to a credential, and building one places no secret anywhere. Creating nothing was rejected because the aliases are a required deliverable of the manual build and an absent record is not a delivered artifact — a build that reports two aliases while the instance carries none is a false inventory, and the flows it feeds bind by name to something that does not exist. The anti-pattern being eliminated is concrete: a module-level key constant passed as a query parameter [`src/data_collection/api_integrators/crunchbase_integrator.py:L11`, `src/data_collection/api_integrators/crunchbase_integrator.py:L22`], a module-level key sent as a static bearer header [`src/data_collection/api_integrators/linkedin_integrator.py:L12`, `src/data_collection/api_integrators/linkedin_integrator.py:L23`], and plain environment keys [`.env.example:L27-L29`]. | Binding is **by name**, so a name mismatch fails at run time rather than at build time and the flow simply falls to its fallback branch. Mitigated by requiring a connection test before either flow is saved. The split carries a second risk: a connection record built with an empty **Credential** field resolves at run time and then fails to authenticate, which reads as an endpoint fault rather than a missing credential. Mitigated by guide 01 recording every unpopulated **Credential** field in its pending-item register, and by no document counting a pending credential as delivered. | High | `R3-3` |
| **D-044** | Move LinkedIn from a static bearer token to **OAuth 2.0**, with client identifier, client secret and refresh token held in the alias. | Keep a static bearer token in a Basic-authentication alias, mirroring the Crunchbase arrangement. | A static bearer token cannot be rotated without editing the caller and does not expire, so a leaked token stays valid indefinitely. The alias supports OAuth 2.0 natively, so the upgrade costs configuration rather than code. This is an authentication-model **upgrade**, not a port of the legacy header [`src/data_collection/api_integrators/linkedin_integrator.py:L23`]. | The two sources now authenticate differently, so the two flows are not configuration-identical and a refresh-token expiry is a new failure mode. Mitigated by the fallback branch treating an authentication failure exactly like any other. | Medium | — |
| **D-045** | **Amended by `D-276`, which is the authority for arm selection: the generic Flow Designer REST step is the primary arm for **both** sources and the scoped script step is the entitlement fallback, with exactly one arm built per instance and a pre-flight that decides which. The clause below that the scoped step is Crunchbase's delivered path rather than a fallback no longer describes the delivery; the two-condition test itself stands and is carried as flag `F18`.** Choose the outbound path **per source**, by a two-condition test: a **Flow Designer REST step** where the instance is entitled to it **and** the source's credential transport is one a connection alias can populate; otherwise a **scoped script step** that resolves the connection and credential by alias name and calls out through the scoped outbound REST message API. For **Crunchbase the scoped step is the delivered path**, not a fallback; for **LinkedIn the REST step is viable** once its endpoint contract is declared. | Use the REST step only, and fail on instances without the entitlement — and, for Crunchbase, ship a build whose live calls cannot authenticate. Or use the script step only, for uniformity. Or place the Crunchbase key in a step field or a data pill so the REST step can send it. Or use a packaged integration content set for each source. | A packaged content set is forbidden outright, and neither of these paths is one — a generic REST step is not a packaged integration, and neither is a scoped script that resolves an alias by name. The **second condition is what forces Crunchbase off the REST step**: Crunchbase v4 authenticates an **`X-cb-user-key` request header**, or the same key as a `user_key` query parameter, and a REST step can set a custom header or a query parameter only from a literal or a data pill, neither of which a credential value is ever exposed as — the platform resolves a credential into the standard authentication headers alone. The two ways to make that work both breach the prohibition on credential material in a flow input or step field, and the third — binding the alias and sending no key — reaches the endpoint unauthenticated. LinkedIn is unaffected, because OAuth 2.0 travels in an `Authorization` header the alias populates natively. Committing to the REST step alone would make the build undeployable on some instances and unauthenticable on one source; committing to the script alone would decline a supported declarative mechanism where it works. | The path differs by source **and** by instance, so the action that gets built is not the same everywhere and the flow test must know which variant it is asserting against. Mitigated by both build guides carrying the two-condition pre-flight check, recording both answers in their build verification, and stating the Crunchbase answer as fixed rather than instance-dependent. The branch itself is carried in the gap register as flag **F18**, whose two conditions — the instance entitlement and whether the connection can supply the credential-bearing header — are what select the arm per source. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-046** | Collapse the five hard-coded scheduler intervals into **one** cadence property, clamped to the range the requirements specify. | Keep five separate intervals, one per ingestion concern. Or hard-code a single interval. | Five intervals meant five places to change and no way to change any of them without a code edit [`src/data_collection/scheduler.py:L13-L17`]. One clamped property makes the cadence genuinely runtime-configurable, which is what the requirements ask for. The provenance is worth recording: those five legacy values were 24, 24, 12, 6 and 48 hours, and their minimum and maximum are **exactly** the bounds the requirements state — which is very likely where the range came from. | One cadence for both flows means Crunchbase and LinkedIn cannot be tuned independently. Accepted: no requirement asks for independent cadences, and a second property could be added without changing the guard. | Low | — |
| **D-047** | Implement the configurable cadence as an **hourly trigger paired with an elapsed-time guard** in the flow's first action, and define a "scheduled run" as an execution that **passed** the guard. | Set the trigger interval to the cadence value and re-edit the flow whenever the cadence changes. Or run a separate job that activates and deactivates the flow. | A Flow Designer scheduled trigger takes a fixed interval and **cannot read a system property at run time**, so a runtime-configurable interval is not natively expressible. The hourly trigger with a guard makes the cadence change by editing one property, which is the stated requirement. Re-editing the flow would make the cadence a development task rather than configuration; an activation job adds a third moving part to control the second. **Where the guard reads its last-run marker is settled separately, at D-105 and as delivered at D-203**: this row fixes the trigger-plus-guard shape; the marker ships as **one** property carrying a per-source entry whose `state` member distinguishes an attempt from a success, not as two properties per source, because the property inventory is frozen at eleven. | At the shipped cadence roughly 23 of every 24 executions are no-ops, so an evidence set that counted them would consist almost entirely of executions that did no work — which is precisely why the scheduled-run definition is part of this decision rather than a separate convention. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-048** | Allocate the six ingested entities across the two flows: **Crunchbase** supplies Startup, Investor, FundingRound and the join rows; **LinkedIn** supplies Founder, Executive and JobPosting; and the Startup upsert path is **shared**, so both flows resolve company identity through one deduplication key. | Have both flows ingest every entity type. Or split by entity family with each flow owning its own startup resolution. | The requirements list the six ingested entities without allocating them, so an allocation was required. The split follows what each source actually knows: company, funding and investor data from the company database, people and job data from the professional network. Both flows ingesting everything would mean two writers per table and last-writer-wins conflicts. Independent startup resolution would let the two flows create two records for one company, which is the exact failure the deduplication rule exists to prevent. | The shared upsert path couples the two flows: a change to the deduplication key affects both. Mitigated by the key living once in the shared mapper. | Medium | — |
| **D-049** | Discriminate Founder from Executive by an **explicit `record_type` value** carried on the staging row, replacing the legacy title-substring heuristic. | Infer the type from the title string, as the legacy integrator did. Or write every person to one table and split later. | The legacy heuristic tested for the literal substring "Founder" and then for a small set of C-level titles [`src/data_collection/api_integrators/linkedin_integrator.py:L119-L120`], which misclassifies a founding engineer, a co-founder whose title omits the word, and any executive title outside its list. It is also unable to represent a person who is both. An explicit discriminator is what carries the binding separate-tables directive of D-017 intact through the staging layer; writing to one table first would violate it outright. | The discriminator must be correct in the source data or on the CSV row, so a mislabelled row lands in the wrong table silently. Mitigated by the value being a constrained choice rather than free text, so an unrecognised value is rejected rather than guessed. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-050** | Have **both** flow guides state all eight steps in full, rather than the second deferring to the first. | Have guide 03 describe only its differences from guide 02. | The two flows share one skeleton and differ in source, alias and target entities, so a difference-only guide would be much shorter. But an operator building the LinkedIn flow would then have to read both documents and mentally merge them, and the traceability matrix would have a gap at the second flow because its steps would exist only by reference. Each guide being independently buildable is worth the duplication. | Two full copies can drift if one is edited alone, and a divergence between them would be invisible to a reader of either. Mitigated by the shared logic living in Script Includes rather than in the guides, so the duplication is prose rather than behaviour. | Low | — |
| **D-051** | Take the four cleaning rules **from the requirements alone**, and port nothing from the legacy cleaning code. | Port the legacy pandas pipeline and adapt it. Or treat the legacy behaviour as the specification where the requirements are silent. | The legacy implementation contradicts **every one** of the four mandated rules, so adaptation would mean reversing it line by line. It deduplicated on name plus website rather than name plus headquarters location [`src/data_collection/data_cleaning/startup_cleaner.py:L57`, `src/data_collection/data_cleaning/startup_cleaner.py:L60`]; it normalised industry through a four-entry placeholder dictionary whose output values match none of the target choices [`src/data_collection/data_cleaning/startup_cleaner.py:L171-L176`]; it filled missing text with the literal "Unknown" [`src/data_collection/data_cleaning/startup_cleaner.py:L86`]; and it **median-imputed missing numeric values** [`src/data_collection/data_cleaning/startup_cleaner.py:L90`] — the exact opposite of rejecting a record that lacks a mandatory field. | Nothing is inherited, so every rule is new code with no field-proven predecessor. Mitigated by each rule being asserted directly in the flow tests. | Medium | — |
| **D-052** | **Do not migrate** the four web-crawler modules. | Port them as a third ingestion path. Or reimplement scraping as a scripted outbound call from a flow. | The requirements name only two sources and require no scraping of any kind, and they exclude news-article ingestion entirely — which was the target of one of the four crawlers. Building a scraping path would add an unrequested integration with its own failure modes and legal considerations. | The corresponding source requirement goes unmet, which is recorded as a conscious exclusion at D-092 rather than left as a silent gap. | Low | — |
| **D-053** | Maintain **two distinct provenance surfaces**: for test runs, a setup step that **derives** the mode and labels the result, writing **no run summary of its own**; and for real scheduled executions, the run-summary records the ingestion itself writes. **The ingestion is the only run-summary writer in the delivery.** | Use one provenance mechanism for both, writing the same marker in both contexts. Or have the test setup step call `IngestionLogger.writeRunSummary()` so the test and the flow share one code path — the obvious reading, and the one this decision rejects. | Conflating them would be a real defect, not a tidiness issue. The test framework **rolls back data created inside a test**, so a provenance row or property written during a test does not survive the run — a single mechanism would therefore leave test runs with no provenance at all while appearing to have some. Labelling each test result `live validated` or `fallback validated` — those exact two-word forms, unhyphenated, so a search for either finds every occurrence — keeps the evidence attached to something that persists, and scheduled runs write records outside any test transaction, so those persist naturally. **A setup step must not write a summary**, because `writeRunSummary()` is not an inert label: it sets `x_bst_startuptrk.ingestion.last_run_provenance`, emits the `run_summary` event and returns the counters. Called before any ingestion it writes a summary carrying **all-zero counters**, which a later assertion reading "the run summary" can find and pass against — reporting a successful ingestion of nothing. It would also make the property record the test author's assumption rather than the run's actual path, which is precisely what a provenance surface must not do. The setup step therefore derives the mode from the source-mode property and the alias's credential state, and every summary assertion binds to the object the ingestion's own call returned. | Two mechanisms mean two places to read when assembling evidence. Mitigated by each acceptance criterion naming which surface it draws on. A second residual risk is that the prohibition is a convention rather than an enforced constraint: nothing prevents a future edit from reintroducing the call. Mitigated by the flow test asserting that **no all-zero run summary exists for the run**, which fails if it is reintroduced. | Medium | — |
| **D-054** | Keep the sample dataset **fallback-only** — the live path is attempted first on every run **while `x_bst_startuptrk.ingestion.source_mode` reads `live`**, and that property exists for deterministic testing rather than as a default — and ship **six** CSV files with none for NewsArticle. | Default to the sample dataset for predictability. Or ship a seventh CSV for news articles for completeness. | The requirements state the dataset is for use when live authentication fails, times out, or credentials are being reissued, so defaulting to it would silently stop exercising the live integration and the provenance labelling would become meaningless. A seventh CSV would imply a news ingestion path that deliberately does not exist: no flow targets news articles and the staging record-type list has no member for them, so the file could never be loaded. | The live path is attempted on every run even when both aliases are known to be unprovisioned, adding a failed call per run. Accepted: the failure is logged, handled, and is what sets the fallback provenance marker. **Refined by D-111 and D-316:** "attempted first on every run" holds only in `live` mode — in `fallback` mode the outbound step is skipped entirely by its `If` condition, so on the instance recorded for this delivery, whose end state D-111 fixes at `fallback`, no call is attempted and no failure is logged per run. The property is also **one shared setting for both flows**, not a per-flow one. | Low | — |

**On D-043, the delivered posture.** The two aliases are `x_bst_startuptrk.crunchbase_api` and `x_bst_startuptrk.linkedin_oauth`. The first is a Basic-authentication alias used as an **encrypted store**: the Crunchbase key is held in its password field, but the provider does not accept HTTP Basic at all, so the flow reads the key through the alias at run time and sends it as an `X-cb-user-key` header — the wire contract is recorded at D-229. The second is an OAuth 2.0 alias, per D-044. All **eleven** system properties hold no secret of any kind; the two base-URL properties carry the corrected endpoint values of D-229 — with the property as the authority and the connection record's Connection URL as its mirror, per D-420 — rather than the stale ones the legacy modules pinned at [`src/data_collection/api_integrators/crunchbase_integrator.py:L10`] and [`src/data_collection/api_integrators/linkedin_integrator.py:L11`].

**What this row does not assert.** Nothing here states that either alias currently holds live credential material on any instance. That is an observation about an instance rather than a decision, it changes with time, and restating it in more than one place is how a package comes to contradict itself. The **single** factual statement of credential readiness for the delivery is the `## Credential posture` section of `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/manual-build/01-connection-credential-aliases.md`, which separates the build gate from the fallback-validation gate from the live-validation gate and records the observation itself, dated, under `### The recorded posture for this delivery`; the decision to publish exactly one such statement is **D-249**, and the decision to date it and to give it the credential-record ownership contract is **D-421**.

## 6. User interface

Eight decisions. The requirements fix the number of routes but not their URL syntax, and they forbid the newer interface builder, so the whole section is about working inside an older component vocabulary.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-055** | Build the company profile with **five** tabs — Overview, Funding, People, Jobs, News — re-specifying the legacy "Team" tab as **People** carrying founders and executives together, and **dropping "Similar Companies"** entirely. | Keep all six legacy tabs and leave the sixth empty or populated by a heuristic. Or keep "Team" as the label and split founders and executives into two tabs. | The legacy component rendered six tabs [`src/frontend/components/CompanyProfile/CompanyProfile.tsx:L86-L91`] and the requirements enumerate five, so one had to go. "Similar Companies" is the one that must: **no competitor data exists in the binding field list**, so the tab could only be empty or filled by an invented similarity heuristic that no requirement asks for and that would present guesses as data. "People" rather than "Team" because the pane carries two distinct entity types from two tables, and "Team" implies employment rather than founding. Splitting them into two tabs would make six again. | A user of the legacy design finds a tab missing and a tab renamed. Assessed as low functional impact, since the removed tab had no data source in either design. | Medium | `DEV` `R3-5` |
| **D-056** | Address portal pages by **query parameter** rather than by path segment. | Reproduce the legacy path form with a rewrite rule in front of the instance. Or accept the platform form but present path-style links that redirect. | Service Portal addresses pages by query parameter, so the legacy path form [`src/frontend/App.tsx:L29-L37`] is not reproducible without URL rewriting, and rewriting means infrastructure outside the platform which is out of scope. The requirements mandate five routes and say nothing about their URL syntax, so the platform form satisfies them. A redirect layer would add a component whose only purpose is cosmetic. | Bookmarks and any external link written against the legacy path form do not resolve. Assessed as no real exposure: the legacy application never ran, so no such link was ever published. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-057** | Declare **1024 pixels** the supported viewport floor, use only the medium and large column classes, and author no small or extra-small refinements. | Author the full responsive range down to phone widths. Or author no breakpoints at all and accept whatever the grid does. | Viewports below 1024 pixels are named out of scope, so building for them would be unrequested work — and untested work, since no acceptance criterion exercises a narrow viewport. Authoring no breakpoints at all would leave the layout undefined even at supported widths. Declaring the floor explicitly is what turns an untested range into a documented boundary rather than an unknown. | The portal is genuinely unusable on a phone, and nothing in the interface says so. Mitigated by the floor being stated in the portal documentation rather than left for a user to discover. | Low | — |
| **D-058** | Build the experience with **Service Portal only**, authoring no records of the newer interface-builder type. | Use the newer interface builder, which is the platform's current direction for new experiences. | The requirements forbid it explicitly, so there is no latitude. Recording it matters anyway, because the choice looks like a technology-currency mistake to anyone who does not know it was mandated, and because it fixes the component vocabulary that D-059 then has to work within. | The delivered interface uses the older of the platform's two experience frameworks, so a future migration to the newer one is a rebuild rather than an upgrade. Accepted as a consequence of a binding constraint. | Low | — |
| **D-059** | Adopt the portal framework's bundled Bootstrap release plus the platform's own styling-variable namespace as the **entire** design vocabulary, with **zero** hard-coded colour, spacing, radius or font values in any widget. | Carry the legacy palette forward as literal values. Or introduce a third-party design system or utility framework into the portal. | This is a genuine introduction rather than a translation, and the finding that settles it is that **there was nothing to translate**: a tree-wide search for theme, token, configuration and stylesheet files returned exactly one file of hard-coded literals — a page background, a link colour, a font stack, a container width and three utility classes [`src/frontend/styles/global.css`] — and the theme module the application entry point imports [`src/frontend/App.tsx:L12`] **was never authored at all**, since `src/frontend/styles/` contains only that stylesheet. No legacy theme value exists to carry forward even in principle. A third-party system cannot be installed into the portal framework in any case. | Every value must resolve to a declared variable, which is a discipline a later contributor can quietly break with one literal. Mitigated by the rule being stated as an authoring constraint wherever widget styling is specified. | Medium | — |
| **D-060** | Resolve the five component gaps inside the system rather than reaching outside it: platform reporting or inline vector markup for charts, the choice-backed multi-select of D-015 for focus areas, the platform loading indicator or a striped progress bar for the spinner, the media object for card imagery, and an alert-based panel for the premium upsell — packaged as **one reusable widget with three hosts**. | Introduce a charting library and a component library to fill the gaps directly. Or leave each gap unfilled and drop the affected surface. | The bundled Bootstrap release has no chart, no multi-select, no spinner and no upsell component, so each gap is real. Filling them with outside libraries would contradict D-059 and add dependencies the platform cannot serve. On charts specifically, **nothing is lost**: the legacy manifest declared charting libraries [`package.json:L19`] that no component ever imported, and the legacy dashboard has no chart imports at all — so the legacy delivery had no charts either. Packaging the upsell once rather than three times keeps its behaviour identical on the company profile, the investor profile and the account summary. | Charts delivered through platform reporting are less customisable than a charting library would be. Accepted; no requirement specifies a chart type or interaction. The upsell widget has three hosts, so a change to its option schema affects all three. | Medium | — |
| **D-061** | Keep the **icon systems separate**: the executive presentation uses the icon set its rule mandates, while the portal uses the platform's own glyph font. | Unify on one set by using the mandated set in the portal, or the platform glyphs in the presentation. | The two are not unifiable in any useful sense. Rule 2 (Executive Presentation) mandates its icon set for a single self-contained HTML file loaded from a content network, and the portal renders inside the platform where the glyph font is already present and no external asset should be fetched. Forcing either into the other's context would add a dependency for purely cosmetic consistency between two artifacts no one views side by side. | The delivery uses two icon vocabularies, which reads as an inconsistency to a reviewer seeing both. Recorded explicitly so that no contributor injects one into the other believing it a correction. | Low | — |
| **D-062** | Serve administrators through the platform's **native list and form views** inside the scope, rather than adding a sixth portal route as an administrative console. | Build an administrative portal route. Or extend one of the five routes with administrative controls shown only to the administrator role. | The requirements enumerate exactly five routes and none is an administrative console, so a sixth breaches the count. The native views are generated from the same dictionary and honour the same access controls, so they need no separate build and cannot disagree with the model — whereas a hand-built console would duplicate every form and drift from the dictionary. Extending a user-facing route with role-conditional controls would put two audiences on one surface. | An administrator works in two visually different places, and the source requirements do ask for an administrative panel. Recorded as a conscious non-implementation at D-095. | Low | — |

**On D-055 through D-062, the delivered surface.** One portal at url suffix `bst` with one theme, five pages — `bst_home`, `bst_company`, `bst_investor`, `bst_dashboard`, `bst_account` — and eight widgets: `bst-startup-search`, `bst-startup-results`, `bst-company-profile`, `bst-investor-profile`, `bst-trends-kpi`, `bst-trends-charts`, `bst-account-summary` and `bst-premium-upsell`. The 17 component types the legacy application imported are replaced wholesale by the bundled Bootstrap vocabulary and the stock widget set.

## 7. Testing and validation

Seven decisions. One of them — two-level XML validation — was settled by experiment rather than by argument, and the experiment is recorded because it changes how seriously the check must be taken.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-063** | Structure the suite as **exactly 10 suites containing 36 tests**: 7 table-CRUD suites, 21 field-by-role access-control tests, 6 REST resource tests and 2 ingestion-flow tests. **Build no parent, master or aggregating suite**: the ten are run as an ordered batch by hand. | Fewer, larger suites grouping several tables together. Or one suite per concern rather than per table. Or **an eleventh suite whose only members are the other ten**, which is the platform's own idiom for a one-click run and the obvious convenience choice. | The coverage floor is stated per table and per resource, so a per-table suite makes coverage readable directly from the suite list rather than inferred from test names — a table with no passing test is visible as a missing suite. Grouping tables would make a single failure block assertions for unrelated tables in the same run. **A parent suite is rejected because the specification fixes the suite count at ten, and a suite record whose only purpose is to nest the others is still a suite record**: it would put the delivered inventory at 11 against a stated 10, and the count is machine-checkable as a row count on `sys_atf_test_suite`. The convenience it buys — one run instead of ten — does not outweigh a delivered inventory that contradicts its own specification. | Ten suites are ten things to run and keep passing, the run is longer than a consolidated one, and without a parent suite there is no single result record for the whole set: ten suite result identifiers must be recorded together. Accepted: the coverage gate is a delivery precondition, so legibility of coverage and a truthful inventory outrank run convenience. | Low | — |
| **D-064** | Assert access control as the **full 21-cell cross-product** of 7 premium fields by 3 roles, each cell under impersonation. | Test one representative field per role, for 3 tests. Or test all 7 fields for the base role only, on the reasoning that the other two roles are permitted everywhere. | Only the full cross-product satisfies verification stated per role and per field, and the shortcuts both hide realistic defects. A single representative field would not catch a field-level control that was never created for one of the other six. Testing only the base role would not catch a control that accidentally denies the premium role, which is a failure that looks like correct behaviour from a base-role test. | Twenty-one tests encode 7 field names and 3 role names, so any rename must propagate into the suite or the tests fail for the wrong reason. Mitigated by the names being fixed by the requirements and therefore stable. | Medium | — |
| **D-065** | Validate the Update Set XML at **two levels** — the outer document, then every nested payload independently after unescaping — and treat this as a hard requirement rather than defensive redundancy. | Parse the file once, as any XML tool does. Or validate with a command-line XML utility. | This was settled empirically rather than argued. A fixture shaped like the deliverable was built and mutated: **single-escaping one ampersand inside an advanced access-control script payload left the outer document perfectly well-formed and passing**, while the unescaped payload was not well-formed at all. The escaping arithmetic explains it — content is escaped once for the nested document and again as payload text, so a double ampersand in a script body appears in the outer file quadruple-escaped, and reducing it leaves a single entity that is still legal at the outer level. A single parse, including one by a command-line utility, cannot see this. Script bodies are unavoidable here: advanced access-control scripts, eight Script Includes, three business rules and one scheduled job all carry code. | This is the **dominant defect class** for a hand-authored Update Set, and it is invisible to every check except this one. The consequence of skipping it is an import that fails at load or preview with an error pointing at a line number inside an escaped blob. | High | — |
| **D-066** | Write the validator against the **Python standard library only**, and give it a documented exit-code contract of 0 for pass, 1 for a validation failure and 2 for a usage or input error. | Use an accelerated third-party XML parser for speed and better error messages. Or wrap a command-line XML utility. | The justification is concrete and environmental. In the planning environment the system interpreter refused a plain package install under its externally-managed guard, virtual-environment creation failed at the bootstrap stage, and the system package index carried neither the accelerated parser nor the command-line XML utilities. A validator with a third-party dependency would be fragile in exactly the environments expected to run it — an operator's laptop or a build agent with no network. The exit codes are a deliberate interface because three other package documents drive their pass and fail conditions from them, and an undocumented contract would be reinvented differently in each. | The standard parser is slower and its messages are terser on a large document. Accepted: the document is validated once per delivery, not in a loop. | Low | — |
| **D-067** | **Do not** wire the validator into the existing continuous-integration workflow; invoke it directly instead. | Add a validation job to the existing workflow. Or author a new workflow for the deliverable. | A step added to the existing workflow would **never execute**. That workflow fails at its Python install step against a requirements file that does not exist [`.github/workflows/ci.yml:L24`] and again at its frontend install step against a directory with no manifest [`.github/workflows/ci.yml:L36-L37`], both before any added step could run. The result would be a gate that appears enforced and is not, which is worse than no gate. A new workflow would mean modifying continuous-integration configuration for a retired stack. | Validation is operator-invoked, so it can be skipped by someone who does not read the deploy order. Mitigated by it being step 1 of that order with a non-zero exit stated as blocking. | Medium | — |
| **D-068** | Express the eleven required post-commit gates as **direct authenticated reads of the seven entity tables**, exactly as AAP section 0.11.2 specifies, and open the entity tables to the native table interface so those reads work. **Superseded in part by D-310 and D-311:** this row no longer describes the delivery, and its central platform claim did not survive re-examination. All ten tables ship sealed, so no row read of an application table is available on any route (`D-310`), and the seven table gates ship as scoped `sys_db_object` metadata reads that also assert the closed posture (`D-311`). The claim in this row's rationale that a field-level read control is never skipped on the native route is **too narrow**: `D-310` records the three paths that defeat it — query-predicate bisection, which infers a gated value from which rows a predicate returns without ever reading the field; the Aggregate API, which answers over values the caller may not read; and an unsecured cross-scope read, which evaluates no access control at all. Prompt section 2.0 requires the base role to receive a genuine access denial on the seven premium fields, and a route carrying those three paths cannot deliver one. What survives is this row's authority principle: the plan governs, and where the plan and a delivered posture conflict the artifact changes — which is why the **count and composition** of the eleven gates are unchanged and only their **expression** moved. That expression departs from the plan's literal text, and `D-455` accepts the departure explicitly by name rather than by argument. | Target no application table and assert the same facts through platform metadata instead, which an earlier revision did. Or keep the metadata form and re-describe the plan's eleven gates as satisfied by it. | **The plan governs and an earlier revision of this row got that backwards.** It resolved a conflict between AAP section 0.11.2 and a delivered table posture in the posture's favour, and it did so on a claim about the platform that is **false**: it stated that opening the interface "would restore an alternate route to the data that skips the rate limiter, the per-field gate and the endpoint controls". The per-field gate is not skipped. The platform table interface **is** access-controlled: it authenticates the caller, evaluates the table-level read control and evaluates every field-level read control before returning a value, so all seven premium fields are omitted from a response to a caller holding only `x_bst_startuptrk.user`, exactly as they are omitted from the application's own API. The two claims that do survive — the rate limiter and the endpoint controls are bypassed — are registered as residual risks with compensating controls under D-027, and neither justifies making the plan's required gates unexecutable. | Three residual risks, all registered under D-027 and in `gaps-and-flags.md` with their compensating controls: the read route is outside the application's rate limiter; it is outside the two endpoint-execution controls, with the table-level read controls carrying the same guarantee by a different mechanism; and cross-scope unsecured record access can reach entity rows, bounded by such a caller already holding instance-level privilege. The staging table stays sealed, so no verbatim upstream payload is reachable by this route. The rollback confirmation is the one place platform metadata remains the right instrument, because an application-table read cannot distinguish **removed** from **present but unreadable** — both answer with a client error, and only the `sys_db_object` read is unambiguous. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-069** | Ship exactly **11 required gates** — the plan's 7 entity-table reads, 3 role-cardinality checks and 1 scope-cardinality check — and carry the four further checks in **two separately counted classes rather than one**: `GATE-COL-01` as **acceptance-required and non-rollback**, and `GATE-SEC-01` through `GATE-SEC-03` as **non-normative diagnostics** that decide nothing and may not be cited as an entry condition by any guide. Two further pre-commit completeness checks are preconditions of the set and are counted apart from it. **Amended by D-311**, which adds `GATE-SEC-04` as a fourth non-normative diagnostic and substitutes a scoped `sys_db_object` metadata read for each of the seven table gates, the departure accepted by name at `D-455`: the further checks are therefore **five** in the same two classes, one acceptance-required and four diagnostic, and the eleven required gates are unchanged. | Publish all fifteen as one flat required set, which is what the delivery first shipped. Or class all four extras as advisory, which would leave the only machine-checkable evidence of the binding 53-column field list carrying no weight. Or make `GATE-COL-01` a rollback trigger. | The eleven are the acceptance contract the plan and the deployment environment define, and the rollback decision is theirs alone. Criterion 1 nevertheless depends on the column count, so classing it as advisory would let a deployment that committed 45 columns be recorded as accepted; and classing it as a rollback trigger would destroy seven correctly committed tables and three correctly committed roles to fix a dictionary defect that lives in the source file. | Three classes are more to read than one. A reader who skims may take `GATE-COL-01` for a deployment gate or for an advisory check; both halves of its status are therefore stated wherever it appears. | Medium | `DEV` |

---

## 8. Packaging and delivery

Eleven decisions. The single-file mandate drives most of the shape, and one of these — the rollback — is the only irreversible operation anywhere in the delivery.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-070** | Deliver **one** Update Set carrying 313 update records, notwithstanding practitioner guidance that keeps a set under roughly a hundred with several hundred treated as an outer limit. | Split the delivery across several smaller sets with a documented import order. Or reduce the record count by omitting the form and list views. | The requirements mandate a single importable file, so a split is not available. The count sits well inside the hard limit and above the comfort guideline, which is a real but bounded cost. Omitting the view records would shrink the set but would leave the administrative surface unusable on commit, which is what serves the administrative requirement without the sixth portal route of D-062. | Preview duration scales with record count, and the preview step has a 600-second timeout — a large set is the one plausible way to hit it. Recorded as a named risk that the runbook must reconcile rather than as an accepted certainty. | High | — |
| **D-071** | Capture each new table as a **single** table-type update record whose payload bundles the table, its collection and column dictionary records and its label records, so a table and its columns commit atomically. | Emit the table and each column as separate records, which is the shape a later single-field addition naturally takes. | Separate records can partially commit, leaving a table present with an incomplete column set — and the 53-column requirement would then be violated by a partial import rather than by an authoring error, which is far harder to diagnose. Bundling makes each table all-or-nothing. | A bundled payload is large, and a single escaping defect anywhere inside it fails the whole table. Mitigated by the two-level validation of D-065, which is what makes such a defect detectable before import. | Medium | — |
| **D-072** | Use **deterministic, stable 32-character lowercase hexadecimal identifiers**, identical everywhere a record is referenced, and **omit** the payload hash rather than risk emitting a wrong one. | Generate fresh random identifiers at emission time. Or compute and include the payload hash for completeness. | Referential integrity is a hard gate rather than a nicety: a role cited by a role join, the REST definition cited by an operation and the scope cited by every payload must carry the identical identifier in every place, and a dangling identifier surfaces at preview as an error-type problem — which aborts the deployment, since the preview step requires that set to be empty. Non-deterministic identifiers make a re-export a different document and turn a re-import into a duplicate rather than an update. On the hash, the platform recomputes it, so a wrong value is a defect while an absent one is not. | Hand-managed identifiers can collide or be mistyped, and the symptom appears only at preview. Mitigated by preview being a mandatory step that aborts rather than warns. | High | — |
| **D-073** | Emit records in **reference-dependency order**: the application, then the startup table, then the five tables referencing it, then investor, then the join table, then the supporting tables, then choices, roles, access controls, role joins, Script Includes, the REST definition and operations, properties, business rules, jobs, views, and finally the menu and its modules. | Emit in an arbitrary or alphabetical order and rely on the platform to resolve references at commit. | Ordering costs nothing to arrange and removes a class of preview problem entirely, since a reference resolved in order can never be dangling at the moment it is read. Relying on resolution makes the import's success depend on behaviour that is not guaranteed for every record type. | The order must be maintained if records are added later, and nothing enforces it mechanically. Mitigated by the order being documented as part of the emission contract. | Low | — |
| **D-074** | Split the delivery so that only artifacts the platform captures into update records ship as **XML**, and everything else ships as **manual build instructions** — the two aliases, the two flows, the portal with its theme, pages and widgets, the test suites and the staging load. | Attempt to hand-author the excluded artifacts as XML anyway. Or add the synchronisation attribute to the tables that lack it so they would be captured. | The split is not a stylistic preference. Only tables carrying the update-synchronisation attribute are captured into update records, and **adding that attribute where it is not present out of the box is unsupported and dangerous** — it can produce records the platform cannot apply. The excluded artifact classes are precisely those whose internal structure the platform generates through its own interface, so hand-authoring them means guessing at internal representations. | The delivery is not a single import: an operator must perform six manual build steps after the commit, and a skipped step leaves the application incomplete in ways the gates do not detect. Mitigated by the deploy order naming every step and by the test suites exercising the manually built artifacts. | Medium | — |
| **D-075** | Order the manual build **01, 02, 03, 04, 06, 05** — placing guide 06 before guide 05 and breaking the filename sequence at exactly one point. | Execute in filename order, 01 through 06. Or renumber the guides so the filename order matches the execution order. | Each hop has a mechanical dependency: aliases before flows because the flows bind aliases by name and a mismatch fails at run time; the staging load before any fallback exercise because the fallback branch reads an empty table otherwise and demonstrates no cleaning rule; pages and widgets before the portal walkthrough; and the test suites **last** because they exercise the tables, the access controls, the resources and both flows. Renumbering was rejected because the numbers group guides by artifact class, which is how a reader looking for one artifact finds its guide. | The single break between filename order and execution order is easy to miss. Mitigated by the build index being declared authoritative for the order and by each guide restating it locally. | Low | — |
| **D-076** | Define rollback as **deleting the scope record**, allowing it to cascade its tables, roles and flows, triggered only on commit failure or a post-commit gate failure. | Reverse the update set record by record. Or leave the failed installation in place for diagnosis and retry over it. | An update set has no reliable record-by-record reversal for a first installation, and a partially installed scope is not a state the gates can reason about. On a clean instance, deleting the scope returns the instance to its pre-import condition in one operation. Leaving the failure in place would mean the next import runs against unknown residue. | **This is the only genuinely irreversible operation in the delivery.** It destroys every application table and every row in them, and it cannot be undone. The guard is that it fires only on the two documented failure conditions, and that the specific failure is reported before it runs. On a non-clean instance the cascade assumption is untested, which is a second, distinct exposure. | High | `R3-2` `IRREV` |
| **D-077** | **Do not provision** the legacy runtimes, though the documented maxima are recorded for the dependency inventory and the traceability matrix. | Install the runtimes so the legacy tree can at least be imported or linted. | Nothing runnable exists to provision them for: there is no requirements file, no frontend manifest, no lockfile and no Dockerfile anywhere in the tree, so neither an install nor a build nor a test run can succeed. The documented ceilings are visible at [`.github/workflows/ci.yml:L19`] and [`.github/workflows/ci.yml:L32`], corroborated by [`README.md:L29-L31`]. Installing them would produce an environment in which the next failure is still the missing manifest. | The legacy code is never executed or linted, so a latent defect in it stays latent. Assessed as no exposure: the tree is retired and reference-only, and its defects are catalogued at D-079 rather than fixed. | Low | — |
| **D-078** | Change **no repository manifest** — nothing added, updated or removed — and **do not author** the missing ones. | Prune the retired dependencies from the root manifest. Or author the absent requirements file, frontend manifest, lockfile and Dockerfile so the legacy tree becomes buildable. | Four independent reasons converge. The platform supplies the design system and every runtime capability the application uses, and none of them has a package identity to declare. The three presentation libraries load from a content network inside a single self-contained file, and the rule mandating them forbids a build step and local file dependencies, so introducing a manifest for them would violate the very rule that requires them. The validator is standard-library-only by D-066. And the legacy manifests are reference-mode — the root manifest is **not even valid JSON**, because a comment block begins after the closing brace [`package.json:L65`], so editing it means first repairing a file that is out of scope. Authoring the missing manifests would resurrect a stack the requirements retire. | The repository declares dependencies that nothing uses, including two interface packages [`package.json:L15-L16`] and a charting package that was never imported [`package.json:L19`]. Accepted: they are historical reference, and pruning them would mean editing an out-of-scope file. | Low | — |
| **D-079** | Leave the legacy tree's **broken references unrepaired**. | Repair them so the tree is at least internally consistent. Or delete the unreachable modules. | Every repair means editing an out-of-scope file in an application being retired, which the scope-containment prohibition of D-007 forbids and which would produce no working software in any case — the four defects are a `src/utils/` package imported by three modules yet absent, one model's database import disagreeing with every sibling's [`src/backend/models/startup.py:L1`], a theme module imported but never authored [`src/frontend/App.tsx:L12`], and an integrator reading and writing columns that do not exist on the model it targets. Fixing all four would still leave a tree with no manifest and no Dockerfile. | The tree contains code that cannot import, which a static analyser will flag forever. Accepted deliberately; the defects are the evidence base for D-006 and are catalogued in the traceability matrix rather than repaired. | Low | — |
| **D-080** | Leave the five legacy documentation files untouched and change **exactly one** legacy file — the root README — adding a pointer to the deliverable and marking the legacy stack declaration, the prerequisites and the build instruction as superseded historical reference. | Update the legacy documentation set to describe the new application. Or leave the README untouched too, for a zero-modification delivery. | The five documentation files describe the legacy system and are the requirement spine the traceability matrix reads; rewriting them would destroy that spine and duplicate the deliverable's own documentation. The README is the one unavoidable exception because it is the repository's entry point and currently instructs a reader to run a build command that **cannot succeed** — no Dockerfile exists anywhere in the tree, so [`README.md:L36`] is an instruction to fail. Leaving it would mean the first thing a newcomer does is run a broken command. | One legacy file is now modified, so the tree is no longer byte-identical to its pre-delivery state. Accepted as the minimum intervention that stops the entry point from misleading. | Low | — |

**On D-070, the delivered composition.** The 313 update records comprise 77 choice values, 74 role joins, 49 access controls, 31 REST operations, 13 declared indexes, 11 properties, 10 tables, 10 list views, 10 form sections, 9 menu modules, 8 Script Includes, 3 roles, 3 business rules, 1 scheduled job, 1 related list, 1 REST definition, 1 application and 1 application menu. That arithmetic sums to 313 exactly. The table records bundle 111 dictionary records and 111 label records inside their payloads per D-071.

## 9. Repository and governance

Nine decisions. None of these is asked for by the requirements; each is forced by one of the three project rules, and several are deviations from a literal reading of the rule that forced them.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-081** | Place Rule 1's two artifacts — this log and the traceability matrix — under **`docs/decisions/`**. | Place them inside the deliverable package at `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/`. Or place them in the pre-existing `documentation/` tree alongside the legacy specifications. | **Rule 1 (Explainability) specifies no path for either artifact, so this placement is a free choice and is recorded here for that reason.** The `docs/` tree already has to exist because Rule 3 (Critical Decision Review Document) names an exact path inside it, so using it costs nothing new. Putting them in the deliverable package would conflate repository-level governance with the platform artifact, implying they describe only the ServiceNow application when they also cover the legacy retirement and the repository-level choices. The `documentation/` tree is legacy and read-only by D-080, so writing new governance into it would contradict that. | The repository now has two documentation trees with similar names, `docs/` and `documentation/`, which is genuinely confusable. Mitigated by the two having disjoint contents — governance and deliverable pointers in one, legacy specifications in the other. | Low | — |
| **D-082** | Create a **new top-level `docs/` tree** rather than reinterpreting Rule 3's path to fit the existing `documentation/` directory. | Treat `documentation/review/CRITICAL_DECISIONS.md` as satisfying the rule, since the repository's documentation lives there. | Rule 3 names an exact path, and a path is either matched or not — reinterpreting it would mean the artifact is not where the rule says it is, and a reviewer following the rule would not find it. The repository has no `docs/` directory at all, confirmed by direct check, so the tree must be created. | A second documentation root appears in a repository that already had one, and the delivery adds three files across two new subdirectories. Assessed as the lesser cost against failing an exact-path requirement. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-083** | Add **three columns beyond Rule 1's four**: a stable `D-###` identifier, an explicit risk level, and a flag marker column. | Carry exactly the four columns the rule names, and convey identifiers and markers in prose beneath each table. | Each addition earns its place. The identifier is necessary rather than convenient: Rule 3's five entries must be a demonstrable strict subset of this log, and sibling documents cross-reference individual decisions — neither is possible without a stable handle. The risk level makes the Rule 3 ordering verifiable from this file. The flag column is what makes the subset relationship, the deviations and the three ambiguity resolutions checkable by filtering rather than by reading every row of a log this long — 342 rows at this revision, and the reason the count is stated as a measure in [Summary counts](#summary-counts) rather than inline in a rationale is precisely that it moves with every review pass. Conveying all three in prose would make none of them checkable. | The tables are wider than the rule's literal four columns, which a strict reader could regard as a departure. Which is exactly why this row exists — the addition is declared rather than assumed harmless. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-084** | **Author** Rule 2's canonical theme stylesheet at the path the rule cites, reconstructing it from the rule's own specification. | Treat the citation as a reference to an existing asset and omit the file. Or inline the styling only, leaving the cited path empty. | The cited file was searched for and **is absent from this repository**, so there is nothing to reference — treating the citation as satisfiable would leave the delivery pointing at a file that does not exist. The rule states its own token block, slide-type classes and component classes in full, so reconstruction is faithful rather than inventive. Omitting the file would leave the cited path broken for anyone following the rule. | A reconstructed canonical file could diverge from whatever the rule's authors hold elsewhere. Mitigated by building it strictly from the rule text and adding nothing the rule does not name. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-085** | **Mirror** that stylesheet's content inline in the presentation and **never link** the local file. | Link the local stylesheet from the presentation, which is what a cited theme file normally implies. Or inline only and skip creating the file. | The rule creates its own tension: it forbids local file dependencies while naming a local theme file. Doing both resolves it — the file exists at the cited path for anyone who wants the canonical source, and the presentation carries the same content inline so it opens anywhere with no build step and no adjacent file. Linking would break the self-contained requirement; skipping the file would break the citation, per D-084. | The same stylesheet content now exists in two places and can drift if one is edited alone. Mitigated by requiring the two to be identical and by making that identity a checkable delivery condition. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-086** | Target **16** slides, inside the 12-to-18 band Rule 2 permits, and hold that number: **the delivered deck carries exactly 16 `<section>` elements** and every `of N` slide comment reads `of 16`. | Target the lower bound at 12 for brevity, or the upper bound at 18 for coverage. Or let the count drift within the permitted band as content is added, since 17 still satisfies Rule 2's own gate. | Sixteen is the rule's own stated target, and the content divides naturally into it: a title, a headline summary, a before-and-after architecture pair, then alternating dividers and content slides across the data model, access control, the API, ingestion, the portal, testing and risk, and a close. Twelve would force two topics onto one slide and breach the four-bullet limit; eighteen would add slides with nothing new to say. **Drift is rejected even inside the permitted band** because the figure is stated as sixteen in the agreed plan, in this row and in the traceability matrix: a delivered 17 would satisfy Rule 2's gate while contradicting three documents, and a count stated in three places and true in none is worse than either bound. Where content growth pressed against the target, two adjacent closing topics were **merged onto one slide** rather than the count being allowed to rise — see `D-110`. | A fixed target constrains content: a topic needing more room must be compressed, and a merge puts two subjects under one heading. Accepted, since the audience is non-technical leadership and compression is the point. | Low | — |
| **D-087** | Extend the requirement side of the traceability matrix from the **8** source features the plan mapped to the **10** the requirements document actually defines. | Map the 8 the plan named and leave the other two unmapped. | Direct inspection of the requirements document confirms ten features, each with five sub-requirements, at `documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L367` through `:L493`. Two of them — Data Visualization at `:L479` and Notification System at `:L493` — were real and unmapped. Rule 1 requires the matrix to have no gaps, so a matrix built on eight features fails the rule outright; the correction is mandatory rather than discretionary. Of the newly mapped feature, two sub-requirements map to the dashboard route and one to the investor profile, and the remainder are recorded as excluded at D-094 and D-093. | Two more features mean two more exclusions to justify, which is why the flag inventory also grows, at D-088. Assessed as strictly better than an incomplete matrix. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-088** | **Superseded as a count by `D-252` and `D-300`: the inventory now stands at nineteen, `F1` through `F19`. The extension principle below stands and is the reason it grew.** Extend the flag inventory from **10** entries to **12**, and widen the no-counterpart entry to three source requirements by adding saved searches. | Keep the ten the plan named and treat the additional exclusions as covered by existing entries. | The requirements demand that anything with no clean platform equivalent be flagged rather than silently omitted or half-implemented, and the two features newly mapped at D-087 brought genuinely unflagged exclusions with them. Folding them into existing entries would leave a reader unable to find them by name, which is the same failure as not flagging them. | Twelve flags is more surface to keep consistent across documents — and the inventory has since grown to nineteen, so the risk was real and materialised: several documents restated a stale count. Mitigated as originally intended by the inventory living in one place, with a computed summary table and a disposition roll-up there, and every other document referencing rather than restating it. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-089** | **Superseded again as a count by the review tranches through `D-557`: thirty sections and 557 rows, the figure [Summary counts](#summary-counts) carries. Superseded again as a count by `D-447`: twenty-three sections and 457 rows. Superseded again as a count by `D-411`: eighteen sections and 350 rows. Superseded as a count: the log then stood at 322 decisions across seventeen sections, reconciled arithmetically under [Summary counts](#summary-counts). The refusal-to-merge principle below stands and is the reason it grew.** Log **309** decisions across sixteen sections, exceeding the **44** decisions the plan states this file carries one row for each of — the 60-to-75 range this row once attributed to the plan is not a figure the plan states — rather than merging rows to land inside it. The overrun is reconciled arithmetically under [Summary counts](#summary-counts). | Merge related rows until the count falls in the estimated range. Or split the log across several files by theme. | The estimate predates the artifacts. The first tranche alone came in at 101, because eleven documents, an Update Set of several hundred records and two flow builds each raised choices a competent engineer could have taken differently, and Rule 1 requires each to carry its own row. The remaining 221 rows arrived as the delivered artifacts were reviewed under seven separate lenses, and Rule 1 treats an unexplained deviation as a **defect**, so merging until a target count was met would trade a real compliance failure for a cosmetic one. Splitting the file would break the single-source-of-truth property every sibling document depends on. | A 322-row log is long, and length discourages reading. Mitigated by seventeen themed sections, the section map, the at-a-glance summary counts, and the two closing indexes, which let a reader reach any decision without a linear read. | Low | `DEV` |

## 10. Conscious exclusions

Twelve decisions. Every row here exists so that an absence reads as a decision rather than an oversight. Several correspond to requirements in the source requirements document that the authoritative prompt does not carry.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-090** | **Flag** premium subscription billing, payment processing and checkout as having no platform equivalent, and implement none of it. | Build a partial billing surface — an order form, a subscription record, an invoice table — to show intent. Or implement an integration against a payment provider. | The platform grants entitlements through roles and has **no commerce, checkout or payment capability whatsoever**, so there is nothing to configure. The requirements direct that anything without a clean equivalent be flagged rather than silently omitted **or half-implemented**, which rules out the partial surface explicitly — a subscription table that never takes a payment is worse than nothing, because it implies a capability that does not exist. The source requirements agree that this is external: they frame payment gateways as third-party [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L107`], ask for a subscription management system [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L446`], and make gateway integration a success criterion [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L255`]; the project proposal excludes financial transactions on the same grounds [`documentation/Software Project Proposal.md:L165-L166`]. | The premium tier has no self-service path to entitlement, so the commercial model is incomplete. Recorded as the headline flag rather than concealed. | High | — |
| **D-091** | Exclude **automatic entitlement transition** from the base role to the premium role on payment, leaving an administrator granting the role manually as the operational path. | Build the transition against a placeholder payment signal. Or grant the premium role on self-service request. | The transition depends entirely on the billing capability excluded at D-090 — there is no payment event to react to. A placeholder signal would be a role grant triggered by nothing, which is an access-control defect rather than a feature. Granting on request would make the premium tier free, defeating the distinction the seven field-level controls exist to enforce. | Entitlement is a manual administrative act, which does not scale. Accepted as the correct consequence of D-090 rather than papered over. | Medium | — |
| **D-092** | Exclude the **three source requirements with no counterpart in the authoritative prompt**: web crawlers, saved searches and scheduled report delivery. | Implement them as unrequested additions, since each is a real requirement of the source document. | The prompt is authoritative for application content by D-001, and it names none of the three. Web crawling [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L373`] is excluded because only two named sources exist and no scraping is required, per D-052. Saved searches [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L403`] would need a persistence table outside the binding seven and a route outside the mandated five. Scheduled report delivery [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L461`] would need an email capability no requirement establishes. Implementing any of them would add scope the acceptance criteria do not test. | Three documented source requirements go unmet, and a reader of that document alone will find them missing. Mitigated by each being named individually in the flag inventory with its citation. | Medium | — |
| **D-093** | Exclude the **entire notification feature** — in-app notifications, an email service, notification preferences, real-time updates for followed startups and digest emails. | Implement a subset, most plausibly in-app notifications alone. | No notification, follow or email requirement appears anywhere in the authoritative prompt, and the feature is self-consistently absent from it: there is no follow relationship in the binding field list to notify about, no preference storage among the seven tables, and no route among the five to manage preferences on. The feature is declared at `documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L493` with its five sub-requirements at `:L499-L503`. A subset would still need a follow table and a preferences surface, so it is not meaningfully smaller. | An entire source feature is unimplemented. Surfaced as its own flag rather than folded into a general exclusion, so its scale is visible. | Medium | — |
| **D-094** | Exclude both **geographic distribution maps** and **growth-rate comparisons** from the visualization feature, while covering its other three sub-requirements. | Approximate the map by geocoding the headquarters text at display time. Or approximate growth from the employee band. | Both approximations would present derived guesses as data. The headquarters column is a free-text place name of bounded length with no coordinate pair [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L489` states the requirement], and geocoding it would need an external service no requirement authorises. Growth [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L487`] has no source at all: the binding field list carries a single current employee band and no history, and the legacy growth-rate column [`src/backend/models/startup.py:L9-L21`] is one of the attributes D-023 excludes. A band cannot yield a rate without a prior value. | Two of five sub-requirements of one feature are unmet. Mitigated by the other three being covered and by the feature being fully accounted for sub-requirement by sub-requirement. | Low | — |
| **D-095** | Exclude an **administrative console as a portal route**, despite the source requirements asking for an administrative panel. | Add a sixth portal route as an administrative console. | The requirements enumerate exactly five routes and none is an administrative console, so a sixth breaches the count. The capability itself is served by the native views of D-062, which is why this is a non-implementation of the requested **surface** rather than of the function. The source requirement appears at [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L858`] with a mockup reference at [`documentation/Software Requirements Specifications (SRS).md:L871`]. | An administrator does not get the purpose-built console the source document describes. Assessed as low impact, since the native views expose every table with the same access controls. | Low | — |
| **D-096** | Retire the named infrastructure — caching, search, containerisation, reverse proxy and process supervision — building no platform equivalent for any of it, and leaving every configuration file on disk untouched. | Build platform equivalents where one exists, most plausibly a caching layer. Or delete the configuration files, since nothing reads them. | All five are named out of scope, so building equivalents is unrequested work. Search specifically is re-expressed rather than replaced: portal and API search use encoded platform queries, which is why the absence of a search engine costs nothing. Deleting the configuration files would breach the read-only posture of D-003 and remove evidence the traceability matrix records. | The application has no caching layer, so every read reaches the database. Accepted under the prototype focus, and no requirement states a latency target. | Low | — |
| **D-097** | Use **generic outbound REST steps** bound to credential aliases and install or reference **no packaged integration content set**, treating the distinction between the two as material. | Install a packaged content set for each source, which would supply prebuilt actions. | The requirements forbid packaged content sets while simultaneously requiring the aliases to be referenced by name in flow steps, which only makes sense once the distinction is recognised: a packaged content set is prebuilt integration content for a named third party, whereas a generic REST step is a platform primitive and is not one. Both paths of D-045 stay on the primitive side of that line. | Generic steps mean the request and response shaping is hand-built for each source rather than supplied. Accepted; the two sources need one call each. | Low | — |
| **D-098** | Author **no responsive refinement below 1024 pixels**. | Author the full range down to phone widths anyway, as a courtesy. | Viewports below that width are named out of scope, so refinements there would be unrequested and, more importantly, unverified — no acceptance criterion exercises a narrow viewport, so any such work would ship untested. This is the same boundary as D-057, recorded here as an exclusion so it appears in the inventory of what was deliberately not built. | Narrow-viewport rendering is undefined rather than merely imperfect. Mitigated by the floor being documented explicitly. | Low | — |
| **D-099** | **Invent no performance target**, and add no capacity, caching or observability layer — while distinguishing that from **correctness hardening**, which is built wherever the absence of it would be a defect rather than a missing optimisation. | Set reasonable throughput and latency targets and design to them. Or add an observability, caching or load-testing layer as good practice. Or read the prototype focus as licence to leave a known correctness gap unclosed. | The requirements state a prototype focus, preferring correct and testable functionality over hardening, and state **no** throughput, latency or availability figure anywhere. Inventing one would create an acceptance criterion nobody asked for and could not be validated against a requirement. But a prototype focus is not a reason to ship a **known** defect: a lost counter update, an unvalidated URL written to a record, an unescaped value in a log line, an over-broad erasure or a duplicate-creating replay are each wrong at any volume, so each is closed — the lost counter update at **D-040**, the unvalidated URL at **D-159**, the unescaped log value at **D-415**, the over-broad erasure at **D-429** and the duplicate-creating replay at **D-180**. (An earlier revision of this row cited D-111 to D-114 for four of the five; those rows govern the ingestion tests' fixtures, tokens, counters and the branch-determined source mode, and none of them closes a defect named here.) Three specific inefficiencies are likewise avoided because each would be a defect: the portfolio recalculation is scoped to affected investors per D-014, the count is stored rather than recomputed per D-013, and the total is an aggregate rather than an iteration per D-041. | No load testing, no horizontal-scale design, no caching and no observability stack, so behaviour under real volume is unknown. Accepted explicitly and stated rather than implied. The boundary between "correctness" and "hardening" is a judgement, so the five closures above are logged individually rather than left to this row to imply. | Medium | — |
| **D-100** | Record the exclusion of a **path foreign to this project** as a defensive note, while treating the operative half of that exclusion — any backend not part of this scoped application — as fully active over the entire legacy source tree. | Ignore the clause entirely as inapplicable. | A tree-wide search confirms no such path exists in this repository, so the named half is inapplicable — but silently dropping a stated exclusion would leave a requirement unaccounted for. The operative half is genuinely active and does real work: it is what places the whole of `src/` outside the delivery. | Recording an inapplicable exclusion may read as padding. Mitigated by stating plainly that the named path was searched for and is absent, and that the general clause is what applies. | Low | — |
| **D-101** | Retire the **third API key** declared in the legacy environment template alongside the two integration keys, with no target artifact of any kind. | Provision a third credential alias for it. Or carry it forward as a property for future use. | The template declares three keys — the two integration keys and a third at [`.env.example:L29`] — but the requirements name only two data sources, so the third has no consumer: no flow calls it, no alias holds it and no property references it. Provisioning an alias for an unused credential would create a secret with no purpose, which is a liability rather than a capability. | If some future integration needed it, the credential would have to be reprovisioned. Assessed as no exposure: retiring an unused secret strictly reduces surface. | Low | — |

## 11. Remediation of the delivered artifacts — documentation, tests and completeness

Twenty-three decisions — `D-102` through `D-104` on the ingestion artifact decomposition, `D-105` through `D-119` from the first remediation round and `D-120` through `D-124` from the second. Every row here was taken while correcting the delivered artifacts against a review of them, and each answers the same shape of question: an instruction was satisfiable in a way that produced a passing check which proved nothing, and a different construction was chosen instead. The rows are grouped that way rather than by artifact because the reasoning is common to them — **a check that cannot fail is worse than an absent one**, since an absent check is visible and a vacuous one is not.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
