Or log the values and rely on the log's access controls. | A 32-bit space is exhaustively searchable in negligible time and collides at a few tens of thousands of values, so the delivered fingerprint was neither collision-resistant nor recovery-resistant and was doing no real work. Replacing it with SHA-256 would have been stronger and still **not anonymisation**: an unkeyed digest over a value drawn from a small guessable set — a person's name, an employer, an email address — is recoverable by dictionary attack, so the log would still hold personal data while reading as though it did not, which is the more dangerous outcome. An HMAC closes that and needs a key, and prompt section 4.0 forbids this delivery from holding any secret, so it is unavailable. Correlation by **reference** needs no derivation of the value at all: the ordinal locates the record within the run and the reference locates the stored row, and neither can be reversed into a name because neither is computed from one. | Correlation is per run rather than across runs: the same person arriving in two runs carries two unrelated ordinals, so a cross-run trace must be reconstructed from the staging rows rather than read from the log. Accepted deliberately — a cross-run correlator over personal values is precisely the property this decision removes. A reader expecting a digest finds none, which is why the absence is stated positively in `data-model.md`, in `sample-data/README.md` and in both flow guides rather than left to be inferred. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-159** | Expose **one** canonical URL allowlist and **one** canonical address allowlist as public methods of `RestQueryHelper`, and call them from both write paths — the REST create and update contract, and `IngestionMapper.coerceField` before any staging-to-entity write — refusing an unsafe value with a bounded reason and continuing the run. | Leave the regular expressions private to the REST layer, as delivered, and accept that ingestion validated URLs only by length. Or duplicate the patterns in the mapper. | Nine stored columns are read back and bound into Service Portal link and image targets, and the portal cannot tell which write path a stored value arrived by. With the allowlist on one path only, a locator no REST caller could submit — `javascript:`, `data:`, `http:`, an embedded credential — could be written by an ingestion run and reach a template by the back door. Duplicating the patterns is the drift failure this project avoids everywhere else. The authority check is new: the credential form `https://user:pass@host/` is refused by reading the authority component and rejecting an `@` in it, while an `@` later in a path stays legal. | The ingestion path is now stricter, so a previously stored non-`https` locator would be refused on a re-run; that is the intended behaviour. None of the nine columns is mandatory, so a refusal drops one field rather than rejecting a record, which means a source that supplies malformed locators loses them quietly — mitigated by every refusal being logged with the field name and a bounded reason. | Low | — |
| **D-160** | Restrict every ingestion step output that is not a data-path value to a **bounded code from a closed vocabulary**, discard the platform's own error text, and recommend the orchestrator construction on privacy grounds as well as evidence grounds. | Pass `response.getErrorMessage()` and `e.message` through as the delivered guides did. Or add a diagnostics output carrying a sample of the response. | Flow Designer persists step inputs and outputs in the platform's flow execution context, which is a retention surface outside this application's scope and outside its access control. The platform's error text can quote the endpoint, the composed request and a fragment of the response body, so passing it through writes upstream content into that surface for no diagnostic gain a four-value code does not give. Two outputs must carry upstream content because the pipeline cannot run otherwise — the response body step 4 parses and the mapped row set step 5 cleans — and those two are named, bounded to four binding rules, and forbidden from being copied or widened. The orchestrator construction keeps every per-record intermediate inside one call, so none of them enters the context at all. | A four-code vocabulary is less informative than platform text when an integration fails for an unusual reason, and the operator must then reproduce the call outside the flow. Accepted: the alternative writes personal data into a surface with no retention bound. The two data-path residencies remain and are governed rather than removed. | Medium | — |
| **D-161** | Record the **flow execution context retention and access procedure** as a platform-owner obligation with four actions and an explicit unmet status, rather than implementing it. | Add a scheduled job that prunes the flow execution tables. Or say nothing, on the reasoning that the application's own logs carry no personal data. | The flow execution tables are platform tables outside `x_bst_startuptrk`, and prompt section 6.0 forbids this application from modifying anything outside its scope, so a pruning job would be a scope breach as well as a twelfth artifact against a plan-mandated inventory of one scheduled job. Saying nothing was the delivered position and it was actively misleading: `data-model.md` stated that the logs need no erasure sweep because no personal data reaches them, which is true of what the application **writes** and says nothing about what the platform **records around** it. The procedure is therefore written out in full — the surface, the two values, why it falls to the platform owner, four actions with their confirmation evidence, and the delivered side of the boundary. | The obligation is unmet and will stay unmet on any instance whose owner does not act, so upstream personal data can persist in the flow execution context for as long as that instance's default retention allows. Mitigated only by the entry stating its own status as unmet rather than implying discharge. | Medium | — |
| **D-162** | Make the ATF inbound-REST identity **ephemeral by preference** — created inside each REST test, rolled back by the framework — with a hardened persistent identity as a documented fallback, and bind five properties to both: exactly one scoped role, `web_service_access_only` true, a generated secret never written into the package, a named owner and expiry, and a teardown proved by query. | Keep the delivered persistent `bst.atf.rest` account with `web_service_access_only` false and no teardown. Or give the tests an administrator credential, which would be simpler and far worse. | The delivered account could sign in to the user interface, its password was described in a committed document as one the operator sets, nothing owned it, nothing expired it, and the guide stated it was **expected** to persist. That is a standing interactive login created for a test run and left behind. An ephemeral identity makes the owner and expiry questions disappear rather than answering them. The persistent form survives only because the REST step may accept its Basic Auth Configuration solely as a design-time reference, which is an unknown about the step rather than a preference — so the guide makes it a recorded design-time check with a branch, not an assumption. | The ephemeral construction depends on the framework rolling back a user its script step created; a test that errors with its transaction open leaves the account behind, which is why both residual queries are part of the teardown and a residual record invalidates the whole run. On the persistent construction the account exists between runs, deactivated, and a deactivated account with a live credential is still a credential — which is why deletion, not deactivation, is the stated completion condition. | Medium | — |
| **D-163** | Give guide 01 **two readiness paths**: verify and bind where the live credentials are provisioned, and **create the alias, connection and credential metadata records with every secret-bearing field empty** where they are not, then force the sanctioned fallback and label every result `fallback validated`. | Keep the delivered verify-and-bind-only posture, which stops the guide when an alias is absent. Or create the records with placeholder secrets so the connection test passes. | Prompt section 4.0 states the live credentials are already provisioned, and the plan built guide 01 on that premise. On the target instance **neither alias exists and no credential material was supplied**, so the delivered guide stopped before guides 02 and 03 and the fallback dataset that the same prompt section sanctions for exactly this condition — live authentication failing, timing out, or credentials being re-issued — was unreachable. Creating metadata is not creating a secret: every secret-bearing field is left empty, and the LinkedIn provider, profile and token records are deliberately **not** created because each needs material only the owner can supply. Placeholder secrets were rejected outright: a test made to pass without a provisioned credential is a fabricated result. | The build proceeds without live integration ever being demonstrated, so the acceptance evidence for the ingestion criterion is fallback-only — which the criterion explicitly accepts **provided the mode is recorded**, and the labelling rule is stated in six documents. A `fallback validated` result is not retrospectively upgraded when credentials arrive; live validation needs its own runs. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-164** | Drive the three form-based deployment routes through **one fail-fast session procedure**: a permission-restricted cookie jar inside the working tree, a login whose success is asserted from the jar rather than from a status code, a non-empty `sysparm_ck` assertion, the same jar on every request, no credential or token value echoed, and guaranteed cleanup on any exit. | Let each route authenticate independently, as the delivered runbook implied. Or hold the session token in an environment variable. | The upload, preview and commit routes are form endpoints, not REST endpoints, so they need a browser-shaped session and a cross-site request token. Authenticating three times triples the failure surface and leaves three token lifetimes to reason about. Asserting from the jar matters because the login endpoint returns a success status for a **failed** login and simply serves the login page again, so a status-code assertion passes while no session exists and the next request fails somewhere unrelated. Reporting lengths rather than values keeps the transcript free of both the password and the token. | A single session that expires mid-deployment fails the remaining routes together rather than one at a time. Accepted: the failure is then unambiguous, and the procedure is re-runnable from the start. The jar is a file on disk for the deployment's duration, mitigated by mode 600, by placement inside the working tree, and by a cleanup trap on every exit path. | Medium | — |
| **D-165** | Give every deployment a **unique run identity** in the update set's name — the clone index and a timestamp nonce — and capture and compare the retrieved record **by that identity**, requiring exactly one new match. | Identify the retrieved update set by recency, as the delivered runbook did. Or by the file name. | Recency is not identity. Parallel clones share one instance, so two deployments in flight make "the most recent record" a race, and a retry after a partial failure makes it ambiguous even for one. Requiring exactly one match turns a collision into an abort rather than into a commit of somebody else's file. | The name is longer and carries a nonce a human must copy accurately into subsequent requests. Mitigated by the runbook exporting it once as a shell variable used everywhere thereafter. | Low | — |
| **D-166** | Set the retry policy **per operation** rather than per status: retry idempotent reads freely, retry a non-idempotent upload, preview, commit or delete **only after re-reading the state** to establish whether the first attempt took effect, and state that this precedence overrides the generic rule for a server error. | Keep the delivered single rule that retries any server error once after thirty seconds. Or never retry anything. | A blind retry of a non-idempotent operation is how one deployment becomes two. Retrying an upload that in fact succeeded creates a second retrieved update set; retrying a commit that in fact succeeded is at best wasted and at worst racing the first. The state re-read is what makes the retry safe, and stating the precedence explicitly is what stops the generic server-error rule being read as licence. | The policy is longer than one rule and an operator may not read to the end of it. Mitigated by the failure matrix naming the operation class in every row rather than only the status. | Low | — |
| **D-167** | Permit the scope-deletion rollback **only** on a proven clean install, behind four further preconditions: a recorded installation mode from pre-flight, exactly one matching scope record, a confirmation token typed for the current run, and an abort on zero or multiple matches — with a documented update-set backout path for an upgrade install and a four-surface post-delete confirmation. | Keep the delivered procedure, which read `result[0].sys_id` and deleted. Or remove the rollback entirely and leave every failure to manual recovery. | The delivered rollback was one unguarded index away from deleting a scope this deployment did not create, on an instance where the scope pre-existed — destroying data, flows, portal records and test suites it cannot restore. It is the only irreversible operation in the delivery, recorded at D-076, so it is the one place where a precondition is worth more than a remedy. The installation mode is recorded at pre-flight precisely because after a failed commit it can no longer be established. An upgrade install needs a different remedy, so one is documented rather than the rollback being stretched to cover it. | The rollback is now refusable, so an operator on an ambiguous instance is left with a partially installed application and a referral to the instance owner. That is the correct outcome: an unclean instance is recoverable and a deletion that removed someone else's scope is not. | High | — |
| **D-168** | Establish the release floor **objectively**, with a family-token extraction from the instance's own version string compared against an ordered release list, and an abort branch carrying the plan's instruction to request a new instance. | Record the version string and proceed, which the delivered runbook did. Or drop the check. | A check that records a value and proceeds regardless has asserted nothing: a pre-floor instance passes it exactly as a current one does, and the first evidence of the shortfall is a Flow Designer capability that is absent halfway through guide 02. The ordered list is explicit because the release naming restarted the alphabet, so a lexical comparison is wrong. | The list needs extending as releases ship, and an unrecognised family token must abort rather than be assumed current. Stated as such in the runbook. | Medium | — |
| **D-169** | Validate the environment **before any request**: require `SERVICENOW_INSTANCE_URL` to be exported, `https`, host-only with no path and no trailing slash, and require both credential variables to be non-empty — reporting the password's **presence** and neither its value nor its length. | Assume the variable is set, as the delivered runbook did in referring to it without exporting or checking it. | The variable is not injected by the environment, so an unset one composes request URLs beginning `/api/now/...` and the failure surfaces as a confusing transport error rather than as the configuration error it is. A trailing slash produces a double slash that some routes reject and others accept, which is worse than either. A boolean carries the whole of the diagnostic information the operator needs, while a length narrows an offline guessing space and distinguishes one operator's credential from another's in a shared transcript, so the delivered block reports presence alone. | Four more lines before the first request, and a stricter URL rule than the platform itself needs. Accepted: the check is executable in seconds and was verified against five inputs. | Low | — |
| **D-170** | Replace the blind instruction to delete a failed update set and its customer updates with an **exact-identifier, cardinality-guarded, confirmed** procedure, keyed on the captured record identifier alone, with three explicit prohibitions. | Keep the delivered instruction, which selected by name and state. Or omit the cleanup and leave failed sets on the instance. | Selecting by name and state on a shared instance can match another deployment's set, and deleting customer updates by that selection is unrecoverable. Keying on the identifier captured earlier in the same run makes the target unambiguous, and the cardinality guard makes a mismatch an abort. The prohibitions close the three routes back to the original hazard. | Failed sets accumulate where the guard refuses to act, leaving instance housekeeping for the owner. Accepted, and stated in the procedure: an unclean instance is recoverable; a deletion that removed someone else's update set is not. | Medium | — |
| **D-171** | **Amended by `D-394`, which replaces the absent `error_detail` field with the execution tracker and the retrieved output records, and by `D-396`, which normalises the identifier scheme of each rollback branch.** Define the **commit validation contract** explicitly: the validation call's response shape, the status and answer assertions, tracker polling to a terminal state, and an abort branch **before** the commit call is issued. | Call the commit processor directly, as the delivered runbook did after an undefined validation step. | The validation processor exists precisely to refuse a commit that would fail, and its answer is the refusal. Issuing the commit without asserting on that answer discards the one signal that distinguishes a set that will apply from one that will not, and the failure then arrives as a commit failure whose remedy is the irreversible rollback. Polling the tracker to a terminal state is what stops a still-running validation being read as a pass. | Two more polling loops and a longer step. Accepted: the alternative trades minutes for a rollback. | Low | — |
| **D-172** | Document the **uniform** access policy on every system-property record — `x_bst_startuptrk.admin` for both read and write on all eleven — annotated per property, rather than describing only some as role-restricted. | Leave the delivered statement, which said only two properties were role-restricted. Or vary the policy per property so the delivered statement would become true. | The delivered statement was simply false: every property record carries the admin role on both read and write, and the two it named as exceptions were the two later removed at D-036. Varying the policy was rejected because no property holds a secret and none needs a wider reader; a uniform policy is one rule to verify rather than eleven. | A reader wanting a non-administrative surface to read a property must go through a Script Include, which is the intended path and is how every component already reads them. | Low | — |
| **D-173** | Turn the accepted advisory of **D-133** into a **signable disposition record**: every field the delivery can establish is written out — item, pinned and fixing versions, the authority for the pin, the assessment date, the scope of the risk, the mitigations, the residual risk, the recommended disposition and the review trigger — and exactly **four** fields are left reading `REQUIRED — not yet supplied`, being the decision, the risk-owner role, the accepting person's name and the acceptance date. Accompany it with a table that names each identified advisory, the range it states, whether the pinned release falls inside that range, and whether the code path it describes is **reachable in this deck**. | Leave the acceptance as the prose statement it was, and rely on the gate wording to force a signature. Or record only a summary count of advisories without per-advisory reachability. Or re-pin the library and re-derive the digests. | An acceptance that names no owner and carries no date is indistinguishable from an assumption, and the checklist could be ticked without anyone deciding anything. Writing the record with named blocking fields makes refusal the default: the gate, the definition-of-done item and the final sign-off table all fail while any of the four is unfilled. Per-advisory reachability is what makes the recommendation checkable rather than reassuring — six advisories were identified, one does not apply to the pinned release at all, and the other five describe paths this deck does not exercise: five literal `flowchart` sources, one `mermaid.initialize` call taking an object literal, no `setConfig`, no `updateSiteConfig`, no in-diagram initialiser, and no input surface of any kind. Re-pinning stays unavailable for the reason **D-133** gives. | The record can still be signed without the re-query it asks for, leaving a stale snapshot behind a fresh signature. Mitigated by making the re-query an evidence item of the gate rather than a suggestion. The reachability findings are properties of the delivered file and would have to be re-established if the deck's diagram set ever changed. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-174** | Harden the integrity-verification command in place rather than trusting its earlier form: **name the two binaries it requires and check for them**, bound the connect and total time and the retry count, **download to a file and refuse an empty body**, digest the file rather than a pipe, **compare each digest against the attribute the deck declares**, and exit non-zero on the first failure. Add a **standard-library Python equivalent inline** for hosts without the digest binary. | Leave the original one-line pipeline and rely on the operator reading the output. Or ship the verifier as a new script file in the package. | The original piped a download straight into the digest utility with no failure handling, so a network error produced the digest of an **empty body** — a plausible-looking value that matches nothing, printed with no indication that anything went wrong, in a check whose entire purpose is detecting substituted bytes. Writing to a file makes the empty case detectable, and comparing against the deck's own attributes turns an eyeballed transcription into a mechanical assertion. A new script file was rejected because the planned file set is closed at thirty; an inline alternative satisfies the same need without adding one. | The command now depends on the deck's attribute order matching the URL order in the loop. Mitigated by asserting the attribute count is exactly five before comparing, so a deck edit that adds or removes an asset fails loudly instead of comparing the wrong pair. | Medium | — |
| **D-175** | Reduce the four explanatory comments in the deck's **layout** stylesheet block to plain statements of what each rule does, re-homing the reasoning here, and **leave the canonical theme block unedited** even though two of its comments carry the same kind of clause. | Strip the effect clauses from the theme block too, for uniformity. Or leave all six as they were, treating an effect clause as description rather than rationale. | The layout block's own header states that its rationale lives in this log and that the block carries none, so a clause explaining why a rule is written the way it is contradicted the file in the file. The theme block is different in kind: it is the canonical stylesheet the presentation rule specifies, mirrored byte-identically into the deck, and editing its comments would deviate from the rule's own text — which outranks a comment-style heuristic. The asymmetry is therefore deliberate and is recorded rather than tidied away. | A reader comparing the two blocks may read the remaining theme comments as an oversight. Mitigated by the layout header scoping its claim to `this block` and by this row. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-176** | State the three-way staging contract as **delivered, with per-leg evidence a reader can check**, rather than as a table with one leg marked absent: each leg names the artifact that carries it, its status, and the specific count or section that proves it, closing on the identity `88 = 88 = 88`. **Corrected by D-431:** recounted from the delivered fixtures, the identity is `92 = 92 = 92` over **41** distinct column names, distributed 19, 12, 16, 14, 14 and 17 across the six files. The per-leg evidence form stands; the figure does not. | Leave the leg marked absent and let the reader reconcile it against the guide that already delivers it. Or delete the table and rely on prose. | The contract's whole purpose is that a change to any one leg is visible as a mismatch against the other two, and a table asserting a leg is absent while the guide delivers eighty-eight field maps defeats that: the reader cannot tell whether the table or the guide is stale. Naming the evidence per leg — six header rows over eighty-eight positions and forty distinct names, forty staging dictionary elements, eighty-eight one-to-one maps — makes the closing identity an assertion rather than a claim. | The three counts must be re-derived whenever a column is added to any leg. That is the intended cost, and it is the same cost the contract exists to impose. | Low | — |
| **D-177** | **Quote every designed-whitespace field** in the six sample files — fourteen in total — rather than leaving twelve of them bare, and state the rule in both the column contract and the import guide. | Leave them bare, relying on every downstream tool to preserve leading and trailing space. Or remove the fixtures and drop the whitespace-trimming assertion they exist to support. | The fields exist to prove the trimming rule fires, and a bare leading space is exactly what a spreadsheet, a text editor or a careless copy silently removes. The failure is the dangerous kind: a stripped fixture still loads, the trim rule has nothing to do, and the assertion **passes for the wrong reason**. Quoting does not change what a parser reads — the parsed values were compared before and after and are identical — so the fixtures keep their meaning while becoming resilient to the tools that would erase it. | A reader may take the quotes to mean the value is special rather than merely whitespace-bearing. Mitigated by the contract naming all fourteen in a table and by the guide carrying a matching convention row. | Low | — |
| **D-178** | Clear the busy flag in the portal controller from a **named helper invoked by both settled handlers**, rather than from a bracket-form `finally` handler on the promise. | Keep the bracket-form handler. Or duplicate the flag reset inside each handler. | The runtime the widget targets treats `finally` as a reserved word, which is why the bracket form was used at all; a named helper reaches the same guarantee without the reserved word and without duplicating the reset. It also removes a second, unrelated hazard: in a fenced code sample the bracket form is indistinguishable from a Markdown link, so a link checker read it as a link to a target that cannot exist. | Two call sites must both keep calling the helper. Mitigated by the guide stating the invariant as a bullet beside the sample. | Low | — |
| **D-179** | Call the deck's Mermaid configuration **synchronously while the inline script is still parsing**, rather than lazily from the first render pass, and clear a `data-processed` stamp left by any earlier pass before rendering. | Leave the configuration where it was and accept whichever pass rendered first. Or drop the early return on `data-processed`, letting every pass re-render every container. Or pin a library release whose automatic start defaults to off. | The library's **automatic start defaults to on**, and it registers a window-load handler. Configuring it from a pass chained off webfont readiness and the presentation framework's ready event therefore lost the race: the library's own pass rendered all five containers first, with **default** configuration, while their slides were still not displayed — which produced eight `translate(undefined, NaN)` errors, three diagrams collapsed to a 13-by-13 box that overprinted neighbouring copy, HTML labels instead of the configured text labels, and the default palette instead of the brand one. It then stamped every container processed, so the deck's own themed, health-checked, retrying pass **never ran at all**. Configuring during parse is the only point that precedes a window-load handler. Re-pinning was unavailable for the reason **D-133** gives. | The fix depends on the inline script remaining a classic, non-deferred script placed after the library tag; making it a module or adding `defer` would restore the race. Recorded here, and the ordering is stated in the comment at the call site. | Medium | — |

**On D-154, what the corrected check establishes and what it does not.** It establishes three properties of the bytes the upload will post: no byte-order mark precedes the content; nothing at all precedes the declaration, whitespace included; and the declaration is a declaration — `<?xml`, a whitespace byte, and a `version` pseudo-attribute before `?>`. It does **not** establish that the declaration's `version` or `encoding` values are the ones the instance expects, nor anything about the document below it; those are the province of the well-formedness parse that follows. The runbook's independent `od -c` observation of the first bytes remains, and the runbook states that the validator's exit code and that observation together establish the prologue, neither alone.
## 14. Remediation of the database, performance and integration review

Forty-nine decisions, in two rounds. The first fifteen — `D-180` to `D-194` — were taken in response to a **database and performance** review of the delivered artifacts. The next thirty-four — `D-195` to `D-228` — were taken in response to a second review of the same artifacts under an **integration** lens, covering the ingestion contracts, the provider contracts, the deployment mechanics, the test construction and the published inventories. Every row in this section was taken **after** the ten sections above were written, and each replaces an earlier choice that a review found unsound. They are collected in their own section rather than interleaved because the identifiers above are cited by fourteen sibling documents and by the Rule 3 review, and this log states that an identifier is never reused or renumbered — so appending is the only way to add a decision without invalidating a citation. Where a row supersedes an earlier one, the earlier row is named in its rationale.

**One row in this section has a different provenance, and says so here rather than reading as though a reviewer had raised it.** `D-228` was not a review finding: it came from runtime verification of the presentation carried out while closing the integration round, which reproduced an intermittent rendering defect the review had no way to see in a static reading. It is logged in this section because it was taken in the same round and replaces an earlier construction that verification found unsound — the distinction being only who found it.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-180** | Give every one of the six ingested record types a **natural key** and make every write an **upsert** against it: startup on `name` + `headquarters_location`, investor on `name`, founder and executive on `startup` + `name`, job posting on `startup` + `title`, and funding round on `startup` + `round_date`. | Keep the two keys that existed and let the other four insert on every run. Or deduplicate after the fact with a periodic merge job. Or add a source-identifier column to each entity table and key on that. | A scheduled flow re-reads an overlapping population every run, so a type with no key inserts a fresh row each time and the table grows without bound while every derived figure — `portfolio_count`, every `total_count`, every dashboard summary — counts the duplicates. A post-hoc merge job cannot recover the correct row: it has no way to tell which of several identical rows the downstream references point at. A source-identifier column is unavailable because prompt section 1.0 declares the entity field list binding and complete, which is also why the keys are built from columns that already exist. Each key's equality components lead a declared index per **D-188**, so the lookup is indexed rather than a scan. | A natural key is only as stable as the source values behind it. A company that changes its headquarters, or a person whose name is spelled differently upstream, presents as a new record and the old row is left behind rather than updated. That is the safer of the two failure directions — a stale duplicate is visible and repairable, a wrongly merged pair is neither — but it is a real cost, and it is why the match is confirmed on normalised values per **D-182** rather than on raw equality. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-181** | Require the live path to declare a **record type per call site** in a typed envelope, and make `record_type` mandatory at that one seam, rejecting a row that arrives without it. | Infer the type from the shape of the payload. Or default an untyped row to `startup`. Or accept the untyped object and let each downstream field mapping fail individually. | Every mapping decision downstream — which mandatory fields apply, which choice lists, which natural key, which table — is a function of the record type, so the type is not an attribute of a row but the precondition for reading one. Shape inference is guesswork that succeeds silently on the wrong branch: a founder payload and an executive payload are structurally identical. Defaulting is worse, because it writes real rows to the wrong table. Rejecting at a single documented seam means the failure is loud, is attributable, and cannot be reached by a row that got halfway. | The seam is a contract the flow builder must honour by hand, and a flow that pushes bare source objects gets **nothing** ingested rather than a partial result — a total failure that reports no transport error. Mitigated by making it the first thing both flow guides specify at the source-call step, and by an ATF assertion that drives an untyped control batch and requires `0` accepted with every row rejected, so the seam is proved rather than assumed. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-182** | Resolve a natural key with a **bounded tolerant candidate set plus a normalised confirmation**, not with an exact-equality prefilter: apply the equality components as indexed conditions, apply the text component tolerantly, confirm each candidate on trimmed case-folded values, stop at two confirmed matches, and cap the candidate set at 200 rows, reporting a key that exceeds the cap as **ambiguous** rather than resolving it on partial evidence. | Prefilter on exact equality, which is indexed and simple. Or read the whole table and confirm in memory. Or store a normalised key column and match on it exactly. | The cleaning rules require deduplication to be case- and whitespace-insensitive, and an exact prefilter defeats that: a stored `  Beacon Robotics ` never reaches the confirmation step for an incoming `beacon robotics`, so the very duplicate the rule exists to catch is inserted again. Reading the whole table is unbounded. A normalised key column would be the cleanest answer and is unavailable, because prompt section 1.0 forecloses adding a column to an entity table. Bounding the candidate set and failing closed on the cap keeps the read bounded without ever resolving an identity on incomplete information. | A tolerant text condition can match more rows than an equality one, so the cap is load-bearing rather than defensive: a very common name reaches it and is reported ambiguous, which stops the row rather than merging it into the wrong company. That is a deliberate fail-closed choice and it means a legitimate row can be refused. The reason is recorded on the row so an operator can see which key was ambiguous. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-183** | **Amended by `D-389`, which logs a bounded warning for a malformed entry, and by `D-393`, which fixes the published read path as the `AppProperties` marker accessors.** Persist the cadence state in a **scoped property rather than in the system log**, keyed per source so the two flows' cadences are genuinely independent, and read the whole cadence decision through `AppProperties` accessors rather than through a log query. **The delivered form is the single structured run marker** — the existing `ingestion.last_run_provenance` property, keyed by flow, written by `completeRun()` and read by `readRunMarkers()`, `getLastSuccessAt()` and `getCadenceHours()` — because the plan's eleven-property inventory admits no twelfth, and the two per-source properties this row first proposed would have been a twelfth and a thirteenth. | Mine the most recent run-summary line out of `syslog`, as the earlier design did. Or store the marker in a scoped table. Or hold one shared marker for both sources. | A log is not state. The run summary is written at **info** severity and `x_bst_startuptrk.logging.level` may legitimately be raised to `warn`, at which point the guard finds no marker and treats **every hourly execution** as a scheduled run — silently reducing a 24-hour cadence to one hour against a rate-limited third-party API. Instance-wide log retention is equally outside this application's control, so a pruned log makes an established flow look as though it had never run. The query was also an unindexed `CONTAINS` scan of the largest table on the instance, once per hourly execution, for a value the application could simply store. A scoped table would work and is more machinery than one string needs. One shared marker is wrong outright: either flow would then suppress the other. | A property is a single mutable value with no history, so a wrongly written marker suppresses the next real run for a full cadence window. Two mitigations: the accessors normalise the source token and accept only `crunchbase` and `linkedin`, so a typo writes nothing rather than something wrong; and the ATF flow suite captures the marker property in its first step and restores it in its last, because a test that left one stamped would make the next scheduled execution a no-op while the suite reported success. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-184** | Bound source discovery explicitly: iterate the source with a **page size**, a **per-run record ceiling** and a **resumable cursor** per source, all four values originally carried as properties (`ingestion.page_size`, `ingestion.max_records_per_run`, `ingestion.discovery_cursor_crunchbase`, `ingestion.discovery_cursor_linkedin`). **Superseded in part by the delivered inventory:** the bound itself ships and is asserted, but as declarations inside the discovery action — a `PAGE_SIZE` of 100 and a `MAX_CALLS` ceiling of 200 — with keyset pagination advancing on the last row's identifier within the run. The property inventory is frozen at eleven, so none of these four exists, and no cursor persists between runs; the natural-key matcher is what makes a re-enumerated population idempotent instead. | Look up a fixed list of known records, as the earlier design did. Or request the whole population in one unpaginated call. Or paginate without a ceiling and run to exhaustion. | A flow that acquires a hard-coded permalink, or one company looked up by name, is not discovering anything — it demonstrates the transport and ingests a fixture, and no volume of clean runs over it says anything about the real population. An unpaginated request for the whole population is one unbounded response, and running to exhaustion is one unbounded execution; both put the run at the mercy of the source's size. A ceiling with a cursor makes each execution bounded **and** makes successive executions make progress, which is what turns a large population into a series of finite runs. | A cursor is state that can be lost or corrupted, and a stale cursor silently skips a slice of the population. `setDiscoveryCursor()` therefore strips the value to the platform's token vocabulary and truncates it, and both guides state that clearing the cursor restarts discovery from the beginning — which the natural-key upserts of **D-180** make safe to do at any time, because a repeat pass updates rather than duplicates. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-185** | Insert participant links as a **batch**, then refresh the round's `participating_investors` projection **once** and recalculate each affected investor **once**, rather than per link. | Leave the per-insert business-rule cascade in place, which is the platform-idiomatic form. Or suppress the business rules for the batch and recalculate nothing. | The link business rule refreshes the projection and recalculates the investor on every insert, so a round with ten participants performed ten projection rebuilds and ten portfolio derivations for a result identical to doing each once. Suppressing the rules and recalculating nothing is the opposite error: it leaves the projection and every `portfolio_count` stale, and the plan requires flows to run business rules precisely so derived state cannot drift. Batching and then converging once keeps every derived value correct and makes the work proportional to the round rather than to its participant count. | The window between the batch insert and the single convergence is a window in which the projection and the counts are behind the join table. It is inside one flow action and closes before the run summary is written, and nothing caller-facing reads during it. A crash inside that window would leave both stale, which is why `InvestorPortfolioService` exposes `recalculateAll()` for an operator to run after any bulk load. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-186** | **Amended by `D-412`, which is the authority for how an unreconciled round is reported: the round's own `import_state` is set to `error` and the run summary counts it in `skipped`. There is no `partial` counter on any delivered surface; the propagation principle below stands.** Propagate the participant-link result out of the write and mark a round whose links did not all succeed as **partial**, counting it among the rows **not** cleanly processed. | Report the round as processed once the row is written, since the row itself succeeded. Or fail the whole round and write nothing when any link fails. | A funding round whose participants are missing is not a correct record — it under-reports participation, and every `portfolio_count` derived through the participation path is wrong for exactly as long as the omission goes unnoticed. Reporting it as processed is what makes that silent. Failing the whole round discards a row that is otherwise complete and correct, over a link that may fail for a transient reason. Writing the row, reporting it as partial and counting it outside the clean total keeps the data and surfaces the defect. | As `D-412` delivers this, the round is counted in `skipped`, which also carries per-record application errors — so a reader who needs the cause reads the run's events rather than the counter. Stated in the outcome table of both flow guides and asserted in the ATF ledger, where the invariant is that processed plus rejected plus duplicates equals the rows seeded. The earlier reading of this row — that a `partial` counter existed and was a subset of `rejected` — no longer describes the delivery. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-187** | Identify an erasure subject by **conjunction**, require an identifier that is unique, and **refuse** a name-only request that does not resolve to exactly one person: every supplied identifier narrows the query and none widens it. | Match a name **or** an address, as the earlier design did, so any supplied identifier finds the subject. Or require an address unconditionally and never accept a name. | Disjunction is the wrong operator for a destructive operation. `name = X OR contact_email = Y` erases every person who shares the name **and** every person who shares the address, so a request naming one subject destroys data belonging to others, irreversibly and without reporting that it did. Requiring an address unconditionally would be safe but would refuse a legitimate request for a subject whose address the application never held. Counting the distinct subjects first, then accepting a name only when the count is exactly one, is what makes a name sufficient **when it is** and refused when it is not. | A refusal is a request an operator must resolve by hand, and a common name will be refused. That is the correct direction for an irreversible operation. The refusal states how many subjects the name matched, so the operator knows to supply the address, and the returned outcome reports `subjects` alongside the three per-surface counts, so a request that matched nothing is reported as matching nothing rather than as done. **Superseded by the delivered inventory.** The staging-table privacy lifecycle this row configures is **not delivered**: the ninth Script Include, its two properties and its second scheduled job were removed to hold the plan's frozen inventory (D-036, D-265), so nothing in the application performs minimisation, age-bounded pruning or subject erasure. The residual exposure is recorded as flag `F19` and discharged by the operator-owned clear-down procedure in `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/data-model.md` (D-240). | High | `DEV` |
| **D-188** | Declare **twelve** indexes, each tied to a predicate the application actually issues, and **remove** the three-column index on the counter table that served none. A thirteenth was added later at **D-196**, so the delivered count is thirteen. | Keep the original five. Or declare an index for every column a query mentions. Or declare none and let the platform's own indexes serve. | An index for a query nobody runs is not free: it is written on every insert and update of its table and read by nothing. The removed one was exactly that — `caller`, `api_resource`, `window_start` on the counter table, while `RateLimitService` looks a row up by its composite `window_key` and never by those three. The twelve that remain each name their predicate: the unique pair constraint on the join table, its reverse traversal, the lead path of the portfolio derivation, the counter pruning range, the six natural keys of **D-180**, the child-collection reads behind the profile panes, and the three-column equality predicate of the bounded staging read. Declaring one per mentioned column would reintroduce the cost the removal eliminated. | Twelve indexes — thirteen after D-196 — are that many write-time costs on tables that a scheduled flow writes in bulk. Accepted because every one of them serves a read the application performs on every ingested row or on every profile view, and because the alternative for the natural keys is a table scan per row. The unique index on the join table is a **constraint** rather than an optimisation and will reject a duplicate pair written by any path, including a form or an import. | Medium | — |
| **D-189** | Reference a value in a log line by a **random opaque token allocated per run**, and write no hash, digest or fingerprint of any upstream value anywhere. **Scope clarified by `D-358`:** this row governs **upstream values** — personal or provider-supplied field content — of which no hash, digest, pseudonym or fingerprint is written anywhere. It does not govern **fault text** generated by this delivery, over which `AppProperties.fingerprint` is taken so two occurrences of one fault can be correlated without carrying its content. The two are different inputs and the rows are not in conflict. | Keep the salted 32-bit hash the earlier design used. Or use a cryptographic digest with a secret salt. Or write the value itself. | A reference computed from the value is a reference an attacker can **confirm**: with a candidate value and the salt — both of which appeared in the log lines themselves — the hash is simply recomputed and compared, and a short digest over a small candidate space such as a set of company or person names is exhaustible outright. A cryptographic digest with a genuinely secret salt would resist that, at the cost of a secret this application would then have to own and rotate. A random token has no relationship to the value at all: within one run the same value maps to the same token, which is what makes a log readable, and across runs and across values it carries no information. | A token is meaningless outside its own run, so correlating a subject across runs from the log alone is impossible. That is the intended property rather than a limitation, and it is why the run identifier is carried on every line: an investigation proceeds run by run. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-190** | Persist only a **controlled vocabulary** in `x_bst_startuptrk_ingest_staging.error_message`, enforced on the write, and send anything that fails the check to the application log as a controlled code plus an opaque reference. **Extended by `D-358`:** the vocabulary is now a **closed** set named in one place, `IngestionMapper.FAULT_CODES`, and the raw `error.message` that still reached `error_message` on one path is gone. What is persisted is a member of that set plus an opaque diagnostic identifier. | Persist the exception message, as the earlier design did. Or persist nothing and log everything. | An exception message is upstream text of unknown content and unknown length: it can carry a URL, an address, a record locator or a fragment of the payload, and once written to a column it inherits **this table's** retention rather than the log's. Persisting nothing would leave an operator with a rejected row and no reason for it. Testing every value against a conservative pattern and substituting `record_error_uncontrolled_detail (diagnostic <token>)` when it fails keeps the column readable, keeps the detail available in the log, and makes the guarantee a property of the write rather than of the caller's discipline. | A controlled reason is less specific than the original text, so diagnosing an unusual failure takes one extra step through the log reference. Accepted: the alternative is a column that accumulates arbitrary upstream text on a table that holds unvalidated personal data. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-191** | Sweep only **settled** staging rows — `processed`, `rejected` or `error` — in both the minimisation and the pruning pass, so a `pending` row is never minimised and never deleted, and warn when a pending row has aged past the retention bound. | Prune by age alone regardless of state, as the earlier design did. Or delete a pending row that has aged out, on the grounds that it is stale. | A `pending` row is **unconsumed input**. Pruning by age alone deletes work that was never done, and does it silently: the fallback population simply shrinks, and no run reports a failure because nothing failed. Deleting an aged pending row makes the same mistake deliberately. An aged pending row is a symptom — the flow is not draining the queue — and the right response is to say so, not to remove the evidence. | A staging table with a stuck flow now grows instead of being trimmed, which is the correct direction but is still growth. The warning is what makes it visible, and the run ceiling of **D-184** means successive runs drain a backlog in bounded slices rather than never reaching it. **Superseded by the delivered inventory.** The staging-table privacy lifecycle this row configures is **not delivered**: the ninth Script Include, its two properties and its second scheduled job were removed to hold the plan's frozen inventory (D-036, D-265), so nothing in the application performs minimisation, age-bounded pruning or subject erasure. The residual exposure is recorded as flag `F19` and discharged by the operator-owned clear-down procedure in `servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/data-model.md` (D-240). | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-192** | Bound every child-collection read in the Service Portal and answer every dashboard figure from an **aggregate or a single pass**, never from a per-bucket secured count scan. | Load each profile's children in full, as the earlier design did. Or paginate in the browser after loading everything. | A profile pane that reads an unbounded child set is bounded only by the data: a company with many job postings or news articles makes one page view read every row of them. A dashboard that counts each bucket with its own scan performed ten to thirteen full secured passes for figures that one grouped aggregate answers, which is what `RestResponseBuilder.countByGroup()` now does. Paginating in the browser fixes what the user sees and none of what the server does. | A bounded pane shows a slice, so a widget must state its total and offer a way to reach the rest. The page-size properties are shared with the REST layer, so changing one changes both surfaces — which is intended, and is recorded in the portal guide as a rename-impact note. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-193** | Add `startup_headquarters_location` to the staging table — its **41st** column — so a child row names its parent by the same composite identity the parent is keyed on. | Resolve a child's parent by name alone, as the earlier design did. Or resolve by the parent's `sys_id`, requiring the CSV author to know it. Or accept the first name match and log the ambiguity. | The startup natural key of **D-180** is `name` + `headquarters_location`, so a child that names its parent by name alone cannot express the identity the parent is stored under: two companies sharing a name in different cities are distinct records, and a name-only reference resolves to whichever the query returned first. Requiring a `sys_id` is unusable in a hand-authored CSV fallback dataset. Accepting the first match and logging it attaches real child records to the wrong company. The column is available because the staging table is a **supporting** table and not part of the closed entity field list, and it matches that table's stated design as a flattened superset of scalar columns. | The column is a three-way contract between the CSV headers, the staging dictionary and the guide 06 import mapping, and a change to any one of the three breaks the fallback path silently. Held together by the column-contract check in the sample-data README and by an automated field-count check over all six CSVs against the dictionary. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-194** | **Amended by `D-396`, which normalises this branch's identifier scheme.** Make the rollback depend on the **install mode** recorded at pre-flight: scope deletion on a confirmed clean install, **back-out and restore** on an upgrade over an existing scope, and **abort** an upgrade that has neither a recorded data export nor named authorisation. | Keep the single scope-deletion rollback for every case, as the earlier design did. Or abort any deployment onto an existing scope outright. | Deleting the scope cascades its tables, so on an installation that already holds data the documented rollback destroys the very data the deployment was meant to extend — and the earlier pre-flight explicitly permitted an existing scope to proceed, which put those two statements one commit failure apart. Aborting every upgrade is safe and makes the artifact undeliverable as an upgrade at all. Recording the mode before anything is uploaded, and choosing the rollback from it, keeps both paths available and makes the destructive one reachable only where there is nothing to destroy. | The upgrade path depends on an export taken **before** the commit, and an operator who skips it has no recovery from a failed back-out. That is why the pre-flight aborts without one rather than warning: an upgrade with no export and no authorisation has no recovery path at all, and discovering that after a commit failure is the worst moment to discover it. | High | `IRREV` `DEV` |
| **D-195** | Derive `Investor.portfolio_count` with a **two-stage bounded traversal**: aggregate on a field of the table being read, then read the related values in batched identifier queries. | Keep the single aggregate that grouped on a dot-walked field of a related table. Or iterate every round and every join row per investor. Or store the reach set on the investor and maintain it incrementally. | A grouping on a related table's column is not supported: the platform resolves the group on the queried table, so participation rows were dropped and the count under-reported with no error anywhere. The replacement stays inside supported behaviour — an aggregate grouped on `funding_round`, which is a real column of the join table, then a batched `sys_id IN` read of the rounds in slices of 200 — and the all-investor pass walks bounded windows of the round and join tables instead of one unbounded grouping. Iteration per investor is correct and quadratic; an incrementally maintained reach set is a second stored aggregate with its own drift problem, which is the failure this derivation already has to guard against once. | Two stages read more rows than one aggregate would if the unsupported grouping had worked. Bounded by the batch size and the window size, and the cost falls on a business rule and a maintenance method rather than on a caller-facing read. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-196** | Make the `funding_round` natural key an **ordered pair of alternatives**: the provider locator `source_url` first, and `startup` plus `round_date` only for a round that carries none. Declare an index on `source_url`, the thirteenth. **Implemented by D-323:** this row was recorded but not built — the delivered `IngestionMapper.IDENTITY_KEYS.funding_round` still carried `round_type` in its first alternative until D-323 applied this decision literally. | Keep the single key of `startup` + `round_date` + `round_type`. Or key on `startup` + `round_date` alone. Or require `source_url` and reject a round without one. | `round_type` was in the identity and is an updatable attribute, so correcting a round's stage created a **second** round rather than updating the first — the precise failure an upsert exists to prevent. `source_url` is the only value a provider guarantees to be stable for a round, so it is the right primary key; but a manually entered round legitimately has none, so requiring it would refuse real data. The ordered pair takes the stable key when it exists and falls back to a key that is usually unique, reporting an explicit same-day collision that names `source_url` as the remedy rather than guessing between two rounds. | Two rounds of one startup on the same date, both without a `source_url`, cannot be distinguished and are reported as a collision rather than written. Accepted: the alternative is to attach one round's data to the other. **D-320 narrows the primary alternative:** `source_url` is not published by Crunchbase v4, so on the live path the first alternative is never complete and the `startup` + `round_date` fallback governs; the provider locator is exercised by the fallback dataset, which stages it. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-197** | Require the **whole composite startup key** of every child row — `startup_name` **and** `startup_headquarters_location` — and remove the name-only fallback entirely. | Keep the fallback for a child row whose parent name is unique. Or resolve on the name and warn. Or infer the headquarters from another column of the row. | The fallback resolved on a **different key from the one rule 2 de-duplicates on**, so a child could attach to a company the source never named — and it did so silently, because a unique name produces exactly one match and looks like a clean resolution. There is no threshold at which guessing a parent is preferable to reporting that the row did not identify one, so the fallback is removed rather than narrowed. Every carrier now supplies both halves: the live envelope through the source's location field and the fallback CSV through its own column. | A child row that omits the second half is rejected where it previously loaded. Intended. The reason names the remedy, and no shipped fixture regresses, because every row that omits the column already fails a mandatory check first. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-198** | Try an **exact normalised equality query first** — **amended by D-440:** the exact pass may short-circuit only when it has already decided **ambiguity**, never on a single match, because a case-variant second row would otherwise stay hidden on a case-sensitive collation — and fall back to the bounded tolerant candidate scan only when it returns nothing. | Keep the candidate scan as the only path. Or replace the scan with equality alone. | The scan alone paid for a bounded read and a per-candidate normalisation on every lookup, including the overwhelmingly common case of a value that already matches exactly. Equality first answers that case with one indexed read. The scan is kept because it is the only thing that matches a stored value differing by case or whitespace, which is what makes a recurring run update rather than duplicate. | Two queries where one previously ran, in the case where the equality misses. Bounded and rare. | Low | — |
| **D-199** | Carry a participant list in a **structured carrier that never uses the comma**: a JSON array, a `&#124;`-delimited string, or one bare name, with the shipped CSV using the JSON array. | Keep comma splitting. Or quote a comma-bearing member and split on unquoted commas only. Or carry one participant per staging row. | An organisation name legitimately contains a comma — `Beacon Hill Ventures, LP` is one investor — so splitting on it fabricated names that resolved to nothing and dropped a real participant from the round, again with no error. Quote-aware splitting is a parser to write and get wrong inside a column that has already been through a CSV reader. One participant per row multiplies staging rows and moves the problem to reassembly. Neither `[` nor `&#124;` occurs inside an organisation name, so both carriers are unambiguous and the JSON array is what the fixture ships. | A carrier the import wizard must load verbatim: a transform that re-delimits or pretty-prints the value breaks it. Stated in the guide and in the sample-data contract, and detectable because the value's first character is `[`. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-200** | **Amended by `D-412`: an unresolved lead or participating reference marks the round `error` and is counted in `skipped`, not in a `partial` counter, which no delivered surface publishes. The reporting principle below stands.** Report a funding round whose **lead or participating investor references did not resolve** as `partial` and count it among the rows not cleanly processed. | Leave it counted as processed with a warning, as before. Or reject the whole round. | A round written without its lead investor is not the record the source described, and reporting it as processed made the run's counts say the opposite of what happened. Rejecting the round outright discards real funding data over a reference the next run may resolve. The outcome for a round whose links could not all be written already existed, so an unresolved reference joins the same outcome — `error` on the round and `skipped` in the summary, per `D-412` — rather than inventing a third. | The round is one of the rows not cleanly processed and must not be added to `rejected` when assembling evidence. Stated wherever the counts are consumed, and delivered as `skipped` per `D-412`. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-201** | Reconcile a round's participation links as a **diff**: insert what the row declares, delete what it does not, and recalculate the portfolio count of every investor added **and** removed. | Keep insert-only reconciliation. Or delete all links and re-insert. Or never remove a link. | Insert-only meant a corrected round kept an investor the source had removed, for ever, and the portfolio count of that investor stayed inflated. Delete-and-re-insert churns rows and momentarily empties a round that a concurrent read would see as having no participants. The diff also fixes the recalculation asymmetry: a removed investor's count was never recomputed, so removal silently left a wrong figure behind. | A row that declares **no** participant value deliberately removes nothing, because an absent value is not an assertion of emptiness. Without that rule a partial feed would strip every round it touched. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-202** | Wrap **each row** of an ingestion batch in its own exception boundary, and write the run summary from a `finally` so it is produced on every path out of the loop. | Leave the loop unguarded. Or wrap the whole loop in one boundary. Or abort the run on the first exception. | The requirements mandate logging the record, skipping it and continuing the run — and an unguarded loop does the opposite: one thrown row abandoned every remaining row **and** the run summary, so the run left no record of itself at all. One boundary around the whole loop still loses the remainder. Aborting contradicts the requirement outright. A per-row boundary stamps a controlled outcome on the failing row and continues; the `finally` guarantees the summary that the acceptance evidence reads. | A row that throws is recorded with a controlled code rather than a diagnosis. That is the trade the controlled-vocabulary decision already makes, and the opaque diagnostic token correlates the row to its full log line. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-203** | **Amended by `D-265`, which removed the two per-source properties this row introduced and folded the state into `ingestion.last_run_provenance`, and by `D-393`, which fixes the published read path. The attempt-versus-success distinction below stands and is carried by each entry's `state` member — `running` for an attempt in flight, `succeeded` only for a clean batch.** Split the cadence state into **attempt** and **success** markers per source — `ingestion.last_attempt_<source>` written unconditionally, `ingestion.last_success_<source>` written only when the batch was clean — and keep the guard reading the success marker. | Keep one marker per source written on every completion. Or write the marker only on a clean batch with no record of the attempt. | One marker meant a **failed** run advanced the cadence, so the next attempt waited out a full window from a failure — with a 24-hour cadence, a transient provider outage cost a day. Or drop the webfont wait entirely so nothing needs ordering. | The library's auto-start is enabled by default and fires on a document event, so any configuration deferred behind an awaited promise can lose the race — and when it does, the auto-start measures containers that the presentation framework has given a zero width on off-screen slides, producing a collapsed drawing and a run of attribute errors from a code path this file does not drive. Configuring at parse time makes that path unreachable rather than merely unlikely. The marker is set when a render **finishes**, not when it **succeeds**, so a render that measured a mid-transition width leaves a degenerate drawing behind a marker that says the work is done; trusting it would make that state permanent for the rest of the session, which is precisely the failure a reviewer would see and the author would not. The webfont wait still gates the render passes, where it earns its keep — label metrics depend on the actual font face, and Rule 2 requires every diagram to render legibly. | A health check can only test properties it knows to look for: a drawing that is wrong in some way `healthy()` does not measure would still pass. Each repair spends one attempt from a fixed budget of three, so a genuinely unrenderable diagram is reported once and left rather than retried forever. Verified by nineteen loads across four navigation profiles, including a measured 5.8-times processor slowdown, with no console error and all five diagrams healthy on every load. | Medium | — |

## 15. Remediation of the scripting review

Twenty-five decisions, `D-229` through `D-253`. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised against the delivered artifacts, and each **amends or extends** an earlier row rather than replacing it — the earlier row's identifier is cited so the history stays readable. They are collected in one section rather than inserted into sections 1 through 10 because this file's own rule at [How to read this log](#how-to-read-this-log) forbids renumbering, and inserting rows mid-section would break the contiguity every sibling document cites.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-229** | **Amended by `D-391`, which makes the Basic store the single primary one, and by `D-392`, which validates the endpoint's authority before the header is attached.** Send the Crunchbase key as an **`X-cb-user-key` request header** against the base `https://api.crunchbase.com/api/v4`, and correct **both** shipped base-URL property values rather than only the code defaults. Amends D-043 and D-045. | Keep the HTTP Basic specification the guide originally carried. Or send the key as a `user_key` query parameter, which the provider also accepts. Or correct only the `AppProperties` defaults and leave the shipped property records as they were. | The provider does not support HTTP Basic in any form, so the original specification could never have authenticated — it was a specification defect rather than a style choice. A header is preferable to a query parameter because a query string reaches access logs and browser history while a header does not. Correcting only the code defaults would have been worse than correcting neither: a property **record** overrides a code default, so the delivered set would have driven every call to the retired `v3.1` and `/v2` bases while the code, the reference documentation and the guide all claimed otherwise. | The provider can retire or re-shape `/api/v4` independently of this delivery, and the version is in the base URL rather than in a header, so a provider change is a property edit rather than a code change — which is the intent, but it does mean the value is load-bearing configuration. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-230** | **Amended by `D-390`: the alias is matched on the `sys_alias.id` column against the dotted value, not on `name`. The singularity assertion and the fail-closed abort below stand.** Resolve a credential alias by **name to exactly one `sys_alias` record**, assert that singularity, pass the resulting **sys_id** to the connection-info provider, and **fail closed** with a typed error on zero or more than one match. Amends D-045. | Pass the alias **name** to the provider, as the guides originally specified. Or take the first match and continue. Or resolve the alias once at build time and hard-code the identifier into the script. | The provider takes an alias identifier, not a name; passing a name yields no connection and the failure surfaces as a null dereference deep in the step rather than as a diagnosable error. Taking the first of several matches would silently authenticate against the wrong credential. A hard-coded identifier does not survive an export and re-import into another instance, which is the one thing an update-set-delivered application must do. | The alias name is now a runtime contract between the guide and the instance, so a rename breaks the flow at run time rather than at build time. Mitigated by the flows asserting singularity and by the build gate of D-249 requiring the name to resolve before the flow is saved. | High | — |
| **D-231** | Build both live paths as **iterate → paginate → capture each call's outcome → return a typed aggregate envelope**, with every emitted row wrapped so it carries its `record_type` and its `source`. Amends D-045 and D-048. | Emit whatever the first response contains, as the guides originally specified. Or aggregate untyped rows and let the mapper infer the type. Or paginate only when the provider signals more pages, with no bound. | A single unpaginated call silently truncates the source at the provider's page size, and an untyped row cannot be dispatched: the mapper selects its target table from `record_type`, so a row without one is unroutable. Capturing each call's outcome individually is what lets one failed page be logged and skipped while the run continues, which is the mandated failure semantics. | Pagination against a keyset provider depends on the cursor the provider returns, so a provider change to that cursor stops the walk after one page — visibly, in the run summary's counters, rather than silently. | High | — |
| **D-232** | **Amended by `D-403`, which supplies the assertion this row's risk paragraph promised: `rule3_deviations` is now asserted as a counter in its own right, at two per source, alongside `unmatched`.** Make the six-list `Other` conflict of D-022 **observable and countable** rather than resolving it in either direction: an unmatched value in a list with no `Other` member is **left unwritten**, logged with the exact outcome string `left unwritten: the prompt 1.0 choice list declares no Other member (flagged deviation from cleaning rule 3)`, counted in a dedicated `rule3_deviations` counter, and carried on the event as a `deviation` member. Amends D-022. | Add an `Other` member to each of the six lists, honouring cleaning rule 3 and breaching the binding field list. Or reject the whole record, which is what an earlier reading of D-022 claimed. Or write the raw unmatched value, breaching the choice list. Or drop the value silently. | Two binding statements conflict, so any resolution breaches one of them; the honest response is to breach neither silently. Leaving the field unwritten keeps the record — the rest of which is valid and mandated — while making the loss visible in three places at once: a counted metric, a distinct log outcome and a test assertion. Rejecting the record would discard mandated data over one imprecise field. Writing the raw value would put a non-choice value in a choice column, which the portal and the API would then surface as data. | The delivery is a **partial** implementation of cleaning rule 3 and must be reported as such everywhere it is claimed. A reader who sees `unmatched` without also reading `rule3_deviations` will over-report compliance, which is why the two counters are separate and both are asserted. | High | `DEV` `AMB-4` |
| **D-233** | Treat LinkedIn founders, executives and job postings as having **no public read API**, make that flow's live call an **organisation-identity probe** rather than an acquisition, and state that `LinkedIn Ingestion` can therefore **never** be labelled `live validated` in any credential posture. Amends D-048 and D-053. | Keep the `/companies`, `/employees` and `/jobs` paths the guide originally named. Or scrape the data, which D-052 already excludes. Or drop the LinkedIn flow entirely and serve those three record types from the fallback dataset with no flow at all. | Those paths do not exist on the versioned API, no public endpoint enumerates a company's people, and the Job Posting API is a **write** API — so the original specification described an integration that cannot be built by anyone, not one that is merely unentitled here. Dropping the flow would breach the requirement for two scheduled flows. Keeping the flow with an honest probe preserves the mandated shape, exercises the alias and the cadence guard for real, and leaves the record acquisition where it can actually work. | The flow's live branch does useful but **limited** work, so a reader may over-read a green run. Amends D-053. | Claim the flow execution log without writing to it, as the guides originally did. Or log only to `syslog` and point acceptance there. Or write one flow-log line per record rather than one drained block per run. | The mandated failure semantics require per-record evidence to be **findable**, and an operator opening the flow's execution history is where they look first; a claim with no writer behind it is worse than no claim. Rendering the event's own members rather than a `line` member matters concretely — `drainEvents()` returns structured entries and carries no `line`, so a renderer reading one would have published a block of severities with empty detail. One drained block per run keeps the flow log readable while `syslog` carries the per-event stream. | The flow-log message column name is release-dependent, so an assertion against it must probe a candidate list rather than hard-code one. Or hold it on a row of the staging table. | A log-derived guard fails in two ordinary configurations: the marker is written at `info`, so raising the log level suppresses it and the guard then fires every hour, and log retention eventually deletes it and the guard then fires once more forever. Neither failure is visible until the cadence is wrong. A property is unaffected by both. One shared property would make either flow's success suppress the other's run. | A value written by one component and read by another, mitigated by both sides going through typed accessors that validate the source token and the timestamp format and refuse anything else. The earlier reading of this row — two further properties, `ingestion.last_success.crunchbase` and `ingestion.last_success.linkedin`, outside the frozen inventory — no longer describes the delivery; the correction is at **D-265**. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-236** | Carry the LinkedIn API version as a **non-secret value the delivery owns**, sent on every request as the mandatory `LinkedIn-Version` header, with a six-digit `YYYYMM` format check and a shipped default. A property would have made the rotation a configuration change with no code edit, which is the same posture as the base URLs. | An operator must know to advance the value before the pinned version is retired; the failure is a clear provider error rather than silent corruption, and guide 01 makes reading and recording the value a step. The earlier reading of this row — a twelfth system property `linkedin.api_version`, outside the frozen inventory — no longer describes the delivery; the value is the code constant `AppProperties.LINKEDIN_API_VERSION` and the correction is at **D-266**. | Medium | — |
| **D-237** | Isolate **every record** inside the ingestion loop with its own try/catch that logs, skips and continues, and guarantee the **terminal run summary** with a try/finally around the whole run.
