| **D-253** | Add **Subresource Integrity** to every remote asset of the executive presentation whose bytes are fixed by its pinned version — a `sha384` digest computed from the fetched bytes, with `crossorigin="anonymous"` — and **keep the pinned versions unchanged**, recording the residual advisory exposure of the pinned diagram library as a matter for human decision rather than resolving it with a version bump. Amends D-085. | Upgrade the diagram library past its published advisories, which would resolve the exposure outright. Or pre-render every diagram to inline vector markup at authoring time and drop the library. Or leave the assets unpinned by digest, as delivered originally. Or vendor the three libraries into the repository. | The presentation loads three third-party libraries from public content networks and executes two of them, so a substituted file executes in the reader's browser with nothing to detect it; a digest turns that from an undetectable substitution into a refusal to load, and costs nothing. **The version cannot be changed here:** the governing rule pins the presentation engine, the diagram library and the icon set to exact versions, so an upgrade would breach the rule that mandates them. Pre-rendering would also breach it, because the same rule requires the diagram library to be driven from the presentation's ready and slide-change events. Vendoring would breach its prohibition on local file dependencies. The font stylesheet is deliberately left without a digest and with `crossorigin` only, because its response body is negotiated per user agent — any digest would be correct in one browser and wrong in the next — and it is a stylesheet rather than an executable. | **A residual exposure remains and is not closed by this decision.** The pinned diagram library predates its published cross-site-scripting advisories, and the pin is a rule constraint rather than an engineering preference. The digest guarantees the reader receives **exactly** the audited bytes; it does not make those bytes free of defects. The mitigating facts are that the presentation renders only diagram sources authored in this repository, takes no input from a reader, and is opened as a local file — so there is no untrusted input path into the library. **What a human must decide:** whether to relax the version pin for this artifact so the library can be upgraded, or to accept the exposure on the strength of there being no untrusted input. Until that decision is taken the pin stands, because a rule may not be overridden by an implementer. | High | `DEV` |

**On D-232, the fourth ambiguity.** Rule 3 requires ambiguity-resolving assumptions to be called out, and this is a fourth alongside the three the earlier sections carry. The ambiguity is not a gap in the requirements but a **collision** within them: cleaning rule 3 mandates coercing an unmatched value to `Other`, and the field definitions that fix the choice lists declare themselves binding and complete with no `Other` member in six of the ten lists. Both statements come from the same authority, so neither can be preferred on authority grounds, and every resolution breaches one. The assumption made is that a **visible, counted partial implementation** is better than a silent breach of either — and that the field list, being the narrower and more explicit statement, is the one that governs the column while the cleaning rule governs the reporting.

## 16. Remediation of the API, backend and observability review

Fifty-six decisions, `D-254` through `D-309`. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised when the delivered artifacts were reviewed against the **frozen** Agent Action Plan, and the direction of travel is the opposite of section 15's: where section 15 mostly closed correctness gaps the plan had not anticipated, this section mostly **removes** work the plan does not license and restores what it froze. Each row **amends or extends** an earlier row rather than replacing it, and the amended row is corrected in place and says what it supersedes. The decision to open a section rather than extend section 15 is itself recorded, at `D-309`.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-254** | Deliver the **seven entity tables reachable over the native table interface** — `access` `public`, `read_access` true, `ws_access` true, with create, update and delete all false — and keep the three supporting tables closed on both routes. Amends D-027 and D-068. **Superseded by D-310:** this row no longer describes the delivery. All ten tables ship `access` `package_private` with `read_access` and `ws_access` false, and the seven required table gates read table metadata instead of table rows. | Keep all ten tables closed and assert the post-commit gates against platform metadata, as delivered originally. Or open all ten, including the staging table and the rate-limit counter. | The plan freezes eleven post-commit gates of which seven are reads of these tables, and a closed table makes that gate set unexecutable. The read route is also the only place a **denial is observable**: a table that cannot be reached answers identically whether a field is gated or not, so closing it hides the very enforcement the delivery is meant to demonstrate. Every table-level and field-level control is evaluated on that interface, so nothing is unguarded by opening it; write access across scopes stays false, and the supporting tables — internal machinery no caller outside the scope reads — stay closed. | A second read route exists that the rate-limit counter does not meter. Recorded plainly wherever the posture is documented: the limiter is a fair-use control on the application's own API and **not** a security boundary, and a reviewer must check field-level denial on both routes rather than on one. | High | `AUTHZ` |
| **D-255** | Restore the post-commit gate set to **exactly the eleven** the plan freezes, and **re-home** rather than delete the three checks that leaves out: the 53-column walk into the acceptance checklist, the 21-cell access-control cross-product into the test suite, and the parser-property check into the instance prerequisites. Amends D-068 and D-069. | Keep the sixteen gates and the two pre-commit checks. Or drop the eleven-gate framing and present all sixteen as one set. Or delete the three extra checks outright. | A gate decides an irreversible operation, so the gate set is exactly the set the plan licenses to do that — no more. But the three checks were each answering a real question, and deleting them would have traded one defect for another. Each moved to the place that can answer its question better: a column walk belongs where a human reads it column by column, an access-control cross-product belongs under impersonation in a test, and a check on instance configuration this Update Set does not create belongs before the import rather than after the commit. | A reader comparing this delivery to an earlier revision finds five fewer gates and must look in three places for what moved. Mitigated by the gate document closing with a table naming each re-homed check and where it now lives. | Medium | — |
| **D-256** | Assert the instance's **XML-parser properties** as an instance prerequisite before the import rather than as a post-commit gate. Amends D-069 and D-076. | Keep it as `GATE-SEC-04` with a documented exception that does not roll back. Or drop the check entirely. | The check tests instance configuration the Update Set neither creates nor owns, so it can fail on a perfectly installed application — which is why it had to carry an exception. An exception inside the gate set is corrosive: it makes the rollback conditional on **which** gate failed, and an operator reading a gate report then has to remember one gate is different. Moved before the import, the same fact is asserted with the same command and the remedy is the same, while every gate failure again means the same thing. | One more prerequisite for an operator to satisfy before starting, and a prerequisite is easier to skip than a gate. Mitigated by the runbook listing it with the exact read and the exact remedy, and by the property being read again by nothing later in the sequence. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-257** | Assert import completeness with **one check inside step 3** — the retrieved update set's `summary` reports the delivered record count — rather than two pre-commit checks outside the gate set. Amends D-069. **Superseded by D-419:** completeness is asserted by **two** named pre-commit checks, `PRE-COMMIT-01` and `PRE-COMMIT-02`, both read before the commit and both against the count the validator reports. They remain outside the eleven-gate set, as this row intended. | Keep `PRE-COMMIT-01` and `PRE-COMMIT-02` as separately named checks. Or rely on the preview problem set alone. | Both pre-commit checks existed to catch one failure, an Update Set whose customer updates did not attach to its header, and one assertion at the point the state is already being polled catches it at the same moment for a fraction of the ceremony. The other half of the same defence is now on the file rather than the instance, at D-263, where it costs nothing at all. | A single assertion is easier to overlook in a step description than a numbered check. Mitigated by the expected number being stated as a literal in the runbook and in the checklist, so a mismatch is arithmetic rather than judgement. | Low | — |
| **D-258** | Resolve the uploaded update set by **difference against a pre-upload baseline**, with a run-unique name, asserting exactly one match and reading back its `application_scope`. | Trust an identifier returned by the upload. Or query by name alone and take the newest match. | The upload mechanism this instance accepts is a form post that answers with a redirect rather than a record, so there is no identifier to capture — and a name query alone is ambiguous the moment two clones or two attempts exist, which is exactly when picking the wrong record is most damaging. A baseline difference plus a singularity assertion is unambiguous, and reading the scope back confirms the record is this application's rather than a namesake. | A concurrent upload by another operator between the baseline and the resolve would produce two matches; that is reported as a failure to be re-run rather than resolved by heuristic. | Medium | — |
| **D-259** | Assert the **preview and commit processor responses** before polling anything, and require a 32-character hexadecimal execution-tracker identifier from the preview response. | Poll the update-set state directly after firing the processor and infer success from the state changing. | A processor call that is accepted and ignored is indistinguishable from one that worked if the only evidence is a later state read — and the delivery already knows that failure mode, because the documented `PATCH` transitions behave exactly that way. Asserting the response shape first turns a silent no-op into an immediate failure, and the tracker identifier is what the poll is supposed to be following. | The response shape of the commit processor is release-dependent and could not be verified on the target instance, which was hibernating. Recorded as such: the runbook asserts the response is non-empty and decides the rest at runtime rather than hard-coding a shape it has not observed. | Medium | — |
| **D-260** | Upload through the platform's **file-upload processor** with a scraped session token, and drive preview and commit through the corresponding **`xmlhttp.do` processors**, rather than through the Table API insert and `PATCH` state transitions the deployment brief specifies. | Follow the brief literally. Or wrap the brief's calls in retries and hope. | The brief's shapes were tried against the target instance and do not work: the Table API insert of an update-set payload answers `HTTP 400`, and the `PATCH` state transitions answer `HTTP 200` and change nothing — the second failure being the more dangerous, because it looks like success. The brief is authoritative for the deployment **procedure**, and the procedure is preserved step for step; only the request shapes that the instance rejects are replaced with the ones it accepts. | The replacement mechanisms are session-based rather than REST, so they depend on a login and a token and are more sensitive to instance configuration than an API call. Mitigated by each step asserting its own response and by the "must not be used" forms being named explicitly so nobody reintroduces them. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-261** | Express the mid-upgrade pre-flight as `upgrade_finishedISEMPTY` rather than as the brief's `state=executing`. | Follow the brief literally. Or drop the check. | The brief's query names no field on that table, and a query on a non-existent field is not an error — it is ignored, so every row is returned and the check aborts a perfectly healthy deployment every time it runs. A check that always fails is worse than no check, because it trains an operator to skip it. The replacement asserts the same fact against a field that exists. | The field name is release-dependent. Mitigated by the runbook stating what the check is for, so an operator on a different release can restate it rather than delete it. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-262** | Accept `HTTP 400` alongside `403` and `404` from any confirmatory application-table read after a rollback, and take the **authoritative** confirmation from table metadata. | Require `404` or `403` as the brief specifies. Or confirm from an application-table read alone. | The target instance answers `HTTP 400` for a table that does not exist, so the brief's expected statuses would report a successful cascade as a failure. More fundamentally an application-table read cannot distinguish **removed** from **present but unreadable**, since both answer with a client error — so it is kept only as a corroborating signal and the metadata read decides. | A metadata read establishes that no table definition remains, not that no data remains anywhere. Accepted: the cascade removes the tables, and a residual definition is the failure this check is looking for. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-263** | Have the validator enforce **header linkage** — every `<sys_update_xml>` carries a `<remote_update_set>` equal to the single header record's `<sys_id>` — and fail gate G-1 on any mismatch. | Check well-formedness and document shape only, leaving linkage to the instance. Or warn rather than fail. | A detached update record is the one defect that survives every other check: the file parses, the load succeeds, the preview reports no problem, and the commit reports success while applying nothing. It is also trivially detectable on the file, before anything reaches an instance and while the remedy still costs nothing. A warning would not do, because the whole point is that the downstream signals all say success. | The rule assumes one header record per file, which the same gate already requires. A legitimate multi-header file would be rejected; no such file is in scope, and the shape is asserted rather than assumed. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-264** | Have the validator enforce the **update-name contract** — each record's `<name>` is `<table>_<sys_id>` of its payload's primary record, or that record's own declared update name — and fail gate G-2 on anything else. | Accept any non-empty name. Or check only that a name is present. | A customer update is addressed by the name of the record it carries; an update named anything else cannot be resolved when another record in the same set references it, and the failure surfaces as a preview problem or a missing reference long after the cause. The contract is mechanical, so it is checked mechanically, and the two accepted forms are both derived from the payload rather than from a convention the generator has to remember. | A payload whose primary record carries no identifier can be named by nothing, so it is reported as a failure rather than skipped. That is the intended direction: an unnameable update is a defect. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-265** | **Amended by `D-388`, which makes the source-mode accessor fail closed, and by `D-389`, which logs a bounded warning for a malformed marker entry.** **Remove** the ninth Script Include, the second scheduled executable and the five properties outside the frozen inventory, and fold per-source cadence state into `ingestion.last_run_provenance` as `source=provenance\|state\|stamp\|run`. Amends D-036, D-235, D-239, D-240 and D-243. | Keep the artifacts and continue tabulating the difference from the plan. Or keep the classes and delete only the properties. Or drop per-source cadence state altogether and share one marker. | The plan fixes the inventory at eight classes, eleven properties, three business rules and one scheduled executable, and it is frozen — so code aligns to it rather than the reverse, however defensible the additions were on their own terms. Two facts still had to survive the removal: the severity normaliser and the boundary validators are behaviour the API depends on, so they moved onto existing classes rather than disappearing; and per-source cadence state is load-bearing, because one shared marker would let either flow's success suppress the other's run. Encoding both sources in the one property the plan already lists keeps the behaviour and the inventory simultaneously. | A compound property value is less obvious than two plainly named ones, and a reader who expects a bare provenance label will find a structured string. Mitigated by exactly one accessor pair writing and reading it, by the format being stated wherever it is read, and by the residual privacy exposure the removal leaves being escalated as flag **F19** rather than dropped. | High | — |
| **D-266** | Carry the LinkedIn API version as the **code constant** `AppProperties.LINKEDIN_API_VERSION` rather than as a twelfth system property. Amends D-236. | Keep the property. Or hard-code the version at each call site. | The property inventory is frozen at eleven and this value was the twelfth. It is also the one configuration value in the set that is not really operational: the provider retires versions on a published schedule, and advancing the pin is a delivery change that wants the same review as any other code change, not a runtime edit. A single constant on the class every component already reads keeps one source of truth without the inventory cost. | Advancing the version now requires a delivery change rather than a property edit. Accepted and stated: the constant is named in the ingestion guides so an operator knows where the value lives, and the format check that guarded the property guards the constant. | Medium | — |
| **D-267** | **Amended by `D-406`, which states the counted-table set as eight — the seven entity tables and the join table the portal counts.** Authorize the aggregate `total_count` with a **role test** rather than with a probe read — three sequential role checks ahead of the aggregate, answering `403` when none holds. Amends D-041. | Keep the probe read on a secured record as the gate, as delivered originally. Or drop the gate and answer every caller a number. | The probe was inconclusive rather than merely unsupported: on an empty table, on a newly loaded table, or on a table whose rows the caller's plan filters away it returns the same answer as a denial, so it could neither permit nor deny reliably — and a gate that cannot decide is not a gate. The role test decides the same question directly, and it is exactly as strong here because record-level visibility is identical for all three roles by invariant; what the gate protects is the **size** of a set, not a field value. | The role test is only equivalent while that invariant holds. Recorded as an invariant in the access-control documentation rather than as a coincidence, with the consequence spelled out: the moment a record-level restriction is added, an aggregate over-reports for restricted callers and must be replaced by a secured count. | High | `AUTHZ` |
| **D-268** | Document the rejected probe read as **inconclusive**, not merely as unsupported, in both the data-model and access-control documents. | State only that the probe is not used. Or omit the alternative entirely. | "Unsupported" invites a reader to reinstate the probe on a release where it is supported, which would reintroduce the defect. The reason it cannot work is a property of the question rather than of the platform, and stating that is what makes the decision durable. Writing it in both documents rather than one keeps the two from drifting, since each is read by a different audience. | Two statements of one fact can diverge. Mitigated by both being short, and by the assertion harness checking each. | Low | — |
| **D-269** | Suppress the **duplicate count** on every windowed query with `setNoCount()`, and document it as a duplicate-count removal rather than as a performance optimisation. Amends D-042. | Leave the second count in place. Or pass the window bounds and a count flag together and let the caller choose. | A list operation needs exactly one count, and the delivered pairing performed two: an explicit aggregate for the envelope, then an implicit one inside the windowed query whose result nothing reads. Removing the second changes no result and no envelope member. Framing matters as much as the change: the plan forbids inventing performance targets, so this is recorded as removing work nobody consumes, not as making anything faster. | A reader could mistake the call for a functional change to pagination. Mitigated by the API reference stating the count budget as a two-row table — once for the aggregate, never for the windowed read — and by the seven call sites being paired one-for-one with the seven window calls. | Low | — |
| **D-270** | Reconcile a funding round's participating investors as an **exact set** — delete the links the payload no longer carries, add the ones it does — gated on the payload being complete, with two guards that refuse to delete. Amends D-010 and D-011. | Add missing links only, as delivered originally, so a removed participant persists forever. Or delete every link and re-insert. | Add-only reconciliation cannot express a correction: a participant recorded in error, or one the provider later retracts, stays in the join table and in every derived count for the life of the application. Delete-and-reinsert would churn identifiers and would lose the set entirely if the second half failed. What makes exact-set safe is knowing the payload is authoritative, so the operation refuses to delete when the payload's participant list is absent and when it cannot be parsed — an incomplete payload leaves the set untouched and reports it. | An authoritative-looking payload that is actually partial would remove real links. That is the reason for the two guards, and the reason a reconciliation reports `linked`, `removed`, `kept`, `failed` and `reconciled` separately rather than a single count. | High | — |
| **D-271** | On a **partial** participant-link failure, mark the staging row `error` while leaving the entity record written. | Roll the whole round back. Or report the row processed because the round itself was written. | Reporting `processed` is the worse of the two failures available, because it is silent: the round exists, its participant set is wrong, and nothing in the run summary says so. Rolling the round back would discard a correct record for an incomplete relationship and would fight the mandated per-record semantics, which are to log, skip and continue. Marking the row `error` keeps the record and makes the incompleteness visible and re-runnable. | A round can exist with an incomplete participant set until the row is re-run, and a derived portfolio count computed in between is correspondingly low. Stated in the guide with the re-run procedure, and the run is unhealthy while any such row exists. | Medium | — |
| **D-272** | Match incoming identity **collation-independently**: narrow with a `STARTSWITH` pair covering both cases of the first character, then decide in script. Amends D-238. | Rely on the database's case-insensitive comparison, as delivered originally. Or store a normalised key column and match on that. | Case-insensitive matching in a query depends on the collation of the instance's database, which the delivery does not control and cannot assert — so the same payload could deduplicate on one instance and duplicate on another. A stored normalised key would be the cleanest answer and is unavailable: the field list is binding and complete, so a new column is not permitted. Narrowing on a two-way prefix keeps the query selective while the comparison that actually decides runs in script, where the semantics are the delivery's own. | The narrowing is a prefix rather than an equality, so a script-side comparison runs over more candidates than a direct match would. Bounded by the prefix being the first character of an indexed column, and by the identity keys being few and specific. | Medium | — |
| **D-273** | Have the projection write **check its own return value** — **superseded by D-434:** no projection write exists, so there is no return value to check and no stale projection to report; the value is derived on read — and report a stale projection at error severity, aggregating across the batch rather than aborting the run. Amends D-245. | Assume the write succeeded, as delivered originally. Or abort the run on the first failed write. | An unchecked write is how a cache silently diverges from its source: the join table stays correct, the projection goes stale, and no signal exists until a reader notices a wrong number. Aborting would be a different defect, because one unwritable row would stop the recalculation of every other investor in the batch. Aggregating the failures reports the exact scope of the staleness while completing the work that can be completed. | The projection can be stale for some investors while the run is otherwise healthy, so the report is the only place that shows it. Mitigated by the failure count being carried into the run summary and by the recalculate-all entry point existing for exactly this recovery. | Medium | — |
| **D-274** | Answer an **internal-error 500** when rate-limit accounting cannot be persisted, unconditionally — even when the count that was observed is already over budget. Amends D-040. | Answer the mandated `429` because the observed count is over budget. Or fail open and serve the request. | A `429` is a statement about the caller: it says the caller exceeded a quota and tells them when to retry. When the counter could not be written, the delivery does not know that — the number it has is unreliable by construction, and quoting a retry interval derived from it would be a false statement with an actionable number attached. Failing open silently removes the control. A `500` says what is true: the server could not complete the request. | A caller that is genuinely over budget receives a `500` rather than a `429` while accounting is broken, which is a less informative answer to a real condition. Accepted and documented as a two-row caller-guidance contract, so a consumer can tell the two apart and neither is mistaken for the other. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-275** | Replace the unkeyed 32-bit fingerprint of logged values with **omission, opaque identifiers and per-run arrival ordinals**. Amends D-241. | Keep the fingerprint. Or key it with a secret held in a new record. Or log the values verbatim. | An unkeyed short digest of a low-entropy value is reversible by anyone who can guess the input space, which for an email address or a company name is the whole point of logging it — so the mechanism intended to protect the value disclosed it. A keyed digest is the textbook answer and needs a secret record outside the application scope, which the requirements forbid. What the logs actually need is the ability to **correlate** entries within a run and to name a record without quoting it, and an opaque identifier plus an arrival ordinal delivers exactly that with no recoverable content at all. | Correlation is per-run rather than global: the same subject in two runs carries two ordinals and cannot be joined across runs from the log alone. Accepted as the correct trade, and stated where the log format is documented. | High | — |
| **D-276** | Build **exactly one outbound arm**, selected by a recorded pre-flight: the generic Flow Designer REST step where both conditions hold, and the scoped script step otherwise. Amends D-045 and D-231. **Corrected by D-321:** this row called the REST step "primary" and the script step a mere "entitlement fallback" without carrying D-045's per-source qualification, which made it contradict the same guide's statement that the REST step is not buildable for Crunchbase. The arm is **determined per source** — always the script step for Crunchbase — and only genuinely selected per instance for LinkedIn. | Build the script arm as primary because it works everywhere, as delivered originally. Or build both and select at runtime. | The plan names the declarative step as the primary path and the scripted resolution as the fallback, in that order, and it is frozen. The ordering is also the better engineering: the declarative step is the supported mechanism, it is inspectable in the flow, and it needs no code to hold a credential reference. Building both and choosing at runtime would double the surface a flow test has to assert against for no benefit, since which arm exists is a property of the instance and does not change between runs. | The flow that gets built differs by instance, so a reviewer must establish which arm is present before reading the flow, and the flow test must assert against the arm that exists. Mitigated by both guides carrying the pre-flight as a numbered step and by the arms being labelled identically in both. **D-321 narrows this:** for Crunchbase the arm does not differ by instance at all, so the only per-instance variation a reviewer must establish is on the LinkedIn flow. | Medium | — |
| **D-277** | Decide fallback on **transport and envelope validity alone**, and treat row cardinality as data: a well-formed live response carrying no rows is a successful live run. | Treat an empty result as a failed live call and fall back, as delivered originally. Or fall back only on transport failure and accept a malformed envelope. | Falling back on an empty result inverts the meaning of the provenance label, because a live provider that legitimately has nothing new produces a run labelled `fallback` — and the acceptance criterion turns on that label being true. The distinction the fallback exists to make is "could the provider be reached and understood", which is answered by the transport and the envelope; how many rows came back is the answer, not the question. | An upstream fault that manifests as a well-formed empty response is recorded as a live run with no rows rather than as a failure. Bounded by the closed fallback-reason set naming the conditions that do fall back, and by cardinality being reported in the run summary where a persistent zero is visible. | Medium | — |
| **D-278** | **Amended by `D-386`, which is the authority for the claim mechanism; the ownership principle below stands and its mechanism has moved from a fifth `import_state` member to a reserved `run:` prefix in the existing `import_run` column, with a claimed row staying `pending`.** Claim work with an **atomic source lease**, a GUID run identifier and a row claim that makes the ownership of a staged row an observable fact. | Rely on the scheduler not overlapping runs. Or claim rows with a query-then-update. | Two overlapping executions of the same flow both read the same `pending` rows and both process them, which duplicates entities and corrupts every count derived from them — and the cadence guard cannot prevent it, because a manual run and a scheduled run can coincide. A query-then-update leaves the same window open, only narrower. A lease and a claimed intermediate state make the ownership of a row an observable fact rather than a timing assumption. | A run that dies mid-claim leaves rows no later run will pick up unless the abandonment is detectable. Handled explicitly: an abandoned claim is a `pending` row whose `import_run` begins `run:`, the reserved prefix is documented wherever the choice list is, and the staging guide carries the procedure for releasing such a claim. The earlier reading of this row — that a fifth `import_state` member was added beyond the four the plan lists — no longer describes the delivery. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-279** | Bound the synchronous work of each run with **six named budgets** rather than with durable asynchronous continuation. | Let each run process everything it finds. Or split each run across queued continuations. | An unbounded synchronous run inside a flow is a transaction that grows with the upstream corpus, and the failure mode is a cancelled transaction that has already written half its entities. Durable continuation is the right answer at real volume and is disproportionate here: the plan's prototype focus is explicit, and a continuation design adds state, resumption and idempotency concerns to a run that currently reads a bounded fallback dataset. Named budgets bound the work, and the run reports which budget stopped it. | A run that reaches a budget has processed less than the corpus, and reaching one is reported rather than treated as an error. Stated in both guides with the budget names, so an operator can see whether a short run was bounded or faulted. | Medium | — |
| **D-280** | Fail reconciliation on **malformed staged identifiers** and recover the claims those rows hold. | Skip an unparseable identifier list and continue. Or abandon the run and leave the claims. | Silently skipping is how a claimed row becomes permanently invisible: it stays `pending` under a lease no live run holds, no run will pick it up, and nothing reports it. Abandoning the run without releasing claims produces the same orphan at larger scale. Failing loudly and returning the claims leaves the staging table in the state a later run can act on, which is the only outcome that is both honest and recoverable. | A single malformed row fails a reconciliation that might otherwise have completed. Accepted: an unparseable identifier list means the delivery does not know which rows it holds, and proceeding under that uncertainty is what produces the orphan. | Medium | — |
| **D-281** | **Amended by `D-404`, which fixes part `8c` of action `A7` as the single writer of the marker this row gates.** Stamp the completion marker **only after** every final health check has passed, and have an unhealthy run stamp nothing and release its lease. Amends D-235. | Stamp on reaching the end of the run, as delivered originally. Or stamp at the start and correct it afterwards. | The marker is what the cadence guard reads, so stamping it for an unhealthy run tells the next run that this one succeeded — the fault is hidden **and** the retry is suppressed for a full cadence interval. Stamping at the start has the same effect sooner. Ordering the stamp last makes the marker mean exactly what its name says, and releasing the lease keeps the source available to the next attempt. | An unhealthy run leaves the marker at its previous value, so a persistent fault produces repeated attempts at every cadence interval rather than one attempt and silence. That is the intended direction, and the escape hatches are bounded by an explicit preferred / acceptable / **never** table in both guides. | High | — |
| **D-282** | Assert publication **by sequence** — the outcome of the Log action itself, in the run's own evidence line — rather than by querying a log table. Amends D-234. | Query the flow log for the run's context and assert a record exists. Or trust that the action ran. | A query against a log table asserts the platform's retention and the caller's read access as much as it asserts publication, and the target instance was hibernating throughout, so that posture could not be verified. The action's own outcome is available at the point it matters, inside the run, and a run that could not publish its evidence is not a healthy run — so the assertion belongs in the health predicate rather than in a later read. | The assertion establishes that the action reported success, not that a human later found the record. Mitigated by the test suite additionally reading the flow log for the context it started, so the two levels of evidence exist without the run depending on the second. | Medium | — |
| **D-283** | Render the **mandatory run summary before** the loop that can exhaust the message ceiling, and report omission as an integer output plus an explicit trailer. Amends D-234. | Render events in arrival order and let the summary fall off the end when the ceiling is reached. Or truncate the block mid-line. | The summary is the one line the mandated evidence requires; every other event is detail. Rendering in arrival order means the run that produced the most events — the one most worth reading — is the run whose summary is missing. Truncation is worse than omission because it is invisible: a reader cannot tell a short block from a complete one. Ordering the summary first and counting what was dropped makes the loss explicit and quantified. | The evidence block can be incomplete, and a reader must notice the trailer to know it. Mitigated by the omitted count also being a flow output and by parse loss or truncation failing the health predicate, so an incomplete block cannot be reported as a clean run. | Medium | — |
| **D-284** | Require `skipped == 0` for a run to be **healthy**, while `rejected > 0` does not bar health. | Treat both counters as faults. Or treat neither as a fault. | The two counters mean opposite things. A rejected record is the cleaning rules working as specified — a record missing a mandatory field is supposed to be refused — so a run that rejects is a correct run. A skipped record is a record the delivery **failed** to process for a reason it did not anticipate, which is precisely the condition a health signal exists to surface. Collapsing them would either make every normal run unhealthy or make every unhandled error invisible. | An operator who reads `rejected` as a fault will over-escalate, and one who reads `skipped` as routine will under-escalate. Mitigated by both counters appearing in the run summary with their meanings stated, and by the health predicate being written out member by member in both guides. | Medium | — |
| **D-285** | Emit only a **redacted status projection** from flow outputs, excluding every data-bearing member. | Return the aggregate envelope as the flow output. Or return nothing and rely on the log. | A flow output is visible in the execution record and to anything that consumes the flow, so returning the envelope publishes upstream personal data into a surface with different access controls from the tables it was written to. Returning nothing would leave the test suite and the operator with no machine-readable outcome at all. A status projection carries the counters, the branch, the provenance and the fault class, which is everything a consumer needs and none of the content. | A consumer that wanted a row from the output must read the tables instead. Accepted and stated: the projection's member list is published in both guides so nothing depends on a member that is deliberately absent. | Medium | — |
| **D-286** | **Amended by `D-399`, which publishes the four-member child-collection contract the panes render.** Carry a **bounded, redacted per-call log** into the run's logging, its published evidence and its health predicate. | Log each call verbatim. Or log nothing per call and report only the aggregate. | Without per-call outcomes a multi-page run that failed on its third page is indistinguishable from one that succeeded, which makes both diagnosis and the fallback decision guesswork. Logging verbatim would publish request URLs and provider responses, which carry both credentials-adjacent material and personal data. Bounding the entry count and redacting each entry keeps the diagnostic value and drops the content, and wiring it into health means a run whose calls partly failed cannot report itself clean. | A very long run's call log is truncated to its bound, so the earliest or latest calls may not appear. Bounded deliberately and stated with the limit, and the aggregate counters remain complete regardless. | Medium | — |
| **D-287** | Publish **one exact flow graph** per guide, with explicit guard and error-handler placement, rather than a prose description of the steps. | Describe the steps in prose and let the builder infer the graph. Or publish a diagram without the handler placement. | A flow is a graph, and two builders reading the same prose produce two different graphs — most consequentially around the guard, where placing the cadence check after the first provider call turns every no-op execution into a live request. Handler placement has the same property: a handler attached one level out swallows the errors it was meant to isolate. An exact graph removes the inference. | A graph is harder to keep in step with the step-by-step text than prose is. Mitigated by the graph and the step table being generated from one step list per guide, and by the assertion harness checking their consistency. | Medium | — |
| **D-288** | Map provider errors to a **closed per-flow code set**, with bounded redacted detail, from **one** `redact()` helper that guides 01, 02 and 03 carry character for character. **Superseded by `D-359`:** there is no `redact()` helper. The five character-for-character copies that guides 01, 02 and 03 carried are collapsed into the **single** delivered method `AppProperties.safeText(value, limit)`, which every probe and flow script calls rather than reproducing. A copy that is reproduced is a copy that drifts, which is what this row was trying to prevent by other means. | Let each guide pass the provider's own message through. Or share a prose description of redaction and let each guide implement it. | An open error string is an exfiltration path: provider messages routinely echo the request, including query parameters and headers. A closed code set makes the fault class assertable in a test, which an arbitrary string is not. Sharing the helper by prose rather than by bytes is how three implementations drift, and the one that drifts is the one that leaks — so the three copies are identical text and the harness checks that they are. | Three literal copies must be kept identical by discipline rather than by a shared module, because a Markdown guide cannot import one. Mitigated by the equality being asserted mechanically rather than reviewed by eye. | Medium | — |
| **D-289** | Make the **authenticated provider probe a hard activation gate** for the shipped base URL rather than a diagnostic step. | Ship the base URL as active and let the first scheduled run discover it. Or probe and warn. | An unverified base URL fails at the first scheduled run, in a flow, where the failure is a fallback with a transport reason rather than a clear statement that the URL is wrong — and the fallback masks it indefinitely because the run still completes. Probing before activation moves the same discovery to the moment an operator is looking at it, with the remedy in front of them. A warning would be ignored precisely because the fallback makes the consequence invisible. | An operator with no provider credential cannot pass the gate and therefore cannot activate the live path. That is the intended outcome, and it is exactly what the fallback dataset exists for; the guides state the fallback-only route explicitly so the gate does not block the delivery. | Medium | — |
| **D-290** | Re-specify the LinkedIn live call as a **bounded two-page, two-call identity probe** whose `probe_ok` member is excluded from the health predicate, and build its REST arm as three explicitly enumerated actions with the page list computed up front. Amends D-233. | Leave the live arm unbounded. Or include the probe outcome in health. Or remove the live arm entirely. | The record types that flow ingests have no public read API, so its live call cannot acquire anything — it can only establish that the credential and the organisation resolve, which is a bounded question and needs a bounded number of calls. Including the probe in health would make every fallback-mode run unhealthy, which inverts the meaning of the signal for the flow that can **only** run in fallback mode. Removing the arm would discard the one live signal available and make the credential posture unverifiable. | A run can be healthy while its live probe failed, so a reader must look at `probe_ok` separately to know the credential state. Stated in the guide and carried as a distinct member rather than folded into the fault counters. | Medium | — |
| **D-291** | Resolve each flow by its **`internal_name`** against `sys_hub_flow` with a singularity assertion, end the runner chain in **`.run()`**, and assert a 32-character hexadecimal context identifier read from what `.run()` returned. Amends D-247. | Name the flow by its display label. Or end the chain at `inForeground()` and read the identifier from the builder. Or assert only that the identifier is non-empty. | Every one of the rejected forms produces a test that passes without running a flow, which is the worst available outcome for a coverage claim: the label is not what the runner resolves, a chain that never reaches `.run()` builds a runner and discards it, and a builder asked for a context answers an empty value that a non-empty check would have to treat as a failure anyway. Asserting the identifier's **shape** is what distinguishes a real execution from a builder answering, and the singularity check on the flow record is what stops a namesake in another scope being started instead. | The assertion depends on the platform's context identifiers being 32-character hexadecimal, which is the documented form. Mitigated by the failure message naming the likely cause, so a release that changed the form would be diagnosed rather than worked around. | High | — |
| **D-292** | **Amended by `D-401`, which fixes the swept and asserted subject as one disposable identity per suite rather than a class of identities.** Guarantee credential cleanup by **convergence, `finally` and detection** — the suite's first test sweeps any identity a previous run abandoned, every mint is paired with a retirement in a `finally` block, and four post-suite residue queries make a survivor detectable — rather than by a trailing teardown step or a teardown test. Amends D-246. | Add a teardown step at the end of each test. Or add a dedicated teardown test at the end of the suite. Or rely on the framework's rollback. | A trailing step does not run when the step before it aborts, which is the case that matters, and a dedicated teardown test does not run when the suite is stopped early — so both mechanisms are absent exactly when they are needed. The framework's rollback covers data a test created through it, not an identity minted to authenticate an inbound call from outside the transaction. Convergence makes the next run clean regardless of how this one ended, `finally` makes the common case clean immediately, and the residue queries make the remaining case visible instead of theoretical. | A survivor exists between an aborted run and the next run's sweep, and during that window it is a live web-service credential. Bounded rather than eliminated: the identity holds no role and no interactive access, the window is stated plainly at D-246, and the four residue queries are gate-blocking in the acceptance checklist rather than advisory. | High | `AUTHZ` |
| **D-293** | Make `ingestion.source_mode` an **operator precondition** rather than test-managed state, and declare the run-state marker **unrestorable by design** with three enforced obligations. | Have each test write and restore both values. Or assert them and skip when they are wrong. | A test that writes a shared property and restores it afterwards is correct only if it always reaches the restore, and the flow tests are precisely the tests that can abort mid-run. The run-state marker is worse than shared: the flow rewrites it as part of the behaviour under test, so a restored value would contradict what the run actually did. Declaring the marker unrestorable and requiring the mode to be set beforehand replaces a fragile guarantee with an asserted precondition and three obligations a reader can check. | The suite depends on instance state an operator must set, so a suite run against an unprepared instance fails on its precondition rather than on its subject. Accepted as the correct failure: the precondition is numbered in the guide and asserted in the suite's first step. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-294** | Remove the rate-limit window race by **seeding both window boundaries** rather than by waiting for the window to roll. | Sleep for the window duration inside the test. Or shorten the window property for the test. | A sleep makes a test slow **and** non-deterministic, because the assertion then depends on where the wall clock fell relative to the boundary; and a test that edits the window property is editing shared configuration for every other caller during its run. Seeding a counter row on each side of the boundary makes the boundary the subject of the assertion rather than a hazard to be waited out, and the result is exact. | The test asserts against seeded state rather than against naturally accumulated state. Accepted: the accumulation path is asserted separately by the over-budget test, so both the counting and the boundary are covered without either depending on timing. | Medium | — |
| **D-295** | **Amended by `D-402`, which extends the secure generator to the run token as well as the minted secret.** Mint the test caller's password with `GlideSecureRandomUtil.getSecureRandomString(40)` plus a four-character class supplement, rather than deriving it from a generated identifier. Amends D-246. | Derive it from a GUID, as delivered originally. Or use a fixed literal in the guide. | A generated identifier is not secret material: its alphabet is 16 characters, its structure is published, and the same value is often written into a record where it can be read back. A fixed literal in a build guide is a published password. A cryptographic random string is the platform's own answer to this and costs one call; the class supplement exists only so the value satisfies a complexity policy an instance may enforce, which is why it is appended rather than relied upon. | The password exists in memory during the test and is never stored or logged. Mitigated by the identity's short life at D-292, and by the value being generated per run so no two runs share one. | Medium | — |
| **D-296** | Guard the record-watch subject **before** registering, deregister every listener on `$destroy`, and tolerate both handle forms the platform may return. | Register unconditionally and rely on the page being discarded. Or assume one handle form. | A watch registered against an absent record or an empty identifier is a subscription to nothing that never ends, and a listener that outlives its scope fires against a destroyed controller — both are leaks that grow with navigation, which on a profile page a user pages through repeatedly is the normal case rather than the edge case. The handle form could not be verified against the instance, which was hibernating, so both forms are handled rather than one guessed. | Tolerating two handle forms is defensive code for a contract the delivery could not observe. Recorded as exactly that, so a later verification can simplify it rather than wonder why it is there. | Medium | — |
| **D-297** | Publish the staging load as **four dependency-ordered groups under one outer step**, with a `<source>-manual-<numeric>` run identifier, rather than renumbering the existing nine steps. | Renumber the steps into a flat dependency order. Or leave the order to the operator. | The nine steps are cited by identifier from three other documents, so renumbering them would silently invalidate every citation — and the dependency constraint is not a different order but a **grouping**, since the files inside a group are order-independent and only the groups are not. Grouping expresses the real constraint and leaves the citations intact. The run identifier carries its source because the marker is per-source, and a manual load that omits it is indistinguishable from a scheduled run in the summary. | An operator must read the group structure rather than a flat numbered list. Mitigated by the groups being stated as a table with the reason each precedes the next, and by the summary flags an operator must assert after each group. | Low | — |
| **D-298** | **Superseded by `D-547`.** Keep `linkInvestorToRound()` in the delivery and document it as an **off-path administrative helper** rather than deleting it as dead code. | Delete it. Or wire it into the ingestion path so it has a caller. | Nothing in the delivered ingestion path calls it, because participant links arrive through the reconciliation of D-270 — but it is the only entry point for an administrator correcting a single link by hand, and it maintains the derived projection while doing so. Wiring it into the ingestion path would give the same operation two implementations. Deleting it would remove the correction path and leave the manual remedy in the staging guide with nothing to call. | A method with no caller inside the delivery looks like dead code to a reviewer and to a coverage report. Mitigated by it being named as an administrative helper in both the access-control and staging documents, with its one intended caller stated. **The mitigation did not hold: the premise that wiring it in would create two implementations was inverted, because the reconciler already carried its own copy of the insert. See `D-547`.** | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-547** | Make `linkInvestorToRound(roundId, investorId, suppressRules)` the **single insert implementation** for `x_bst_startuptrk_m2m_round_investor`, and have `IngestionMapper.linkParticipants()` delegate its insert step to it with `suppressRules` `true`. Supersedes `D-298`. | Leave the two copies and only correct `D-298`'s rationale. Or extract a private insert used by both, keeping the public helper a thin wrapper. Or delete the helper and leave the reconciler's inline copy as the only implementation. | `D-298` reasoned that wiring the helper into the ingestion path “would give the same operation two implementations”. That was inverted: the join insert already existed **twice** — once inline in `linkParticipants()` and once in the unreferenced helper — which is the duplication the AAP's “each business rule exists once” forbids. Delegating removes the second copy while keeping the administrative entry point every one of the six documents that names it depends on. `suppressRules` is what preserves the batch design: the reconciler still writes each row with the join table's rule suppressed and still closes with one `_converge()` over the affected investors, so the recalculation stays scoped rather than firing per row. Deleting the helper would have removed the documented manual correction path; a private-insert extraction would have kept two public shapes for one write and exposed an internal across a class boundary. | The helper's pre-existence query now runs once per **newly inserted** link, which the in-memory `existing` map had previously made unnecessary. That cost is bounded by one round's participant count and is the mechanism that makes the write idempotent against the table's unique index on `(funding_round, investor)` — a stale map previously produced a refused insert counted as a spurious failure, so the query removes a real failure mode rather than only adding work. An administrative call still omits `suppressRules`, so a hand-linked pair maintains `portfolio_count` as the row is written. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-299** | Document the table posture as **two postures, not one**, and state that the rate limiter is a fair-use control and **not** a security boundary. **Superseded by D-310:** there is now one posture, not two — all ten tables are sealed. The sentence naming what the rate limiter is **not** stands as recorded. | Present the posture as a single rule with exceptions. Or omit the limiter's status. | A single rule with exceptions is how the closed posture came to be asserted of ten tables when it is true of three, and the two postures exist for different reasons — one to make denial observable, one to keep internal machinery unreachable — so collapsing them loses the reason as well as the fact. The limiter's status has to be explicit because the Table API route is unmetered: a reader who believed the limiter was a security control would read that route as a hole rather than as a documented property. | Two postures are more to hold in mind than one, and a reader who remembers only the first may apply it to the wrong table. Mitigated by the entity and supporting tables being listed separately wherever the posture appears, and by the sentence naming what the limiter is not. | Low | — |
| **D-300** | Extend the flag inventory to **nineteen**, adding **F18** — both flows calling their provider through one of two arms decided per instance — and **F19** — no retention, minimisation or erasure of the verbatim upstream payload — and mark F19 for prominence alongside the two existing prominent flags. Amends D-252. | Leave the inventory at seventeen and describe both facts only where they arise. Or fold them into existing entries. | Both are exactly what the flag obligation is for: a requirement met in a way a reader would not assume, and a requirement not met at all. F18 changes what a reviewer will find in the flow, and describing it only in the ingestion guides leaves a reader of the gap register believing there is one arm. F19 is the delivery's one residual privacy exposure and the direct consequence of D-265 removing the lifecycle — an unflagged omission there would be a silent half-implementation, which is the specific thing the obligation forbids. | A reader who counted flags in an earlier revision finds two more. Mitigated by the count being stated wherever it appears and by both additions naming precisely what falls short. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-301** | Renumber the acceptance document's deployment section into **four subsections** rather than deleting the removed one and leaving a gap, and record the three re-homed checks in a **performed-elsewhere table** inside the gate subsection. | Delete the pre-commit subsection and leave three subsections numbered 1, 3 and 4. Or fold the prerequisites into the first subsection's bullets. | A numbering gap in a sign-off document reads as a missing check, which is the opposite of the intended message. Folding the prerequisites into bullets would hide the distinction that matters most in that section — a prerequisite is satisfied before the import and never rolls anything back, a gate is asserted after the commit and does — so the two stay separately headed. The performed-elsewhere table exists because a reader who expects sixteen gates needs to be told where the other three went, in the place they would look for them. | Subsection identifiers changed, so any external citation of the old numbering is stale. Mitigated by the section being cited by name rather than by number from the two documents that reference it. | Low | — |
| **D-302** | Have the acceptance document assert the **failure mode** as well as the success: an empty flow-context identifier is a **failure**, not a "not yet started" state. | Assert only that a context identifier is present. Or leave the interpretation to the operator. | The empty value is exactly what a builder answers when the chain never ran, so an operator who reads it as "not started yet" will re-run the step and record a pass on the second attempt without a flow ever having executed. Naming the empty value as a failure closes the one reading that turns a broken test into a green result. | An operator meeting a genuine transient could record a failure where a retry would have succeeded. Accepted: the retry is still available and the distinction is stated, and a false failure is the safe direction for an acceptance gate. | Medium | — |
| **D-303** | Require **two named evidence sources** per row in the flow-evidence table, rather than reading the flow's own `healthy` member. | Read `healthy` from the run's own summary. Or require one source per row. | A run reporting on itself is the only witness the delivery must not rely on for acceptance, because the failure being guarded against is a run that reports success incorrectly. Two sources — the run's evidence line and an independent platform read of the same fact — make the assertion falsifiable. One source would be either the self-report or the read, and the self-report is the one an operator reaches for first. | Two sources per row make the table longer and the sign-off slower. Accepted for the acceptance document specifically, where the cost is proportionate to what the signature asserts. | Medium | — |
| **D-304** | **Repair** the one pre-existing broken table row in the acceptance document rather than documenting it and leaving it. | Leave it and note it as pre-existing. Or note it in the report only. | The defect rendered a paragraph inside a table cell in the document an operator signs, so it was materially misleading in the place least able to carry a rendering fault — and the fix is a blank line. Leaving a known rendering defect in a sign-off document because it predates the current work is the letter of scope discipline against its purpose. | Touching a line no finding named. Recorded here, and the change is confined to inserting the separating blank line the table needed. | Low | — |
| **D-305** | **Amended by `D-407`, which records the disposition as decision B — accept — with a named risk-owner role, leaving only the countersignature open.** Render every diagram at a **non-default security level** under the frozen library pin, rather than advancing the pin to a release that closes the same exposure. Amends D-253. **Corrected to `securityLevel: 'strict'`:** the `'sandbox'` level draws each diagram into an iframe whose `src` is a `data:` URL, which the deck's own content-security policy forbids with `frame-src 'none'`, so every diagram was blocked from display while rendering correctly inside the document. `'strict'` sanitises the same source and renders inline, which the policy permits unchanged, and `flowchart.htmlLabels` is `false` besides, so no label is parsed as markup. The advisory's own reachability answer in the gaps document is **No**, because every diagram source is a literal in the file. | Advance the diagram library to a later version. Or leave the default security level and rely on the content being authored in-repository. | The pinned version is frozen by both the plan's dependency inventory and the presentation rule, so advancing it is not available however cleanly it would resolve the advisory. Sandboxing is the mechanism the pinned version itself provides for exactly this exposure, and the diagrams are static text the delivery authors, so nothing is lost functionally. Relying on the content being trusted would leave the exposure open to whatever a later editor pastes in. | Sandboxed rendering would have placed each diagram in an opaque-origin frame, so the page could not inspect the rendered result — which cost the delivery its ability to detect the library's own error graphic and required the render-health test to be rewritten, recorded at D-306. Rendering inline at the strict level returns both: the health test reads the drawing directly, and the per-class height caps apply to the drawing rather than to a frame. | Medium | — |
| **D-306** | Judge a render by **the drawing it produced** — viewBox extent, label count and the absence of the library's error graphic — configure the library **synchronously** before any document-ready handler, and retain the frame branch of the health test, together with the `max-height: none` frame exemption, for a sandbox-level render. | Reach into the frame to inspect the result. Or decode the frame source at runtime. Or mirror the existing per-class caps onto the frame. | The frame is opaque-origin by construction, so inspection is not available at any price, and decoding its source would re-parse every diagram on every slide change. Height is the one property the library publishes on the frame it creates, and it is sufficient to separate a render from a failure. Synchronous configuration removes a pre-existing race in which a cached library auto-initialised with default settings before the deck configured it. The media cap needed `max-height: none` specifically because a frame **clips** where an image scales, so a 95 % cap silently removes the bottom of a diagram rather than shrinking it. | One style rule depends on beating a theme selector on specificity, with the specificity arithmetic recorded next to the rule. The frame branch and the frame exemption are inert at the delivered strict level, and are kept so a sandbox-level render remains supported rather than silently mis-measured. | Medium | — |
| **D-307** | Bring every content slide inside the presentation rule's forty-word limit by making the 21 entitlement cells **icon-only with accessible names**, and by **merging** two related risks into one row rather than dropping a risk. Amends D-086. | Split the over-long slides into more slides. Or drop rows, columns and the risk. Or shorten the prose and accept the limit being missed. | The limit is a rule, and splitting would have pushed the deck past the upper slide bound, so the words had to come out of the slides that had them. An icon with an accessible name carries the same information as the word it replaces and contributes no body words, which is what let the full seven-by-three matrix survive the reduction intact. Dropping a risk from a risk slide is the one reduction that changes the message rather than its length, so the two risks that share a reviewer and a mitigation were merged instead. | An icon-only cell depends on its accessible name for meaning, and a merged risk row is less granular than the two it replaces. The first is asserted mechanically by counting the labels; the second keeps both risks named inside the row. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-308** | Strip justification from comments while **preserving branch-contrast phrasing**, and apply the two stylesheet comment edits to the canonical file and the inline mirror from **one shared pair list**. Amends D-005 and D-085. | Strip every comment that mentions an alternative, including descriptions of what a branch does. Or edit the two stylesheet copies separately. | The rule forbids rationale, not description, and a comment stating that a call **suppresses** business rules or that an overflow **empties rather than truncates** describes observable behaviour — removing those would degrade the code to satisfy a rule aimed at something else. Editing the two stylesheet copies separately is how byte-identity is lost; deriving both from one pair list makes identity a property of the construction rather than of the reviewer's attention. | The line between description and justification is a judgement, so a later reader may disagree with an individual comment. Mitigated by every surviving flagged comment being listed with the reason it is descriptive, and by the boundary being stated in the review document. | Low | — |
| **D-309** | Record this remediation round as its **own section** rather than extending section 15, and correct each superseded row in place with a sentence naming what no longer describes the delivery. Amends D-251. | Extend section 15, as D-251 prescribes for remediation decisions. Or renumber the file. Or rewrite the affected rows without saying what changed. | The two rounds answer different questions and a reader needs to tell them apart: section 15 closed gaps the plan had not anticipated, this section restores what the plan freezes and removes what it does not license — several of its rows **reverse** a section 15 row. Merging them would produce a section whose rows contradict each other with no indication of order. Renumbering would break every citation from fourteen documents. Rewriting silently would erase the very history Rule 1 exists to preserve. | Two remediation sections rather than one, so a reader tracing a decision may have to follow an amendment chain two hops long. Mitigated by every amended row naming its corrector by identifier, in both directions. | Low | `DEV` |

**On D-265 and section 15.** Five rows of that section — D-235, D-236, D-239, D-240 and D-243 — described behaviour that lived on artifacts outside the plan's frozen inventory. The behaviour they were introduced to secure was not discarded with the artifacts: severity normalisation and boundary validation moved onto classes the inventory already carries, per-source cadence state moved into the one property the inventory already lists, and data-subject erasure was removed outright and recorded as flag **F19**. That last one is the only capability this round removed without replacement, which is why it is flagged rather than merely logged.

## 17. Remediation of the frontend-to-API seam review

Decisions `D-310` onward. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised when the delivered artifacts were reviewed for **contradictions across boundaries** — a document stating one contract while the delivered code implements another, two documents stating incompatible procedures for the same artifact, or an arithmetic that does not close. The direction of travel is single-valuedness: where an earlier round added detail, this one removes the second version of a fact. Every row below cites the row it amends or supersedes, and every superseded row carries a sentence in place naming what no longer describes the delivery, per `D-309`.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-310** | **Seal all ten application tables** — `access` `package_private`, `read_access` false, `ws_access` false, with every write, schema and configuration flag already false — so the Scripted REST API is the only route by which any caller reaches any row. Supersedes D-254 and D-299, and supersedes in part the read-route sentence of D-027. | Keep the seven entity tables open and add enforceable query-match and query-range controls plus aggregate controls on each of the seven premium columns, with a negative test per column. Or keep them open and accept the exposure as a documented residual, which is what the superseded rows did. Or seal only the two currency-bearing tables, leaving the four boolean and email columns exposed. | A field-level read control omits a value from a **response**, and three native routes obtain the same value without one ever being serialised. A query predicate such as `total_funding_usd>50000000` is evaluated in the database before serialisation, so a caller who cannot read the column recovers the figure by bisection in a handful of requests. The native Aggregate API returns a computed sum or average, so there is no element on which a field control could be evaluated at all. And `access` `public` with `read_access` true admits unsecured record access from any other application scope, which consults no access control whatsoever and returns every column exactly. Two further exposures travelled with them: a native list route sat outside `StartupSearchService`'s inclusion plan, returning records prompt section 1.1 excludes from search results, and outside the fixed-window rate limiter. **None of the four is fixable inside the access controls**, which is why the posture and not a further control is where they are closed. The query-control alternative was rejected because it would add seven controls plus seven negative tests to defend a route no requirement asks for, and would still leave the cross-scope unsecured read open; the accept-the-residual alternative was rejected because prompt section 2.0 requires a genuine access denial and a value recoverable by bisection is not one; the partial-seal alternative was rejected because `contact_email` and `institutional_funding_last_5yrs` are equally gated and a boolean is the easiest of all to recover from a predicate. | Sealing costs the ability to verify a table by reading one of its rows over the native interface, which is how the plan's eleven post-commit gates were expressed. That cost is met at D-311 rather than absorbed. Nothing at run time is affected: the portal widgets, both flows, the three business rules and the ten test suites all execute inside the scope, and a Scripted REST API is unaffected by a table's `ws_access` value. The residual is instance-level privilege, which reaches every table on the instance regardless of this posture; it is bounded by `GATE-SEC-01` recording the posture of all ten tables on every deployment. | High | `AUTHZ` `DEV` |
| **D-311** | Express the seven required table gates as a **metadata read that also asserts posture** — one `sys_db_object` record per table, requiring exactly one match and `package_private` / `false` / `false` — and add a **fourth class-3 diagnostic**, `GATE-SEC-04`, a scoped background script that opens each entity table through the secured record path and reports `valid` and `can_read`. Amends D-255 and D-256; consequence of D-310. | Read a row of each table as the plan words it, which D-310 makes impossible. Or drop the posture assertion and read only the definition record's presence. Or make `GATE-SEC-04` a twelfth required gate. | The plan's gate asks two questions of each table — did it commit, and can it be read — and a sealed table cannot answer the second over an external route. The metadata read answers the first more precisely than a row read did, because it also proves the table committed with the posture the whole access-control design rests on; a row read proved only that some posture admitted the request. Dropping the posture assertion would leave the one thing most worth checking unchecked, since an unsealed table is exactly the defect D-310 closes.
