| **D-329** | Extend the error-and-retry contract to the **initial load of the two dashboard widgets**, and delete the `width: auto; height: auto` declarations from `.bst-charts-svg`. | Leave both widgets without an error path, on the ground that neither issues a server call after its first render. Or publish the error but omit the retry. Or keep the CSS and drop the element's bound geometry. | The first render *is* a server call: every figure both widgets publish comes from an aggregate, and an aggregate that throws left them rendering an empty panel, a bare `0` or a chart with no bars, with nothing to retry and nothing said. The CSS was worse than redundant — a CSS declaration overrides a presentation attribute, so `width: auto` discarded the element's `width="100%"` and `height: auto` discarded the server-computed `ng-attr-height`, defeating the geometry the server had just calculated. | Two more widgets carrying a `c.load()` and a `c.retry()`, and the SVG's sizing now lives entirely in the template, where a future CSS rule on the same class would silently break it again. | Medium | — |
| **D-330** | Render **exactly one `<h1>` per page, from the widget that is the page's subject**, make every panel heading an `<h2 class="panel-title">`, and render the KPI figure as a `<p class="bst-kpi-value h2">` rather than an `<h2>`. | Leave the levels as delivered, where four of five routes opened at `<h3>` and none had a first-level heading. Or add a page-level heading widget, which would be a ninth widget. Or keep the KPI figure in an `<h2>` because the size is right. | A portal page is assembled from widgets, so no page record can carry the heading and the subject widget is the only place it can live. The KPI figure in an `<h2>` put a bare number into the document outline beneath its own panel's heading — a heading element used for size alone, which is what the Bootstrap `.h2` utility class exists for. | Nine template elements changed, and a hierarchy that a future widget added to an existing page can break by starting at `<h1>`. The `<h1 class="h2">` form reads as a contradiction until the class-versus-element distinction is understood. | Low | — |
| **D-331** | Give all six data tables a `<caption class="sr-only">`, `scope="col"` on every column header and a `<th scope="row">` as each body row's first cell. | Leave them as delivered, with bare `<th>` elements and no caption. Or add an `aria-label` on the `<table>` instead of a caption. | Five of the six tables had neither a caption nor a scoped header, so a screen reader could announce a cell's value with no reliable way to say which column and which row it belonged to — and these are the tables that carry the funding amounts and the `Premium` markers, where the column is the whole meaning. `.sr-only` is Bootstrap's own utility, so the caption costs no layout. | The row header is now the date on the funding table and the company on three others, which is a semantic choice a reader could disagree with; and a caption is easy to omit on a table added later. | Low | — |
| **D-332** | Announce **each state once, by one region**: `c.statusText()` returns the empty string when `data.error` is set, leaving the visible `role="alert"` panel as the sole announcement, and every static premium notice is a `role="region"` with an `aria-labelledby` rather than a `role="alert"`. | Keep both announcements, as delivered. Or drop `role="alert"` from the error panel and let the polite region own the state. | Two live regions carrying one fact are announced as two events, and `aria-atomic="true"` on the polite region means the caller hears the failure twice with no way to tell whether something failed twice. The error panel is the region that must own it: it is the visible one and it carries the **Retry** control. A premium notice is present from the first render and states a standing fact, so `role="alert"` interrupted for something that had not just happened. | A caller who reaches the polite region after a failure and never sees the panel hears nothing about it until the next state change. The upsell's accessible identifier is derived from `data.context`, so a fourth host would need a fourth context value to stay unique. | Low | — |
| **D-333** | Guard overflow at the 1024-pixel floor with **token-only CSS and a concise link label**: `max-width: $navbar-height` beside the existing `max-height` on both logo rules, `overflow-wrap: break-word` on the four containers that render unbroken 100-to-255-character values, and `Visit the company website` / `Visit the investor website` in place of the address as the link text. | Bound the logo with a percentage or pixel `max-width`, which the zero-literal rule forbids. Or declare a new theme token for the logo box, which would not be a Bootstrap or `$sp-*` variable. Or truncate the address with `text-overflow: ellipsis`, which needs a width. | `max-height` alone bounds nothing horizontally: a 2000-by-50 wordmark satisfies it and stays two thousand pixels wide. A 255-character URL is one unbreakable word, and it was being rendered as its own link label inside a `.pull-right` span. A square box built from the token that already bounded the height is the only non-arithmetic, token-only bound available, and a concise label is the better accessible choice as well as the narrower one. | A wide wordmark now scales into a square box and renders smaller than its author intended. The stored address is no longer visible in the portal; it remains the link target and is readable on the platform form view. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-334** | State that **no write path is built inside the portal by any widget for any role**, and record G2's write path as the platform's **native form view** on the scoped table: the stock **Form** widget and all three `spModal` members are listed as available and called by nothing. | Place a Form widget on a page, or open one in an `spModal`, and fully specify the admin-only path — the review's second option. Or delete the four rows and say nothing about them. | The delivered guide claimed a Form-and-`spModal` write path that no widget implemented and no page placed, and the AAP already resolves the administrative surface to the platform's native list and form views inside the scoped app with no sixth portal route. Specifying an in-portal write path would add a surface the requirements do not ask for; deleting the rows would leave an operator to discover the framework's dialogue API and use it unguided. | The portal is read-only with no in-page way to correct a record, so an administrator moves between the portal and the platform UI. Four table rows now describe capabilities nothing uses, which reads as dead documentation until the reason is read. | Medium | — |
| **D-335** | Publish **one premium treatment per granularity**: `<span class="label label-info">Premium</span>` at each of the twelve denied field positions, and `bst-premium-upsell` **once per surface**, with `.label-default` reserved for categories. | Keep the delivered text, which named `.label-default` for the marker while every template rendered `.label-info`, and described the alert as rendered in place of each gated field. Or standardise on `.label-default`. Or render the alert per field. | Three descriptions of one treatment cannot all be built, and the one the prose named was not the one the templates rendered. `.label-default` is this portal's *category* marker — job title, seniority, working pattern, focus area — so using it for a withheld value would make the denial read as one more attribute of the record. An alert per field would repeat one call to action up to six times on one profile. | The results widget renders the same notice inline rather than embedding the partial, so the pattern has two packagings and a reader must know why. Twelve field positions is a count that a new gated column changes. | Low | — |
| **D-336** | Render **one `type="submit"` control** in the search form — the `.input-group-btn` beside the name field — and leave **Clear** as the only other action. | Keep both submit buttons, as delivered. Or give the two different behaviours, such as a name-only quick search and a full search. Or remove the `.input-group-btn` and keep the actions-row button. | Two identically-labelled submit buttons in one form announce `Search, button` twice with no way to tell them apart, and `ng-submit` on the `<form>` already means the Enter key in any field does exactly what either button did — so the second added no behaviour. Inventing two behaviours would invent a requirement. The `.input-group-btn` is kept because the design-system mapping names it as the search field's component. | The only submit control now sits beside the name field, which can read as search-by-name although it submits all three filters; the paragraph under the template says so explicitly. | Low | — |
| **D-337** | Record `spUtil.addInfoMessage()` as **called by nothing**, with the reason, rather than as the premium-role request acknowledgement. | Leave the claim. Or build a request mechanism so the acknowledgement becomes true. | The same guide states, twice, that the upsell offers no request control and that the account page offers no control that claims a request was recorded — there is no request table, no approval and no notification, so an acknowledgement would be a claim the application cannot honour. Building the mechanism is F1 and F2 territory: entitlement is granted by an administrator assigning a role. | A reader looking for the message API finds it recorded as unused, which is the intended answer but requires reading the reason. | Low | — |
| **D-338** | Publish **every member a template binds** from the server script that owns it, and spell each member one way: `data.gatedAny` — not `data.anyGated` — and `data.upsellPage` on the results widget, `data.dimension` and `data.dimensionLabel` from one closed map, `data.largest` and `data.largestLabel` from the bucket loop, and `data.chart.description` assembled into a named local. | Leave the bindings unpublished and let the template render nothing. Or move the derivations into the client controller, which would put record-derived logic on the client. | Five members were bound by templates and published by nobody, and one — `c_description` — was referenced by a server script as a variable no step assigned, which raises a `ReferenceError` and leaves the chart unbuilt. Two names for one flag, `gatedAny` and `anyGated`, meant the results upsell could never render. A closed map is the single place the three dimension labels are written, which matters because the dashboard renders two instances of that widget at once. | More published members to keep in step with the templates. The dimension map is closed by design, so a fourth dimension is a change in two places — the map and the option's documented range. | Medium | — |
| **D-339** | Derive this file's four self-describing structures — [Summary counts](#summary-counts), the tranche table, the [section map](#section-map) and the [Index of deviation rows](#index-of-deviation-rows) — **from the body rows themselves**, and state that derivation rule here, rather than maintaining any of them by hand. Each deviation-index entry is the first sentence of that row's own Decision cell, truncated on a word boundary. | Maintain each structure by hand and reconcile it at review time, which is what the file did until this row. Or drop the structures and let a reader count. Or keep only the totals and remove the per-section and per-row indexes. | A hand-maintained index of a 349-row table drifts silently: a review of this file found the deviation index carrying 116 entries against 130 marked rows, with four entries pairing one row's text against another row's identifier. A derived structure cannot disagree with the body, and the derivation is re-runnable, so the same review is a check rather than a repair. Dropping the structures would remove the only mechanism by which the Rule 3 subset claim and the deviation count are verifiable by inspection. | The derivation is not executed by any committed script, so the guarantee rests on the next editor re-deriving rather than editing in place. Stated as an instruction in the preamble of each structure. A truncated first sentence loses the qualifying clauses of a long decision, so each entry carries its row identifier and is a pointer rather than a substitute. | Low | — |
| **D-340** | Normalise the `Flags` column to **one** vocabulary: space-separated markers, and a plain em-dash where a row carries none. | Leave the column as authored, with one comma-separated cell and five backticked em-dashes among 343 rows. Or introduce a second column per marker class. | The column's purpose is to make the deviation count and the Rule 3 subset **machine-derivable**; a cell that spells the same thing two ways defeats that, and a naive count of one variant silently under-reports. One vocabulary makes every count in [Summary counts](#summary-counts) reproducible by a single filter. | A marker vocabulary is only useful while it stays closed. A future editor adding a sixth class must extend the summary table in the same edit, which the derivation rule of D-339 makes visible but does not enforce. | Low | — |
| **D-341** | State the count gate as **`hasAnyAppRole()`** in every document that describes it — the portal build guide, the traceability matrix, the access-control document and the API reference — and retire the earlier "table-level readability check" and `GlideRecordSecure.canRead()` phrasings wherever they described the delivered behaviour. Documentation consequence of D-267, which chose the role gate; amends the count sentences that D-041 and D-225 introduced. | Leave the approximate phrasing, since the three roles are exactly who the layer-1 read controls grant. Or change the code to match the older phrasing by probing a secured read. | The two phrasings are not equivalent claims: one names a role test, the other an access-control evaluation on a record. A builder following the older sentence would write `canRead()` on an unpositioned secured object, whose answer is not a table-level right, and would believe a gate was in place. Changing the code instead would reintroduce exactly that defect. | A reader who knows only the role gate may assume the count respects record-level access control, which it does not; the closed-role invariant this rests on is restated at every site, together with what must change if a record-level rule is ever added. | Medium | — |
| **D-342** | Document the refusal of an **unaccountable** request as **two** log lines with distinct tags — `RateLimitService` naming the window key and attempt count, then `RestResponseBuilder.serverError()` carrying the correlation identifier — and state that only the correlation identifier reaches the caller. | Describe one line, as the API reference did. Or suppress the service's own line and log only through the response builder. | A reader triaging a `500` searches the log by tag; a description naming only the response builder's tag hides the line that actually says which window failed and after how many attempts. Suppressing the service's line would lose the window key, which is the only field that identifies the failing accounting row. | Two lines per failure is more log volume under a persistent counter outage, bounded by the fixed retry ceiling of three attempts per request. | Low | — |
| **D-343** | Publish the checklist's flag obligation as **one** item stating **nineteen** flags with their four dispositions, name `OPEN-1` in that item as the page's only non-flag entry, and tick the retention obligations **only** in the acceptance-blocking retention section. | Keep both generations of the item, which is what the file carried — one saying sixteen flags and one saying twelve, with the erasure obligation attributed to `F16` rather than `F19`. Or drop the counts and point at the flag page. | A definition-of-done list that states two different flag counts cannot be ticked honestly, and the misattribution pointed a reviewer at the wrong flag: `F16` is the three columns the live path cannot fill, and `F19` is the retention and erasure gap. Dropping the counts would remove the arithmetic that makes the obligation checkable at all. | The count now appears in both this log and the flag page, so a future flag must be added in both. Both sites state the same four-way disposition split, and the flag page's own roll-up is the authority the checklist cites. | Low | — |
| **D-344** | Report a **terminal** diagram failure in the diagram's own container — a marked container carrying a short notice, a Lucide glyph and the diagram's `accTitle` — as well as on the console, and report the one terminal branch that previously returned silently: a container carrying a processed marker over a degenerate drawing with no attempts left. Extends D-125's render loop. | Leave the console warning as the only report, which is what the deck did. Or fail the whole render pass when one diagram is terminal. Or leave the exhausted-processed branch silent, since the same container is re-inspected on every slide change. | A console warning is invisible to the person presenting: an unrendered diagram read as an empty area on the slide, which is indistinguishable from a slide that was authored without a visual. The silent branch was the one most likely to run, because it is reached by re-visiting a slide whose diagram had already failed. Failing the pass would take fifteen healthy diagrams down with one sick one. | A visible notice is itself a presentation defect if it ever fires in front of an audience, which is the point: it is a failure state, and it should be legible rather than quiet. The notice replaces the container's text, so a diagram that fails cannot then be recovered by anything except a reset, which clears the marker. | Low | — |
| **D-345** | Present the post-commit acceptance decision on the proof slide as **twelve** checks with the **eleven** required gates as the only roll-back branch and the twelfth routed to fix-and-re-issue, rather than as eleven gates leading to acceptance. | Leave the eleven-to-acceptance form, which contradicted the timeline slide's twelve. Or drop the numbers from both slides. | The two slides stated different arithmetic for the same decision, and a reader comparing them cannot tell which is the contract. Twelve is the acceptance decision and eleven is the roll-back decision, and both are needed because the twelfth check blocks acceptance without destroying a correct installation. Dropping the numbers would remove the only quantified statement of what acceptance means. | Three branches out of one node is more to read than two. The branch labels carry the arithmetic so the diagram is legible without the notes, and the accessible description states the same split in words. | Low | — |
| **D-346** | State on the proof slide that the **two package checks** pass on the delivered file today and that the other **seven** pre-delivery checks are review checks recorded in the acceptance list, replacing the claim that all nine pass. | Keep "the nine checks pass now". Or drop the claim entirely and say only that everything needs an instance. | Only two of the nine are established by a shipped executable — the two levels of the Update Set validator — and the delivered package contains no script that establishes referential integrity, scope containment, secret hygiene, field-list fidelity, deliverable completeness, deck structure or governance completeness. A blanket pass claim in an executive deck is the kind of overstatement that is acted on. Dropping the claim would understate a real, re-runnable result. | A reader may take "review checks" for unchecked. The acceptance list is named in the same sentence, and every one of the seven carries its own row there with its evidence. | Low | — |
| **D-347** | Present the acquisition posture per source on the integration slide: Crunchbase as the one source with a **published read contract**, not live until its key is supplied; LinkedIn as publishing **no read interface** for people or third-party job postings, its call confirming company identity only; the fallback dataset as the emphasised path carrying **every row today**; and a closing statement that nothing in this delivery is labelled `live validated` yet. | Keep both sources on equal footing feeding the flows, which is what the diagram showed. Or remove LinkedIn from the diagram, which would hide a delivered flow. Or omit the fallback emphasis. | The earlier diagram implied two live feeds, and a reader would reasonably conclude that founder, executive and job-posting rows arrive from LinkedIn. They do not and cannot: the flow, the alias and the actions are built and the rows come from the sanctioned fallback dataset, per `F15` and `OPEN-1`. Removing LinkedIn would hide the same fact in the other direction. | Four labelled edges on one slide is dense for a leadership audience. The labels are three words each, the accessible description carries the full sentence, and the footnote states the labelling rule that keeps the two modes distinguishable. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-348** | Disclose the **outstanding dependency-advisory signature** on the risk slide of the executive deck, rather than leaving it visible only in the gaps document, the acceptance list and the sign-off table. Extends D-173's signable record. | Leave the deck silent, since the record and the two gates already carry it. Or add a sixth row to the five-reviewer risk table. | The four fields of that record are a **human** act, and the audience of this deck is the audience that supplies them; an open item that only appears in a specification is an open item nobody is asked to close. The five-row table is the five reviewer personas of the review document, so a sixth row would break that correspondence — the footnote is the right surface. | A footnote is easy to skip. It sits beside the two other open items the same slide already discloses, so the three read as one list rather than as an aside. | Low | — |
| **D-349** | Stash and restore each diagram container's source as **markup** — `innerHTML` rather than `textContent` — so a label carrying an authored `<br/>` survives the render loop's reset path. Amends D-126, which specified the stash. | Keep the `textContent` stash and drop the authored line breaks, letting the library wrap each label where it chooses. Or keep the stash and the breaks, and accept that a repaired container re-renders mangled. | The container is parsed as HTML, so an authored `<br/>` becomes a real `br` element: `textContent` drops it and joins the two halves of the label into one word, which is what a repaired container would then draw — `Crunchbaseread contract published`. Automatic wrapping is the alternative that was measured and rejected: it broke three labels mid-phrase and left three others as run-ons, and it produced a wider drawing whose labels rendered at 11.4 pixels rather than 12.3. | `innerHTML` restores markup rather than text, so the stash must never carry anything but the authored diagram source. It is taken once, before any render, from a container the delivery authors and no user or upstream value ever reaches, and the library sanitises what it parses at the strict security level the same rendering path already sets. | Low | — |

## 18. Remediation of the security review

Decisions taken while resolving the findings of the **final security review**, which returned `NOT APPROVED` against the delivered artifacts. This round is recorded as its own section on the reasoning of `D-309`: the questions it answers are not section 16's. Section 16 mostly widened what the delivery exposes and asserts; this round mostly **closes** routes, **narrows** what leaves the boundary, and **refuses** where the previous build proceeded on an assumption.

Rows are added as each finding is resolved, so the section grows through the round and its identifiers run from `D-350` upwards. Where a row of an earlier section no longer describes the delivery, that row is corrected in place with a sentence naming what changed, rather than being deleted — also per `D-309`.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-350** | Assert the **authorization** half of the REST contract, not only the field-level read half: add to the `BST REST — startups` test an unauthenticated `GET` expecting `401`, a base-role `POST` expecting `403` with the delivered message and **zero** records written, an administrator `POST` expecting `201` carrying **both** premium keys, and one `DELETE` of a non-existent identifier issued from both callers, expecting `403` to the base role and `404` to the administrator. Mint a second ephemeral identity holding exactly `x_bst_startuptrk.admin` for the administrator half. | Leave write authorization to the ACL records and the 21-cell field cross-product, on the reasoning that the write ACLs are declarative and therefore not worth asserting. Or assert the `403` only, without the paired `201`. Or reuse the existing base-role identity and grant it the administrator role for part of the test. | The delivered suite exercised **reads only**, so three properties of the delivered code were asserted nowhere: that an unauthenticated caller is refused before the rate limiter counts the request, that a base-role caller cannot mutate, and that the omission of a premium key is a **decision about the caller** rather than a property of the serialiser. The last of these is the reason the administrator `201` is not optional — the same `serialize()` call that omits `total_funding_usd` for the base role must be shown to emit it for an administrator, or "omitted, not nulled" rests on a single-sided observation. The identical `DELETE` answering `403` to one caller and `404` to another is the cheapest possible proof that the guard precedes the lookup, and it needs no record to exist. A second identity rather than a re-granted first one keeps each caller's role set constant for the whole test, so a failure cannot be explained by when the grant landed. | The five claims are asserted against `/startups` only; `PUT` and the read-denial branch on the other five resources remain covered by no test, which is recorded as a coverage gap in the traceability matrix rather than left to be discovered. The window keys the counted request creates are written by inbound HTTP in their own session, so the framework does not roll them back and the teardown must delete them by key. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-351** | **Refuse to parse at all** unless the runtime Expat carries the disproportionate-allocation defence, evaluated **before any path is opened**, and provide **no option to override it**. Two routes satisfy the gate: a reported version at or above `2.7.2`, or a version at `2.7.1` whose loaded library exposes both `XML_SetAllocTrackerActivationThreshold` and `XML_SetAllocTrackerMaximumAmplification`. Report the refusal as exit code `2`, and pin the decision with twelve fixtures behind the existing `--self-test` flag. Supersedes in part `D-153`, whose third clause only **reported** the parser version. | Test the version alone and require `2.7.2` or later, which is what the finding's suggested resolution says. Or keep reporting the version and add a warning line. Or add an `--allow-unpatched-parser` escape hatch for constrained environments. Or vendor a hardened third-party parser, which `D-066` had already ruled out on installation grounds. | A version test alone is **wrong in the direction that matters**: the review runtime reports `expat_2.7.1` yet resolves both tracker entry points, because the distribution backported the fix without moving the version string — so a literal `>= 2.7.2` gate would refuse a **correctly patched** runtime and the first operator to meet it would be taught to work around the gate. Probing for the entry points tests for the mitigation rather than for a number. A warning was rejected because the exposure it warns about is realised *during* the parse it precedes: the validator's own byte, payload and record ceilings do not bound allocation amplification, so a document far below all three can exhaust memory before any verdict exists. An override flag was rejected for the same reason — it would reintroduce precisely the exposure the gate closes, and an override that exists is an override that gets used. Exit `2` rather than `1` because a refusal is a statement about the runtime and not about the file; classing it as a validation failure would let a green re-run on a patched runtime read as a document that had been corrected. The `2.7.1` floor on the backport route is deliberate: the entry points appearing on an older base is refused, because the probe establishes the presence of that one defence and not of the others the `2.7.1` series carries. | The gate is a proxy: two resolvable symbols are strong evidence of the defence but not proof that it is wired into the parser the interpreter actually uses, and a build could in principle expose them without honouring them. It is also fail-closed on a runtime whose library cannot be probed at all — no `ctypes`, or a platform that will not open the process image — which refuses a runtime that may well be patched; `--self-test` is the escape from the resulting confusion, because it parses nothing, therefore runs anywhere, and reports the gate's verdict on the current runtime. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-352** | Close the **cross-scope read route** on the seven entity tables: `access` `package_private`, `read_access` `false`, with create, update and delete already false. Supersedes `D-254`. Re-express the seven post-commit table gates as reads of `sys_db_object` and `sys_dictionary` **metadata** plus a Scripted REST read, rather than as native Table API reads of the tables themselves. | Keep the tables `public` and rely on the field-level access controls, as `D-254` did. Or keep them `public` and add record-level rules. Or close them and drop the seven post-commit gates as unrunnable. | `access` `public` means a `GlideRecord` query executed from **another scope** reads the table directly, and that path evaluates neither the role grants nor the seven field-level read rules — so every premium field was readable by any code able to run in a different scope, and the whole four-layer scheme was optional rather than enforced. `package_private` removes the route instead of guarding it, which is the only form of that fix that cannot be forgotten at a call site. The gates had to move with it: a gate that asserts a **successful table read** cannot survive closing the table, so it is replaced by one that asserts the **closed posture itself** — which is strictly better evidence, because it fails if the posture ever regresses. | Anything outside the scope that legitimately needed a direct read now cannot have one; within this delivery nothing does, and the Scripted REST surface is the sanctioned route. A future scoped application wanting to consume this data must be granted access explicitly, which is the intended consequence rather than a side effect. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-353** | Set `ws_access` `false` on the same seven tables, removing the **native Table API** as a second read route. | Leave `ws_access` `true` and rate-limit the Table API separately. Or leave it `true` and document that consumers must not use it. | With `ws_access` `true` the platform's own Table API served the same records **outside** the Scripted REST layer, which means outside the inclusion filter, outside the per-field omission gate and outside the rate-limit counter. Two read routes with different rules is not a convenience, it is a contradiction: the criterion-3 pagination and `429` evidence would have been true of one route and false of the other, and the inclusion filter that criterion 5 signs off would have been trivially bypassable. Documenting a prohibition was rejected because an unenforced prohibition is indistinguishable from none. | Any consumer written against the Table API stops working; none exists in this delivery. Diagnostic reads by an administrator now go through the platform UI or the Scripted REST surface. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-354** | Validate the **origin** of each provider base URL before a credential is attached: `AppProperties.providerBaseUrlState(sourceSystem)` returns an allow decision with a reason, checking scheme, userinfo, host against an allowlist, port and path prefix, and both flow guides require it to pass **before** the alias binds. | Trust the property, as the delivered code did. Or validate the URL's syntax only. Or hard-code the two endpoints and drop the properties, which the plan's eleven-property inventory forbids. | `getCrunchbaseBaseUrl()` and `getLinkedinBaseUrl()` returned whatever the property held, and that value selected the host a **credential** was then presented to. Anyone able to write a scoped property could redirect an authenticated call and harvest the credential, and the failure would read as a provider outage rather than as an exfiltration. Syntax validation alone does not help, because a syntactically perfect URL to another host is exactly the attack. Validating the origin turns the property into a switch between **approved** endpoints rather than an arbitrary destination. | The allowlist is a maintained list: a legitimate provider host change needs the allowlist updated as well as the property, which is one more step and is deliberate. The nine reason codes are a closed set and are reported instead of the offending value. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-355** | Read a participating-investor name list from **three carriers** — a real array, JSON array text, and pipe-delimited text — falling back to treating the whole value as a single name, and **stop splitting on commas**. Make the live path carry a JSON array rather than a comma-joined string. | Keep comma splitting. Or require one carrier and reject the others. Or split on commas but escape embedded ones. | Comma splitting silently corrupts the data it is meant to parse: `Bain Capital Ventures, LP` becomes two investors, and the published contract in `sample-data/README.md` and `data-model.md` already said JSON array or pipe-delimited, so the code and its own documentation disagreed. Escaping was rejected because it invents a carrier no provider emits. Accepting the three carriers the pipeline actually produces — and one unambiguous fallback — makes every documented input parse, and makes a name containing a comma survive. | A single name that itself looks like JSON array text would be parsed as a list; no provider emits such a name and the fallback ordering makes the behaviour deterministic and stated. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-356** | Make the ingestion **run lease per source** and **merge inside the write**: `acquireRunLease`, `completeRun` and `releaseRunLease` read the marker set, apply one source's change and write back within the same call, then **read back** to confirm; and have `completeRun` **refuse** a run that does not hold the source. Supersedes the shape and the accepted loss of `D-156`. | Keep the single shared property written from a snapshot taken earlier, as delivered. Or add a twelfth property or a lease table, either of which breaks a plan-mandated inventory. | Both flows wrote one property from a snapshot they had taken before doing their work, so whichever finished second **erased the other's marker** — and the erasure was silent, leaving the cadence guard treating the erased source as never having run. `completeRun` also accepted any run identifier, so a stale or duplicated execution could close a run it did not own and stamp a provenance that was not its own. Merging inside the write closes the window without a new record, and the read-back turns a failed write into a reported error rather than an assumed success. Refusing an unowned completion is what makes the provenance marker evidence rather than an assertion. | The merge is still not a database transaction, so two writes in the same instant can conflict; the read-back detects it and the caller reports `run_completion_refused` rather than proceeding. | **D-357** | Give both flow guides **one row-ownership model end to end** — rows are claimed and read in the state the mapper actually queries — align the A4 step's declared outputs with `IngestionMapper.ingest`'s six actual members, and **remove the prescriptive flow-start script** from the ATF guide, since no test starts a flow. Supersedes `D-247` and the runner half of `D-291`. | Keep the guides' `in_progress` claim and change the mapper to query it. Or keep the flow-start script and mark it best-effort. Or leave the two constructions coexisting and note the discrepancy. | The guides claimed each row `in_progress` and then called `ingestStaging()`, which queries `import_state='pending'` — so the claimed rows were **exactly the rows the mapper would not process**, and a run would report success having ingested nothing. The A4 step also published outputs the mapper does not return, so downstream steps read `undefined`. On the test side the guide asserted in one place that both flow tests start the published flow and, twenty lines away, that no ATF step configuration can — and the assertions that depended on starting one read flow-execution artifacts the framework's rollback removes, so they would have failed. Changing the mapper was rejected: the mapper is the shared component and the guides are the callers, so the caller yields. | Half A no longer exercises the flow's own trigger, guard or REST step; where that evidence lives instead is stated explicitly in the guide rather than implied, and the operator's manual runs are what produce it. | High | `DEV` |

Or persist the message but redact patterns from it. | A provider or platform exception message is **attacker-influenceable and content-bearing**: it can carry a request URL with a query-string key, a row's field values, or an internal path, and the `_failure()` this decision replaced sent it straight into `staging.error_message` and the application log, where it persists at a lower access bar than the data it describes. Truncation and pattern redaction both keep an open-ended string and rely on the redactor anticipating every shape; a closed code set has nothing to anticipate. Or keep the copies and add a test that they match. | The five copies each removed a **keyword** and left the secret's value behind — `Authorization: Bearer eyJ...` lost `Bearer` and kept the token — so the redaction was cosmetic in all five places at once. | **D-360** | Submit the deployment password on **stdin only**, never in `argv`, and report its presence as a **boolean** rather than as a character count — at all three sites — and state at step 1 that the form given there is the **only** login form in the runbook. | Keep the length print, on the reasoning that a length is not a value. Two descriptions of one binding is not flexibility — only one of them is what the code does. Or drop the count from the documents and cite only the validator. | Three different counts were in circulation — `305`, `314` and `326` — across five documents, and two of them fed **acceptance gates**: `PRE-COMMIT-01` and `PRE-COMMIT-02` compare an observed count against a cited one, so a wrong citation makes a complete import look incomplete or an incomplete import look complete. It has been re-derived and now agrees with the validator, and it is named here as a citation site so the next re-derivation does not miss it again. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-366** | Add a blocking rollback step — **A6** — that snapshots, deletes by `sys_id` and then proves **zero** remaining `sys_user_has_role` rows for the three application roles, and **blocks re-deployment** until it does. | Leave the rollback at scope deletion, as delivered. Or delete the grants by query rather than by `sys_id`. Or note the dangling grants as a known residue. | Deleting the scope removes the three `sys_user_role` records but **not** the `sys_user_has_role` rows pointing at them — and the three role `sys_id` values are **deterministic**, fixed in the Update Set. So a re-deployment recreates the roles with identical identifiers and every surviving grant **silently re-attaches**, handing back access that the rollback was performed to remove. That is the worst available failure mode: the rollback appears to have succeeded and the privilege returns without any action that looks like a grant. Deleting by query was rejected because a query re-evaluated at delete time can match rows the snapshot never showed; deleting by `sys_id` deletes exactly what was seen. The snapshot pages past 1000 rows deliberately, because a truncated snapshot omits precisely the rows nobody will look for. | Three extra sub-steps and one more blocking condition in an already long branch. The step reads and writes `sys_user_has_role`, which is outside the scope — it is an operator action in the runbook and correctly absent from the Update Set. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-367** | State **one** outbound-call retry policy identically in all five places that describe one: bounded single attempt, no in-call retry, with the fallback path as the recovery. | Leave the retry promised in one document and absent from the others. Or implement in-call retries to match the promise. | Three documents promised a retry that the flows do not perform, and two described a single attempt — so an operator reading the wrong one would attribute a single-attempt failure to an exhausted retry budget and look for a provider fault rather than reaching for the fallback. Implementing retries was rejected: the flows already have a defined recovery in the fallback dataset, and an unbounded or hidden retry against a rate-limited provider is a worse failure than a clean single attempt. Stating it identically in five places is what makes the behaviour readable wherever an operator happens to look. | A transient provider blip now sends a run to the fallback path rather than retrying through it. That is the intended behaviour and it is labelled `fallback` in the run provenance, so it is visible rather than silent. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-368** | Regenerate every mechanism description in `access-control.md` and `api-reference.md` **from the delivered artifacts** — the Script Include and property inventory, the rate-limit accounting mechanism, the provenance marker format and the Script Include call graph — rather than leaving the designs those documents described. | Correct the specific figures the review named. Or annotate the documents as approximate. | The documents described a **rejected** design as though it were delivered: an aggregate `canRead` scheme that does not exist, nine Script Includes and sixteen properties where the Update Set carries eight and eleven, a JSON provenance object written by a method that writes no property, and an accounting mechanism keyed on a triple the delivered table does not key on. A reader implementing against them would have built against nothing. Correcting only the named figures was rejected because the figures were symptoms of documents drifting from the artifact; regenerating from the artifact and then **diffing programmatically** — every documented method against every delivered public method, reporting zero missing — is what makes the description checkable rather than merely corrected. | The documents must be regenerated whenever the service layer changes; the diff is the mechanism that detects the drift, and it is cheap to re-run. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-369** | Own the **`window_key`** rather than the `caller` + `api_resource` + `window_start` triple, seed **both** keys a single test can fall into, and **remove** the boundary wait rather than sleeping through it. Supersedes `D-038` and `D-106`. | Keep owning the triple and keep the `gs.sleep()` clock guard. Or seed one key and accept a boundary flake. | The delivered table keys on a mandatory unique `window_key`, so a test that deletes and asserts over the triple neither cleans nor proves anything about the row the limiter will actually read — and the guide simultaneously published a superseded construction that contradicted the delivered one. A test that spans a window boundary lands in the next bucket, which the old form handled by sleeping; seeding both candidate keys removes the race entirely, and a removed race needs no wait. Seeding one key and accepting a flake was rejected because an intermittent `429` assertion is indistinguishable from a broken limiter. | Teardown must delete both keys for both callers. The keys are written by inbound HTTP in their own sessions, so the framework does not roll them back and the deletion is explicit. A prohibition on reinstating `gs.sleep()` is stated in the checklist so the wait is not reintroduced as a fix for something else. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-370** | Gate the diagnostic that names an unreadable reference's `sys_id` behind the **configured logging level**. | Leave it unconditional, as delivered. Or remove the line. | The line wrote a record identifier to the application log on **every** request that dot-walked a reference the caller may not read, regardless of the configured level — so a property set to `warn` did not suppress it, and the log accumulated identifiers of records the reader of the log might have no grant for. Removing it was rejected: the line is genuinely useful when a reference is misconfigured. Gating it puts it under the same control as every other diagnostic in the scope, which is what the property exists for. | The diagnostic is absent at the default level, so an operator investigating a dot-walk failure must raise the level first. Stated at the call site as what the line does, with the reasoning here. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-371** | Re-query the pinned presentation library's advisory set, publish it as **two tables** — in range and not in range — with a per-advisory reachability basis, promote the re-query from an evidence suggestion to a **required field** of the disposition record, publish a **dated re-query procedure**, and give the advisory-scope heading a **dateless anchor**. Extends `D-173`. | Keep the six-record snapshot and the four required fields. Or date the heading, so each re-query renames the section. Or re-pin the library, which a project rule forbids. | The snapshot had aged into two open cells reading `Re-query at acceptance` and one identifier, `CVE-2026-50159`, that **could not be substantiated at all** — and an unsubstantiated identifier in a security record is worse than none, because it can never be re-queried or closed and it makes the rest of the table look equally unverifiable. Both open cells are now answered and one of them **reverses** what the snapshot implied. Making the re-query a required field is what stops a stale snapshot being signed behind a fresh signature; publishing the procedure is what stops the field being improvised. The heading is deliberately dateless because an anchor carrying a date breaks every inbound link on the next re-query — the date belongs in the body and in the record. | The disposition stays `OPEN` until five human fields carry values, and the delivery cannot close it on its own behalf — which is the intent. | **D-372** | Record the flag inventory's growth from **twelve to nineteen** as a decision in its own right, and reconcile every count, disposition total and identifier range that cites it. | Let the inventory grow silently, since each individual flag is already documented. Or renumber the flags so the additions are not visibly late. | A flag inventory is a **claim about completeness**, so the number is load-bearing: `D-088` records the growth from ten to twelve and nothing recorded twelve to nineteen, which left seven flags present in the detail sections and absent from four summary statements, two disposition totals and one definition-of-done item that still said twelve. Renumbering was rejected — the identifiers are cited from other documents and from the checklist, and a late addition should read as late. The reconciliation is the substance of this row: nineteen flags, `F1` through `F19`, five plus eight plus three plus three, with `OPEN-1` counted separately under its own vocabulary. | Every future flag must update the same set of summary statements; the totals are stated adjacent to the table so a mismatch is visible rather than distributed. | Medium | `DEV` |

---

## 19. Remediation of the ingestion, rate-limit and test-model review

Decisions `D-373` onward. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised when the delivered artifacts were reviewed against the **frozen** Agent Action Plan. This round's direction of travel is narrower than section 16's. Rows `D-373` through `D-376` and `D-381` close a gap between a **delivered mechanism** and the **document or test that describes it**, where the two had come apart and the document was the one that was wrong. Rows `D-377` through `D-380` and `D-382` through `D-384` close a gap between a **delivered artifact** and the **obligation or rule that governs it**, where the artifact was the one that was wrong. Row `D-385` closes a third kind of gap, inside one document: between a **declared vocabulary** and the **rows that use it**. Per `D-309` this round opens its own section rather than extending section 16, and every superseded row is corrected in place with a sentence naming what no longer describes the delivery.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-373** | Carry a funding round's participating investors on **three explicit carriers** — a real array, a JSON array serialised as text, or a `\|`-delimited string — with a single bare name accepted as a one-member list, and **never** treat a comma as a member delimiter at any stage of the ingestion pipeline. Resolve the carrier once, in `IngestionMapper._flattenByKind()`, so `coerceField()` and `_pickPresent()` cannot re-flatten a list into a comma-joined scalar in transit. Amends D-270. | Keep the comma delimiter and re-author the shipped CSV fixture to quote member names, which is what the delimiter the mapper implemented required. Or accept both delimiters, splitting on a comma only when no JSON array and no pipe is present. Or carry participants in a side table keyed by the staging row rather than in a column at all. | An investor's legal name routinely contains a comma — `Harbor Ventures, LP` is the ordinary form of the name, not an edge case — so a comma delimiter cannot express the data the pipeline is required to carry, and every such name silently became two members neither of which resolved. Re-authoring the fixture would have preserved a delimiter that is wrong for the domain, and dual delimiters make the failure conditional on the data rather than removing it. The single resolution point matters as much as the parser: the defect that suppressed all 26 join rows was **not** in the parser but in `coerceField()`, which reached the value first and comma-joined the array through JavaScript's default array-to-string conversion before the parser ever saw it. A pipe cannot appear in a company name, and the JSON carrier covers the case where it somehow does. | A payload that genuinely intended two members separated by a comma and no other delimiter now arrives as one member, and normalises to a single unresolved name rather than to two. That outcome is reported per row rather than silently accepted. **Superseded in part by D-453:** the canonical in-transit form is the **JSON array**, not the pipe carrier, and there is no carrier selector — `_flattenByKind()` routes a `namelist` to `_flattenNameList()`, which always serialises to JSON, so no member can force a form and the pipe carrier is an accepted input rather than an emitted one. A caller composing a carrier by hand can still get it wrong, which is the risk that stands. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-374** | **Amended by `D-387`: both boundaries are addressed by the four-column tuple rather than by a key column. The two-boundary rule and the never-read-the-clock rule below stand.** Make the deterministic `429` assertion address **two window boundaries** — the current and the next, both set to the budget, with the previous also swept and read — and **never** read the clock: no remaining-time measurement and no `gs.sleep()`. Amends D-106. | Address only the bucket open when the seeding step runs, which is what "pre-seed a counter row" reads as. Or measure the time left in the window and sleep past the boundary when it is near, which an earlier build did. Or shorten the window property for the duration of the test. | The counter is a **fixed** window, so the boundary can roll between the step that seeds the row and the request that is supposed to trip on it, and the request then lands in an empty bucket and returns `200`. Sleeping does not close that race, it narrows it: the check-then-sleep-then-seed sequence reads the clock three times and any of the three can straddle a boundary, so the window the row is written in and the window the request lands in are still only probably the same. It also blocks the whole transaction — up to the full guard interval by design, with the ATF runner holding the transaction open throughout — which is a reliability hazard on a shared instance and makes the suite's duration depend on when it happened to start. Setting both candidate boundaries removes the race rather than narrowing it: whichever bucket the request resolves to is already full, with no clock reading to get wrong. Shortening the window property was rejected because it mutates shared configuration the portal and the API both read. | Two rows are written per run instead of one, and both must be swept; step 170 asserts one window at budget + 1 and the other still at the budget, which is what makes the pair observable rather than merely tolerated. The assertion no longer proves which boundary the request resolved to, only that it resolved to one of the two that were full. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-375** | **Amended by `D-404`, which fixes the accessor this teardown reads as `getRunProvenance(source)`.** Make suite 10's teardown assert a **defined, recorded state** rather than restore a previous one: release any `running` claim, require this source's entry to be absent or `succeeded`, require the **other** source's entry to be byte-identical to the value the setup step captured, and write the resulting value into the step output. Amends D-219. | Capture the marker before the first write and restore it in a teardown step, as `D-219` prescribed. Or restore it out of band after the suite. Or leave the marker as the suite left it and say nothing. | A restore is not expressible here, because the thing being "restored" is the thing under test: the passing branch **runs the flow**, and a healthy execution legitimately stamps a new `succeeded` entry through `markRunComplete()`. Promising to put back what the suite's own purpose is to overwrite would be a promise that cannot be kept, and a teardown that runs only on the happy path is not a teardown. What can be guaranteed is that the marker is left in a state an operator can reason about, and that the *other* source's cadence is untouched — which is the only cross-contamination the single property makes possible. | The operator must read the recorded value rather than assume the previous one survived, and must keep the suite outside a criterion-4 evidence window — `W10` and precondition 15. A suite that aborts before step 160 can leave a `running` claim; the next run's step 40 writes this source's entry unconditionally, so the claim does not persist beyond one run, and the residue query names it. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-376** | Assert the cadence guard's **refusal** as a step of its own, on four independent facts — no staging row claimed, no entity row written, the marker unmoved, and nothing published — with the entity probe **bounded by the stamp the start step wrote**. | Assert only that the refused execution reached a non-error state, which is what the start step already does. Or assert only that no entity row exists, unbounded over the table. Or drive the guard once, on the passing branch only. | A refusal is defined by what did *not* happen, so asserting the execution "completed" says nothing: a guard wired to the wrong property, or placed after the batch step, also completes. Each of the four facts fails a different build defect, and the marker fact matters most because a guard that stamps on the refusal path corrupts the state the passing branch depends on. The probe is bounded by the start step's own stamp because an unbounded count would report guide 06's rows on a populated instance and fail for a reason that is not a defect. | The bound is a creation-time comparison, so a row created by another process in the same second would be attributed to the refused execution. Accepted: the suite requires precondition 13, no concurrent operator. | Low | — |
| **D-377** | Transfer the staging retention, minimisation and data-subject erasure obligation to a **named human owner with an acceptance-blocking gate** — obligation `OB-1`, carrying an owner role, a schedule, a retention period and four evidence fields — rather than delivering a retention Script Include, scheduled job and property. Amends D-265 and the disposition of flag F19. | Deliver the mechanism: a ninth Script Include, a second scheduled job and a retention property, which an earlier build had and `D-265` removed. Or leave F19 as *flagged, not implemented* with no owner and no evidence, as delivered. Or narrow the staging columns so no personal value is ever staged. | The plan freezes the artifact inventory at eight Script Includes, eleven properties and one scheduled executable, and `D-265` established that code aligns to the frozen inventory rather than the reverse — so the mechanism cannot be built without breaching it. But a flag with no owner, no schedule and no evidence is indistinguishable from an oversight when read a year later, and personal data persisting indefinitely is not a documentation problem. Transferring the obligation keeps the inventory intact **and** puts a name against the data. Narrowing the columns was rejected because prompt section 4.0 requires the staging shape to mimic the expected API response, which is precisely what makes the payload personal. | The control is only as good as the operator who performs it, and a missed month leaves data in place with no automated backstop. Mitigated by making it acceptance-blocking rather than advisory: the delivery is not accepted while the evidence fields are blank. An erasure remains complete only for data present when it runs, because nothing suppresses a later run from recreating the subject — stated in the obligation and in F19 rather than left for a reader to infer. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-378** | Make the flow-execution retention and read-access confirmation **acceptance-blocking** — obligation `OB-2` — rather than recording it as flagged and unmet, and state the minimisation this application does deliver alongside it. | Leave the four platform-owner actions recorded with an unmet status, as delivered. Or attempt to suppress the two step outputs that carry upstream content. Or accept the exposure and stop describing it. | The four actions are on platform tables outside the application scope, which prompt section 6.0 forbids this application from modifying, so they cannot be delivered — but "unmet, and outside our scope" reads as permission to ship without them. Making it blocking forces the platform owner's answer to exist before acceptance. Suppressing the outputs is not available: step 4 parses `response_body` and step 5 cleans `rows`, so removing either breaks the pipeline; the minimisation that *is* available — the flow's own logging level at its least verbose setting that still records step outcomes, set before activation — is already required by both flow guides, and saying so is what makes the boundary auditable rather than convenient. | The obligation can be discharged with a poor answer: a platform owner may record a long retention window and a wide read set and still tick the box. The evidence fields require the observed values and the observer, so a poor answer is at least a recorded one. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-379** | **Amended by `D-405`, which returns this obligation to the recommended defence-in-depth posture AAP section 0.8.3 sets: acceptance requires only that the decision to adopt or decline it be recorded, and no gate or checklist treats it as blocking. The reasoning below for why the rule is worth adopting stands; its acceptance status does not.** Move the platform **inbound rate-limit rule** on the seven Table API routes from *recommended* to **required and gated** — obligation `OB-3` — with the observed `429` as part of its evidence. Or accept the gap on the grounds that the application's own endpoints are limited. | Of the three residual risks on the Table API read route, two have delivered compensating controls and this one had none: a caller holding a scoped role can read the seven entity tables on a route the application's fixed-window counter never sees. Closing the route is not available — `ws_access` false makes the eleven required post-commit gates unexecutable, and that gate set is fixed. Requiring the rule is therefore the only compensating control available, and requiring an **observed** `429` rather than the rule's mere existence is what distinguishes a control from a record. Setting the platform threshold at or above the application budget matters: a lower one would refuse requests the application's own API would have served. | The rule is instance configuration, so it survives independently of the Update Set and can be removed without any application-side signal. | **D-380** | Bring the closing slide inside its own two caps by **rewording** the takeaway heading to five words and **merging** the two governance bullets into one, keeping all four claims the slide made, and **gate both caps** in `G-8` and in the Rule 2 sign-off so they are measured rather than trusted. | Drop the fourth bullet outright, which is the shortest edit. Or split the closing into two slides. Or delete only the word `instance` from the heading. Splitting is not available, because a deck has one closing slide by type and a second would take the section count to seventeen with nothing new to say. Deleting only `instance` leaves six words, at the cap with no margin, and loses the word that names **where** the pending validation happens — the phrasing kept is five words and keeps that word. The gate is the actual root cause: `G-8` measured the section count, the visuals, the type census, the content-slide caps, the emoji scan, the pins and the asset digests, but never the closing slide's own two caps, while the Rule 2 sign-off promised that the slide *observes its own constraint* without ever naming what that constraint is. An unnamed constraint is not a gate, which is why both breaches survived to review. | The merged bullet is a three-clause line and is less scannable than the two short lines it replaces. The heading's `Fully specified` reads as a completeness claim about the specification, which the flags and the acceptance-blocking obligations qualify on other slides rather than on this one. Five words leaves one word of margin, so a later reword can breach the cap again — mitigated by the gate now stating both the caps and the delivered figures to reproduce. | Low | — |
| **D-381** | Correct the two deck gates that measure the render mechanics so each measures what the deck actually does: state that the legitimate Mermaid count on **first load is 0 of 5**, name the **presence gate** that produces it and the slide at which the count must instead be taken, record **three** hook registrations rather than two, and select unreplaced icon placeholders as **`i[data-lucide]`** rather than `[data-lucide]`. Or strip `data-lucide` from each generated `svg` after the icon pass so the bare selector reaches zero. Or leave both gates as written and let each reviewer work out the discrepancy. | The presence gate is not an oversight: an off-screen slide gives its container a zero-width layout box, a render into that box produces a collapsed drawing, and the library reports that render as successful — which `D-129` and `D-228` were taken to prevent. Three of the five containers measure 0 by 0 under `display: none`, so relaxing the gate reintroduces exactly the failure those rows removed. Stripping the attribute fights the library for the benefit of one query and breaks the deck's own health check, which uses the attribute to identify what it rendered. What was actually wrong was the pair of gates: one asked for *the rendered diagram count on first load* without saying that **0** is the correct answer, and the other named the attribute rather than the element, so a reviewer reproducing either mechanically records a failure against a correct deck. Both were reproduced in a browser during this remediation and both produced that false failure. | A gate that states its expected value can be satisfied by reading the value instead of measuring it; both items therefore require the count at the diagram's own slide **and** after a full forward-and-back walk, neither of which can be produced without navigating. | **D-382** | Admit **exactly five origins** in the presentation's content-security policy, exempt the one asset whose bytes are User-Agent-dependent from integrity pinning rather than dropping either the asset or the policy, and name `cdn.jsdelivr.net` in `font-src` and `connect-src` as well as `script-src`. Extends D-132 and D-253. | Omit the policy and rely on the integrity pins alone. Or pin the font stylesheet by hash anyway. Or self-host the fonts so the origin can be dropped. Or issue a nonce instead of permitting `'unsafe-inline'`. | An integrity pin fixes the bytes of the assets it covers and says nothing about what else the document may load, so the two controls answer different questions and the policy is what closes the second. The font stylesheet cannot be hash-pinned: Google Fonts serves different bytes per User-Agent, and a fixed digest blocks the stylesheet on whichever browsers it was not computed against — its exposure is stylesheet-only, because `style-src` cannot introduce script. Self-hosting is unavailable: the presentation rule forbids local file dependencies. A nonce cannot be issued without a server, and the rule requires a single self-contained document with no build step, so `'unsafe-inline'` is the only way the inline theme, the inline render driver and Mermaid's own generated style attributes execute at all. The two extra directives are not a widening: `font-src` covers the eight font binaries reveal's own theme stylesheet requests through a stylesheet it imports, and `connect-src` covers the `sourceMappingURL` both bundles carry — a tighter value produces an enforced violation on every load, and both origins are already trusted for script. | `'unsafe-inline'` means the policy does not defend against injected inline script, so the defence against a substituted asset rests on the four integrity pins. The font stylesheet is the one asset that could be substituted without failing to load; its reach is limited to CSS. Verified in a browser: zero policy violations and eleven of eleven requests served across a cache-bypassing load. | Medium | — |
| **D-383** | Correct the two presentation defects Mermaid's own timeline stylesheet causes by **overriding in the deck's layout block** — remove the `brightness(120%)` filter from every event card with `!important`, and give the timeline container a shorter height cap and a tighter vertical box than the other diagram classes. Extends D-134 and D-136. | Re-declare the `cScale` values pre-divided by 1.2 so the filter lands on the brand value. Or drop the timeline diagram for a flowchart the library does not filter. Or leave the cards bright and the fourth bullet below the fold. | Pre-dividing was measured and rejected: the filter multiplies each channel and then clamps, so the mint token `rgb(178,255,255)` saturates two channels and no pre-divided input recovers the hue — the three painted values measured `rgb(109,68,255)`, `rgb(54,34,143)` and `rgb(178,255,255)` against brand tokens they are set from. `!important` is not stylistic here: the library scopes its rule to the generated `svg` id, so the rule carries an id in its specificity and is regenerated on every render, leaving no stable author selector able to outrank it. The height cap is the same arithmetic: the timeline is the one diagram whose intrinsic aspect is near square, so it reaches its height cap rather than its width, and the slide carries four bullets beneath it on a stage that does not scroll — without the shorter cap the fourth bullet renders below the fold. Dropping the diagram would cost the deck its only ordered view of the migration. | An `!important` override is invisible to a later author reading only the library's stylesheet, and it survives a library upgrade that removes the filter, at which point it becomes inert rather than wrong. The height cap is tuned to this slide's bullet count; adding a fifth bullet would need it re-measured. Both are verified by measurement rather than by eye: the rendered svg is 986.8 by 500.0 at the cap, and the four bullets sit above the fold. | Low | — |
| **D-384** | Extend the no-rationale sweep from the three bodies of **code** to the whole delivered package — the **HTML comments** of the presentation and every **prose surface** of the seventeen documents, including a table's column **heading** and a paragraph's bold **lead-in** — and hold the boundary at one test: a passage may state what an artifact does, what constrains it and which requirement or decision governs it, and may not state why that choice was made, what else was considered, or what risk it carries. Amends D-151 and D-308. | Keep the sweep on the three code bodies, as `D-151` scoped it. Or read the rule as covering only sections headed *Why*, which is the narrowest reading. Or move every contrastive clause out of every artifact, including the behavioural ones. | `D-151` reasoned that exempting the presentation because it is also a document would make the rule turn on a file extension — and its own scope then did exactly that one level down, naming the presentation's inline script and its two style blocks and **not** its HTML comments. The gate item was the broader of the two, listing HTML comments in its deck sub-bullet, and the narrower decision is the one the sweep followed. That is where the largest single concentration of weighed alternatives in the delivery was found: the supply-chain block above the security policy, arguing two exemptions and two directive widenings. The documents were outside both scopes — the check reached the Update Set's scripts, descriptions and hints and the six manual-build guides, and none of the eleven other prose documents — and they needed the widening for a different reason: a column headed *Why* and a lead-in reading **Why X rather than Y** announce rationale in the heading, so the passage is rationale however factual the cells beneath it are. The third option was rejected on `D-308`'s ground, which still holds: a statement that an overflow **empties rather than truncates**, or that a call **suppresses business rules**, describes observable behaviour, and removing it degrades the artifact to satisfy a rule aimed at something else. | The boundary is a judgement, so a later reader may disagree about an individual passage. Two things bound that: a mechanical sweep for the announcing forms — a heading, a lead-in or a column naming *Why*, *Rationale* or *Alternatives* — reports **zero** across every artifact but this log and the review document, and every relocated passage names the row it moved to. The sweep does still report clauses that contrast two outcomes of the delivered behaviour; those are the `D-308` class and are left in place. | Low | — |
