Or re-type it as `Replaced`, since the actions and the flow are delivered in full. Or leave the value in use and undeclared, as it stood. | The row's own target column states that the ten actions, the flow and the alias definition are built in full and that the **live path alone** is gated on an endpoint-contract declaration. `Retired` asserts that no target exists, and `Replaced` asserts that the target supersedes the source outright; each contradicts that cell. A fifth value is the only one of the four options under which the vocabulary and the row agree, and leaving it undeclared is what allowed a row to carry a value the vocabulary did not define. | A fifth value is one more distinction a reader of section 1 must hold, and a later row could be typed `Open` where the honest answer is `Retired`. Mitigated by the value's own definition naming its single occurrence, and by the section 5.2 distribution, in which a second `Open` row would show as a count change. | Low | — |


**On section 16 and this round.** Two rows of that section are amended here rather than corrected in place, because what changed is the mechanism and not the description: `D-270` (participant-link reconciliation as an exact set) still holds, and `D-373` supplies the carrier contract that reconciliation reads; `D-106` (own every candidate window key) still holds, and `D-374` fixes which boundaries "every candidate" names. Both original rows are retained in full, per `D-309`.

---

## 20. Remediation of the schema, credential and contract review

Decisions `D-386` onward. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised when the delivered artifacts were reviewed against the **frozen** Agent Action Plan, and this round has a single direction of travel that the earlier rounds did not: **two rows of it delete delivered schema rather than add to it.** `D-386` and `D-387` remove the fifth staging state and the counter's fifth column, and `D-411` states the principle they discharge — that a departure from a plan-mandated schema is closed by changing the artifact, never by continuing to declare it. The rest of the section divides into three groups. `D-388` through `D-396` close a gap between a **document and the delivered API it describes**, where the document named a property value, a method or a field that no artifact carries. `D-397` through `D-404` and `D-409` and `D-410` settle **one buildable construction per artifact** where two competing ones had been published, in the two flow guides, the portal guide and the test guide. `D-412` and `D-413` remove two claims about **surfaces that do not exist** — a `partial` counter no summary publishes, and a blanket denial of person data in flow context the delivery cannot make. `D-405`, `D-406`, `D-407` and `D-408` correct **acceptance claims** — an obligation that was gated harder than the plan asks, a counted set that was rounded to the tidier figure, an open question that was really an unsigned decision, and a discriminator kept for uniformity rather than for readability.

Every row below names the row it amends, or names none where the decision is new. Two rows amend rows that were themselves amendments, which is why `D-038`, `D-040`, `D-106` and `D-278` each carry more than one marker: the mechanism they describe has moved twice, and both moves are recorded rather than the earlier one being overwritten.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-386** | Return the staging `import_state` choice list to the **four members the plan freezes** and carry a run's claim in the **`import_run` column under a reserved `run:` prefix**, leaving a claimed row in `import_state` `pending` for as long as the lease is held. Amends `D-278` and `D-280`. | Keep the fifth `in_progress` member, as first delivered. Or add a sixth column to the staging table to hold the claim. Or add a separate lease table. Or drop the claim concept and rely on the cadence guard. | The ownership fact `D-278` was introduced to secure is real and is kept; what cannot stand is the mechanism, because AAP section 1.0 fixes the staging `import_state` list at `pending`, `processed`, `rejected` and `error`, and this log's own authority rule says a delivered artifact may not widen a frozen closed list — the artifact changes, not the specification. No new column or table is needed either: `import_run` is already on every row, a CSV batch token contains no colon, so a `run:` prefix is unambiguous against every value the import wizard writes, and the existing three-column index on `source_system`, `import_state` and `import_run` serves both the claim query and the abandonment sweep unchanged. | A reader who greps `import_run` now meets two value shapes, a batch token and a lease token. Mitigated by the prefix being reserved and documented at the choice list, in the staging guide's claim procedure and in the sample-data column contract. An abandoned claim is still possible and is now found as a `pending` row whose `import_run` begins `run:`, which is a query rather than a state inspection. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-387** | **Amended by `D-435`, which restores the fifth `window_key` column and makes it the only column the limiter queries; the tuple semantics below are superseded.** Identify a rate-limit counter row by the **four-column tuple the plan names** — `caller`, `api_resource` and a bucket-aligned `window_start` — and **delete the `window_key` column**, with every read summing every row that matches the tuple and every deterministic seed deleting the tuple's rows before writing one. Amends `D-038`, `D-040`, `D-106`, `D-188` and `D-374`. | Keep the fifth `window_key` column as a single-column identity, as first delivered. Or keep it and add a uniqueness constraint so a tuple can hold at most one row. Or hold the window in a single wide record per caller. | AAP section 0.4.2 sketches this table with four columns, and the same authority rule that governs `D-386` governs here: a fifth column is a departure that has to be removed rather than declared. The tuple carries exactly the information the composite key carried, so nothing is lost. Because a tuple may transiently hold more than one row — two simultaneous first requests each insert — the read is an aggregate SUM over the tuple rather than a single-row read, which is what makes a duplicate row harmless instead of a lost count; and a seed that must produce an exact starting total therefore deletes the tuple's rows first rather than updating one it hopes is alone. | A tuple query is three equality terms rather than one, and the delivered index set carries only `window_start` on this table, so the most selective equality term is indexed and the remaining two are filtered. Accepted under the prototype focus of prompt section 6.0, which sets no performance target; the alternative is a fourth index whose only reader is the counter. The atomic increment of `D-040` and the two-boundary determinism of `D-374` are unaffected — both now address the tuple. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-388** | Make `AppProperties.getSourceMode()` **fail closed**: trim and lower-case the stored value, return `live` only when it is exactly `live`, return `fallback` for every other value including absent and malformed, and emit one bounded warning naming the rejected shape. Keep the **configured end state on the target instance** at `fallback` until both aliases pass the **provider-correct authenticated probe** of guide 01, and carry no instruction that restores `live` before that — while shipping the property record with the plan's own default of `live`, because AAP section 0.4.7 fixes that default and the artifact does not depart from it. Amends `D-054`, `D-111` and `D-265`. | Return the stored value unchanged and let the caller interpret it. Or default an unrecognised value to `live` on the reasoning that live is the intended steady state. Or throw on an unrecognised value and abort the run. | An unrecognised value must never be read as permission to send credentials to a provider, so the safe default is the one that reaches no provider at all. `D-054` still holds that the sample dataset is a fallback dataset and not a primary source, but its clause that the live path is attempted on every run no longer describes the delivery: the property is a precondition gate, and a run whose gate reads `fallback` reaches staging without a provider call. Throwing would convert a configuration typo into a failed scheduled run, which prompt section 8.0 asks the ingestion path never to do. | An operator who writes `Live` or `LIVE` gets a fallback run rather than an error. Mitigated by the warning, which names the rejected value's shape and the accepted spelling, and by the property's description carrying the same. The mode a run used is always readable from its provenance marker, so a surprised operator has a second surface to check. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-389** | Log **one bounded, non-sensitive warning per malformed run-marker entry** before discarding it, naming the source and the entry's shape and never its value. Amends `D-156`, `D-183` and `D-265`. | Discard a malformed entry silently, as first delivered. Or fail the read and let the cadence guard treat the marker as absent. Or log the entry verbatim so an operator can see what was stored. | A silently discarded entry is indistinguishable from an entry that was never written, so a marker corrupted by a manual edit would present as a first-ever run and re-ingest a window that had already been processed. Logging verbatim is not available: the marker is a delimited value assembled from run identifiers, so echoing it would put an identifier into a log surface the redaction posture keeps identifiers out of. Naming the shape is enough to diagnose and cannot leak. | One warning per malformed entry could in principle repeat on every read of a marker nobody fixes. Bounded by there being at most one entry per source, so the ceiling is two lines per read. | Low | — |
| **D-390** | Resolve a credential alias by matching the **`sys_alias.id` column against the dotted alias value** — `x_bst_startuptrk.crunchbase_api` and `x_bst_startuptrk.linkedin_oauth` — retaining the exactly-one-row assertion and the fail-closed abort. Amends `D-230`. | Match on `sys_alias.name`, as first delivered. Or match on either column and accept whichever answers. Or hold the alias record identifier in a system property and read that. | `D-230`'s contract — one record, asserted, then its identifier handed to the connection-info provider — is right and is kept; only the column it matched on was wrong. The dotted, scope-qualified value the two flows and three guides reference is the alias's `id`, while `name` carries the human label, so a `name` query against a dotted value returns no row and the fail-closed abort fires on a correctly configured instance. Accepting either column would make the singularity assertion meaningless, because two aliases may legitimately share a label across scopes. | An instance whose alias was created with the dotted string typed into the label field rather than the identifier will now fail the lookup that previously appeared to work. That is the intended outcome: the guide's creation step names which field carries which value, and a failed lookup names the column it searched. | Medium | — |
| **D-391** | Make **Basic authentication the single primary Crunchbase credential store** and demote the API-key credential record to an explicitly labelled alternative that the guide describes but does not build. Amends `D-043` and `D-229`. | Ship both stores and let the operator choose at build time, as first delivered. Or ship the API-key record as primary, since the provider calls the value a key. | Two live credential stores for one provider is two places a secret can be stale, and nothing in the flow selects between them, so a rotation applied to the wrong record produces an authentication failure whose cause is invisible. The transport `D-229` fixes is a request header populated from the resolved credential's password, which a Basic record carries and an API-key record does not expose the same way, so Basic is the store the delivered arm can actually read. | An operator following provider documentation may expect an API-key record and create one anyway, leaving an unused record on the instance. Mitigated by the alternative being described in place, with the reason it is not the built path. | Medium | — |
| **D-392** | Validate the **endpoint's authority before any credential material is attached**: require HTTPS, require the host to equal or be a subdomain of the provider's approved registrable root, require the connection record's URL and the corresponding scoped property to agree, and abort without attaching a header or a token when any of the three fails. Amends `D-229` and `D-288`. | Trust the connection record's URL, as first delivered, since only an administrator can edit it. Or validate the scheme alone. Or pin the full URL as a literal in the flow and ignore the connection record. | A credential is only as confined as the endpoint it is sent to: a connection record edited to an attacker-controlled host would receive a live provider secret on the next scheduled run, and no later check can recall it. Validating before attachment is what makes the failure mode a refused call rather than a disclosed secret. Pinning the URL as a literal would defeat the alias indirection prompt section 4.0 requires; validating the scheme alone leaves the host free. | A legitimate provider migration to a new registrable root now needs a code change rather than a connection-record edit. Accepted, and the approved roots are named in one place per provider so the change is a single edit with a review attached. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-393** | Read the ingestion run marker through the **`AppProperties` marker accessors** — the state, stamp and run members of `getRunProvenance`, `isRunHeld` and their siblings — and publish that schema, rather than through a reader method no delivered artifact defines. Amends `D-183`. | Add the missing method to `IngestionLogger` so the documented call becomes real. Or have the runbook parse the raw property value itself. Or drop the provenance step from the runbook. | The marker's shape is already owned by `AppProperties`, which is where the cadence guard, the lease and the completion stamp all read and write it; a second reader on another class would be a second parser of the same delimited value and would drift from it. Parsing raw in the runbook has the same defect with no class to own it. Publishing the accessors that exist also makes the runbook step executable as written, which the earlier text was not. | The runbook now names methods on a scoped class, so a rename in `AppProperties` breaks a document as well as its callers. Mitigated by the rename-impact list in the API reference carrying this class. | Medium | — |
| **D-394** | Report a failed load, preview or commit from the **execution tracker's own state and message and the update set's retrieved output records**, and remove every reference to a `sys_remote_update_set.error_detail` field. Or scrape the preview problem list for every failure class. | The field does not exist on this instance's table, so every failure path that promised detail delivered an empty string — the worst outcome available, because the runbook reads as though it reported a cause. The tracker carries a state and a message for exactly the asynchronous operations that fail here, and the retrieved records carry the per-record outcome, so both surfaces are real and are already polled by the surrounding steps. The preview problem list covers one failure class of three. | Two surfaces to read rather than one field, and the tracker's message is provider-worded rather than curated. Accepted: an imperfect real message beats a well-formed empty one. | Medium | — |
| **D-395** | Address every validation gate by its **stable identifier and document anchor** rather than by a row number in the gate table. Amends `D-108`. | Keep row-number references and re-check them at each revision. Or inline each gate's text at every reference. Or drop the cross-references. | A row number is invalidated by any insertion above it, and the gate table has grown in every review round, so the references were wrong by construction rather than by accident. Every gate already carries an identifier of the `GATE-` form, and an anchor derived from it is stable across insertions. Inlining the text would duplicate a normative statement, which is how the two copies then disagree. | An identifier renamed later breaks every reference to it. Mitigated by the identifiers being the same strings the post-commit step and the acceptance checklist already assert, so a rename is already a breaking change with its own review. | Medium | — |
| **D-396** | Give each rollback branch **one identifier scheme**, used in its precondition list, its steps, its evidence rows and every cross-reference to it. Or merge the two branches into one numbered sequence. | Two schemes inside one branch make a precondition and the step it guards look like different things, and the branch that ends in an irreversible scope deletion is the last place a reader should have to infer which identifier means which. Merging the branches would erase the distinction between the clean-install deletion and the non-destructive backout, which is the distinction that decides whether an operator may proceed at all. | Existing external notes that cite an old identifier no longer resolve. Accepted: the branches are internal to one document and the identifiers appear nowhere the delivery does not control. | Medium | — |
| **D-397** | Publish **one seven-action graph, `A1` through `A7`, for each flow**, mirrored between the two sources, with the requirement identifiers `REQ-RB-01` through `REQ-RB-09` and Branch A's steps renumbered `A1` to `A5`; and delete every competing construction so each consumed pill corresponds to a declared output of a declared action. Amends `D-102`, `D-104` and `D-247`. | Keep the single published orchestrator action `D-102` prescribes and describe the rest as narrative. Or keep both constructions and mark one preferred. Or publish per-flow graphs that differ where the sources differ. | `D-102`'s reason for one published action — that cleaning, upsert and logging must not be reimplemented per flow — is preserved, because `A5` is that action and both flows call it. What could not stand is two graphs describing the same flow with different action counts and pills bound to outputs no action declared: a builder following either one produces a flow that cannot be saved. Mirroring the two graphs makes the asymmetry that remains explicit and small rather than structural. | Seven actions is more surface to build than five, and a mirrored graph forces the LinkedIn flow to carry an action whose body differs from its Crunchbase twin. Accepted: the count is what the delivered outputs already required, and the mirror is what lets one verification procedure cover both guides. | Medium | — |
| **D-398** | Accept a **per-flow ingestion-output asymmetry** — fifteen members from the Crunchbase call, thirteen from LinkedIn — rather than padding the shorter flow to a common shape. | Pad LinkedIn with two always-empty members so both flows publish the same envelope. | **D-399** | Publish a **four-member contract — `rows`, `total`, `limit`, `truncated`** — for all five company-profile child panes and both investor rounds tables, render `total` as a badge and `truncated` as a bounded notice, ship `full_list_page` empty so the notice degrades to text until a full-list page exists, and declare `spUtil.addInfoMessage` unused rather than describing a request workflow. Amends `D-286`. | Publish `rows` alone and let each template infer the rest. Or ship a sixth portal page for the full lists. Or build the premium-request workflow the earlier text implied. | A pane that renders a bounded list without publishing the bound tells a user they have seen everything when they have not, so `total` and `truncated` are the members that make the bound honest; `limit` is what lets one template render any pane. A sixth page would breach the five routes prompt section 5.0 enumerates, so the option is carried as an empty setting a later delivery can fill rather than as a route. The request workflow was described but never built, and describing an unbuilt behaviour in a build guide is the defect, not the absence of the behaviour. | An empty `full_list_page` means the notice names no destination, which reads as a truncated sentence to a user who expects a link. Mitigated by the notice being phrased to stand alone without a link and by the setting being documented at the widget. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-400** | Carry state between tests in a suite through a **parked handoff record the suite creates and reads**, rather than through step `outputs.*` members the test framework does not expose to a later test. Amends `D-247`. | Keep the `outputs.*` references and hope the framework resolves them. Or repeat the setup in every test that needs the state. Or collapse the dependent tests into one long test. | A step output is addressable inside the test that produced it and nowhere else, so every cross-test reference was unbuildable as written — the suite could be typed in and would fail at the first dependent step. Repeating setup multiplies the fixtures and the teardown surface; one long test loses the per-test result granularity the coverage gate is counted from. A parked record is data, which the framework does carry across tests in a suite, and it is removed by the same teardown that removes the fixtures. | A parked record is a fixture that must be cleaned up like any other, and an aborted suite can leave one behind. Mitigated by the convergence sweep the suite's first test performs and by the residue check at the end. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-401** | Use **one disposable REST caller identity per suite**, created by the suite, retired in a `finally` step, swept for by the suite's first test, and asserted absent at the end — with every convergence and residue check addressing that exact identity rather than a class of identities. Amends `D-292` and `D-295`. | Mint a fresh identity per test. Or reuse a long-lived test account. Or sweep by name pattern rather than by identity. | One identity per suite is the smallest surface that still isolates the suite from the instance: a per-test identity multiplies the mint-and-retire pairs that can be abandoned, and a long-lived account accumulates entitlements nobody reviews and cannot be asserted absent. Sweeping by pattern is what makes a sweep destructive — it can retire an identity another run is holding — so every check names the identity it owns. | A suite that dies between creation and the `finally` step leaves one identity behind until the next run's sweep. Accepted and bounded: the sweep is the first test, so the residue lives at most one run, and the identity holds only the scoped roles under test. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-402** | Derive the **run token from `GlideSecureRandomUtil`** as well as every minted secret, so no value that identifies or authenticates a test run comes from `Math.random()`. Amends `D-295`. | Keep the arithmetic generator for the token on the reasoning that a run token is not a secret. Or derive the token from the run's start time. | A token that collides between two concurrent runs makes each run's isolating filter select the other's rows, which is a correctness failure rather than a security one — and the platform's secure generator is the same one call, so the weaker source buys nothing. A timestamp collides precisely when two runs start together, which is the case the token exists to separate. | None identified beyond the generator being a platform API the test now depends on, which every other minted value already depends on. | Low | — |
| **D-403** | Assert **`rule3_deviations`** as a counter in its own right, expecting two per source, and publish the uncoercible values that produce them. | Assert only that the counter is present. Or fold the deviations into the unmatched count. | A counter nobody asserts a value for is a counter that can silently reach zero, and this one is the only evidence that cleaning rule three coerced what it could and reported what it could not. Two per source is derivable from the fixtures rather than asserted: each source carries exactly two values whose target choice list has no `Other` member, so the value is left unwritten and the deviation counted. Folding it into the unmatched count would lose that distinction. | A fixture change that adds or removes an uncoercible value changes the expected figure. Accepted: the figure is derived beside the fixtures and the two values are named. | Low | — |
| **D-404** | Make **part `8c` of action `A7`** the single writer of the durable completion marker, and read source-specific provenance with **`getRunProvenance(source)`** rather than the cross-source last-writer accessor. Amends `D-281` and `D-375`. | Keep the marker write in the orchestrator's final phase as well, so a run that ends early still stamps. Or write the marker from both and let the later write win. | Two writers of one marker is how a run comes to report a provenance it did not use, and the health predicate `D-281` gates the stamp on lives in `A7` — so `A7` is the only place that knows whether a stamp is earned. Reading with the cross-source accessor was the same defect on the read side: it answers with whichever source stamped last, so a Crunchbase assertion could pass on a LinkedIn run's marker. | A run that fails before `A7` stamps nothing, so its provenance is absent rather than partial. That is the intended reading and is what `D-281` chose; the absence is itself asserted. | Medium | — |
| **D-405** | Return the platform **inbound rate-limit rule on the Table API routes to a recommended defence-in-depth measure**, with acceptance requiring only that the decision to adopt or decline it be recorded, and remove it from every gate and checklist that treated it as acceptance-blocking. Amends `D-379`. | Keep it required and gated, as `D-379` set it. Or ship the rule itself inside the Update Set. | AAP section 0.8.3 makes this rule explicitly optional — the mandated `429` contract is the application counter's, and the platform rule is named as a higher-threshold backstop — so requiring it is the artifact departing from the plan, which this log's authority rule resolves in the plan's favour. The rule also cannot ship: it is a Global-scope record, and prompt section 6.0 forbids the delivery from touching anything outside its scope. What acceptance can honestly require is the recorded decision, which is what an operator controls. | An instance that declines the rule has one fewer backstop against a caller that ignores the application's `429`. Disclosed as a residual risk with the threshold to configure if it is adopted, so declining is a recorded choice rather than an oversight. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-406** | State the caller-facing counted-table set as **eight** — the seven entity tables and the join table the portal counts — rather than reducing the figure to seven to match the entity-table count. Amends `D-041` and `D-267`. | State seven and treat the join-table count as an internal read. Or stop counting the join table and derive the participant total from the array the API already assembles. | The count is a caller-facing contract, and the portal genuinely issues a count against the join table to size a funding round's participant list, so seven would be a figure chosen for its tidiness against a delivery that counts eight. Deriving the total from the assembled array would make the participant count a function of the page size, which is the defect the counting contract exists to prevent. | Eight disagrees with the seven-table count every other inventory uses, which invites a reader to treat it as an error. Mitigated by the set being enumerated wherever the figure appears rather than left as a number. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-407** | Record the diagram-library advisory disposition as a **decision — option B, accept — with a named risk-owner role and a date**, leaving only the human countersignature open, rather than carrying it as an open question. Amends `D-305`. | Leave the disposition open until a person signs. Or advance the library pin, which the presentation rule freezes. Or remove the diagrams the advisory concerns. | An open question in an acceptance document blocks acceptance without telling anyone what would unblock it, whereas a recorded decision with an owner and one missing signature states exactly what remains and who supplies it. `D-305` already established that the exposure is closed by rendering at a non-default security level under the frozen pin, so the residual question was never technical; it was who accepts the residue. Advancing the pin would breach the rule that sets it. | The countersignature is the one item in this delivery no agent can supply, so the record ships with a named gap. Stated as such in the gaps document, in the acceptance checklist and in the resolution report, with the accepting role named. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-408** | Keep the `/news` negative control on the **`startup` discriminator**, so all five child resources share one isolation mechanism, rather than switching it to the `source` filter the resource also supports. | Use `source`, which the resource accepts as a CONTAINS filter and which reads more naturally for news. Or give each resource whichever filter suits it. | One mechanism across five resources is one thing to get right and one thing to verify; `startup` is an exact identifier match on every child resource, so the control isolates by construction. A CONTAINS filter isolates only as far as the fixture text is unique, which is a property of the data rather than of the test. | The `/news` test exercises the `startup` filter and not the `source` filter, so one supported filter goes unexercised by the isolation test. Disclosed in the coverage note beside the test. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-409** | Publish the ATF **step inventory as a derived table totalling 510 steps**, per suite and per test, rather than an approximate figure. | Keep the approximation. Or omit the count. | A build guide's step count is what an operator plans against, and an approximation cannot be checked — it also cannot reveal that a suite is missing steps, which is exactly what the earlier figure hid. A table derived from the final suite sequences is checkable by addition and fails visibly when a suite changes. | Any suite edit changes the total and the table has to be regenerated. Accepted: the table is derived mechanically from the sequences it documents. | Low | — |
| **D-410** | Give **every ATF fixture intended to resolve both halves of the startup natural key** — the parent name and the parent headquarters location — and keep exactly one fixture that deliberately omits the location, distinct from the fixture whose name matches nothing. | Relax the mapper to resolve a parent on name alone. Or drop the missing-location fixture and rely on the unmatched-name one. | The mapper resolves a parent by name together with headquarters location and refuses a blank location before it queries, so fixtures carrying only the name were rejected for a reason no assertion named — the suite passed while testing something other than what it claimed. Relaxing the mapper would break the deduplication key prompt section 4.0 fixes. The two rejection reasons are different sentences from different guards, so one fixture each is what proves both guards. | Ten child fixtures now carry a location column that only two of them are testing, which is fixture verbosity. Accepted: the alternative is a suite whose rejections cannot be attributed. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-411** | Close the two declared departures from the plan by **changing the artifact rather than restating the exception**: the fifth staging state and the counter's fifth column are removed, and no row of this log now records an outstanding departure from a plan-mandated schema. Amends `D-278`, `D-038` and `D-089`. | Keep both departures and keep declaring them, as the previous revision did. Or record them as accepted variances with an owner. | This log's own authority section says the specification governs, that a departure is a defect in the artifact, and that it remains one until the artifact changes — so a log that declared two schema departures indefinitely was using a decision row to authorize a conflicting implementation, which is the one thing the section forbids. An accepted variance with an owner would be the same thing with a name attached. Both departures had cheap in-plan mechanisms available, so there was never a cost that justified the exception. | The two mechanisms that replace them are less obvious than the columns they remove, so both need their reserved conventions documented wherever the schema is. Discharged at `D-386` and `D-387` and in the data model, the staging guide, the sample-data contract, the API reference and the acceptance checklist. | High | `DEV` |

| **D-412** | Report a funding round whose participant or lead links did not all reconcile through **the round's own `import_state` set to `error`** and **the run summary's `skipped` counter**, and publish **no `partial` counter** on any surface. Amends `D-186` and `D-200`. | Add the `partial` counter the two amended rows described, so the outcome names this case directly. Or count the round in `rejected`. Or leave the round `processed` and report the link failure only in the log. | The distinction those rows exist to preserve — that such a round is not cleanly processed — is preserved exactly, because `error` on the row and `skipped` in the summary both say so, and both already exist. A tenth member on the `run_summary` event would widen a published envelope that the acceptance checklist, both flow guides and the test ledger all assert member by member, for no information the two existing surfaces do not carry. Counting it in `rejected` would conflate a round that was written and is incomplete with a row that was refused and was never written. Leaving it `processed` would make the ledger's own invariant false. | `skipped` now carries two causes — a per-record error raised during application, and an unreconciled link — so a reader who needs the cause must read the run's events rather than the counter. Stated where the counter is documented, and the per-record event names which cause it was. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-413** | Replace the blanket claim that **no** person data reaches flow execution history with a **delivered residency statement**: no *published* flow output, input or logged value carries person data, and what the platform itself retains in a flow's context is described in a residency table whose exact table names are owned by obligation `OB-2`. Amends `D-212` and `D-378`. | Keep the blanket denial, as first delivered. Or enumerate the platform's own context tables in the guide. Or drop the residency question from the guides entirely. | The delivery controls what it publishes and can state that absolutely; it does not control what the platform retains about an execution, so a blanket denial asserted more than the delivery can know — and a data-protection claim that overstates is worse than one that is bounded. Enumerating the platform's context tables would put version-specific internal names into a build guide where they would silently rot; `OB-2` already exists to have a named human confirm them against the instance. Dropping the question would remove the only place the residency is discussed at all. | The residency table names a class of storage rather than specific tables, so a reader wanting the exact names must wait for `OB-2`'s confirmation. Disclosed in the table itself, in the gaps register and in `OB-2`'s evidence procedure. | High | `DEV` |

**On sections 16, 17 and this round.** The counter model has now moved twice — `D-038` set a tuple, `D-040` moved the identity to a composite key, and `D-387` returns it to the tuple the plan names — and each move is recorded in place rather than by rewriting its predecessor, so a reader who arrives at `D-038` is told twice where authority now sits. The same is true of the staging claim at `D-278` and `D-386`. Three rows of section 17 are amended here: `D-374` (two-boundary determinism) still holds and now addresses the tuple, `D-375` (teardown as a defined state) still holds and now reads its marker through the published accessors, and `D-379` (the inbound rate-limit obligation) is returned to the recommended posture the plan sets. Nothing in sections 1 to 15 is retired by this round.

---

## 21. Remediation of the final comments and consistency review

Twenty decisions, `D-414` through `D-433`. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised when the delivered documents were read against the delivered artifacts and against each other, so the direction of travel is different again from sections 15 and 16: this round reconciles what the package **says** with what it **does**, and most of its rows correct a figure or a contract that several documents had each restated differently. Every row **amends or extends** an earlier row unless it states otherwise, the amended row is corrected in place and says what supersedes it, and the decision to open a section rather than extend section 16 is itself recorded, at `D-414`.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-414** | Record this consistency round as its **own section 17** rather than extending section 16, and correct every superseded row in place with a sentence naming what no longer describes the delivery. Amends D-309. | Extend section 16, as that section's own convention would allow. Or rewrite the superseded rows silently. Or renumber the file so the rounds read as one sequence. | The two rounds answer different questions: section 16 removed work the plan does not license, while this round reconciles what the delivered documents *say* against what the delivered artifacts *do* — several of its rows correct a figure or a contract that three or four documents had each restated differently. Merging them would produce a section whose rows disagree about the same artifact with no indication of order. Rewriting silently would erase the history Rule 1 exists to preserve, and renumbering would break every citation from fifteen documents. | Three remediation sections rather than one, so an amendment chain can now run three hops. Mitigated by every amended row naming its corrector by identifier, in both directions. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-415** | Redact the **whole value together with its separator**, and persist **no raw provider or exception text**: `redact()` removes a sensitive keyword, the separator that follows it and the value that follows that, and `faultReason()` maps every transport failure onto a closed five-value vocabulary before anything is written. Both helpers are declared character for character in guides 01, 02 and 03. Amends D-207 and D-288. | Keep the keyword-only replacement, which left the value in place whenever the separator was not a bare equals sign. Or log the provider's own message text and rely on the reader not to quote it. | The delivered helper replaced the keyword and stopped, so a header written with a colon and a space kept its secret in the log; and an exception message from an outbound call can quote the composed request, including the credential it carried. A closed vocabulary cannot quote anything, and a separator-aware full-value replacement removes the secret whichever punctuation the provider used. Declaring the helpers identically in three guides is what stops one guide's copy drifting into the weaker form again. | A closed reason vocabulary discards diagnostic detail that would sometimes shorten an investigation. Mitigated by the bounded diagnostic token of D-207, which correlates a log line with the failure without carrying its text. | High | — |
| **D-416** | Parse participant carriers **by shape and never by comma**: `IngestionMapper._participantNames()` accepts a real array, a serialised JSON array or a pipe-delimited string, and `_flattenParticipants()` pipe-joins on the way to staging, so the shape the fixtures ship and the shape the mapper reads are the same one. Amends D-010. **Superseded in part by D-453:** the parse-by-shape rule and the ban on the comma stand exactly as recorded, but the delivered names are `readNameList()` and `_flattenNameList()`, and the flattener serialises to a **JSON array** rather than pipe-joining — which is what actually makes the shipped fixtures and the mapper's read the same shape, since every shipped CSV carries a JSON array. | Keep the comma split and change every fixture to a comma-joined string. Or accept both delimiters and split on either. | Every shipped fixture carries a JSON array, so the delivered comma split resolved a whole array to a single unparsed name and the join table received one row where it should have received several. Investor names legitimately contain commas — a firm styled with a suffix after a comma is ordinary — so accepting the comma as a delimiter cannot be made correct, only differently wrong. | A pipe-delimited carrier is unusual, and an operator hand-editing a staging row may type a comma out of habit. Mitigated by the staging column's own field hint, which names the three accepted carriers. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-417** | **Amended by the delivered gate set, which adds `GATE-SEC-04` and classifies all four `GATE-SEC` checks as non-normative diagnostics that are recorded on every deployment and block nothing, so twelve checks block acceptance and eleven of them roll back.** Classify the three `GATE-SEC` checks as **class 3 — security, acceptance-required, non-rollback** — so fifteen checks block acceptance while the eleven post-commit gates remain the only rollback trigger. Amends D-108 and D-069. | Keep them as non-normative diagnostics whose outcome decides nothing. Or promote them into the eleven, making a failure roll the deployment back. | Each of the three asserts a posture the required eleven cannot see, and each failure names a concrete exposure: raw staged payloads reachable over an external route, a cross-scope write permitted on an application table, or a REST endpoint control absent. A check whose failure names an exposure cannot also be a check whose outcome decides nothing. Rollback is still wrong for them, because the commit did produce the application and deleting the scope would not fix a dictionary attribute. | Fifteen acceptance-blocking checks against eleven rollback triggers is a distinction a reader can miss, and missing it in the rollback direction is the expensive mistake. Mitigated by stating both halves — blocks acceptance, never rolls back — everywhere the class is named. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-418** | Prove the rollback deletion by the **absence of metadata**, not by a status code: the `sys_scope` row is gone **and** no `sys_db_object` record remains for the application's tables. Amends D-076. | Keep asserting a `403` or `404` from a table read. Or widen the accepted set to include this instance's `400`. | A status code from a read of a table that no longer exists is instance-specific — this instance answers `400` where the environment brief expects `404` — and every one of those codes is also what an access-control refusal returns, so a code alone cannot distinguish a deleted table from a table the caller may not read. Two metadata absences distinguish them unambiguously and are the same on every instance. | Two queries where there was one, and both must be run as the operating administrator for the absence to mean anything. Mitigated by naming that requirement in the step itself. | Medium | — |
| **D-419** | Assert import completeness with **two named pre-commit checks**, `PRE-COMMIT-01` and `PRE-COMMIT-02`, both read before the commit and both against the record count the validator reports for the delivered file, and state the delivered figure — **313** — once rather than in every gate. Amends D-257 and D-223. | Keep the single check inside step 3. Or hard-code the delivered figure into each check. | One check confirmed the retrieved update set's own summary and could not see whether the payloads had all loaded; the second counts what actually landed, and the two together fail differently — a transport truncation shows in one, a payload rejection in the other. Hard-coding the figure is what produced three documents asserting three different counts, so each check reads the validator's figure and the delivered value is named in exactly one place. | A generic check is less immediately readable than a literal number, and an operator who does not run the validator first has nothing to compare against. Mitigated by making the validator run a precondition of the upload step. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-420** | Make the ingestion base URL **the system property**, with the connection record's Connection URL as its **required mirror** rather than a second authority. Amends D-229 and D-230. | Make the connection record authoritative, since the outbound step reads it. Or keep both authoritative and require them to agree. | Two authorities with no stated precedence is how the two shipped base URLs came to disagree with each other in the first place. The property is the one an operator can change without opening the connection record, it is the value the probe reads, and it is the value the manual update procedure edits first — so it is the authority, and the mirror is a step in that procedure rather than a fact about the design. | A mirror can drift silently, because nothing at run time compares the two. Mitigated by the connection test in guide 01, which fails against a stale mirror. | Medium | — |
| **D-421** | Publish **one dated posture record** in guide 01 — instance, date observed, per-alias state and the path in force — with a three-class contract for who owns each credential record and per-path completion criteria, and have every other document link to it rather than restate readiness. Amends D-249. | Keep the undated statement. Or let each document state the posture it observed, with its own date. | An undated posture reads as a permanent property of the delivery rather than an observation of one instance on one day, which is how eight documents came to assert a current credential state in the present tense. A dated record can be superseded by re-observation without any other document changing, and a link cannot drift from what it points at. | A dated record goes stale by construction, and a reader may take a stale date as current. Mitigated by the record naming the instance and the date in the same row as the state. | Medium | — |
| **D-422** | Test each flow through **one executable sequence** of sixteen display orders, seeding the flow's own fixture batch under a distinct run token at step 120 and starting the published flow **twice** — once on the no-op branch and once on the run branch — rather than describing two alternative constructions. Amends D-105, D-247 and D-291. | Keep both constructions and let the builder choose. Or start the flow once and assert only that it returned a context. | Two constructions in one guide is two contracts, and the guide asserted both — that the flow is started and that the framework cannot start it. One sequence with numbered steps makes every assertion traceable to the step that makes it. Starting twice is what separates the cadence guard from the ingestion: the no-op start proves the guard held the run back with the staged rows untouched, and the run start proves the same flow ingests them. | A single sequence is longer and its steps are order-dependent, so a builder who reorders them gets failures that do not name the cause. Mitigated by the step table stating the display order of every step and by the fixture batch carrying its own run token. | Medium | — |
| **D-423** | Make the `429` deterministic by **owning both window keys** — the current boundary and the next — reading the count as a sum across the two, and **removing every `gs.sleep()` guard**. Amends D-038 and D-106. | Keep the three-column ownership procedure and the boundary wait. Or widen the window property so a boundary cannot fall inside the test. | The counter's uniqueness moved onto a single `window_key` column, so a procedure that queries three columns separately can act on a row the limiter will not read. And the inbound request resolves its bucket from the clock when it arrives, so no arrangement of clock readings closes the boundary race — owning both buckets closes it outright. A blocking wait also holds the runner's transaction open and makes the suite's duration depend on when it started. Widening the window would change a delivered property to suit a test. | Two keys to seed and sweep rather than one, so a step that forgets the second leaves a row behind. Mitigated by the baseline assertion at the first counter step, which detects on the next run anything the sweep missed. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-424** | Number the test guide's warnings **contiguously, `W1` to `W13`**, renumbering bottom-up so no existing reference moves, rather than carrying letter-suffixed insertions such as `W4b` and `W6c`. | Keep the letter suffixes, which preserve the original numbers. Or append new warnings at the end, out of narrative order. | The suffixed set had drifted out of order — one warning sat between two others whose numbers did not bracket it — and the inventory statement counted eleven where thirteen headings existed. Every reference to a warning is inside that one guide, so renumbering costs nothing outside it, and a contiguous set is checkable by counting. | Any external note that quoted an old identifier now names a warning that no longer carries that number. Mitigated by the identifiers being confined to a single guide, verified by search before the renumbering. | Low | — |
| **D-425** | State the test guide's three implicit preconditions **explicitly** — the inbound authentication profile shell, that no criterion-4 evidence window is open, and that the source-mode property reads `fallback` — taking its precondition count from thirteen to sixteen. | Leave them implicit, since five later passages already assume them. Or state them only in the steps that depend on them. | A precondition that five passages rely on and none states is a precondition the builder discovers by failing. Listing them with the other thirteen puts every entry condition in one place, which is where a builder checks before starting. | A longer precondition list is more likely to be skimmed. Mitigated by each entry naming how to confirm it, so skimming still leaves a checkable list. | Low | — |
| **D-426** | **Amended by `D-457`, which records the 2026-08-09 re-query the delivered disposition carries: eleven identified records, seven in range and four not, with `CVE-2026-50159` withdrawn. The removal of unverifiable identifiers below stands.** Rebuild the dependency-advisory snapshot from the **maintainer's published records**, dated 2026-08-08: seven records, **five** applying to the pinned version and two not, every row carrying a published affected range and a published fixing release, and **three unverifiable identifiers removed** rather than left standing with a range to be re-queried later. Amends D-133 and D-173. | Keep the earlier snapshot and re-query at acceptance, as its own cells instructed. Or drop the table and cite the advisory database. | A row with no verifiable range asserts an exposure it cannot substantiate and cannot be closed, so three such rows made the record less trustworthy rather than more cautious. Two further rows were wrong in the other direction: one advisory's own patched list names a release the pin exceeds, so it never applied, and another's range begins above the pin. The corrected set is five records, all closed by a single pin at or above one release, and each reachability answer now rests on a property of the delivered file rather than on a range nobody could check. | The snapshot is still a snapshot, and the database is live. Mitigated by the review trigger, which names the two events that require re-assessment. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-427** | State the LinkedIn provider capability **once**: there is no generally available read path for the three record types, and the only route to one is an approved provider partner grant — so the flag records the shortfall and the open limitation records the route, rather than one denying what the other promises. Amends D-233 and D-103. | Keep the absolute statement that no credential can ever produce a live route. Or keep the statement that a declaration and a credential close the path. | Both statements were true of different things and false as written of each other: a credential alone genuinely cannot produce acquisition, and a partner grant genuinely could. Read together they told a reader that the same path both closes and never closes. Separating the shortfall from the route keeps each accurate and makes the precondition — a grant this delivery can neither obtain nor assume — legible. | A reader who takes the route as available may plan a live integration that never becomes possible. Mitigated by the route naming the grant as its precondition and by the flag holding its own disposition. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-428** | Publish the flag inventory as **nineteen flags across five dispositions**, with the open integration limitation counted separately from them. Amends D-088. | Keep the twelve-flag count and treat the later entries as an appendix. Or fold the open limitation into the flag numbering as a twentieth flag. | Seven entries were added after the artifacts were first delivered, and the document went on asserting twelve in ten places while enumerating nineteen in its own summary — so three other documents each inherited a different figure. The open limitation is a different kind of item, closing when information arrives rather than when a requirement changes, so numbering it as a flag would make the flag count mean two things at once. | A reader who remembers the twelve-flag figure from an earlier draft will find every count changed. Mitigated by the addition history stating which entries arrived when and against what. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-429** | Record the ingestion log as **in scope for an erasure request**: the sanitiser narrows what the log can hold and does not prove it holds nothing, so an erasure runs the same identifier search over the flow execution log and the system log. Amends D-158 and D-187. | Keep the statement that the logs hold nothing to erase. Or harden the sanitiser until the statement becomes true. | The sanitiser is a name-and-shape filter: it replaces a sensitively named field wholesale and an address-shaped or locator-shaped substring inside a retained value, but a personal datum that is neither sensitively named nor shaped like either can still be written. Hardening it to an allow-list would have to drop the bounded diagnostic detail the run summary depends on. The honest statement is the narrower one, and it costs an erasure two extra searches. | Two more searches per erasure request, over surfaces whose retention the platform owner controls rather than this application. Mitigated by the platform-owner obligation already recorded for those surfaces. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-430** | Make the funding-round natural key the ordered pair `startup` plus `round_date` plus `round_type`, then `startup` plus `round_date`, and keep the provider-locator index as a **lookup** index rather than a natural-key index. Amends D-196 and D-188. | Keep the provider locator first, as delivered. Or key on the locator alone and reject a round that carries none. | The locator is absent from most fixture rows and is not stable across provider roots, so a key that reads it first falls through to its alternative on the majority of rounds and matches nothing on a root change — which is how the same round arrived twice. The three-column form distinguishes two rounds a company closed on one date, which the two-column form cannot, so both alternatives are needed and their order matters. | Two companies could in principle report the same round type on the same date and be merged if the startup reference were wrong; the startup reference is resolved first and is mandatory, which bounds that to a mis-resolved parent. The locator index is retained, so no query that used it regresses. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-431** | Close the three-way staging contract on the identity **92 = 92 = 92 over 41 distinct column names**, with the per-file distribution stated, rather than on the earlier figure. Amends D-176. | Keep the earlier identity and correct only the leg that disagreed. Or drop the identity and state the three legs separately. | The identity is the check, so an identity computed from a stale figure is worse than none: two documents closed on one number while a third enumerated another, and the fixtures themselves carry a third. Recounting from the delivered files gives one figure every leg can be measured against, and stating the per-file distribution lets a reader recount it without opening the fixtures. | A figure recounted from the artifacts must be recounted again whenever a fixture column is added. Mitigated by the guide's own step, which reads the count from the staging dictionary rather than from prose. | Low | — |
| **D-432** | **Amended by the delivered deck, which brings the two remaining over-cap slides inside the limit by *shortening* their footnotes rather than removing them, keeps the traced-row footnote on the comparison slide — which measures exactly forty body words — and carries the pinned-library advisory disclosure on the closing slide, which the cap does not govern, with the footnote ink inverted there for the dark slide types.** Bring the six over-cap content slides inside the presentation rule's forty-word limit by **shortening or removing a footnote and one heading**, and leave the title and closing slides — which the rule's content-slide cap does not govern — as they stand. Amends D-307. | Split a slide in two, which would take the deck to seventeen sections. Or drop a table row from each. Or reword the rule's cap as applying to body copy only. | The over-cap words were in footnotes and one nine-word heading, not in the substance: every table row, KPI card and diagram is retained unchanged, so the trim costs no fact the slide was carrying. Splitting slides would move the deck's section count for a word count, and dropping table rows would remove the evidence the slides exist to show. | A shorter footnote carries less context, and two figures that were on a footnote — the traced-row total and the two open items — now appear only on the slides and documents that own them. Mitigated by both figures being stated in the traceability matrix and the flag inventory. | Low | — |
| **D-433** | Hold the presentation's own **CSS and policy comments to the same standard as the delivered code**: each states the enforced behaviour, the invariant it holds, or the requirement it satisfies, plus a decision-log pointer, and none carries a measurement, an alternative weighed or a risk assessed. Amends D-308. | Treat the deck as documentation rather than code and leave its comments arguing. Or delete the comments entirely. | The deck is delivered code as much as it is a document, and its comment blocks were the last place in the delivery where a *why* still lived outside the log — including a content-security-policy block that argued blast radius and trust boundaries, and a stylesheet comment carrying measured colour values. Deleting them outright would lose the invariants a maintainer needs to keep the layout correct. | A comment that states an invariant without its reasoning is easier to remove by a maintainer who does not see why it matters. Mitigated by every one of them naming the decision row that carries the reasoning. | Low | — |

## 22. Remediation of the boundary B backend-to-database review

Thirteen decisions, `D-434` through `D-446`. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised when the delivered service layer, dictionary and access controls were read against the boundary between the application's server code and its tables. The direction of travel is narrower than section 17's: this round is about **what the code does to the data**, so most of its rows change a delivered script rather than a document, and several of them supersede an earlier row that described a mechanism the code no longer uses.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-434** | Deliver `x_bst_startuptrk_fundinground.participating_investors` as a **calculated, virtual, read-only** column whose calculation calls `InvestorPortfolioService.participantList()`, so the join table is the only store of participation and the column derives its value on every read. Supersedes the stored projection of **D-010**, and with it the refresh mechanics of **D-185**, **D-245** and **D-273**. | Delete the column outright, which is the literal reading of the plan's "no second representation". Or keep the stored projection and keep refreshing it, as D-010 had it. Or keep the projection and add a reconciliation job. | The plan makes the join table authoritative and forbids a second **stored** representation, and it also fixes the seven entity tables at **53** columns with a machine-asserted per-table split — so deleting the column would have broken a passing acceptance gate and forced a renumbering of the 53-row field walk, while keeping it stored left two places for the same fact to disagree. A calculated virtual column satisfies both at once: nothing is stored, so nothing can drift, and the column still exists for the binding field list and for the form. `participantList()` reads the join table through the same service that owns the derivation, caps the rendered value at the 121 identifiers the declared length holds, and logs at warn when it cuts. | The value is computed on every read, so a form or list that renders many rounds pays one join-table query each. Bounded by the same indexes the derivation uses, and acceptable because the column is not queryable or sortable in any delivered path — which is itself a consequence: a virtual column cannot appear in an encoded query, and no delivered query uses it. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-435** | Carry a **fifth** column, `window_key`, on `x_bst_startuptrk_rate_limit_counter`, beyond the four the plan infers, and make it the **only** column the limiter queries. | Query the three-column tuple `caller` + `api_resource` + `window_start` directly, which is what the plan's four columns imply. Or add a database index on the tuple and keep the three-column query. | A single unique column is what makes the find-or-create atomic in one statement and what lets every counter step in the test suite query one value rather than reconstruct a tuple; the tuple query needs three equality conditions and a composite index to be equivalent, and a mismatch in any one of them silently creates a second row for the same window. The four plan columns are all present and unchanged; this is an addition, not a substitution. | A derived key can disagree with the columns it is derived from if it is ever written by hand. Mitigated by `RateLimitService.windowKey()` being the only producer, called rather than copied by every test step. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-436** | Give the `source_url` index a **delivered caller** rather than removing it: `RestQueryHelper.getUrlParam()` validates an absolute `https` value and the `/funding-rounds` list operation accepts it as an equality filter. Amends **D-188**, **D-196** and **D-430**, under which the index served no query once the locator stopped being the natural key. | Remove the index, which is what the plan's index discipline would otherwise require of an index no query reads. Or leave it in place with no caller and document it as speculative. | Removing it would change the delivered record count, which is asserted verbatim in seven documents and reconciled arithmetically in this log, to remove an index that a real consumer wants — a provider locator is exactly the value an integrator holds when it wants one round. Adding the filter closes the gap in the direction that adds capability rather than subtracting it, and it costs one operation parameter. | One more filter parameter on one list operation, and one more validation path. Bounded: the validator is the canonical URL allowlist of D-159, not a new one. | Low | — |
| **D-437** | Establish run ownership with a **guarded conditional update** — one statement whose query includes the expected prior value, followed by a record-level read-back that compares the owner — for both the source lease on the run-state property and the `pending` → `in_progress` staging claim, and report `atomic` on every result so a fallback is observable. Implements **D-278**, whose read-then-write-then-read sequence was not a compare-and-set. | Keep the read-then-write-then-read sequence, which reads as atomic but is not. Or add an eleventh table to hold a lease row. Or hold the lease in the rate-limit counter table. Or accept the race under the prototype focus. | Two overlapping runs both passed the earlier sequence and both believed they held the source, which is the failure the lease exists to prevent — and the same held for a staging row, so one run could read rows another had claimed. A guarded update matches the row only while it still carries the expected value, so exactly one of two contending runs wins. A new table was not available: the plan fixes ten, and two acceptance gates assert that count. The counter table was wrong on purpose — its documented purpose is per-caller accounting and its prune job would delete a lease row. | A guarded update on a single-row property is a narrow serialisation point, and the fallback path — taken only when the guarded statement cannot be applied at all — writes through the property API and reports `atomic` false rather than failing. An operator who ignores `atomic` false therefore loses the guarantee silently, which is why it is a member of every result and is asserted. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-438** | Require **ownership** to settle a run: `completeRun()` refuses when the caller does not hold the source's claim, and `releaseRunLease()` likewise. | Let any caller settle a `succeeded` marker, as the earlier implementation did. Or check ownership only when the marker is `running`. | Without the check, a late-finishing run could overwrite the marker of the run that legitimately holds the source, moving the cadence stamp to the wrong run and hiding a failure behind another run's success. The check makes the marker's `run` member meaningful: whoever stamped it is whoever claimed it. | A run whose claim expired and was taken over cannot record its own completion, and reports that it does not hold the claim. Correct behaviour rather than a cost: the takeover already transferred the source. | High | — |
| **D-439** | Make an unresolved lead or participating investor reference produce an explicit **partial** outcome that reaches the staging row, the batch counters and the run summary — `code=relationship_incomplete`, `state` `error`, `partial` incremented, `accepted` **not** incremented — while the entity record itself stays written. Implements **D-186**, **D-200** and **D-271** as delivered. | Log the unresolved reference and report the round as processed, which is what the earlier code did. Or reject the whole round and write nothing. | A round reported as processed with a missing lead investor is a silent data-quality loss: the counters agree, the log carries a line nobody reads, and the round looks complete. Rejecting it outright discards a round whose amount, date and startup are all good. Marking it partial keeps the data and makes the incompleteness countable, which is what an operator needs to decide whether to re-run. | A partial round counts in `errors` as well as `partial`, so a batch with many unresolved investors reports errors that are data-quality findings rather than faults. Distinguished by the code, which names the class exactly. | High | — |
| **D-440** | Bound every natural-key match: an **exact normalised equality pass first**, then a tolerant scan capped at `MATCH_SCAN_LIMIT` of **201** rows that stops as soon as a **second** candidate is found, reporting truncation and ambiguity separately. **Amends D-198**: the exact pass may short-circuit only when ambiguity is already decided — two matches — and never on a single match, because a case-variant second row would otherwise stay hidden on a case-sensitive collation. | Short-circuit the tolerant pass whenever the exact pass finds exactly one row, which is the obvious reading of D-198 and what the first draft of this fix did. Or run only the tolerant scan. Or run only the exact query. | The exact query is collation-dependent: on a case-sensitive collation it finds one row and misses its case variant, so short-circuiting on a single match turns a genuine ambiguity into a confident wrong answer — the defect was found by running the fix against the delivered code rather than by reading it. Stopping at two is what makes ambiguity detectable without walking an unbounded set, and 201 rows bounds the scan while leaving the 200-row window one row of headroom to detect its own truncation. | A population with more than 201 candidates sharing a key prefix reports truncation rather than a match, so an ingested row is refused rather than guessed. Intended: the alternative is to attach data to the wrong record. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-441** | Suppress the join table's business rule on **every** participant row inserted or deleted, and close the batch with **one** explicit convergence step that recalculates each affected investor exactly once, reporting `converged` and `recalculated` and marking a non-converged round **partial**. Amends **D-185** and **D-245**. | Leave business rules running on every join write, as the earlier code did, so the count is maintained row by row. Or suppress them and rely on the periodic recalculate-all. | A round linking twelve investors recalculated twelve times, each recalculation a two-path traversal, and the last one was the only one that mattered. Suppressing the per-row rule and converging once is the same result for a fraction of the work — but only if the convergence is not optional, which is why its failure marks the round partial rather than being logged and forgotten. | Suppressing a business rule is exactly the pattern that lets a stored aggregate drift, and it is documented as forbidden everywhere else in the package. The suppression is therefore narrow — join rows only, never an entity write — paired with an asserted convergence, and stated in all three guides so no reader takes it as licence. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-442** | Persist a **controlled outcome code and an opaque reference** in `error_message` — `code=<code> ref=<kind>:<token>` — drawn from a closed fifteen-member vocabulary, and route every raw provider or exception detail to the run log instead. Extends **D-190**, and adds `run_abandoned` as the fifteenth member, written only by a flow's terminal-state sweep. | Persist the reason text, as the earlier code did by storing the caught exception's message. Or persist a code and drop the detail entirely. | The earlier write put whatever a provider or an exception said into a column that an operator lists, exports and screenshots, so upstream text and potentially personal data reached a surface with no redaction on it at all. A code plus an opaque reference is queryable, stable across releases and safe to publish; the detail stays in the log, which is sanitised, level-guarded and prunable. The sweep needed its own code because "the run ended before this row was processed" is neither a cleaning refusal nor a write failure. | Every consumer that matched on reason text had to change with it — three assertions in the test guide, one table in the staging guide and two documents — and a future consumer that queries prose will match nothing. Mitigated by publishing the vocabulary in four places and by asserting the column's exact two-token shape in the test suite. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-443** | **Amended by the delivered step sequence, in which the disposable caller is minted at step 15 rather than step 5; the single-lifecycle principle below stands unchanged.** Standardise **one** REST caller lifecycle — a persistent Basic Auth Configuration shell plus a disposable per-test caller minted at step 5 and retired at step 190 — and remove impersonation from the REST suite entirely. Supersedes the two competing constructions the test guide carried. | Keep both constructions and let the operator choose, as the guide did. Or keep the persistent account with a live password. Or keep the impersonation and mint the caller anyway. | Two constructions meant two cleanup contracts, two residue prefixes and two step numberings, and a suite built from both leaks a live credential exactly when a test fails. Removing the impersonation is not a convenience: steps 5 and 190 write `sys_user`, `sys_user_has_role` and `sys_auth_profile_basic`, none of which a caller holding only a scoped application role can write, so an `Impersonate` step before either would have made the mint fail or the retirement fail **silently**. Nothing in the suite needed an impersonated session — the identity under test authenticates over HTTP. | The suite's fixtures are now written by the operator's own session rather than by a scoped-admin fixture writer, so they do not exercise the application's write controls. Covered elsewhere: suites 1 to 7 write every table under an impersonated scoped admin. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-444** | Move every run-time value the test suite passes between script steps onto the **handoff record** the test creates, and assign no output other than the two stock ones. | Assign custom step outputs and read them, as several scripts did. Or re-derive each value in the consuming step. | A `Run Server Side Script` step exposes exactly `record_id` and `table`; an assignment to any other name is discarded, so the consuming step read `undefined` and asserted against it — a test that runs, passes its own emptiness and proves nothing. Re-deriving is worse for the two window keys, because a boundary that rolls mid-test yields a different pair than the one the threshold was seeded against. The handoff record is the platform's own answer, and the suite already used it for the run token. | One more record read per consuming step, and a step whose constant is left empty fails on the record read rather than silently reading `undefined` — which is the intended failure mode. | Medium | — |
| **D-445** | Bound every traversal in the portfolio derivation: grouped aggregates for both paths, and a **windowed cursor** of `BATCH_SIZE` rows advancing on `sys_id` for the recalculate-all pass. Implements **D-195**. | Keep the unbounded record walks the earlier code used. Or bound only the recalculate-all pass. | The documented design was already a two-stage bounded traversal; the code walked every funding round, every join row and every investor without a limit, so the document and the code disagreed and the cost grew with the table. Aggregates answer the two path questions in one query each, and the cursor makes the write pass a series of finite reads instead of one unbounded one. | A windowed pass sees rows inserted mid-pass in a later window and rows deleted mid-pass not at all, so a concurrent bulk load can leave one investor's count one revision stale. Bounded by the same recalculate-all being safe to re-run, and by the business rules maintaining the count on every ordinary write. | Medium | — |
| **D-446** | Return the **canonical** choice member from `RestQueryHelper.getChoiceParam()`, not the caller's casing. | Return the raw value, as the earlier code did, and let the query match case-sensitively. Or reject a value whose casing differs. | The helper validated case-insensitively and then handed back what the caller typed, so `?industry=fintech` passed validation and then matched no row on a case-sensitive collation — a filter that silently returned an empty page for a value the API had just accepted. The canonicalising helper already existed and was already used by the write-path validator; using it here makes the two paths agree. | A caller that depended on the echo returning its own casing sees the canonical form instead. No delivered consumer does, and the canonical form is what the choice list publishes. | Medium | — |

## 23. Remediation of the final acceptance review

Eleven decisions, `D-447` through `D-457`. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised when the whole delivery — the Update Set, the seventeen package documents, the six sample files, the three governance artifacts and the deck — was read against its own authority documents at the final acceptance checkpoint. The direction of travel is different from every earlier round: **almost nothing here is a new design choice.** The review found that the delivery had begun to contradict itself, in one consistent way — a decision taken late reached some of its consumers and not others. Two rows close a decision that had been recorded and never built (`D-448`, `D-452`); two correct a record that had drifted from a correct artifact (`D-453`, `D-454`); two make an accepted departure and an unavailable procedure explicit where an operator meets them (`D-455`, `D-456`); and one discloses a limit that had been overstated (`D-451`). The rest is mechanics.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-447** | Record this acceptance round as its **own section 23** rather than extending section 22, and correct each superseded row in place with a sentence naming what no longer describes the delivery. Amends D-414. | Extend section 22, which would make the boundary-B round unreadable as the round it was. Or rewrite the superseded rows, which would erase the history Rule 1 exists to keep. Or append corrections as a separate errata list, which would leave a reader of the row itself uninformed. | The same reasoning D-309 and D-414 recorded: a reader arriving at a row needs to learn there that it no longer describes the delivery, and a reader arriving at a section needs to see which review lens produced it. This round's lens is the whole delivery read against its own authority documents, which is a different lens from any earlier one and earns its own section. | The file lengthens again, and a row may now carry two supersession notes from two different rounds — `D-010` and `D-185` both do. Mitigated by every note naming the row that supersedes it, so the chain is followable in one direction. | Low | — |
