| **D-448** | **Complete `D-434` in the artifact.** Set the `x_bst_startuptrk_fundinground.participating_investors` dictionary record to `virtual` `true` with a `calculation` that calls `InvestorPortfolioService.participantList()`, and reduce that method to a **pure reader** returning the derived string. | Reverse `D-434` and deliver the stored projection the earlier rows describe, which would mean re-adding the refresh mechanics of D-185, D-245 and D-273. Or leave the dictionary record as it stood and let the method keep its writer tail. | `D-434` was recorded, its consumers were updated — eleven `data-model.md` sites, `api-reference.md`, and ATF suite 5 step 130, which asserts `virtual` `true` and a calculation naming `participantList` — and the dictionary record was never changed, so the delivery contradicted its own test. The method was left half-edited: its writer tail referenced a `round` variable that does not exist in the function, which is a **guaranteed `ReferenceError`** on the one path that renders the column. Reversing `D-434` was rejected because AAP section 0.8.7 forbids a second stored representation of participation, and because it would have required contradicting a passing test and eleven documents to restore a mechanism whose only merit was that it had been written first. | A calculated column cannot be used in an encoded query or a list sort, which `D-434` already recorded and `data-model.md` already states. The calculation runs on every read of the column, so a list view of many rounds evaluates it many times; the batched reader and the two join-table indexes bound that, and no shipped view sorts on it. | High | — |
| **D-449** | Keep every ordering comparison in the delivered scripts written as a **subtraction tested against the absolute difference**, and document the form **once**, descriptively, at `AppProperties._isBelow`, with a matching descriptive note on the cadence clamp. | Rewrite every comparison to `<` and `>`, which reads more plainly. Or add the reason for the form beside each comparison. Or leave the form undocumented, as it was. | The form is used throughout the delivered scripts, so rewriting it is a wide change for a stylistic gain and a regression surface for no functional one — and one helper already exists as its single home. Explaining the form beside each comparison would put rationale in code, which Rule 1 forbids: the comment says what the expression does and why equality is answered before the subtraction, which a maintainer needs in order to edit it safely, and nothing about why the form was chosen. | A reader meeting `left - right === -Math.abs(left - right)` for the first time still has to stop and read the helper. The comment is the mitigation, and it is at the one place the form is defined. | Low | — |
| **D-450** | **Deliver the run-state write.** Declare `AppProperties.RUN_STATE` as the run-state property suffix; add one guarded writer, `_writeRunState(expected, value)`, performing a single conditional update on the property record whose condition is the value this run read, with a warned `gs.setProperty` fallback that reports `atomic` `false`; have `_writeRunMarker` return that outcome with `atomic` latched across both merge attempts; and have `acquireRunLease` read it. | Leave the machinery as it stood. Or write the property unconditionally through `gs.setProperty`, which is simpler and loses the exclusion entirely. Or move the run state onto a scoped table of its own, which the plan's eleven-property inventory does not admit. | Three defects sat in one subsystem and had one cause — the writer was never finished. `this.RUN_STATE` was read twice and **never declared**, so the property name resolved to `x_bst_startuptrk.undefined`; `_writeRunMarkers()` only serialised the marker set and returned a string, so **nothing was ever written** despite its own comment saying otherwise; and `acquireRunLease` reported `atomic` from an `applied` variable that was never declared, a guaranteed `ReferenceError` on both refusal paths. The consequence was not local: with no marker persisted, `completeRun()` could never stamp `succeeded`, the cadence guard could never advance, and **AAP section 10.0 criterion 4 — three consecutive scheduled runs — was unachievable**. Guides 02 and 03 already published the intended contract in full, down to the `atomic` member, so the fix implements a published contract rather than inventing one. An unconditional write was rejected because the lease is the only thing standing between two executions of one flow. | The guarded update is not a database compare-and-set, which `D-451` records. The fallback path writes through the property API, whose value is cached, which is why every read of the run state goes to the property **record**; and a fallback write reports `atomic` `false` so the run evidence carries it. | High | — |
| **D-451** | State the source lease's guarantee as a **guarded write plus a read-back**, not as a compare-and-set, and disclose the residual window between one execution's write and its read-back — accepting it under prompt section 6.0's prototype focus, on the same footing as the rate-limit counter's read-increment-write race. | Keep the stronger wording, which reads better and is wrong. Or build a second exclusion mechanism — a lease table with a unique key, or a scheduled-job singleton — to make the claim true. Or drop the `atomic` member and say nothing about concurrency. | A scoped script has **no single-statement compare-and-set**: `addQuery('value', expected)` followed by `update()` is a conditional select and then a write, so two executions that both select before either writes can both write, and each then reads back its own marker. The guides claimed "exactly one changes it and the other matches nothing", which is true of the ordinary case and not of that interleaving — and Rule 1 treats an overstated guarantee as a defect, because the next engineer builds on it. Building a second mechanism was rejected on the same ground the plan uses for the counter: prompt section 6.0 asks for correct, testable functionality over production hardening, and the cadence guard already bounds the consequence, since a losing execution finds the cadence unexpired only inside that same window. | The window is real and is now written down: two executions started within it can both ingest one source, duplicating nothing — every write is an upsert on a natural key — but interleaving their evidence. The mitigation named in the guides is to record `atomic` in the run evidence and rely on the cadence guard, and the path deliberately not built is recorded here rather than in the guides. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-452** | **Implement `D-442`'s `error_message` contract in the artifact.** Add `IngestionMapper._stateMessage(entry)` returning `code=<code> ref=<kind>:<token>` with the code drawn from `STATE_CODES` and anything outside it recorded as `unexpected_failure`; write it from `writeStagingState()`; set `unexpected_failure` on the caught-exception path, which had left the initial `rejected` code against `import_state` `error`; and remove the now-unused `REASON_LIMIT`. | Persist the reason prose, as the delivered code did, and re-describe four documents and three test assertions to match it. Or persist the code alone and drop the reference, losing the correlation with the run log line. | `D-442` was recorded, its **vocabulary was declared in the artifact** as `STATE_CODES`, `writeStagingState()`'s own header comment already described the two-token form, its consumers were all updated — guide 06's nine-row code table, the sample-data column contract, and three ATF assertions that query `error_messageCONTAINS code=…` and test the exact two-token shape — and the one write line still persisted `safeText(entry.reason)`. `STATE_CODES` had **zero uses**. Three ATF assertions would therefore have failed, which is an AAP section 0.11.4 coverage-gate failure and not a documentation defect. Persisting the prose was rejected for `D-442`'s own reason: a staging row is retained until an operator clears it, and prose can carry a provider detail or a personal datum onto a surface an operator lists, exports and screenshots. | The composed value is deliberately **not** passed through `safeText()`, whose high-entropy rule would redact a 32-character `sys_id` and destroy the reference; the two tokens are safe by construction instead, the code by vocabulary and the reference by `IngestionLogger.reference()`'s own scrubbing. A future code added to `STATE_CODES` without a guide-06 row would be written but undocumented. | High | — |
| **D-453** | Keep the delivered **JSON-array** participant carrier and correct the record, rather than changing the flattener to pipe-join as `D-373` and `D-416` describe. `readNameList()` accepts a real array, a JSON array serialised as text, or a pipe-delimited string, and reads anything else as one single name; `_flattenNameList()` always emits `JSON.stringify(names)`; no comma is a delimiter at any stage; and there is no carrier selector. | Change `_flattenNameList()` to pipe-join, making the two log rows literally true. Or accept both emitted forms and choose per value, which is what `listCarrier()` was described as doing. | Every shipped CSV fixture carries a JSON array — verified on `crunchbase_funding_rounds_sample.csv` — so JSON is already the one carrier serving both the fallback and the live routes, which is exactly the property `D-416` was recorded to achieve. Pipe-joining would have diverged the emitted form from the shipped fixtures to satisfy a sentence, and a selector reintroduces a per-value branch that JSON makes unnecessary, since a JSON array survives a member containing any character. The rows are corrected in place per `D-447`; the parse-by-shape rule and the ban on the comma, which are the substance of both rows, stand untouched. | An operator hand-editing a staging row may still type a comma, which resolves as one unresolved name and is reported per row. The pipe carrier remains accepted on input and emitted by nothing, which a reader of `readNameList()` alone might not expect; the staging column's field hint names all three accepted carriers. | Medium | — |
| **D-454** | Reconcile every statement of the acceptance contract to **twelve acceptance-blocking checks, eleven of which trigger rollback, and four class-3 diagnostics that block nothing** — correcting six statements left behind by the earlier gate set. | Leave the statements as they stood, since the class table was already correct. Or make the four diagnostics acceptance-blocking, which is what the six statements described. | `D-417` reclassified all four `GATE-SEC` checks as non-normative and added `GATE-SEC-04`, and six statements were not brought with it: `validation-gates.md`'s own roll-up said "15 checks block acceptance" ninety lines below a class table that says twelve; the checklist required "all three security checks" to pass and called each acceptance-blocking beside a table listing four; and four Referenced-documents and section-map entries in three documents described `GATE-SEC-01` to `-03` as acceptance-required. Making them blocking was rejected because `D-417`'s ground stands: the posture they report is not part of any success criterion's pass condition, and a diagnostic that blocks a deployment is a gate whatever it is called. | A reader who accepted an earlier revision's arithmetic will find a different figure here. The class table is the single authority, and every corrected statement now derives from it rather than restating it independently. | Medium | — |
| **D-455** | Accept the **substituted table-gate form explicitly and by name**: the seven table gates read each entity table's `sys_db_object` record rather than a row of the table, which departs from the literal text of AAP section 0.11.2, and the departure is warranted by a named human acceptance in the new acceptance-blocking section **B4a** of the validation checklist rather than by an argument that the two forms are equivalent. Amends D-311. | Keep arguing the equivalence, as `validation-gates.md` did. Or revert `D-310` and open the seven tables so a literal row read works, which is what `D-068` decided. Or record the departure only in this log, where an operator running the deployment would not meet it. | The plan governs, so a departure from it cannot be resolved by the artifact's own reasoning — and the reasoning offered was doing real work in the argument: "asserts strictly more than a row read could" is a true comparison and not a warrant. Reverting `D-310` would reopen the three paths that defeat a field-level read control, which is the material exposure of the whole access-control design. What is left is to make the departure visible to the person who signs: `B4a` is unfilled on delivery, cannot be answered `not applicable`, must be dated on or before sign-off, and the runbook's step 6 now requires it to be recorded as part of that step. `D-068`'s authority principle is honoured rather than set aside: where the artifact **can** change to meet the plan it does, and where it cannot, a human accepts the difference in writing. | An acceptance row can be filled without being read, which no document can prevent. It is bounded by requiring the actor to confirm they have read `D-310`, `D-311` and the sealed-route entry of `gaps-and-flags.md`, and by the row being acceptance-blocking rather than advisory. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-456** | Publish a **three-step manual fallback** for a parser-gate refusal — record `--self-test`, re-run on a conforming runtime, and otherwise leave gates G-1 and G-2 **unrecorded** with delivery blocked — while keeping the gate itself unoverridable; and split the exit-code remedies so that code `1` directs a correction to the XML and code `2` never does. Amends D-351. | Add the `--allow-unpatched-parser` flag `D-351` rejected. Or leave the single-sentence remedy hint, which named a patched runtime and no procedure. Or accept a manual parse or another tool on the refusing runtime as substitute evidence. | `D-351`'s ground against an override stands unchanged — an override that exists is an override that gets used, and the exposure it reopens is realised during the parse it precedes — so the gap the review found is not a missing flag but a missing **procedure**. `--self-test` is the one command that runs on a refusing runtime, because it parses nothing, so it is step 1 and it produces the evidence of what was refused. A substitute at step 3 was rejected substantively rather than procedurally: a parse by eye cannot see the double-escaping defect gate G-2 exists to catch, and another tool on the same runtime realises the very allocation amplification the gate refuses, which none of the byte, payload or record ceilings bounds. | Step 3 blocks delivery on an environment property rather than on a defect in the deliverable, which is the honest outcome and an unwelcome one. An operator may still read "unrecorded" as "passed"; the checklist states that an unrecorded gate is not a passed gate and that the item may not be ticked from a refusal. | Low | — |
| **D-457** | Record the advisory snapshot the delivered disposition already carries — **eleven** identified records, **seven** in range for the pinned `11.4.0` and **four** not, `CVE-2026-50159` withdrawn as unsubstantiable, and `11.16.1` the highest fixing release among the seven — as the log's own statement of the exposure, and mark `D-426` amended. Amends D-426. | Leave `D-426` standing as the log's snapshot and let the log and the disposition record state different exposure sets. Or fold the newer figures into `D-426`'s existing cell without a row of its own, against this log's convention. Or re-state the delivered record at `D-426`'s smaller set. | `D-426` rebuilt the set on 2026-08-08 at seven identified records of which five applied; the delivered disposition record was re-queried on 2026-08-09 and states eleven identified, seven in range, and one withdrawn. Both cannot be the log's position, and the record is the artifact a named person signs, so the log follows it rather than the reverse. Understating the exposure set is the worse direction of error for a **High**-rated risk acceptance, and the reachability answer is unchanged: none of the seven is reachable in the delivered deck, which carries four `flowchart` diagrams, one `timeline`, zero `%%{init` directives, zero `setConfig` or `updateSiteConfig` calls and one `mermaid.initialize` with a literal argument at `securityLevel: 'strict'`. | A snapshot ages again the day after it is taken. Mitigated by the record's review trigger and by its **required** re-query field, which makes a re-query a precondition of the countersignature rather than a suggestion; a signature against a stale snapshot accepts a risk nobody measured. | Low | — |

---
## 24. Remediation of the runtime quality-assurance review

Thirty-two decisions, `D-458` through `D-489`, allocated after the preceding section so no existing identifier moves. Each was taken while closing a runtime review that exercised the two surfaces a reader can actually operate — the executive presentation in a browser, and the portal build guide through a generated surrogate of the five routes it specifies. The lens is therefore narrower and more literal than any earlier section's: **what does the delivered artifact do when it runs**, as distinct from what it says it does. Most rows here change a template, a style rule or a controller obligation; four record a condition accepted against a cited requirement; and three correct a self-check that the artifact asserted about itself and that measurement showed did not hold. Nothing here supersedes an earlier row — where a decision changes an artifact an earlier row created, it names that row.

Three rows are worth finding first, because each closed a defect that **emitted no error of any kind** and so could not have been found by reading the code or watching a console: `D-460`, where one section measured before its diagram had drawn and the export silently produced seventeen sheets instead of sixteen; `D-470`, where an unclamped negative width was discarded by the style parser and left no attribute behind to find, so the only surviving symptom was an accessibility value nobody inspects; and `D-479`, where a template calling a method its controller never declared applied nothing at all, because the expression parser guards a null callee.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-458** | **Detect print view from the class the presentation framework sets on the document element**, and relax the render-eligibility test so a container off the shown slide is still drawn when that class is present. | Force every slide present during export. Or key the relaxation off a print media query. | Export runs under **screen** media with a class on the document element, so a print media query never matches it and would have relaxed nothing. Forcing slides present means moving them through the framework's own state machine during export, which it does not expect. The class is the one signal that is actually true at the moment it matters. | A future framework release could rename the class, at which point export would silently return to drawing nothing off the shown slide. The diagram census in the pre-delivery gate is what would catch it. | Medium | — |
| **D-459** | Subscribe the render pass to the framework's **export-ready event, the browser's before-print event and a print media-query change**, rather than to any one of them. | Subscribe to the export-ready event alone. Or poll until the layout settles. | The three fire in different situations — an export URL, a browser print command, and a device or stylesheet change — and no single one covers all three. Subscribing to all three costs three handlers and removes the case where a diagram is missing because the export was reached by a route nobody tested. | A pass can run more than once for a single export. Made harmless by the render queue being idempotent: a container that already holds a healthy drawing is skipped. | Low | — |
| **D-460** | Fix the seventeen-sheet export with a **structural guard on the unrendered container** — an unrendered, non-errored diagram box is clamped and clipped — rather than by delaying the framework's measurement. | Delay the framework's measurement until every diagram has drawn. Or set an explicit height on every section. Or accept seventeen sheets. | The framework measures each section's scroll height **once**, early, and derives the sheet count from it; one diagram was still raw source at that instant, so its section measured past the single-sheet threshold and was allocated two sheets. Delaying the measurement means reaching into the framework's export sequence, which is not a supported extension point. Explicit heights would have hard-coded a layout the deck otherwise derives. Clamping the *unrendered* state is the narrowest possible intervention: it changes nothing about a drawn diagram, and it makes the early measurement read a box the size the drawn diagram will occupy. | The selector depends on a structural pseudo-class to test for a child drawing. On an engine without it the guard does not apply and the export returns to seventeen sheets. Verified in the delivery browser, and the sheet-count vector is a pre-delivery check rather than an assumption. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-461** | Give the deck its **own print block** that keeps heading weight light on the title, divider and closing slide types and restores the deck's own backgrounds. | Rely on the framework's print theme. Or leave print styling undefined. | The framework's print theme is written for a light document and overrides both the background and the heading weight, so three of the four slide types printed as heavy black type on white — a different artifact from the one on screen. The block re-asserts only what the theme took away. | A rule set that applies only under print, so a change to it is invisible on screen. Mitigated by the export check screenshotting the print view rather than only counting sheets. | Low | — |
| **D-462** | Give the title slide's two pills **content-based width** so they shrink-wrap. | Change the flex rule that stretches them. Or set an explicit width. | The pills are stretched by a rule that matches the *shown* slide, so in export — where nothing is shown — the rule stopped matching and the pills widened to the column. Changing the flex rule would have altered the on-screen layout to fix an export defect. Content-based width is correct in both states, which is why it is the right place to fix it. | None identified: the pills contain a single short run of text in both states. | Low | — |
| **D-463** | Equalise the paired before-and-after diagrams by **matching their coordinate-box extents**, not by constraining their rendered heights. | Set both drawings to one CSS height. Or accept the mismatch. | The two drawings carry different node counts, so equal CSS heights would scale them differently and make the same box read as two sizes across the pair — the comparison the slide exists to make would be distorted by its own layout. Matching the coordinate extents makes one unit mean one thing in both drawings. | The equalisation runs after both drawings complete, so a pair where one diagram fails is left unequalised. The health check reports that case rather than hiding it. | Medium | — |
| **D-464** | Raise the **authored label size through the diagram library's theme-variable object**, having established that the library's own font-size initialisation option is inert. | Pass the size to the library's font-size initialisation option. Or set a font size on the drawing in CSS. | The initialisation option was measured to have **no effect at all** — labels are sized from the theme-variable object, and effective device pixels are the authored size multiplied by the draw scale. A CSS font size would be applied after the library has already measured its label boxes against a different size, which produces boxes the wrong width rather than larger text. | The relationship between authored size and rendered size depends on the draw scale, so a future change to the stage geometry changes the rendered size. The floor is measured at the narrowest supported viewport rather than assumed. | Medium | — |
| **D-465** | Raise the faded navigation chevron's **resting opacity to the lowest value that clears the non-text threshold on all three backgrounds it appears over**, rather than changing its colour. | Change the chevron colour per slide type. Or leave the resting state faint and rely on hover. | One opacity that clears the threshold on the lightest, the divider and the closing background is simpler than three colours and cannot fall out of step with a new slide type. Relying on hover would leave the control invisible to anyone who has not already found it. | A more prominent control at rest than the framework's default. Deliberate: it is the only affordance for moving through the deck. | Low | — |
| **D-466** | Caption the two glyph strips with an **image role and a text label on the group**, rather than adding a visible caption. | Add a visible caption line beneath each strip. Or remove the strips. | A visible caption would have added words to two content slides, and both were already at or near the word cap this same review had to bring four other slides under — the fix would have created the defect it was adjacent to. A group label gives the strip a single accessible name and hides its individually meaningless glyphs, which is what the strip actually is: one composite illustration. Removing the strips would have left two slides without the non-text visual the presentation rule requires on every slide. | A sighted reader gets no caption. Accepted: the strip illustrates the heading beside it rather than carrying content of its own. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-467** | Lay the measure panels out as a **wrapping flex row above the medium breakpoint**, so a short final row aligns with the first. | Leave the grid to wrap on its own, which is what the guide claimed it did. Or fix the panel count. | The guide asserted the row wrapped cleanly and it did not: five panels produced three ragged bands, the second offset from the first, because the per-position spans of `D-145` fill a row only when the count divides it. Equal-height flex items wrap to two clean rows at every supported count. Fixing the count would have removed a configuration the widget already offers. **Amends `D-145`**, which chose the spans; the spans remain, the wrapping is what changed. | A flex row on a raw container is the one layout in this portal not built from grid primitives, so it is an exception to the design-system rule and is scoped above the medium breakpoint to keep the floor on the grid. Recorded as an admissible exception rather than left implicit. | Medium | — |
| **D-468** | **Delete both progress overrides** so the theme's own track and bar tokens apply, rather than correcting the override that broke the bar. | Move the colour declaration onto the bar element so the inherited value resolves to the brand colour. Or set the bar colour explicitly. | The override existed only to restate values the theme already supplies, and restating them is what introduced the defect: the bar inherited a colour from an ancestor and painted white on a near-white track at **1.05 to 1**. Correcting the declaration would have kept a redundant rule and left the same class of defect one edit away. Deleting it makes the rendered pair equal the contrast row that documents it, which is the property worth having. | The widget now depends on the theme declaring both tokens. That is guaranteed by the theme declaration block, and the contrast check reads the same two values, so a drift fails a gate rather than a screen. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-469** | **Publish the axis coordinate from the server** and place each value label immediately past its own bar's end. | Draw the labels in a fixed right-hand column. Or omit the axis. | Every label sat at one fixed abscissa regardless of bar length, which on the shortest drawn bar left a **305-pixel gap** between a bar and the number belonging to it — close enough to the next row to be read against the wrong bar. With no zero line there was also nothing to establish where the bars began. Publishing the coordinate keeps every geometry value on the server, which is what lets the widget's own styling stay free of literals. | Two more values carried per render. No exposure: both are derived from constants the server already holds. | Medium | — |
| **D-470** | Clamp the computed share **onto the bucket member itself**, so one statement bounds the drawing width, the companion table width and the accessibility value together. | Clamp into a local variable and use it for the drawing only. Or clamp in each of the three consumers. | The first attempt did exactly the first alternative, and the defect it left is the reason this row exists: the drawing was bounded while the published member stayed raw, so the accessibility value went out of its own declared range at **minus thirteen against a floor of zero** — with no visual symptom to find it by, because an invalid length is discarded by the parser and leaves no attribute behind at all. Clamping once, at the source, makes the three consumers agree by construction. | A consumer that legitimately wanted the unbounded value can no longer read it. None does; the truthful unbounded figure is the count, which is deliberately left alone. | High | — |
| **D-471** | **Leave the bucket count unclamped** so a negative movement is still displayed as negative, while the derived share is bounded. | Clamp both, so nothing negative appears anywhere. Or clamp neither. | The two carry different obligations. The share is a **geometry input** and a negative one is meaningless — it produces an invalid width. The count is a **reported fact**, and a period in which a measure fell is exactly the thing a trend panel exists to show; suppressing the sign would be a false statement about the data. Clamping neither leaves the defect of `D-470`. | A reader sees a negative number beside a bar with no drawn extent. That is the honest rendering of a negative movement on a chart whose axis begins at zero, and the companion table carries the value in text. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-472** | Let the drawing's **aspect ratio govern its height**, rather than pairing a full-width drawing with a fixed pixel height. | Keep the fixed height. Or set both dimensions in pixels. | A drawing declared full-width with a fixed height resolves to a different aspect ratio at every viewport, so the box reserved before layout does not match the box the drawing occupies after it — which is what a layout-shift measurement attributes the shift to. Letting the ratio govern makes the reserved and final boxes the same. | The panel height now varies with viewport width, so a very narrow viewport produces a tall panel. Below the declared floor, which the scope excludes. | Low | — |
| **D-473** | Give the list item a **block formatting context** so a wrapped floated element contributes to its height, and apply it to all three widgets that float inside a list item rather than only the one where the defect was reported. | Add a clearing pseudo-element. Or set overflow on the item. Or fix only the widget named in the finding. | A float that wraps to a second line leaves the item's height measured from the text alone, so the overflow **occludes the following row** rather than sitting beside it. A block formatting context is the mechanism for that and expresses the intent directly; a clearing pseudo-element achieves it as a side effect, and setting overflow would clip anything that legitimately extends past the box. Three widgets float inside a list item and the finding named one — fixing only that one would have left the same defect twice over, waiting for different data to expose it. | A containment behaviour rather than a measured length, so it is not a design-system token and had to be recorded as an admissible exception. Support is universal in the delivery browser. | Medium | — |
| **D-474** | Make the wrapped chip run breakable by giving **the chip itself inline-block display**, with right alignment on the float to hold the wrapped run in place — a choice made by **measuring seven candidates in an isolated probe**, not by reasoning. | Widen the float. Or give the float inline-block display. Or make the item a flex container. Or normalise word spacing. | The probe settled it: two of those alternatives render **byte-identical images to applying no fix at all** — the float's width constraint was already unset, and floating re-blockifies an inline-block box — and a third merely moves the overhang from one side to the other without ever wrapping. The measured cause is that the chips form a run of inline boxes with **no break opportunity between them**, so the run's minimum and maximum intrinsic widths are equal at 355.5 pixels and a shrink-to-fit float cannot get narrower than its content. Making each chip an atomic inline-level box introduces the break opportunity, taking the minimum to 70 pixels. | A four-chip run whose maximum width already fits stays on one line. Correct rather than a defect: forcing a wrap where there is slack would be a new one. | Medium | — |
| **D-475** | Align the caption bindings with the **published member names and retire the competing publication block**, so exactly one place assigns onto the payload. | Rename the published members to match the template. Or publish both names. | Two publication schemes existed for the same two figures, the template read the names from the scheme that was not the survivor, and the result was a caption bound to nothing beside output nobody read. Publishing both names would have preserved the ambiguity that caused it. The mechanical rule — one block, named in the contract, is the only place a member is assigned — is what makes the class of defect unrepeatable rather than fixed once. | A consumer written against the retired names breaks. None exists outside this guide, and the contract sweep across all eight widgets confirms every template reference now resolves to a declared member. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-476** | **Derive the construction-time busy value from whether a payload is already present**, rather than asserting it true. | Assert it true and clear it on first load. Or drop the flag. | The guide asserted the flag was true from construction until the first payload arrived, which cannot happen on this platform: the server script runs **before** the widget is delivered, so the payload is already present when the controller constructs, and the only thing that cleared the flag never ran on the initial render. Every one of the eight widgets therefore kept a progress element in the document, reported itself busy indefinitely and **never announced any outcome**. Deriving the value makes the two channels agree in both situations. Urgency came from an adjacent fix giving that element a visible width — shipping both without this would have turned a permanently invisible bar into a permanently visible one on all five routes. | A widget that genuinely renders before its payload would start un-busy. No such widget exists here; every one is server-rendered. | High | — |
| **D-477** | Resolve the **focus destination at the moment of the move**, preferring a marked element inside the acting widget and falling back to the widget root. | Bind the destination once at construction. Or always move focus to the widget root. Or move it to the heading. | The heading that would be the natural destination **does not exist while the failure being retried is on screen**, so a destination bound at construction, or fixed on the heading, resolves to nothing in exactly the situation focus must be moved. The fallback is not theoretical: on the account route with its body gated away, no marked descendant survives and the root is the only destination left — measured, with the indicator painted on it. | Focus can land on a container rather than a heading, which reads less specifically. Preferred to losing focus to the document body, which is what happened before. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-478** | Make the space key activate the anchors styled as buttons, **rather than converting them to button elements**. | Convert the seven anchors to buttons. Or leave the space key inert. | They are real navigations with real addresses, so converting them would discard the address, the middle-click and the open-in-new-tab a reader expects of a link, to gain a key binding. The profile tab strip already binds the key this way and is the precedent the other seven were never extended from. Leaving it inert meant the key **scrolled the page by 390 pixels and produced no activation at all**, measured on every one of them. | A handler on seven elements rather than an element choice made once. Mitigated by the handler being one shared method. | Low | — |
| **D-479** | Supply the indeterminate bar's width from the **controller**, and require every widget that renders one to declare that method in its own controller items. | Declare the width in the widget styling. Or give the bar a determinate value. | The framework's own stylesheet ships the bar at zero width so a determinate bar can be driven inline, so a bar with no width source **paints nothing inside a full-width trough** — which is indistinguishable from the element being absent. A percentage in widget styling is a measured value the design system forbids; a bound value is not a declaration, which is the same reasoning the chart geometry already relies on. The controller-items requirement exists because the template half alone is not enough: the expression parser guards a null callee, so a template that calls an undeclared method applies nothing **without throwing and without a console message**. That precise failure was reproduced on the fourth widget and measured at a zero ratio before being closed. | A third exception to the no-literal rule, and a template-and-controller pair that must be changed together. Both are recorded, and the pair is stated as a failure mode in the guide so it cannot drift again. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-480** | Gate stale content away behind **wrapper elements**, and deliberately leave the escape-hatch control outside the gate. | Gate each element individually. Or leave the content beside the error. | A failure that leaves the previous result set on screen presents stale data as current, and the profile widget already replaced its content on failure — so the inconsistency was between widgets rather than a missing behaviour. One wrapper per region is fewer conditions than one per element and cannot be partially applied. The escape hatch stays outside because gating it would leave a failed route with an error message and no way out of it. | A wrapper element with no purpose other than gating. Accepted as the smaller cost. | Low | — |
| **D-481** | Have the three display helpers return the **empty string** for input they cannot use. | Return the input unchanged. Or return a zero-valued rendering. | Returning the input unchanged is the defect being fixed — it is what put a currency prefix and raw dates on screen. A zero rendering is worse than either: it states a value the data does not contain, and a reader cannot distinguish it from a real zero. An empty cell is the only rendering that is not a false statement. | A malformed value renders as a blank rather than announcing itself. The run log is where a malformed payload is diagnosed; the screen is not. | Medium | — |
| **D-482** | Take the focus indicator's **width and offset from the radius scale**, and record why. | Write the two lengths as literals. Or use a spacing token. | The design system publishes **no outline-width variable** — the framework removes the outline rather than styling it — so there is no semantically matching token. Literals are forbidden by the portal's own rule, and the spacing scale's nearest values are one and five pixels with a meaning of padding rather than stroke. The radius pair is the only three- and four-pixel lengths the scale defines, which keeps the declaration on the scale and free of literals. | Token names that describe a corner while sizing a stroke, which a later reader could "correct" into a literal. Recorded in two places for exactly that reason. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-483** | Enter the focus indicator in the contrast contract at the **worse of its two backgrounds**, as a row of its own. | Omit it, since both its numbers already appear. Or add one row per background. | The indicator produces no number the data-graphic rows do not, but it is a **different requirement** — those rows are graphics conveying data, this is the focus indicator the shared contract puts a three-to-one floor under, and it is the only pair whose background varies by widget. Entering it at the worse background makes the check fail if **either** surface regresses, which one row per background would not improve on. | A row whose numbers duplicate two others, which reads as redundancy. A paragraph beside it states why it is not. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-484** | Preserve the untrimmed input value in the model and **write the normalised value back on submission**. | Trim on input. Or leave the divergence. | Trimming as the reader types moves the caret and prevents typing a space between words. Leaving it produced a three-way divergence in which what was displayed, what was held and what was searched for all differed, so the visible field misrepresented the search that ran. Writing back at submission normalises at the one moment the value becomes a query. | The visible value changes at submission, which is a small surprise. Preferred to a field that disagrees with its own results. | Low | — |
| **D-485** | Bring four content slides under the word cap by **shortening prose and terse table cells**, keeping every figure and every table row. | Split the slides. Or convert the tables into diagrams, whose source text the count does not see. Or leave them over. | This repeats the reasoning of **`D-137`** against four slides it did not reach, and reaches the same conclusion: the cap is a statement about density, so splitting preserves the prose while inflating the slide count, and moving prose into a diagram satisfies the measurement rather than the intent. Every removed word was editorial rather than factual — a reworded footnote, four shortened cells, two shortened labels — and the four slides moved from 42, 43, 44 and 44 words to 39, 39, 39 and 39. **Amends `D-137`.** | Terser cells. Three further slides sit at exactly the cap with no headroom, so any future addition to them breaches it; stated here because the measurement is not part of any automated gate. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-486** | Give the portal criterion an **explicit entry condition and a third recorded outcome of unevaluated**, distinct from not met. | Leave the criterion as a met-or-not-met pair. Or record an unbuilt portal as not met. | This is the only success criterion whose subject **does not exist when the deployment finishes** — the portal is built by hand afterwards, because only tables carrying the synchronisation attribute are captured into an update set and the portal's tables do not. Every required post-commit gate reads a table, a role or the scope, and not one touches a portal record, so a fully accepted deployment can have five unbuilt routes. Recording that as not met asserts a walkthrough happened and failed; the third outcome states what is true. The sibling criterion already carries a third outcome, so the form is established rather than invented. | A third outcome an operator could reach for to avoid doing the walkthrough. Bounded by four record counts that must be confirmed before the walkthrough begins, and by the run book naming the same two criteria at the handoff. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-487** | Publish the withheld-field list as **objects carrying both the token and its label**, correcting the contract and the server rather than the template. | Publish label strings and repeat by index. Or publish strings and key on the label. | The template already repeated on a token key and rendered a label member, so a string array would have failed twice — every tracking key resolving alike, which the framework rejects outright as soon as two fields are withheld, and every label rendering blank. The token is the stable identity: two columns can share a display label, as one of these six does across two tables, while the token is unique in the allowlist. Keying on the label would reintroduce the collision. **Notable for how it was found** — a runtime surrogate could not surface it, because the surrogate was written from the template and so implemented the objects the template needed; only reading the contract against the template exposed it. | A contract row that is now more specific than the prose that built it. The per-widget contract sweep is what keeps the two aligned. | Medium | — |
| **D-488** | Replace the inert horizontal chip margin with a **vertical margin on the list item**, and narrow the checklist row that forbade a font size in widget styling to name its one sanctioned exception. | Delete the inert rule and add nothing. Or make the chips flow horizontally. Or delete the font-size declaration to satisfy the row as written. | The chips are one per line, each the only inline child of its own block item, so a horizontal margin has nothing to separate from and a vertical margin on the inline chip would not affect the line box — the item is the only element that can carry it; measured at zero separation before and six pixels after. Making them flow horizontally would have reintroduced the unbreakable-run problem of `D-474`. The checklist row was the mirror image: it forbade every font size, while the chart's drawn labels have no framework class that sizes them and their size is **load-bearing geometry** the axis alignment depends on — so the declaration was right and the row was over-absolute. Deleting the declaration to satisfy the row would have broken the alignment to satisfy a sentence. | A named exception in a checklist row invites a second. The row states that any further occurrence is a defect. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-489** | **Accept four reported conditions against a cited requirement** rather than changing behaviour: no offline capability, layout shift below the viewport floor, controls below the enhanced target size, and a mapped-but-unused dialogue component. | Vendor the presentation's dependencies locally for offline use. Or refine the layout below the floor. Or enlarge every control. Or introduce a dialogue to justify the mapping. | Each is closed by a requirement rather than by judgement, and the citation is the whole justification. Vendoring is **forbidden** by the presentation rule that mandates network-loaded pinned dependencies and no local file dependency, so offline non-capability is a consequence of a mandated design. The plan excludes responsive design below the declared floor, and the shift is measured only beneath it — the underlying aspect-ratio cause was nonetheless fixed at `D-472`. The target-size finding is an enhanced-level criterion, not the level this delivery targets, and the conformance-level audit passes; the design system's own control sizes produce those targets, and enlarging them would breach the rule that outranks an accessibility heuristic. The dialogue component is **mapped as available** in the design-system inventory, which is not a requirement to use one, and no route needs it. | Four conditions ship as reported. Each names the requirement that permits it, so a reader can disagree with the requirement rather than assume it was missed. | Medium | `DEV` |
## 25. Remediation of the final deployment and platform-provisioning review

Three decisions, `D-490` through `D-492`. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised when the delivered package was read against the deployment lifecycle it has to survive — the upload, the preview, the commit, the eleven post-commit gates and the configuration the running application reads. The lens is narrower than section 13's: that section settled *which mechanisms the target instance accepts*, and this one settles *what the delivery says about itself and what happens when the transport misbehaves*. Two of the three arise from measurements taken against a hibernating instance, which is itself the reason the third is documentation rather than code: the review could observe the delivery's static claims and its edge's behaviour, and could not observe the platform, so nothing here changes a behaviour the review was unable to exercise.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-490** | Correct the Update Set header's own `<description>` to the file's true composition — **313 update records and 77 choice values**, not 314 and 78 — and change nothing else about the artifact. | Leave the header alone on the ground that the platform stores `description` as free text, parses no count from it, and imports identically either way. Or restate 314 as the normative figure and change the eleven documents and the gate that say 313. Or remove the counts from the description entirely so no figure can drift. | The figure was wrong in exactly one place and right in eleven: `validation-gates.md` requires `PRE-COMMIT-01` to read a count of exactly **313**, and the package README, the validation checklist, the deployment runbook, the traceability matrix, this log and the executive deck all state 313. The error propagated from the choice count alone — the description's own enumeration sums to 314 with 78 choices and to 313 with 77 — so one substitution repairs both numbers and the sentence becomes self-checking. Leaving it would have been harmless to the import and harmful to the operator: after the load, the retrieved update set displays its `description` beside the record count, so a person cross-checking the two would read a one-record shortfall inside the delivered package and could stop a complete import as incomplete. Restating 314 would have propagated a wrong number into eleven documents and a gate. Removing the counts would have removed the only place the artifact describes itself, and the description is what makes the header legible on an instance where the file is no longer to hand. | The description is prose, so it can drift again as the artifact changes; nothing in the platform enforces the agreement. Mitigated by the figure now being derivable from the file — the enumeration sums to the claimed total, so a future edit that changes one without the other is detectable by reading the sentence rather than by trusting it. Both substitutions are the same length, so the artifact is still 1,455,999 bytes; its SHA-256 moves, and no document pins that digest. | Low | — |
| **D-491** | Guard the **delivered header identifier** with a pre-upload check rather than making it unique per run: query `sys_remote_update_set` by the fixed `sys_id` the file carries, require `HTTP 404` before step 1, and clear any record holding it through the existing guarded removal procedure. Extends the run identity of **D-165**, which isolates by `name`. | Mint a fresh identifier into the header on every run, and rewrite the 313 `<remote_update_set>` values to match. Or rely on the existing name-based snapshot and treat whatever appears under `{RUN_NAME}` as this run's record. Or drop `<sys_id>` from the header and let the platform allocate one. | The identifier cannot be randomised: it is what links all 313 customer updates to their header, it is what gate `G-1` asserts, and a header whose identifier no longer matches the 313 `<remote_update_set>` values loads with nothing attached — `PRE-COMMIT-01` would then read a count of `0`. Nor can the name-based snapshot cover it, and this is the failure the check exists to catch: because the identifier is fixed, a load against a record that already holds it is an **update**, which takes the incoming `<name>` — so the pre-existing record is renamed to `{RUN_NAME}` and then satisfies the "exactly one record under this name" assertion while still carrying an earlier build's updates and state. The QA run of 2026-08-09 found exactly that record on the target instance, `previewed` with `summary=309` from a 309-record build. A check by identifier is one read, it is decisive, and it hands the deletion to the procedure that already requires ownership to be proven. | The check adds a read before step 1 and a stop condition an operator must act on, and where ownership of the holding record cannot be proven the deployment halts rather than proceeding — deliberately, since the alternative is loading over work that may not be this deployment's. Two parallel clones must now serialise the load-preview-commit window, which `CLONE_INDEX` alone never achieved; that constraint existed before this row and was simply unstated. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-492** | Define a **retryable server error** as `HTTP 500`, `502`, `503` **or** `504` and apply the single 30-second retry to the whole class, rather than to `HTTP 500` alone. The widening applies to **idempotent reads only** — every pre-flight, every poll, both preview-problem reads, both pre-commit checks and every gate — and changes no non-idempotent rule. Extends **D-166**, whose per-operation precedence it leaves intact. | Keep the literal `HTTP 500` of the deployment brief's failure matrix. Or retry the whole `5xx` range including statuses the edge does not emit. Or retry a transport failure and a `200` carrying HTML as well, on the reasoning that both are also "not a real answer". | Requests do not reach the platform directly; they pass through an edge that answers in its own right, and that edge was measured returning `502 Bad Gateway` on between 21.7 % and 25.0 % of requests issued in parallel. Under the literal reading a single momentary `502` on a required gate fails that gate on its first response — and a failing required gate **initiates the rollback**, so the narrower rule could have destroyed a deployment that committed correctly. That is the asymmetry the widening removes: the cost of one extra 30-second read is trivial, and the cost of a false rollback is the installation. `502`, `503` and `504` are statements about the transport, exactly as `500` is. A `200` carrying HTML is deliberately **excluded**, because a hibernating or unauthenticated instance answers that way and no wait resolves it — retrying it would convert an immediate, correct abort into a delayed one. The plan's own wording supports the class over the status: it words this rule as "retry once after thirty seconds on a **server error**". | The delivery is now wider than the deployment brief's literal `HTTP 500` row, so a reviewer comparing the two finds a difference; it is called out in the Rule 3 review's entry 2 rather than left to be discovered. A persistently failing edge costs one extra 30-second wait per read before the same abort, and a gate that is genuinely broken behind a `503` is retried once before failing. Neither changes an outcome. | Medium | `DEV` |

---

## 26. Remediation of the frontend and deck quality review

Thirty-four decisions, `D-493` through `D-526`. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised when the executive deck and the eight Service Portal widget specifications were read and rendered against the design system, the accessibility rules and the plan's own constraints. Fourteen of them, `D-493` to `D-506`, are the deck's own render and degradation decisions and are cited by identifier in the deck's comments. The remainder cover the widget specifications, four shared rules the review established across all eight widgets, and the package's documentation consistency. Where a row amends an earlier one it says so, and the earlier row carries a sentence naming what no longer describes the delivery.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-493** | Hide an unrendered Mermaid source with `pre.mermaid:not([data-processed]) { visibility: hidden }`, and contain its overflow, in the deck's layout block **only** — never in the mirrored theme block. | Leave the source visible until the library replaces it, as delivered. Or set `display: none`, which the library needs the box for. Or put the rule in the theme block. | The library rewrites the element in place and marks it `data-processed`, so before it runs the raw graph definition paints as monospace text on the slide. `visibility: hidden` keeps the box in the flow, which the renderer measures, while painting nothing. The theme block is byte-identical to `blitzy-deck/references/blitzy-reveal-theme.css` by Rule 2, so a rule added there would break that identity; the layout block carries no such obligation. | A source that is never processed stays invisible. Mitigated by `D-503`, which gives every diagram a static note, and by the terminal-failure state that replaces the source with a visible notice and is exempted from this rule. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-494** | Carry a `<noscript>` notice **and** a `deck-degraded` marker class that restores the sixteen slides as one readable stacked document when scripting or the presentation engine is unavailable. | Leave the no-script case unhandled, as delivered. Or ship a second static document. Or inline the engine. | The engine's own stylesheet is what hides every slide but the current one, so with scripting blocked the page carries sixteen slides the browser refuses to paint and the reader sees nothing at all. A marker class removed by the first inline script means a scripted load never paints the fallback and an unscripted one keeps it. Rule 2 forbids a build step and a local file dependency, so a second document is not available. | The stacked document is not the authored reading order's pagination and loses the slide framing. Accepted: a readable stacked document is worth more than a blank page. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-495** | Call the deck's existing failure notice when `configureMermaid()` returns false, rather than only when the library is present and a diagram throws. | Leave the branch unreachable, as delivered. Or drop the notice entirely and let the container stay blank. | The notice was reachable only while the library **was** loaded — the one case that does not need it. A blocked or failed library returned false and the deck reported nothing, which is the case a reader most needs told. | One more branch through the failure path. Mitigated by routing every remote-asset failure into the single banner `D-494` establishes, so two failures read as two sentences rather than two competing overlays. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-496** | Paint a Lucide-missing icon placeholder with a **declared gradient** in its declared box, and state the cause in the banner, rather than leaving a transparent hole. | Leave the empty `<i data-lucide>` element, as delivered. Or substitute a Unicode glyph. Or drop the icon. | Rule 2 requires every slide to carry at least one non-text visual, and on several slides the only visual is an icon strip. An unresolved icon element paints nothing, so the slide silently stops satisfying the rule. A Unicode substitute is unavailable because the same rule forbids emoji outright. | The placeholder is not the authored iconography and a reader may take it for a design element. Mitigated by the banner naming the cause and by `D-497`'s caption carrying the strip's meaning in words. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-497** | Caption each icon strip in visible text, restating in words the meaning the strip already carries as an accessible name. | Leave the accessible name as the only carrier, as delivered. Or label each icon individually. | An icon strip whose meaning exists only in an accessible name is unavailable to a sighted reader of a projected deck, which is this artifact's primary reading mode. One caption per strip carries it without repeating the accessible names or spending the slide's word budget on twelve labels. | One more line against Rule 2's forty-word content limit. Verified within the limit on every slide carrying a strip. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-498** | Declare the body typeface on the **document element** as well as on the deck root. | Leave it on the deck root alone, as delivered. Or set it only on `body`. | A computed-style read on `body` reported a browser default rather than the deck's own stack, so the deck's typography could not be verified by measurement even though it rendered correctly. Declaring it in both places makes the rendered result and the measured result agree. | One redundant declaration. No behavioural effect, since both resolve to the same declared stack. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-499** | Honour `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` by **zeroing the slide-change and pause-overlay transition durations**, while leaving Rule 2's mandated `transition: 'slide'` name in place. | Set the engine's transition to `none` under the query, which would contradict the mandated value. Or ignore the preference. | The engine drives both from a CSS transition, so zeroing the durations removes the movement without touching the configuration Rule 2 fixes. Setting the engine option would have made the delivered configuration disagree with the rule that mandates it. | A reader who expects no motion still gets an instantaneous change rather than an explicit step. Verified: eight durations at `0s` and zero running animations, against twenty-five in the control. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-500** | Give the navigation chevrons and the progress bar a `forced-colors: active` treatment built from **system colour keywords**, and accept that this is the one place in the deck where a value is not a palette token. | Leave the palette values, which forced colours discards. Or hide the controls in that mode. | Forced colours replaces author colours with system ones, so a palette token is exactly what that mode removes — a control painted only in palette values becomes invisible. Rule 2 requires the full palette applied, and this is a stated departure from it rather than an oversight. An unavailable direction is left alone, because the engine withholds it with `visibility` and a control that leads nowhere should stay unpainted. | A departure from the palette rule. Bounded to two controls and one media query, and recorded here for that reason. Verified at 17.40:1 forward, 21.00:1 back and 11.31:1 on the progress fill. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-501** | Carry a tighter list rhythm on the slides where a list sits beside a diagram at its height cap. | Use one rhythm everywhere and let the stack overflow. Or shorten the lists further. | The invariant the height cap serves is that the whole stack fits the mandated 1920-by-1080 stage, which does not scroll. Shortening the lists further would have cost content the slide needs; a tighter rhythm on those slides alone costs nothing else. | Two rhythms in one deck. Bounded to the slides that pair a list with a capped diagram. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-502** | Reserve the vertical space a diagram will occupy **per size class**, each value being the tallest container that class produces across the supported 1024-to-1920 range. | Reserve nothing and accept the reflow when the drawing arrives. Or reserve one worst-case height for every diagram. | `D-493` makes the container the exact height its drawing will occupy, so without a reservation the slide reflowed when each diagram appeared. One worst-case height would have left visible dead space beside every smaller diagram. | The values are measured maxima, so a future diagram larger than its class would reflow again. Verified: 0.47px and 0.00px shift and zero layout-shift entries on a hard-cut arrival. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-503** | Give every diagram a **static note in words** beneath it, and let the container leave the flow rather than hold a reserved blank area, when no diagram can be drawn. | Leave the reserved area blank. Or show the raw graph source, which is what the deck did before `D-493`. | A reader whose browser is not running scripts cannot draw a diagram and cannot read its source usefully either. The note carries the diagram's meaning in the same place; dropping the reservation stops the slide carrying a blank region no longer explaining anything. | The notes duplicate the diagrams for every reader, and must be kept in step with them. Both express the same before-and-after mapping the traceability matrix states, which is the single source they are checked against. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-504** | Restore focus to a navigation chevron **statelessly**, testing `:focus-visible` at the moment of restoration rather than recording that a previous interaction was a keypress. | Track the last input modality in a variable, as first attempted. Or leave focus where the engine puts it. | The stateful form left a ring behind after a pointer click, because the recorded modality outlived the interaction that set it. Testing `:focus-visible` at restoration asks the browser the question directly, and a browser that cannot evaluate it skips the behaviour rather than guessing. | A browser without `:focus-visible` gets no restoration. Accepted: the alternative was a visible ring after every mouse click. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-505** | Remap the Mermaid connector colour to the body text palette value, **superseding `D-134`**, so a connector measures 12.63:1 against its card rather than 2.85:1. | Keep the muted palette value `D-134` chose. Or declare a new non-palette colour. | Both are declared palette values, so the palette is applied either way and the choice is between two members of it. A connector is the element that carries a diagram's meaning, and at 2.85:1 it was the faintest ink on the slide. | The connectors are now visually heavier than `D-134` intended. Accepted: a diagram whose edges cannot be followed conveys nothing. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-506** | Set the engine's `history` option true, so a slide change pushes a browser history entry. | Leave it false, as delivered. Or intercept the Back button. | With it false the browser's Back button left the deck rather than stepping back a slide, which is not what a reader of a paginated document expects. The option implies hash routing, which is what Rule 2 mandates, so the mandated hash behaviour is unchanged. | A deck stepped through to the end leaves sixteen history entries behind it. Accepted as the ordinary behaviour of a paginated document. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-507** | Contain a long data value with a **Bootstrap grid row** — a label column beside a right-aligned value column — rather than with a float, a clearfix or a percentage `max-width`. | Add a clearfix, which contains a float vertically but does nothing about a float wider than its row. Or cap the float with `max-width: 60%`, which is what the finding suggested. | A `.pull-right` float with `max-width: none` cannot fit beside its label when the value is long, so it wraps to a second line, overflows its own row and is painted through by the opaque row below. The percentage cap is not available: the guide's zero-hard-coded-values rule admits no percentage literal in widget CSS. The grid is what the same rule set prescribes anyway, it carries Bootstrap's own clearfix, and a grid column cannot overflow because it is width-constrained by construction. | Two more elements per row than a float needs. Verified at 991, 992, 1024, 1100, 1199 and 1200 pixels: no adjacent row pair overlaps and the value hit-tests to itself at every sample point, where the float overlapped the row below by 10.00px across the whole 1024-to-1199 band. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-508** | Give every widget that renders a Bootstrap `.label` a `forced-colors: active` border, scoped to that widget's own root. | Declare it once globally on `.label`. Or set `forced-color-adjust: none`, which was the first attempt. | Forced colours discards `background-color`, which on a `.label` is the only thing distinguishing a status chip from bold body text. `forced-color-adjust: none` measured 2.09:1 in that mode and defeats the mode's purpose; `border-style: solid` measured 21.00:1. A global rule would restyle every `.label` on the instance, which the guide's shallow-selector rule forbids. | The same rule appears in six stylesheets and must stay in step. Stated once as a shared rule and asserted per widget at runtime. | Low |  |
| **D-509** | Let a `.label` that carries **free text** wrap, with `white-space: normal` and `overflow-wrap: break-word`, wherever it sits inside a `.list-group-item`. | Leave Bootstrap's `nowrap`. Or truncate with an ellipsis. Or shorten the data. | Bootstrap's `nowrap` suits a one-word marker and defeats anything longer: the chip becomes a single unbreakable box intrinsically wider than its column, and its content escapes right into the next column, which is a later sibling and paints over it. Measured, a focus-area chip ran 58.73px past its cell at 1366 and 97.63px at 1024, losing a third of its own width. Truncation would hide data the column exists to show. | A wrapped chip is taller than a marker chip. Bounded to the two chip texts with no length bound, a Multi-choice value and a role name. | Low |  |
| **D-510** | Give every `.table-responsive` wrapper `tabindex="0"`. | Leave them unfocusable. Or replace the wrapper with a non-scrolling table. | Bootstrap makes the wrapper `overflow-x: auto`, and a region that scrolls but cannot receive focus cannot be scrolled from the keyboard at all. All six wrappers carry it, so the behaviour cannot differ between two tables a caller reads in the same session. | Six more tab stops. Accepted: a scrollable region unreachable by keyboard is worse. | Low |  |
| **D-511** | Have every widget that renders an `.sr-only` node **re-declare the three properties that hide it**, and **remove** the rule from the one widget that renders no such node. | Depend on Bootstrap alone. Or re-declare all six of Bootstrap's declarations, including `width: 1px`. Or keep the rule everywhere for symmetry. | `.sr-only` is a Bootstrap class, so a widget rendering one depends on a stylesheet it does not own for the one thing it cannot afford to get wrong — measured with Bootstrap absent, the node became a static full-width block and the status sentence rendered as visible prose above the heading it displaced. `width: 1px` is a measured literal the zero-hard-coded-values rule forbids, and is unnecessary: an absolutely positioned box clipped to a zero rect paints nothing whatever its size. The search widget renders neither a status region nor a table caption, so its rule had nothing to act on and dead weight in a stylesheet is not carried. | Five stylesheets carry the same three declarations. Chosen to be exactly Bootstrap's own values, so the computed style is unchanged whenever Bootstrap is present and the rule is invisible. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-512** | Have each host **re-state the rules of the embedded `bst-premium-upsell` partial that nothing else on the page supplies**, because a partial embedded with `$sp.getWidget()` has no instance and its CSS field may never be compiled into the page. | Depend on the partial's own CSS field. Or move the rules into the theme's CSS include, which this portal does not have. Or copy the partial's whole stylesheet into every host. | Service Portal compiles widget CSS per page and prefixes each rule with a class derived from the widget record, so what arrives is the CSS of the widgets instanced on that page — and this partial is instanced nowhere. Measured in a render where its stylesheet was absent, exactly the declarations with no second source were lost: the heading sat flush against the body text at a 0px gap and a chip computed `nowrap`. Copying the whole stylesheet would add rules that provably change nothing, since Bootstrap supplies the focus ring and the host's own forced-colours block supplies the chip border. | The same declarations exist in two places and must stay in step. Bounded by re-stating only what has no second source, by resolving every value from the same token so the two cannot disagree, and by prefixing the host root so the cascade resolves on specificity rather than stylesheet order. Verified: zero differences across 5,700 computed-property comparisons per host. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-513** | Use `overflow-wrap: anywhere` on a chip inside a `.list-inline > li`, while **retaining `break-word`** in every definite-width container, and forbid harmonising the two onto one keyword. | Use `break-word` in both places, as first written. Or `word-break: break-all`. Or make the `li` a block, which would end the horizontal chip flow. | The two keywords differ in one respect that decides this case: `anywhere` contributes to a box's intrinsic min-content width and `break-word` does not. A `.list-inline > li` is `display: inline-block` and therefore shrink-to-fit, so with `break-word` the `li` takes the token's full max-content width, the line box equals the token, and the declaration can never fire — measured, a 240-character token rendered as one fragment and overflowed its alert by 96.31px with the rule fully present, and deleting the rule changed the layout by nothing. In a grid-fixed block the line box is bounded and `break-word` does fire, which is why it is kept there; `anywhere` in that position would let an ordinary value break mid-word where wrapping at its spaces would do. | Two keywords for one apparent purpose, which invites a later reader to unify them. Guarded by stating the reason in both rules and forbidding the unification explicitly. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-514** | Narrow the investor widget's heading rule from `.panel-title` to **`h1.panel-title`**, so it reaches the record name it was written for and cannot reach the embedded partial's heading. | Leave the broad selector. Or add a competing rule to the partial's re-statement block. Or give the record heading a bespoke class, which would change the template. | The broad rule outranked Bootstrap's heading line-height on specificity and applied to the embedded partial's `<h2>`, which the partial's own stylesheet is not present to defend — rendering it 19.000px instead of 17.594px and displacing every element below it in the alert by 1.406px. The rule exists for the `<h1>`, which carries the record name and can wrap; the three `<h2>` panel titles are fixed one-line copy that never needed it. A competing rule would have restated a Bootstrap default to undo an author rule, which is harder to read than not making the author rule too broad. | A type-qualified selector, where the guide's convention is class-only and states elsewhere that `.panel-title` is a class rather than a level. Recorded because the element genuinely matters here. Verified: zero differences across 5,700 comparisons afterwards, and the `<h1>` still wraps a long name with 4.00px of inter-line clearance against the control's 2.59px. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-515** | Publish the chart's share figure as **three members** — the ratio, the bar width and the printed text — rather than deriving all three from one rounded value. | Keep one member and round once, as delivered. Or print the bar's width attribute as the text. | The three questions have three different right answers: the accessible value wants the true ratio, the bar wants a width with a floor so a non-zero count is never invisible, and the printed text wants a form a reader can read, including a `less than 0.1%` case. Deriving them from one rounded number made a 0.04% share print as `0%` beside a bar of width zero, which states the opposite of the truth. | Three members where one stood. Verified: widths 440, 2, 2, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1 with no non-zero count at width zero, and a percentage now present in the rendered text where the regular expression previously matched nothing. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-516** | Derive the chart's value-label band from the **digit count of the largest value**, rather than fixing it. | Keep the fixed band. Or shrink the label font. Or abbreviate large values. | A fixed band was sized for four digits, so an eight-digit value overlapped its own bar by 10.9 units and put one digit at 2.746:1. Abbreviating would have changed the data the chart states. | The plot width now varies with the data, so two charts on one dashboard can have different bar spans. Verified: the span is unchanged at exactly 440 for every shipped case and moves only for the two extreme ones, and the fix also closed a previously unnoticed sub-unit overlap on a seven-digit value. | Low |  |
| **D-517** | **Flag** the ragged edge between the two dashboard chart panels rather than fix it, and record the measured figures. | Set a `min-height`. Or make the row a flex container. Or use a spacing token as a stand-in height. | All three remedies are closed by rules the guide keeps: a pixel `min-height` is a measured literal, custom flex on a raw container is forbidden by the layout-primitives rule, and a spacing token used as a height is a measured value wearing a token's name. The design-system gap procedure the plan defines is to flag what the system cannot express rather than break a rule to hide it. | The two panels' lower edges differ by 161px at 1024 and 172.11px at 1366 and above. Decomposed in the record as 58.11px of chart and 114.00px of table so a later reader can act on it. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-518** | Gate each reloading widget's success view on **`!c.busy`**, so a stale record is hidden behind the progress bar rather than rendered beside it. | Gate on `data` alone, as delivered. Or leave the stale view and mark it visually. | `data` is not a usable discriminator: it holds the previous payload for the whole reload, which is exactly why gating on it fails. On the two profile widgets a reload is how a caller moves between records, so the figures on screen during one belong to a **different** company or investor — while the root announces `aria-busy` and the status region reads `Loading`. | The view goes blank during a reload rather than dimming. Verified: zero list items, rows, chips and headings during the busy state on both widgets, an atomic swap with no frame carrying both, and a three-step control restoring byte-identical markup. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-519** | Name the paywall control's **destination** — `Go to account management` — and remove the one commerce term the deliverable actually contained, replacing `Upgrade to read premium fields` with `Premium fields are reserved`. | Keep `See your entitlements`, which the destination page can be configured not to show. Or add a request control, which is what a reader of the old heading would expect. | The requirements flag premium billing as having no platform equivalent and require it to be flagged rather than half-implemented. A control inviting a caller to upgrade, with no upgrade path and no request mechanism, half-implements it. A destination the control names is a promise the application can keep; content on that page is not. | The label is less specific about what the caller will find. Verified: `upgrade` now absent from the rendered text, the serialised DOM including suppressed markup, and the whole source tree, with the only hit across a twenty-two-term scan being the descriptive phrase `premium tier`. | Medium | `DEV` |
