| **D-520** | Allow **one** `font-size` declaration in widget CSS — `$font-size-large` on the chart's SVG text — and document it as a named exception to the guide's own prohibition. | Leave the labels below the legibility floor. Or add a Bootstrap size class, which cannot target an SVG `<text>` node. Or draw the labels as HTML. | No Bootstrap utility class applies to an SVG text node, so the type scale cannot be reached declaratively there. At the dashboard's real render scale the labels measured 8.224px at 1024, below a 9px floor; the token brings them to 10.574px. The declaration names a token, so no literal enters the stylesheet and the rule's actual purpose is preserved. | An exception to a rule stated as absolute elsewhere in the same guide. Closed by documenting it at the rule and at the checklist item that asserts it, so the two no longer contradict each other. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-521** | Have the upsell controller read the **resolved** `data.context` rather than the raw `options.context`. | Leave the controller reading the option. Or change the heading member to read the option too, making both consistent the other way. | The server script publishes the resolved context onto `data` precisely so the controller does not read the raw option, and says so; two members of the same controller were reading different sources for the same question. Making both read the option would have contradicted the server script's stated contract. | None identified. Verified by a differential control: with the option mutated and the resolved value untouched, the delivered form holds and the previous form flips. | Low |  |
| **D-522** | **Superseded by `D-454`, which reconciles the delivered acceptance contract to twelve acceptance-blocking checks — the eleven post-commit gates and `GATE-COL-01` — with all four `GATE-SEC` checks carried as class 3 diagnostics that block nothing. The reasoning below stands as the case for promoting three of those diagnostics, and it is not what the delivery states.** State the acceptance decision as **fifteen checks** everywhere — the eleven required gates, `GATE-COL-01` and `GATE-SEC-01` to `GATE-SEC-03` — with `GATE-SEC-04` the single non-normative diagnostic. Restores `D-417` and amends `D-069` and `D-108`. | State twelve everywhere, which is what the class tables and most of the prose said. Or leave the two figures side by side. | The package stated both twelve and fifteen, and one document's own prose named `all three` while opening with `the four`. Twelve is the unsafe reconciliation: under it a deployment with raw staged payloads reachable over an external route, a cross-scope write permitted, or REST endpoints carrying no authorisation could be recorded as accepted merely because the eleven gates passed. `GATE-SEC-04` is genuinely different, and that is what makes fifteen coherent rather than arbitrary: it is the one check that is not an HTTP request but a background script a human runs in the platform UI, so no deployment pipeline can assert on it. | Fifteen acceptance-blocking checks against eleven rollback triggers is a distinction a reader can miss, and missing it in the rollback direction is the expensive mistake. Mitigated by stating both halves wherever the class is named, and by carrying the figure identically in the gates document, the checklist, the gaps inventory, the runbook, the package index and the deck. | High | `DEV` |
| **D-523** | State in the runbook and the gates document that the **five Service Portal routes do not exist when the commit completes**, and exist only once guide 04's manual build is performed. | Say nothing, since the split is already sanctioned. Or add the portal records to the Update Set. | Only tables carrying the synchronisation attribute are captured into an update set, so the portal, theme, page and widget records are necessarily built through the platform's own interface — which the plan sanctions. But an operator who has just watched every gate pass has no way to know the routes are absent, and the gates deliberately do not test them. Adding the records is unsupported. | None to the delivery. The statement exists so a correct outcome is not read as a failure. | Medium |  |
| **D-524** | Decline four reported findings on cited authority rather than fix them: a defect below the declared viewport floor, two sub-pixel observations with no displacement, and one consequence of the mandated stage geometry. | Fix them anyway. Or record them as open. | Each is closed by a specific provision rather than by judgement: the plan excludes viewports below 1024 pixels, so a 375-pixel defect is outside the supported range; two findings measured a two-pixel and a 67-millisecond effect that displaced nothing and were reproduced identically in the control; and the fourth follows directly from Rule 2's mandated 1920-by-1080 stage with its scale cap, so fixing it would require contradicting the rule that causes it. | A declined finding can look like an unresolved one. Mitigated by naming the provision for each rather than the judgement. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-525** | Renumber the deck's fourteen decision-log citations from `D-414`-`D-427` to `D-493`-`D-506`, leaving the one pre-existing citation untouched. | Leave them. Or renumber the log's existing rows instead. | The identifiers had been allocated on the assumption that the log ended at `D-413`; it did not, and sections 21 to 23 already occupied `D-414` to `D-492`. Thirty-one citations in the deck therefore pointed at unrelated decisions about redaction, gate classification and pre-commit checks. Renumbering the log's existing rows would invalidate every amendment reference that cites them. | A reader holding an earlier copy of the deck has stale identifiers. Verified after the change: the mirrored theme block is still byte-identical to its file, sixteen sections, zero non-ASCII characters and balanced tags. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-526** | Leave the **timeline diagram's authored accessible title and description unrendered** rather than inject them after the renderer drops them, and record the gap instead. The four flowchart diagrams publish theirs correctly; the fifth, the timeline on the closing-phase slide, does not. | Post-process the rendered `svg` in the existing render pass — the deck already parses `accTitle` out of each diagram source for its failure notice — and inject `<title>` and `<desc>` with `aria-labelledby` and `aria-describedby` when the renderer emitted none. Or put `role="img"` with an `aria-label` on the container, which survives rendering. Or point the container at its own already-authored `.diagram-static-note` with `aria-describedby`, which is exposed even though the note is `display: none`. | The deck authors `accTitle` and `accDescr` for **all five** diagrams; Mermaid 11.4.0's `timeline` renderer discards both, while its `flowchart-v2` renderer publishes them as an SVG `<title>` and `<desc>` wired by `aria-labelledby` and `aria-describedby` — measured on the rendered page, four diagrams with both elements and the timeline with neither and with both ARIA attributes `null`. Nothing in Rule 2 requires the property, no document in this package claims it, and no accessibility conformance level is asserted anywhere, so the gap falsifies no statement. Against that, the injection would run inside the one code path four CDN-outage modes were verified through, and `role="img"` on the container would additionally suppress the timeline's own visible labels from assistive technology — a larger change than the gap it closes, taken at the point the deck is otherwise verified. | One diagram of five renders without an accessible name or description, so a screen-reader user reaches the closing-phase timeline with only its visible labels announced. The authored directives stay in the source, so a later Mermaid release that supports them in `timeline` closes the gap with no edit. The measured evidence is in this row so a reader can act on it rather than re-measure. | Low | `DEV` |
## 27. Remediation of the ingestion integration runtime review

Eight decisions, `D-527` through `D-534`. Each was taken while resolving findings raised when the two ingestion flows and the two credential aliases were executed against a ServiceNow emulation of the delivered artifacts — the first runtime exercise of either flow's live outbound arm, its counters and its published evidence.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-527** | Have each Arm-B step **derive** its `APPROVED_ROOT` and `APPROVED_HOST` from `AppProperties.PROVIDER_ORIGINS`, and declare `trimSlash` as a local function of the step. Implements **D-392**, whose endpoint-authority check the delivered scripts referenced through three identifiers that nothing declared, so the step raised a `ReferenceError` on its first live call and the check never ran. | Restate the pinned root and host as string literals in each step, which is what the missing identifiers evidently once were. Or delete the `approvedEndpoint` check and rely on `providerBaseUrlState()` alone. Or add a new accessor to `AppProperties` returning the pair. | A literal in the step is a second home for the pinned origin, and `PROVIDER_ORIGINS` is already declared — in guide 02 step 3.5a's own words — the one place it lives; two homes drift, and the drift is invisible until a credential travels somewhere it should not. Deleting the check was rejected because the delivered error table documents both of its refusals as a contract and the connection-record comparison exists nowhere else: `providerBaseUrlState()` classifies the **property** only. A new accessor was rejected as surface the guide does not describe when the member it would wrap is already documented. | The step reaches into a member rather than calling a method, so renaming `PROVIDER_ORIGINS` breaks two step scripts as well as the Script Include. Recorded here rather than mitigated: the member is a code constant in the same scope, changed only by an update set, and the rename impact list in `api-reference.md` names it. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-528** | Refuse the LinkedIn probe with a **ninth closed code, `organizations_unconfigured`, and zero outbound calls** while the step's `ORGANIZATIONS` constant still holds its `<ORGANIZATION_ID>` placeholder, on both arms, and keep only entries matching `^[0-9]{1,20}$` after the filter is applied rather than before the `MAX_ORGANIZATIONS` cut. | Send the placeholder and let the provider reject it, which is what the delivered scripts did. Or move the identifiers to a twelfth system property so the value is configuration rather than code. Or fail the whole flow rather than the probe. | Calling `/organizationsLookup` with a literal `<ORGANIZATION_ID>` spends one of a small entitled request budget to be told the identifier is invalid, and returns a provider message the operator must then recognise as their own placeholder; the closed code names the constant instead. The property route was rejected because the identifiers are what a specific developer application is entitled to read and travel with the credential rather than with the deployment, which is the position guide 03 step 3.4 already takes, and because the property inventory is fixed at eleven. Failing the flow was rejected because this flow's rows come from staging regardless: the probe is entitlement evidence, not acquisition. | A build published before the credential owner answers now reports a fault where it previously reported a provider error, so an operator reading only `error_code` sees a new code. Mitigated by the code naming its own remedy and by `3.4b` stating the distinction from the healthy empty-result case, which is deliberately **not** a fault. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-529** | Add **one** counter, `errors`, to `IngestionLogger`, defined as the incoming rows whose own write failed and maintained by a new `failRecord()` that increments **both** `skipped` and `errors`. Reconcile the batch on `processed + rejected + duplicates + errors`, and split step 8c's fault into `write_failures` and `operational_skips`. | Leave a write failure in `skipped`, as delivered, and read the staging table to tell the kinds apart. Or redefine `skipped` to exclude write failures. Or add two counters, one per-row and one secondary. | `skipped` is the figure the criterion-4 evidence is read against and every document states it that way, so redefining it would have changed the meaning of a signed-off number; making `errors` a **subset** changes no existing figure while making the distinction available. One counter rather than two because the per-row bucket is the one the arithmetic needs: `skipped` already accounts for the secondary failures as the remainder. The three sites that switch to `failRecord()` are exactly the three that already incremented the mapper's own `outcome.errors`, so the two counts now agree by construction instead of by coincidence. | A caller summing `skipped` and `errors` double-counts. Stated in every table that carries both, and in `counters()`'s own comment. The step-5 action gains a fourteenth output, which the ATF suite's declared-output assertion encodes and which is updated with it. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-530** | Make `evidence_complete` the **substance** of the evidence — the events parsed and the run summary present — and report a ceiling truncation separately in `evidence_truncated`, counted by severity, documented inside the block and logged at warn, but **not** a health fault. Fill the block's remainder `error` then `warn` then `info`, and reserve 220 characters for the omission note. | Keep `evidence_complete` as delivered, meaning nothing was omitted, and accept that a large run is unhealthy. Or raise `BLOCK_LIMIT` above 4000. Or drop the omission note to make room. | A run that correctly rejected three hundred rows for a missing mandatory field was reported `healthy` `false` with reason `evidence_incomplete`, which contradicts this delivery's own rule that a cleaning-rule outcome is expected behaviour and not an error — and it would have made criterion 4 unsignable on any dataset large enough to produce a few dozen events. Raising the ceiling was rejected because 4000 characters is the platform field's own limit, not a preference. The severity ordering is what makes the retained block worth reading: without it a truncation drops the faults and keeps the first informational lines to arrive. | An operator who reads only `evidence_complete` no longer learns that the block was cut; they must read `evidence_truncated`, which is why the truncation is also written into the block itself and logged. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-531** | Route every flow-step log line through **one** new gate, `AppProperties.log(severity, text)`, which resolves the severity and tests `x_bst_startuptrk.logging.level` before writing, and convert all **60** unconditional `gs.*` calls across the 20 step scripts to it. | Leave the steps unconditional and document that `logging.level` governs only the Script Includes. Or repeat the `isLogLevelEnabled` test at each of the 60 call sites. Or drop the property. | A level that half works is worse than one that does nothing: before this, `record_skipped` was suppressed at `error` while `batch_ingested` was still written, so the log looked complete and was not. One gate rather than 60 inline tests because that is the same one-rule-once discipline the claim rule and the cleaning rules follow, and because a hand-built step is where a forgotten test would go unnoticed. | At `logging.level` `error` a clean run now writes **no** application log line at all. That is the operator's instruction, and the three things it does not touch — the run-state property, the flow execution log written by the Log action, and the step outputs the health predicate reads — are stated in the new section 7.2a and proven by a run at that level that still stamps its marker. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-532** | State `getSourceMode()`'s unrecognised-value behaviour as **fail closed to `fallback`** wherever the guides describe it, matching the delivered code and the same guides' own action-level description. | Change the code to return `live` for an unrecognised value, as one sentence in each guide claimed. | The code and one description said `fallback`; one sentence said `live`. Only one of the two can stand, and `fallback` is the safe direction: an unreadable mode must not send a credential to a provider. Correcting the prose costs nothing; correcting the code would have introduced the risk the code was written to avoid. | None. The delivered behaviour is unchanged; only the description of it is. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-533** | **Keep the credential-shaped redaction vectors in guide 01 exactly as they are, and label them explicitly as synthetic** — stating that a secret scanner will match them, that the match is the fixture working as designed, that no vector is or resembles a credential issued to this application, and that a synthetic vector must never be replaced with a real value to "test it properly". | Replace each vector with an obviously inert string such as `REDACT_ME_1`, so no scanner matches the repository. Or move the vectors out of the guide into an attachment. Or drop the shape-specific vectors and test only the length-based rule. | The sanitiser is a **shape** detector: `SANITISERS[1]` keys off a credential-looking label followed by a delimiter and a value that carries a digit, mixes case, is quoted or is scheme-qualified, and `SANITISERS[5]` keys off an opaque run of 20 or more characters. A vector that does not have those shapes does not exercise the rule that has to hold, so making the scanner quiet would cost the gate its meaning — and this remediation exists partly because `SANITISERS[1]` did not redact a space-delimited label, a defect only a realistically-shaped vector reveals. Moving them out of the guide would separate the gate from the procedure that runs it. The residual cost is a scanner match, and a match that is explained in the artifact next to the fixture is cheap. | A reader who skips the disclosure could mistake a vector for a real credential; the paragraph sits immediately above the vector list to make that unlikely. A future secret-scanning gate wired into CI would need these four lines allow-listed, and the disclosure names them so the allow-list can be written precisely rather than broadly. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-534** | **Leave the `malformed` classification in each live-call step reporting only the exception class name**, without adding the exception message, even though a thrown platform exception is classified as `malformed` alongside genuine shape faults. | Add a fourth catch branch classifying a thrown exception as `transport` rather than `malformed`. Or record `safeText(exception.message)` in the detail so the cause is visible. | Section 3.6 of both flow guides already declares `malformed`'s detail to be "a closed token naming the shape fault, or the exception class name. The exception message is not recorded", and the review raised no finding against it — so the delivered behaviour matches its published contract, and changing it would break that contract to no requirement. The exception message is precisely the string that carried a credential in the leak this remediation closed; the class name is bounded and safe by construction, and the eight-character diagnostic reference plus the caught text's length already make the failure traceable without quoting it. | An operator reading `malformed` cannot tell a thrown transport exception from a genuinely malformed body without the diagnostic reference. That is a diagnosability cost accepted in exchange for a leak-proof detail, and it is recorded here rather than left as an unexplained silence. | Low | `DEV` |

## 28. Remediation of the prototype-bounded performance and efficiency review

Twelve decisions, `D-535` through `D-546`. Each was taken while resolving findings raised when the delivered artifacts were measured for **cost** rather than for correctness — the presentation loaded in a real browser with its network, main thread and layout traced, and the delivered Script Includes executed against a ServiceNow emulation that counts every database operation. Four of that review's findings could not be measured at all, because the target instance was hibernating; the last row records how that was reported rather than guessed at.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-535** | **Add `preconnect` resource hints for `https://cdn.jsdelivr.net` and `https://unpkg.com`** to the presentation's head, beside the two Google Fonts hints already there, so both origins carrying the pinned library bundles are warmed before the parser reaches a script tag. | Leave the two origins unwarmed, as delivered. Or `dns-prefetch`, which resolves the name but neither opens the socket nor completes the handshake. Or `preload` the two scripts, which warms the origin and fetches the bytes but does not change when they execute. | Measured on a cold load: the two unwarmed origins carried 849,286 of the deck's 1,027,933 transferred bytes — 82.6 per cent — and no script's request could begin until its origin's connection existed. A hint is four lines of head markup, costs nothing at render time and needs no build step, which is what the presentation rule requires of every dependency in this file. `preload` was rejected because the bytes are not wanted early: `D-536` takes one of the two bundles off the cold path entirely, and preloading it would put the bytes back. | A browser warms only a small number of origins speculatively, so a fifth hint would be likelier ignored than honoured; four is inside every documented budget. A hint left behind by a future edit that removes its script costs a name lookup and a socket for nothing. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-536** | **Take `mermaid.min.js` off the presentation's cold critical path**: remove its parser-blocking `<script>` tag and inject it — identical pinned URL, identical `integrity` digest, identical `crossorigin` and `referrerpolicy` — the first time navigation reaches a slide carrying an unrendered diagram, holding `mermaid.initialize()` to exactly one call and keeping the render invocation on `ready`, `slidechanged` and `slidetransitionend` as the presentation rule requires. **Amends `D-253` in mechanism only**: the digest still pins the bytes and the request is still anonymous, applied now as properties on the element the deck creates rather than as attributes a parser reads. | Keep the tag and add `defer`, which unblocks the parser but still downloads 710 KB nobody looks at on the title slide. Or `async`, which carries the same objection. Or drop Mermaid and ship the five diagrams as static SVG, removing a dependency the rule pins. Or leave it as delivered. | Measured on a cold load: the bundle was 710,183 of 1,027,933 transferred bytes — 69.1 per cent — its evaluation was the page's **only** long task at 63.40 ms, and `mermaid.run()` was called **zero** times, because slide 1 carries no diagram. `defer` and `async` were rejected because what was measured is the download and the compile, not the parser block. Re-measured after the change: 10 requests and 326,848 bytes, **zero** long tasks of any duration, first contentful paint 248 ms against 512.52 ms, all five diagrams healthy, and the bundle fetched exactly **once** per session, 6.50 ms after the keypress that first needed it. | A diagram slide reached before the bundle arrives shows a reserved empty box rather than a diagram; on a throttled connection that window measured 3.85 s and it closes by itself. A failed fetch now fails after the page is interactive rather than before it, so it has to be visible: every awaiting container is marked `data-diagram-error` and renders its notice. And the request now starts on demand rather than during parse, which is why `D-535` warms the origin. The `G-8` digest gate had to move with the digest: four of the five values are still markup attributes and Mermaid's is now the `MERMAID_SRI` constant, so the gate extracts every `sha384-` token in document order — which covers both forms — and lists Mermaid **last**, after Lucide. Both delivered forms of that check were re-run and matched 5 of 5. A network-panel read taken before any diagram slide is reached shows four assets rather than five, which the gate now states as the expected behaviour. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-537** | **Guard the icon factory on the presence of an unreplaced placeholder** — `document.querySelector('[data-lucide]:not(svg)')` — so `lucide.createIcons()` runs only when something is there to replace, while leaving `renderIcons()` itself invoked on `ready` and on every slide change exactly as the presentation rule requires. | Call `createIcons()` once on `ready` and unregister the slide-change handlers, which would stop honouring the rule's invocation requirement. Or hand `createIcons` a narrower root so it walks only the current slide, which reduces the work without removing it. Or leave it as delivered. | The root cause is in the library: lucide 0.460.0's `replaceElement()` writes `data-lucide` onto the `<svg>` it emits, so a rendered icon permanently re-matches `createIcons()`'s own selector. Measured: **61** calls across a full forward-and-backward walk — one `ready` plus two per slide change — of which **60 were pure no-ops** that destroyed and re-created all 63 icons, 3,780 replacements and 124.3 ms of aggregate main-thread work. `[data-lucide]:not(svg)` is the discriminator the library's own output makes necessary. Re-measured: reveal dispatched the same 61 events, `renderIcons()` ran 61 times, `createIcons()` ran **once**, and an independent mutation observer recorded 63 icon elements added and **0** removed. | A placeholder inserted after load by anything other than the deck's own failure notice is replaced on the next hook that finds it unreplaced — which is every hook, so the exposure is one frame. The guard is a document-wide selector match per hook rather than a per-slide one, which measured at no cost. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-538** | **Reserve each diagram container's rendered height in advance**, as a `min-height` per size class in the presentation's own layout style block using the heights measured after render — 568, 274, 327 and 534 pixels — and hold the un-rendered source invisible inside it with a fixed `height`, `overflow: hidden` and `color: transparent` until the container carries `data-processed`. | Reserve each container's `max-height` cap instead, which is the number already in the stylesheet. Or reserve nothing and accept the shift. Or `visibility: hidden` on the source, which reserves nothing because the box still lays out at its content height. Or `display: none`, which would hide the source from Mermaid as well. | The caps are the wrong numbers to reserve: the SVGs are width-constrained, so their rendered heights sit far below their caps and reserving the caps would push the two-column slide past the 1920-by-1080 stage the deck is built for. Only measurement produced usable figures. Measured un-exempted layout shift before: 0.002351, 0.021588, 0.003891 and 0.015703 on cold deep links to slides 2, 9, 12 and 14, and 0.025479 on a programmatic walk, every entry sourced to a footnote or list displaced when an SVG replaced the source. After: **0 with zero entries** in all five sub-sessions, each container's height identical before and after render across 638 to 777 sampled frames. The `color: transparent` half followed a re-measurement that found the raw Mermaid source **legible** for 344 to 447 ms after first paint; a pixel scan of the reserved box then returned 0 ink pixels, against 23,914 for a positive control that forced the colour back. | A reservation is wrong if a diagram is later edited to a different height — it then renders taller or shorter than its box and the shift returns. The four figures are therefore stated in the block's own comment beside the class each belongs to, and the check is one measurement per edited diagram. The second tall container now renders at its class floor of 568 rather than its natural 489, which makes the two side-by-side panels equal height: an improvement here, but a floor rather than a fit. | Low | `DEV` |
| **D-539** | **Cap the two page-size properties absolutely**, at a `PAGE_SIZE_CEILING` of 50 declared in `AppProperties` and applied inside both `getDefaultLimit()` and `getMaxLimit()`, and mirror it as a defence-in-depth constant inside `RestQueryHelper.getLimit()`. | Clamp inside `RestQueryHelper` alone, leaving the portal widget's page size uncapped. Or validate the property on write with a business rule, which cannot reach a value set before the rule existed. Or leave the properties trusted, as delivered, and document that an administrator must not raise them. | `getBoundedInt(key, fallback, 1)` enforces only a floor, so `RestQueryHelper` was trusting the property as its own ceiling. Reproduced over a 300-row table: `rest.max_limit` at 9999 with `sysparm_limit=99999` returned **all 300 rows and echoed a limit of 9999**, and `rest.default_limit` at 9999 with **no** pagination parameter returned all 300 — one property change, no code change, and the pagination bound is gone for every caller. `AppProperties` is the right site because the plan makes it the single typed accessor so nothing reads properties ad hoc, it already carries the mandated 6-to-48 cadence clamp to copy, and both the API and the portal widget read their page size through it. Re-measured: 50 rows and a limit of 50 in both cases, with the four floor controls — 0, -5, `abc` and empty — still resolving to 50. | An operator who legitimately wants a larger page can no longer obtain one by editing a property; it takes an update set. That is the ceiling's purpose, and the plan fixes the maximum at 50 in any case, so the property was never where that decision belonged. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-540** | **Bound both of `IngestionMapper`'s staging reads at a `STAGING_BATCH_LIMIT` of 200 rows**, declared as a code constant rather than a twelfth system property, and have a run that filled its batch say so through a `claim_batch_bounded` note. | Add a twelfth property so the batch size is configuration. Or page with a `sys_id` keyset cursor carried between runs. Or leave both reads unbounded, as delivered, and rely on an operator never loading more than one transaction can hold. | Both reads did `orderBy('sys_id')` and then `query()` with no limit, so a run's transaction was the size of whatever had been loaded into the table: measured at 33, 66, 165 and 330 pending rows the claim cost 67, 133, 331 and 661 database operations, rising without limit. 200 is `InvestorPortfolioService.BATCH_SIZE`, the page size this delivery already uses for a cursor. A property was rejected because the inventory is fixed at eleven by `D-036` and reaffirmed by `D-528`. A keyset cursor was rejected because the `import_state` `pending` filter advances the batch by itself — every claimed row is settled out of that state — while a cursor can step over a row. Re-measured: 330 pending rows claimed 200 in 401 operations with one bounded note, and a 650-row queue drained across runs of 200 and 130 with every row accounted for and zero errors. | A load larger than 200 rows for one source settles across successive runs rather than in one, so an operator expecting a single run to clear the queue must read the note. The delivered dataset is 33 Crunchbase and 32 LinkedIn rows, so no call of the shipped procedure is bounded and every published count is unchanged. The bound is a code constant, so changing it takes an update set. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-541** | Replace `RateLimitService._pruneUnkeyed()`'s per-row delete loop with the **aggregate count, `deleteMultiple()`, re-count** shape the main prune path already uses, and name survivors in one line rather than one line per row. | Add a `setLimit()` to the existing loop, keeping per-row outcomes and pruning across several job runs. Or delete the unkeyed sweep and let a row carrying no `window_key` accumulate. | The loop cost one operation per row — measured at 503 operations and 500 individual `deleteRecord()` calls for 500 unkeyed rows — while the main path in the same Script Include was already constant at 3 to 4 operations from 0 to 5,000 rows, so the shape to adopt was beside it. `setLimit()` was rejected because it keeps the cost proportional and adds runs: the sweep is a safety net for rows a failed write left keyless, and it should cost the same whatever it finds. Re-measured: 500 unkeyed rows plus one current row cost **5** operations with one `deleteMultiple()`, zero per-row deletes and the current row preserved exactly, while the main path still cost 4 at 10, 500 and 2,500 expired rows. | A `deleteMultiple()` reports no per-row outcome, so a row that could not be removed is named by the re-count rather than by its own error line. That is the trade the main path already made, and the re-count is what makes the survivor count exact rather than inferred. | Low | — |
| **D-542** | Keep `x_bst_startuptrk_fundinground.participating_investors` calculated and virtual and **record its export cost explicitly**, rather than batching the calculation per rendered page or removing the column. | Batch the calculation across a rendered page, which no platform calculated-column interface allows. Or make the column stored and maintain it with a business rule. Or remove the column and rely on the related list alone. | The two bounds the review asked for are already delivered and were re-verified: the column is absent from the funding-round default list view and the join table is surfaced as a related list, so the two surfaces that render many rounds at once never reach it — the four REST operations cost a constant 5 operations first-call and 7 steady at page size 1, 20 and 50, with **zero** evaluations. What remains is a report or an export, measured at exactly 1.00 query per evaluated row: 101, 501 and 1,001 queries for 100, 500 and 1,000 rounds. A stored column is what `D-010` decided against and `D-434` made virtual to prevent, because a stored projection can drift with nothing to detect it. So the resolution is the third of the review's three options — state the cost, name the surfaces that pay it, and give the operator the cheap path, which is exporting the join table. | An operator who adds the column to a personalised list view or a report and pages through thousands of rounds pays a query per row. The documentation names that surface and offers the join-table export instead, but nothing in the platform prevents it. | Low | — |
| **D-543** | **Declare no index for the startup inclusion filter** and record in the declared-index section why none would serve it, rather than adding a fourteenth `sys_index` record. | Add an index on `headquarters_location`, or a composite on `active` and `headquarters_location`. Or add a platform text index. Or add an anchored `headquarters_city` column an equality predicate could use. | The predicate was captured by execution rather than read off the source: `active = true` and `headquarters_location CONTAINS` one of two configured tokens, ordered by `name` then `sys_id`, issued twice per list call with byte-identical conditions and costing 4 database operations whatever the filters. A `CONTAINS` is a leading-wildcard match no b-tree can answer, and `active` is a two-value column true on effectively every row, so each candidate index would be written on every insert and update and read by nothing — the rule every other index in that section is justified against. A new column is forbidden outright: the requirements' field list is binding and complete. What does serve the query is the existing `startup(name)` index, which answers the `ORDER BY` the windowed read depends on. | The reasoning is inferred from the declared index definitions set against the issued queries; **no execution plan was observed**, because the instance was hibernating. A non-acceptance-blocking checklist item now requires the plan to be captured on a woken instance, and a plan that contradicts the reasoning is a documentation defect to correct rather than a criterion failure. | Low | — |
| **D-544** | Record the three measured cost baselines — the rate-limit accounting share, the worst-case page size and the absence of a field-projection parameter — in `api-reference.md` as published facts, and **change no code for any of the three**. | Reduce the steady-state accounting cost by deciding on the value the atomic increment returns instead of on a `SUM` read-back. Or add a `sysparm_fields`-style projection parameter. Or say nothing, since the review recommended no fix. | Each of the three follows from a decision already taken and already justified. The four steady-state accounting operations — measured as 4 of 6 on a small list call, 67 per cent, against 2 of 4 on the first call of a window — are what make the count correct under contention, and dropping the read-back would restore exactly the error `D-040` removed. A projection parameter would contradict the binding field list and the omission-not-nulling rule the response shape is built on. And the worst case is bounded by the dictionary and the page ceiling: 50 records at 5,016 bytes is 244.9 KiB for an administrator or premium caller and 241.4 KiB for the base role, and no page can exceed it. Publishing the figures turns three unstated properties into three checkable ones. | A reader could take the 67 per cent as a defect rather than a design cost, which is why the paragraph states what each of the four operations buys immediately below the figure. Transport compression could not be observed on a hibernating instance, so every byte figure is uncompressed and a non-acceptance-blocking checklist item now asks for `Content-Encoding` on a woken one. | Low | — |
| **D-545** | Require the deployment runbook and the acceptance checklist to record the **elapsed preview duration on success**, stated as `observed s / 600 s` with the pass count, rather than only on a timeout. | Leave the runbook recording a duration only when the preview times out. Or split the Update Set into several files to shorten each preview, which the requirements forbid by mandating a single importable file. Or raise the timeout, which belongs to the deployment environment rather than to this package. | The 600-second preview budget across 313 records is 1.92 seconds per record, and that figure is a **prediction**: no deployment of this Update Set has been executed, so the risk cannot be closed by any change to the artifact. What can be delivered is the measurement obligation, so the first deployment produces the number instead of leaving it unknown a second time. Recording it only on a timeout would discard the successful case — which is the case that would establish the headroom. | None to the artifact. The obligation is only as good as the operator who honours it, which is why it sits in the runbook's deployment-log row as well as in the acceptance checklist item. | Low | — |
| **D-546** | Report the four instance-hibernation-blocked findings as **blocked with evidence** — neither passed nor recorded as application defects — on the strength of control probes proving the responses carry no information about the application. | Record them as passed, since nothing observed contradicted the design. Or record them as failures of the application. Or defer them silently to a later checkpoint. | Every path on the instance answered identically: 18 paths, including the six REST list resources, the five portal routes, `/login.do` and a deliberately nonexistent path, all returned HTTP 200 with the **same** 5,904-byte body, SHA-256 `f640471b2e74fbc0…`, served by the edge rather than by an application node and carrying no `Set-Cookie`. The decisive control is that `/sp?id=index`, which must exist, and `/sp?id=__no_such_page_zzz999`, which cannot, produced **byte-identical screenshots**. A response identical for a page that exists and a page that does not evidences neither outcome, so both `passed` and `failed` are unsupportable. Waking the instance needs an authenticated developer-portal session and only instance credentials are supplied; every available wake vector was attempted and none succeeded. | A reader who sees HTTP 200 in a log could conclude the checks passed. That is the false-200 trap, and the delivered artifacts already close it: the runbook requires all four checks rather than a status alone, and the validation gates make an empty or unparseable `result` a failure for every gate. | Medium | — |

## 29. Remediation of the full-stack runtime journey review

Eight decisions, `D-548` through `D-555`. Each was taken while resolving a finding raised when the delivered artifacts were exercised as a running interface rather than read: the widget templates and the shared client helper of [`../../servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/manual-build/04-service-portal-pages-and-widgets.md`](../../servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/manual-build/04-service-portal-pages-and-widgets.md) were rendered in a browser against data shaped exactly as their own server scripts publish, and the Update Set's form records were read against the dictionary they describe. That is why this round's rows are concentrated in one guide: every one of them is a place where the guide's own binding contract and its own template disagreed, which a reading can miss and a render cannot.

**Seven corrections in the same round earn no row**, because Rule 1's non-trivial test is the inclusion criterion of this file and each of them had exactly one sane option: adding the published `largestLabel` member to the chart widget's own Data Contract table; deleting a promise that the shared client helper carries a `finally` clause, which `D-178` had already established the platform's interpreter cannot parse; deleting a local array in the join table's business rule that was populated and never read; restating the developer-portal sign-in gate's observed behaviour in the deployment runbook, where the gate refuses an instance-style user name **silently** — the submit control stays disabled with no message and no `aria-invalid` — rather than with the error text the runbook had claimed; attributing the account widget's `authorised` member to the server step that actually assigns it, its second rather than its first, which is where every **other** widget publishes that member and why the wrong number was written; and naming the member the results widget stores its resolved criteria on, which its own step left unsaid while the two steps either side of it named `data.limit` and `data.offset` explicitly. and restating the one-time-binding rule as the three cases the framework actually distinguishes, because the form `D-554` first gave it — one-time binding "reserved for `options.*`" — was true of the two widgets the finding concerned and false of the other six, which legitimately bind a repeated record's own properties and a single record's fields that way. The last three are consequences of `D-553` and `D-554` rather than findings in their own right: strengthening the sentence that opens all eight Data Contract sections into a claim about **which** server step publishes each member turned two rows that had been merely vague into rows that were false as written, and a rule stated as a slogan rather than as a mechanism forbade twenty-nine bindings that are correct. The mechanism is that `::` stops watching once a value is **defined**, so the rule now turns on whether the widget's own server script publishes a defined value on its failing path — which is exactly the property `data.serverError` marks, and which is true of the two dashboard widgets and no others.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-548** | Have **every** `ng-repeat` over one of the company profile's five child collections iterate `<collection>.rows`, and state that rule in the guide beside the four-member contract it follows from. Implements `D-327`. | Publish the five collections as bare arrays and carry the totals separately, which is what the three defective repeats assumed. Or keep the object and give it an iterable shape. | `childCollection()` returns `{rows, limit, total, truncated}` and the guide's own rule, its `c.isEmpty()` helper and five of its captions all read that object — but three repeats named the object itself. `ng-repeat` over an object iterates its own keys, so `track by round.sys_id` resolved to `undefined` on the second key and the framework abandoned the **whole** repeater: rendered in a browser, the Funding, Jobs and News panes produced **zero** rows each while their captions still advertised 31, 40 and 12. Reverting to bare arrays would have undone the metadata the captions exist to state and would have left the same panes lying about their size in the other direction. | Five repeats now depend on a member name, so a collection published without `rows` renders an empty pane rather than raising — which is why `c.isEmpty()` tests the member's presence and why the empty state is unconditional. The three fixed panes were verified in a browser to render one row per record with no console error. | High | — |
| **D-549** | Guard every stored-locator anchor on the presence of its own column value and render **both** branches: the anchor when the value is present, and an explicit alternative when it is not — the record's own title as plain text where the anchor's text was that title, and a `.text-muted` `Not recorded` where the anchor's text was a fixed label. | Leave the anchors unguarded, as four of the eight were. Or guard them and render nothing on the empty branch, matching the four that were already guarded. Or make the four columns mandatory in the dictionary. | All six locator columns are `mandatory=false`, so empty is an ordinary state; and an `ng-href` that interpolates to the empty string makes the framework **remove the `href` attribute altogether**, leaving an element styled as a link, announced as text and unreachable by keyboard — verified in a browser on both website rows. Rendering nothing would have been safe but would have dropped a job posting's or an article's **title**, which is the row's identity rather than its decoration. Making the columns mandatory was not available: the field list is binding at 53 columns with its mandatory flags fixed. | Two branches per locator instead of one, and a fixed wording, `Not recorded`, that now appears in two places. Bounded: the rule is stated once, in the guide's own stored-locator rules, so a new locator inherits it rather than being decided again. | Medium | — |
| **D-550** | Give a job posting's status **both** branches — `.label-primary` `Open` and `.label-default` `Closed` — rather than the positive branch alone. | Leave the negative branch empty, as delivered. Or filter closed postings out of the pane. Or render the raw boolean. | An empty cell is indistinguishable from a cell whose value could not be read, and this was the one conditional in the whole widget set that supplied a single branch while every gated field supplies its `Premium` counterpart. Filtering closed postings would change what the pane answers — and the caption's `total` counts them, so the figures would stop agreeing with the rows. | One more label class in the Jobs pane. `.label-default` is the neutral treatment already declared in the theme, so no new token is introduced. | Low | — |
| **D-551** | Spell the company profile's gate flag **`gatedAny`** in its Data Contract table, and rename the investor profile's `anyGated` to the same name in its publication, its contract table and its template. Implements `D-338`. | Rename the code to `anyGated` in both widgets instead. Or leave each widget internally consistent under its own name, which is what the investor widget was. | `D-338` had already fixed one spelling for this flag after two names for it made the results widget's upsell unrenderable, and the delivered package then carried the other name in two places: the company profile's contract table documented a member its code never creates, and the investor profile used it throughout. One name across all three surfaces is what stops the next reader from mixing them, and `gatedAny` is the name the decision and two of the three widgets already use. | A rename touches four places in the investor widget, and a template that missed one would render no upsell. Verified by reconciling every member the investor template binds against every member its server script publishes and its contract table documents, in both directions. | Low | — |
| **D-552** | Publish the investor profile's rounds-window metadata **once**, in server item 7's publication block, under the five short names `D-327` named — `ledTotal`, `ledTruncated`, `participatedTotal`, `participatedTruncated` and **one** shared `roundsLimit` — and document exactly those five in the Data Contract table. Implements `D-327`; removes the duplicate publication and the second limit. | Publish the six long `roundsLed*` / `roundsParticipated*` names the contract table documented and change the template to read them. Or keep both publications and document both sets. | The same three facts were published twice under two naming sets, the contract table documented only the long set, and the template read only the short set — so the rounds-**led** caption bound `data.ledTruncated` and `data.ledTotal`, which nothing published at all. Rendered in a browser, that caption was absent from the document entirely while its sibling worked, purely because the sibling's members happened to be duplicated. The short set is the one `D-327` recorded and the one the template already reads, so adopting it makes the recorded decision, the publication, the documentation and the template agree without touching the template. The two `*Limit` members carried the identical value, because one bound read caps both tables, so a single `roundsLimit` is the honest figure rather than a convenience. | A caption reads its total from a `COUNT` aggregate taken under the same condition as its bounded read, so a condition that drifts between the two produces a caption that disagrees with its rows — the risk `D-327` already carries. Both captions were verified in a browser to render with their figures when truncated and to stay absent when not. | High | — |
| **D-553** | Let a server script publish its own failure as **`data.serverError`**, leave `data.error` to the shared client helper alone, and have every error panel bind a new shared **`c.errorText()`** that answers either of the two. | Have the success handler restore a message the server published, by capturing the pre-update value. Or have the two dashboard server scripts throw instead of catching, so the promise rejects. Or delete the server-side publication and accept a silent failure. | The two dashboard widgets catch their own aggregate failure, so `c.server.update()` **resolves** — and the shared helper clears `data.error` both on entry and in its success handler. Rendered in a browser, the first Retry therefore erased the message, and with it the panel that contains the Retry control: the widget was left showing its heading over nothing, with no error and no way to try again. Restoring a captured value would put the recovery contract in the helper's timing rather than in a name, and letting the aggregate throw would discard the widget's own diagnosis of which figure failed. A distinct member also makes the rule the eight Data Contract sections all state — that `data.error` is client-owned — true as written, which is the conflict that hid the defect. | One more member on two widgets, one more helper on six, and a template that binds `data.error` directly would ignore a server-published failure. Bounded by the panel snippet being specified once and by `serverError` appearing in both dashboard contract tables. Verified in a browser: the initial failure shows the panel, a failing Retry keeps the panel and its control, and a succeeding Retry clears it and renders the figures. | High | — |
| **D-554** | Drop the one-time binding from **every** dashboard expression a Retry can change — the two collections, and the record-derived members inside them, and the chart's description — and keep it only on `options.*`, which no round trip alters. | Keep the one-time bindings, as delivered. Or rebuild the widget instance on retry. Or have the retry reload the page. | A one-time binding deregisters once its value is defined, and both dashboard collections are published **before** the first paint — so the repeaters stopped watching immediately. Rendered in a browser, a failed round trip left three stale KPI panels standing over an empty `kpis` array, and by the same mechanism a **succeeding** Retry could not repaint at all, which contradicts the guide's own acceptance step. Reloading the page is not a retry, and rebuilding the instance would discard the busy and error state the panel needs. | Two collections and their inner bindings are now watched on every digest, on two widgets that render at most five panels and eight bars. Accepted deliberately: the prototype focus prefers a correct repaint to a saved watcher, and `options.*` — the values that never change — keep their one-time form. | High | — |
| **D-555** | Put `window_key` on the counter table's **Default form**, as its first element, rather than documenting its absence. | Document the omission and name the intended creation path instead. Or make the column read-only on the form. Or drop the column's mandatory flag. | The column is **mandatory** and **unique**, and it was the one mandatory column in the whole delivery absent from its table's form — so the platform would refuse every native insert for a field the form offered no control for, with nothing the operator could do to comply. `D-435` makes this column the only one the limiter queries and `RateLimitService.windowKey()` its only producer, so the form must expose it for an administrative row to be creatable at all. Read-only would have re-created the same dead end; dropping the mandatory flag would have re-opened the duplicate-window hazard the uniqueness closes. | An administrator can now type a key by hand and produce a row the limiter will never look up. Mitigated the way `D-435` already mitigates it: every automated writer calls `windowKey()`, the acceptance checklist requires seeded rows to be produced by that call, and the table stays administrator-only. | Low | — |

## 30. Remediation of the ATF execution and evidence review

Two decisions, `D-556` and `D-557`. Both were taken while resolving findings raised when the delivered artifacts were tested for **runnability** rather than for content: an attempt was made to execute the ten Automated Test Framework suites on the target instance, and the instance was found hibernating — every path, including paths that do not exist, answering `HTTP 200` with a byte-identical HTML page from the edge tier. Nothing ran. What that produced was not a defect list about the tests but two defects about **what the delivery claims**: one place where a passing gate reads as a stronger claim than it is, and one place where a sleeping instance would be mistaken for a failed deployment.

**Three corrections in the same round earn no row**, because Rule 1's non-trivial test is the inclusion criterion of this file and each had exactly one sane option. Six statements of the ingestion action count still read **five actions** and **four action calls** after `D-397` raised the graph to seven — in the ATF guide's entry condition, its half-B description and its criterion-4 evidence contract, in the build-order diagram of `manual-build-instructions.md`, and in the validation checklist's half-B item — so an operator following the entry condition would build five actions and then fail the very assertion that condition blessed, which asserts `shouldbe: 7`. Those six now state seven, and `D-104` carries the `D-397` amendment marker that `D-102` and `D-247` already carried. In the same sentence of the criterion-4 evidence contract, the orchestrator's declared-output count read **six** where the step-110 message prints the substituted `INGEST_OUT` value, 15 for Crunchbase and 13 for LinkedIn, which is the count guide 02 declares and the assertion checks; the evidence contract now names those. And this file's own tranche table stated a total of 446 rows against a `Summary counts` figure of 455, its arithmetic sentence omitted section 23 and used 40 for a section the table itself gives as 41, and the section map carried no row for section 23 — arithmetic errors in a table this round had to extend, corrected to the figure the sections themselves sum to.

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks | Risk level | Flags |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D-556** | **Declare the ATF operation-level coverage boundary** in the coverage gate of [`../../servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/manual-build/05-atf-test-suites.md`](../../servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/manual-build/05-atf-test-suites.md), stating the exact reach of the suite across the delivered API — **9** of the 31 operations invoked over HTTP with their responses asserted, **3** asserted at the record level by reading `sys_ws_operation` and its script without being invoked, **19** carrying no assertion of any kind — naming all three sets, and stating that a passing gate is therefore not a claim that the write surface is tested. | Extend suite 9 with a test per uncovered operation, which is the fix the finding's own severity invites. Or add the missing calls as further **steps** on the six existing tests, leaving the test count untouched. Or say nothing, on the reasoning that the gate never claimed to be per operation. | The suite and test counts are **fixed at 10 and 36** by the specification, and the guide blocks delivery on 11 as firmly as on 9, so new tests would breach the gate they were strengthening. Adding 22 operation calls as steps was the closer call and was rejected on two grounds: the specification states what the six REST tests assert — response shape, pagination with an agreeing `total_count`, and the 429 contract — and per-operation CRUD is not in it; and the step count is quoted as a derivation in four documents, so an unbudgeted expansion of it would create exactly the cross-document count drift this same round had to repair in six places. Saying nothing was the delivered position and it is the one option that is actually wrong: a reader who sees `100 % of the 6 REST resources` beside a 31-operation API concludes the API is covered, and 19 operations reach delivery with no automated regression test at all. Naming them costs one table and makes the boundary a stated limit rather than an inferred one. | The declaration records a real gap without closing it, and a reader may take the record as a discharge. Mitigated by the boundary stating what is unevidenced in behavioural terms — each unlisted operation's own field allowlist, validation messages, status codes and record effects — and by routing the closure to API-level testing rather than to an eleventh suite. The counts are also now a maintenance obligation: an operation added to the API without a test moves a row from one line of that table to another, and nothing enforces the update but the rename-impact discipline the guide already carries. | Medium | `DEV` |
| **D-557** | Add a **sixth response form** to [`../../servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/validation-gates.md`](../../servicenow-startup-tracker-poc/docs/validation-gates.md) — `HTTP 200` with a non-JSON content type — and classify it as **not a gate outcome at all**: the gate is not evaluated, not recorded as a failure, and **explicitly does not initiate the rollback**; the whole gate set restarts from pre-flight 1. Gate the failure-handling procedure on that test as its step 0. | Rely on pre-flight 1, which already applies the four-part test before the import begins. Or treat the form as an ordinary gate failure, which is the delivered behaviour. Or add a content assertion to each of the eleven gates' pass conditions separately. | The gates run **after** the commit, and the four-part pre-flight test had already passed by then, so nothing in the post-commit path re-tested it — while the failure-handling procedure initiates the rollback "immediately for any status other than `HTTP 500`" whose pass condition fails. A hibernated instance, or merely a session that expired mid-sequence, answers `200` with HTML; every gate's pass condition then fails on a missing `result`, and the procedure would delete a scope over a condition that is not a deployment defect. That is the plan's one irreversible operation firing on a false signal. Restating the four-part test once, as a form with its own disposition table, closes it for all eleven gates and the four diagnostics at once; per-gate assertions would restate the same test fifteen times and let one be forgotten. | The classification depends on an operator applying step 0 before step 1, and a scripted gate runner that checks only the status will still misread the condition — which is why the form is listed **first** among the `200` forms and why the section states the status-only trap explicitly. A second risk is the opposite error: a genuine platform fault that happens to answer non-JSON is now recorded as "not evaluated" rather than as a failure, so a broken deployment could be retried indefinitely. Bounded by the disposition requiring the observed content type, body length and first 200 characters to be recorded every time, so a repeating non-answer is visible as a pattern. | Medium | — |

---

## Index of the five Rule 3 entries

**Rule 3 (Critical Decision Review Document)** requires a review artifact at `docs/review/CRITICAL_DECISIONS.md` listing the five highest-risk decisions, ordered highest first, each with its alternatives, a rationale citing code or convention, a risk level and a named reviewer persona with what that persona should check. Those five entries are a **strict subset** of this log. The table below is the correspondence, and it is verifiable by filtering the `Flags` column of every section above for `R3-`.

| Rule 3 rank | Reviewer persona | Risk level | Decision rows in this log | Subject |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **1** | Security | **High** | **D-024**, with D-025, D-026, D-027, D-028, D-041, D-119, D-246, D-254, D-267, D-292, D-295, D-328 | The field-level access-control design — both layers mandatory, the access-control-respecting read path, per-element checks with the key omitted rather than nulled, and the administrator-override trap that makes an administrator walkthrough appear to prove enforcement never tested. |
| **2** | DevOps | **High** | **D-076**, with D-067, D-068, D-070, D-108, D-167, D-168, D-249, D-255, D-256, D-260, D-262 | The scope-deletion rollback, the one genuinely irreversible operation in the delivery — permitted on a proven clean install only, behind five preconditions, with a non-destructive backout on an upgrade — together with the eleven required gates, the objectively established release floor and the preview-duration exposure of a 313-record update set against the 600-second preview timeout. |
| **3** | API / Integration | **High** | **D-043**, with D-044, D-045, D-111, D-229, D-230, D-236, D-249, D-250, D-266 | Credential handling — both aliases referenced by name with zero secret material anywhere, the Crunchbase key stored in a Basic-authentication alias and transported as an `X-cb-user-key` header, LinkedIn's move to OAuth 2.0, and the branch between the declarative REST step and the scoped connection-resolution script that keeps the build free of packaged integration content. |
| **4** | Data / SME | **Medium** | **D-010**, with D-011, D-012, D-013, D-238, D-244, D-245, D-270, D-272, D-273 | The data-integrity cluster — the join table authoritative for every write with `participating_investors` carried beside it as a stored read-only one-way projection and assembled from the join table for the API, the natural identity keys that make a replay update rather than duplicate, the cascade rules the requirements never specify, and the stored portfolio count with its drift risk when an import path skips business rules. |
| **5** | UX | **Medium** | **D-055**, with D-056, D-057, D-296, D-325, D-326, D-327, D-330, D-331, D-332, D-333, D-334, D-335, D-336 | The interface deviations — five profile tabs instead of the legacy six with Team re-specified as People and Similar Companies dropped for want of competitor data, query-parameter routing in place of path-style routes, and the deliberate 1024-pixel viewport floor. |

The bolded row in each group is the primary decision the review entry names; the others are the facets that entry also covers. Rule 3 additionally requires irreversible operations, authorization decisions and ambiguity-resolving assumptions to be called out, and all three categories are present in this log:

| Rule 3 call-out category | Rows |
| --- | --- |
| Irreversible operation | Two rows, both flagged `IRREV`. **D-076** — the scope-deletion rollback, the only irreversible operation the delivery performs. And **D-194** — the decision that makes that rollback reachable only on a confirmed clean install, with back-out and restore taking its place on an upgrade. No erasure or minimisation operation is delivered, so none is counted here; the absence is flag `F19`. |
| Authorization decision | Eight rows, all flagged `AUTHZ`. **D-024** — the two-layer field-level access-control design. **D-119** — the platform element check as the access oracle, with no helper reporting on itself. **D-109** and **D-246** — the inbound test caller, made web-service-access-only and then made disposable, created and destroyed inside its own test transaction. **D-254** — the read route opened on the seven entity tables and closed on the three supporting tables. **D-267** — the role test that authorizes an aggregate count. **D-292** — the three mechanisms that guarantee the test credential is destroyed. **D-310** — sealing all ten tables so the Scripted REST API is the only route to a row, which is the decision that makes the field-level design enforceable. |
| Ambiguity-resolving assumptions | **D-008** (`AMB-1`) the seven-table count; **D-010** (`AMB-2`) the List-versus-join-table mechanism; **D-029** (`AMB-3`) the inclusion-filter field; **D-232** (`AMB-4`) the collision between cleaning rule 3 and the binding choice lists. |

## Index of deviation rows

Rule 1 (Explainability) treats an unexplained deviation from a literal or obvious reading of the requirements as a defect. **256 rows are marked `DEV`, and every one of them appears below, in row order.** 29 were identified as the artifacts were designed, in sections 1 to 10; the remaining 227 arrived as the delivered artifacts were reviewed, in sections 11 to 30. Each entry below is the deviation as that row's own Decision cell states it, so an entry cannot describe a decision other than the one it names, and this index cannot drift out of step with the body as rows are added. Read the row itself for the alternatives, the rationale and the risks.

| # | Row | The deviation, as that row states it |
| --- | --- | --- |
